Erik Skibsted, Matematisk Institut, Aarhus Universitet Ny Munkegade 8000 Aarhus C, Denmark Xue Ping Wang, Laboratoire de Mathématiques Jean Leray UMR CNRS 6629, Nantes Université 44322 Nantes Cedex, France

Chapter 1
Introduction, examples and notation

1
Scope and results

The spectral and scattering theory for the quantum mechanical one-body problem at zero
energy is a well studied subject. The classical theory
[
JK
,
JN1
,
JN2
]

involves a real potential
V(x)
on
{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
fastly decaying at least like

{\mathcal{O}}(|x|^{-\rho})

for some
\rho>2
. The slowly decaying case for which the decay
rate
\rho\in(0,2)
requires additional conditions, roughly sign
conditions
[
Ya1
,
Ya2
,
Na
,
FS
,
DS1
,
DS2
]
.
The critical case is defined by
V(x)\approx C|x|^{-2}
, possibly
with angular dependence, and the results depend on the coupling
constant
[
Wa5
,
SW
]
. The obtained results for the above models
are highly model and case sensitive and include possible existence of zero
energy bound and/or
resonance states as well as zero
energy asymptotics of the resolvent and scattering matrix quantities
(like scattering phase shifts). Thresholds of an
N
-body
Schrödinger operator are eigenvalues of the sub-Hamiltonians. There
exists much less literature on threshold spectral analysis for the
N
-body problem. We only mention
[
Wa2
]
on the resolvent expansion in a special case of the lowest threshold which is the bottom of the essential spectrum.

The goal of the present work is to present a systematic study of
spectral and scattering theory for the quantum mechanical
N
-body
problem at any negative two-cluster threshold
\lambda_{0}
,
i.e.
,
\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue of (possibly several) sub-Hamiltonians associated with two-cluster decomposition,
but not of those with three or more clusters. These
restrictions on the nature
of the considered threshold exclude the presence of the Efimov
effect there. So for example for the (dynamical nuclei physics)
3
-body
problem, the threshold zero is excluded from
our analysis, while all other
thresholds for this model are negative of two-cluster type. (We shall later in this chapter
give precise definitions.) Philosophically, the two-cluster threshold
problem is amenable to simplification in terms of an effective one-body problem by the Feshbach-Grushin dimension reduction
method. This is indeed realized in
[
Wa2
]
for fastly decaying pair
potentials for the case of the lowest threshold
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
(assumed
non-multiple).
However in the present work we extend the
framework considerably, so that it covers the usual atom
physics models (see Subsections
2.1
and

3.1
) for which the slowly decaying
nature of the Coulomb pair potentials requires refined analysis. Also
we include the cases where the two-cluster threshold
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}

as well as multiple two-cluster and degenerate eigenvalue cases, which
also call for refined analysis, in particular micro-local analysis.

One main ingredient which enables us to attain the goal of spectral
analysis at any two-cluster threshold is the Mourre’s estimate for
the Hamiltonian with one threshold removed. For a given two-cluster
threshold
\lambda_{0}
, the restriction of the total Hamiltonian onto the
orthogonal complement of the associated spectral subspace is a
non-local
N
-body Hamiltonian for which
\lambda_{0}
is no longer a
threshold. We essentially prove the Mourre’s estimate at
\lambda_{0}
for this reduced Hamiltonian and deduce the limiting absorption principles and micro-local resolvent estimates. The limiting absorption principles are used to construct an appropriate Grushin problem such that we can reduce the two-cluster problem to an effective one-body problem near an arbitrary two-cluster threshold.

To be more concrete let us now consider the dynamical nuclei physics
model from Subsection
2.1
(see
(
13
)) and assume that the particle dimension
n=3
. Let a
two-cluster decompostion
a=(C_{1},C_{2})
of
N
particles
be given. We then
write the full Hamiltonian as

H=H^{1}\otimes 1\otimes 1+1\otimes H^{2}\otimes 1+1\otimes 1\otimes p_{a}^{2}+I_{a}

where
H^{k}
,
k=1,2
, are
cluster-Hamiltonians (defined like for
H
in their center of mass
frames),
p_{a}^{2}
is the inter-cluster kinetic energy Hamiltonian and

I_{a}
is the
inter-cluster potential
. Suppose
\lambda_{a}=\lambda_{0}
is an
eigenvalue of the sub-Hamiltonian
H^{a}=H^{1}\otimes 1+1\otimes H^{2}
(
\lambda_{0}
being of two-cluster type, see (
27
)). Picking a
corresponding orthonormal basis
\varphi^{a}_{1},\dots\varphi^{a}_{m}\in\ker(H^{a}-\lambda_{0})\subset L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})
,

m=m_{a}
, the
effective
inter-cluster potential
is the
m\times m
matrix-valued function
in the relative position
variable of the two clusters, viz.
R=R_{1}-R_{2}
,

\displaystyle V(R)_{kl}:=\langle\varphi^{a}_{k},I_{a}\varphi^{a}_{l}\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})}=Q_{1}Q_{2}\delta_{kl}|R|^{-1}+Q_{kl}(\widehat{R})|R|^{-2}+{\mathcal{O}}\big(|R|^{-3}\big).

(1)

Here
Q_{1}
and

Q_{2}
are the
total charge of the particles in the clusters
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
,
respectively, and
\delta_{kl}
is the Kronecker symbol. In
addition we denote by
Q_{a}
the matrix-valued homogeneous
potential
\big(Q_{kl}\big)
and
\widehat{R}=R/\lvert R\rvert
. Let
P^{a}
denote the orthogonal (rank
m
) projection onto

\ker(H^{a}-\lambda_{0})
in

L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})
. Then obviously
\Pi^{a}=P^{a}\otimes 1

projects onto
the span of functions of the form
\varphi^{a}\otimes f_{a}
,

\varphi^{a}\in\ker(H^{a}-\lambda_{0})
, in
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
.

In terms of (
1
) a relevant classification reads:

Case 1

(slowly decaying case)
Q_{1}Q_{2}\neq 0
.

Case 2

(critically decaying case)
Q_{1}Q_{2}=0
and the
function

Q_{a}\neq 0
.

Case 3

(fastly decaying case)
Q_{1}Q_{2}=0
and the
function

Q_{a}=0
.

In general
\lambda_{0}
might be a
multiple two-cluster threshold, which might suggest that we group
the set of thresholds
a
for which
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{a})
, say denoted by
\widetilde{A}
, into
\widetilde{A}={\mathcal{A}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{3}

specified as follows.

{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
:

the effective
inter-cluster interaction is to leading order attractive Coulombic,
i.e.
Q_{1}Q_{2}<0
.

{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
:

the effective
inter-cluster interaction is to leading order repulsive Coulombic,
i.e.
Q_{1}Q_{2}>0
.

{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
:

the effective
inter-cluster interaction is
{\mathcal{O}}(|x_{a}|^{-2})
, i.e.
Q_{1}Q_{2}=0
.

Clearly the elements of
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
are classified as
Case 1, while the elements of
{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
are classified either as
Case 2 or Case 3. This motivates the splitting
{\mathcal{A}}_{3}={\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{cd}}_{3}\cup{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{3}
by specifying

{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{cd}}_{3}=\{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}|\,Q_{a}\neq 0\}\text{ and }{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{3}=\{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}|\,Q_{a}=0\}

corresponding to Case 2
and Case 3, respectively.

For any
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
there are computable numbers

s_{a}\geq 1
and
d_{a}\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
determined by spectral properties of the
vector-valued Schrödinger operator on the unit-sphere
{\mathbb{S}}^{2}
with
the matrix-valued potential
Q_{a}

(see Section
15
) such
that in terms of standard
weighted
L^{2}
-space and weighted Sobolov-space notation (see
Subsection
4.1
for definitions), referring
here to our
most general result (see Section
15
):

Theorem 1.1
.

For any two-cluster
threshold
\lambda_{0}
:

1.

The space of locally
H^{1}
solutions to

(H-\lambda_{0})u=0
in

\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}\Pi^{a}L^{2}_{-3/4}+\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\Pi^{a}L^{2}_{(-3/2)^{+}}+\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}}\Pi^{a}L^{2}_{(-\min\{3/2,\,s_{a}\})^{+}}+L^{2}_{-1/2},

(2a)

say denoted by
\mathcal{E}
,
has finite dimension.

2.

If
{\mathcal{A}}_{3}=\emptyset
, then
{\mathcal{E}}\subset H^{1}_{\infty}
.

3.

The dimension of the space of

resonance states

n_{\mathrm{res}}=\dim\big({\mathcal{E}}/\ker(H-\lambda_{0})_{|{H^{1}}}\big)\leq\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}}d_{a}.

4.

The numbers
s_{a}=3/2
and
d_{a}=m_{a}
for any
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{3}
. In particular if
{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{cd}}_{3}=\emptyset
, then (
2a
) simplifies as

\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}\Pi^{a}L^{2}_{-3/4}+\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\cup{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{3}}\Pi^{a}L^{2}_{(-3/2)^{+}}+L^{2}_{-1/2},

(2b)

and

3
reads

n_{\mathrm{res}}\leq\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{3}}\,m_{a}.

One may view Theorem
1.1
as a
version of
the well-known Rellich theorem for non-threshold energies

[
AIIS
, Theorem 1.4]
(see also Theorem
3.12
)
although the above analogue at a two-cluster threshold is
considerably more complex. For example the
analogue of (
2a
) and (
2b
) in the continuous
spectrum away from thresholds
reads
L^{2}_{-1/2}
,
and we note that for almost all (probably valid for all) such energies the space of
generalized eigenfunctions in
L^{2}_{-1/2-\epsilon}
(for any

\epsilon>0
) has
infinite dimension
[
Sk6
]
. In comparison in the context of Theorem

1.1
with
\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}_{1}=\{a\}
we
show that the space of
generalized eigenfunctions at
\lambda_{0}
in
\Pi^{a}L^{2}_{-3/4-\epsilon}+L^{2}_{-1/2-\epsilon}
is infinitely dimensional (see Theorem
6.13

2
).

The proof of Theorem
1.1
is complicated, in fact we give a full
proof only under the two technical conditions (
245a
) and
(
245b
) (not to be elaborated on in this
introduction), treating the general case in a somewhat sketchy fashion.

One of the threshold phenomena indicated by Theorem

1.1
is the possible existence of resonance
states combined freely with the possible existence of
L^{2}
eigenfunctions
at the two-cluster threshold
\lambda_{0}
. This is completely analogous
to the situation for the one-body problem
for fastly decaying potentials
[
JK
]
(exhibiting a somewhat similar
sophisticated Rellich theorem at zero energy), giving rise to the
classification into a Regular Case (where
\lambda_{0}
is neither an
eigenvalue nor a
resonance of
H
) and Exceptional Cases 1,2 and 3 (see
Subsection
16.1.1
). The resolvent asymptotics
at zero energy for the one-body problem is determined by this
classification. It is a separate issue for us to obtain similar resolvent
asymptotics at
\lambda_{0}
in the present framework. However
our analysis is not complete, mainly due to lack of
strong
decay of Coulombic potentials hampering the analysis. Of course the Regular Case is the
easiest case and we shall actually treat this with

\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\cup{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm{fd}}_{3}
(see Theorem
5.43
). For the Exceptional Cases 1 and
3 (defined by the presence of a resonance) we show the following
result.

Theorem 1.2

(Exceptional
point of
1
st or
3
rd kind)
.

Let
\lambda_{0}
be any
two-cluster threshold for which
n_{\mathrm{res}}\geq 1
,
i.e.
\lambda_{0}
is a
resonance of
H
. Suppose the technical conditions
(
245a
) and (
245b
) (referred to above),

\ran\Pi_{H}\subset L^{2}_{t}\text{ for some }t>3/2,

(3)

where
\Pi_{H}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\ker(H-\lambda_{0})
(i.e. the eigenprojection if
\lambda_{0}
is
an eigenvalue of
H
and zero otherwise), and suppose

\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}_{2}\cup{\mathcal{A}}^{\rm{fd}}_{3}.

(4)

Then the following asymptotics hold for
R(\lambda_{0}+z)=(H-\lambda_{0}+z)^{-1}
as an operator from
H^{-1}_{s}
to
H^{1}_{-s}
,
s>1
, for

z\to 0
in
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\pm}=\{\operatorname{Re}z\geq 0,\,\pm\operatorname{Im}z>0\}

and for some
\epsilon=\epsilon(s)>0
.

R(\lambda_{0}+z)=-z^{-1}\Pi_{H}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{n_{\mathrm{res}}}\langle{u_{j},\cdot}\rangle u_{j}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}).

(5)

Here

\{u_{1},\dots,u_{n_{\mathrm{res}}}\}\subset H^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}
is a basis of resonance states of

H
being independent
of the choice of the sign of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\pm}
.

Among the appearing conditions (
3
) is the
‘unpleasant one’. It is an implicit (possibly redundant) condition appearing as an artifact of
our methods. If
\lambda_{0}
is exceptional
point of
1
st kind, (
3
) is obviously fulfilled since
then
\Pi_{H}=0
. Our Theorem
5.43
as well
the above Theorem
1.2
require
explicitly
{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{cd}}_{3}=\emptyset
.

Under a spectral condition
for certain elements of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{cd}}_{3}
(those for which
(
8
) in Subsection
1.1
is violated) oscillatory
behaviour of the resolvent near the two-cluster threshold is
expected. This is expected thanks to arguments of
[
SW
]
.

1.1
Applications to threshold scattering

Here we briefly outline our main applications in Chapter

6
. One of our results concerns the following generalization
of a result from
[
DS1
,
DS2
]
(see also
[
Fr
]
).

Theorem 1.3
.

Suppose that
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}

is a
two-cluster threshold, the technical conditions
(
245a
) and (
245b
),

\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}_{1},

(6)

and suppose the Regular Case (i.e. that
\lambda_{0}

is neither an eigenvalue nor a resonance of
H
). Let
{\mathcal{C}}

denote the set of scattering channels

\alpha=(a,\lambda_{0},\varphi_{\alpha})
with
a\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}
(note that

\lambda_{0}
is a simple eigenvalue of
H^{a}
). For
\alpha,\beta\in{\mathcal{C}}
the element of the
scattering matrix
S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)
(modelled after

[
DS1
,
DS2
]
) is well-defined for
\lambda
slightly above

\lambda_{0}
and possessing a strong limit as
\lambda\to(\lambda_{0})_{+}
. Moreover the
singular support of the limiting element
S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda_{0})
fulfills

\displaystyle{\mathrm{sing\,supp\,}}S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda_{0})\quad\begin{cases}&\subset\{(\omega,\omega^{\prime})\mid\omega\cdot\omega^{\prime}=-1\}\quad\text{for}\quad\alpha=\beta,\\
&=\emptyset\quad\text{for}\quad\alpha\neq\beta.\end{cases}

The proof of Theorem
1.3
may be
considered as an extension of the one used in
[
DS1
,
DS2
]
to obtain
a similar ‘semi-classical’ result on the
scattering matrix
S_{\rm cou}(E)
for the one-body problem with an attractive Coulomb
potential. See also
[
Va1
,
Va2
]
for
N
-body scattering matrices in the short-range case.

Another result under (
6
) concerns the
difference

S_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)-S_{\rm cou}(\lambda-\lambda_{0});\quad\lambda\in[\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\delta].

Under conditions, in particular including the
non-multiple property
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=1
(primarily
used to simplify
the presentation) however covering the case where
the
two-cluster threshold
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
, we show that this
difference is a
‘partial smoothing operator’ (see Theorem
6.10
and Remark

6.11

3
). Yet another
result is a characterization of the limiting element

S_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda_{0})
given by asymptotics in terms of
appropriate ‘channel quasi-modes’ (see Theorem
6.13

2
).

This leads to another subject of interest, more precisely

non-transmission
at
\lambda_{0}
. This is a ‘geometric
concept’ amounting to the feature

\lVert 1-S_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}S_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)\rVert\to 0\text{ for
}\lambda\to(\lambda_{0})_{+}.

(7)

We derive a formula under (
6
)
(see Corollary
6.15
) indicating that transmission does
occur in this case if
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
(see also Remarks

6.16
and
6.29

4
).

In contrast to the attractive slowly decaying case we do prove non-transmission
in the following three cases (assuming as above in all cases the non-multiple
property
\#\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=1
):

1.

Effective repulsive Coulombic
case, i.e.
\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}_{2}
.

2.

I_{a}(x^{a}=0)=0
, ‘above the
Hardy limit’ and
\lambda_{0}
be regular.

3.

I_{a}(x^{a}=0)=0
and ‘fastly decaying case’,
i.e.
\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{3}
, and
\lambda_{0}
be
‘maximally exceptional of
1
st kind’.

A special case of II) is that
\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{3}
and
\lambda_{0}
neither be an eigenvalue nor a
resonance. The notions in II) and III) are in general given as follows
(see also Section
21
). The
phrase ‘above the
Hardy limit’ refers to a spectral property of the
vector-valued Schrödinger operator on the unit-sphere
{\mathbb{S}}^{2}
with
the matrix-valued potential
Q_{a}
(writing
\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}=\{a\}
), more
precisely

\inf\sigma\big(-\Delta_{\theta}+Q_{a}(\theta)\big)>-1/4.

(8)

For III) the potential
Q_{a}=0
and ‘maximally
exceptional of
1
st kind’ refers to Exceptional Case 1 and the condition

n_{\mathrm{res}}=m_{a}=\dim\ker(H^{a}-\lambda_{0})
. We note for
comparison that if

m_{a}>1
and

n_{\mathrm{res}}=\{1,\dots,m_{a}-1\}
then indeed transmission can
occur for
\lambda_{0}
be
exceptional of
1
st kind (see
Subsection
21.4
).

The last subject of interest concerns total cross-sections for atom-ion
scattering. It is an observed phenomenon at the very beginning of the quantum mechanics that when there is no dipole moment for the atom, the total cross-sections are finite. A mathematical proof for this physics folklore is given in
[
JKW
]
.
The operator under consideration is a special case of the dynamical nuclei physics
model from Subsection
2.1
with the particle
dimension
n=3
. Assume
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}

is a two-cluster threshold, the technical conditions
(
245a
) and (
245b
), (
3
) and
\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{3}
. It is known from
[
JKW
]
that for any channel
\alpha=(a,\lambda_{0},\varphi_{\alpha})
,

a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}
, and any incident direction
\omega\in{\mathbb{S}}^{2}
,

\text{the total
cross-section }\sigma_{\alpha}(\lambda,\omega)

is finite for non-threshold
\lambda
’s
above
\lambda_{0}
. In the present work we
derive bounds and asymptotics of this quantity as
\lambda\to(\lambda_{0})_{+}
(see Section
22
). The result depends on whether
\lambda_{0}
is regular
or exceptional of
2
nd kind (yielding bounded asymptotics, see Theorem
6.33
) or if
\lambda_{0}
is
of
1
st or
3
rd kind (yielding
(\lambda-\lambda_{0})^{-1}
type
unbounded asymptotics, see Theorem
6.34
). Our proof
relies on the derivation of Theorem
1.2
.

2
Many-body Schrödinger operators

Let
H
denote the many-body Schrödinger operator obtained
by the removal of the center of mass from the total
Hamiltonian

\widetilde{H}=-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{2m_{j}}\Delta_{x_{j}}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}V_{ij}(x_{i}-x_{j}),\quad x_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},

(9)

where
x_{j}
and
m_{j}
denote the position and mass of the
j
’th
particle. The pair potentials

V_{ij}
are assumed to be real and relatively compact with respect to

-\Delta
in
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
, and they satisfy for some
\rho>0
the condition

|V_{ij}(y)|\leq C_{ij}|y|^{-\rho}\text{ for }y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\text{
with }|y|>R,

for some
R>0
.
However we shall need some extra regularity. It is convenient to use the following condition.

Condition 1.4
.

There exists
\rho>0
such that for all pair potentials
V_{ij}

there is a splitting
V_{ij}=V_{ij}^{(1)}+V_{ij}^{(2)}
, where

(1)

V_{ij}^{(1)}
is smooth and

\partial^{\alpha}_{y}V_{ij}^{(1)}(y)={\mathcal{O}}\big(|y|^{-\rho-|\alpha|}\big).

(10)

(2)

V_{ij}^{(2)}
is compactly supported and

V_{ij}^{(2)}(-\Delta+1)^{-1}\text{ is compact on
}L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}_{y}).

(11)

The Hamiltonian
H
is regarded as a self-adjoint operator
on
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
, where
{\mathbf{X}}
is the
n(N-1)
dimensional real vector
space
{\mathbf{X}}:=\big\{\sum_{j=1}^{N}m_{j}x_{j}=0\big\}
.
Let
{\mathcal{A}}
denote the set of all cluster
decompositions of the
N
-particle system. The notation
a_{\max}
and

a_{\min}
refers to the
1
-cluster and
N
-cluster decompositions,
respectively.
Let for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
the notation
\#a
denote the number of
clusters in
a
.
For
i,j\in\{1,\dots,N\}
,
i<j
, we denote by
(ij)
the

(N-1)
-cluster decomposition given by letting
C=\{i,j\}
form a
cluster and all other particles
l\notin C
form
1
-particle clusters. We write
(ij)\subset a
if
i
and
j
belong to the same cluster
in
a
. More general, we write
b\subset a
if each cluster of
b

is a subset of a cluster of
a
. If
a
is a
k
-cluster decomposition,
a=(C_{1},\dots,C_{k})
,
we let

{\mathbf{X}}^{a}=\big\{x\in{\mathbf{X}}\mid\sum_{l\in C_{j}}m_{l}x_{l}=0,j=1,\dots,k\big\}={\mathbf{X}}^{C_{1}}\oplus\cdots\oplus{\mathbf{X}}^{C_{k}},

and

{\mathbf{X}}_{a}=\big\{x\in{\mathbf{X}}\mid x_{i}=x_{j}\mbox{ if }i,j\in C_{m}\mbox{ for some }m\in\{1,\dots,k\}\big\}.

Note that
a\subset b\Leftrightarrow{\mathbf{X}}^{a}\subset{\mathbf{X}}^{b}
. Moreover
{\mathbf{X}}^{a}
and
{\mathbf{X}}_{a}
give an orthogonal decomposition for
{\mathbf{X}}

equipped with
the quadratic form

q(x)=\sum_{j}2m_{j}|x_{j}|^{2},\hskip 28.45274ptx\in{{\mathbf{X}}}.

For
x\in{\mathbf{X}}
, we have the corresponding orthogonal decomposition:

x=x^{a}+x_{a}
with
x^{a}=\pi^{a}x\in{\mathbf{X}}^{a}
and
x_{a}=\pi_{a}x\in{\mathbf{X}}_{a}
.

With this notation, the many-body Schrödinger operator
H
introduced above
can be written in the form

H=H_{0}+V

where
H_{0}=p^{2}
is (minus) the Laplace-Beltrami operator on the
Euclidean space
({\mathbf{X}},q)
and

V=V(x)=\sum_{a=(ij)\in{\mathcal{A}}}V_{a}(x^{a})
with
V_{a}(x^{a})=V_{ij}(x_{i}-x_{j})
for the
(N-1)
-cluster decomposition
a=(ij)
. More precisely, for example,

\displaystyle x^{(12)}=\big(\tfrac{m_{2}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}(x_{1}-x_{2}),-\tfrac{m_{1}}{m_{1}+m_{2}}(x_{1}-x_{2}),0,\dots,0\big).

We note the following geometric properties for
N\geq 3
: For all

a,b\in{\mathcal{A}}
with

\#a=2
,
\#b=N-1
and
b\not\subset a

\displaystyle\ran\big(\pi^{b}\pi^{a}\big)=\ran\,\pi^{b},

(12a)

\displaystyle\pi^{b}:{\mathbf{X}}_{a}\to{\mathbf{X}}^{b}\text{ is
bijective}.

(12b)

2.1
Principal example, dynamical nuclei

Consider a system of
N
particles interacting by Coulomb forces. The
Hamiltonian then reads

H=-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{2m_{j}}\Delta_{x_{j}}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}q_{i}q_{j}|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{-1},\quad x_{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n},\,n\geq 3,

(13)

where
x_{j}
,
m_{j}
and
q_{j}
denote the position, mass and charge of
the
j
’th particle, respectively.
H
is regarded as a self-adjoint operator in
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
(with mass center removed).

Let us consider a two-cluster decomposition
a=(C_{1},C_{2})
. For
convenience assume
C_{1}=\{1,\dots,J\}
and
C_{2}=\{J+1,\dots,N\}
. We
can write

H=H^{1}\otimes 1\otimes 1+1\otimes H^{2}\otimes 1+1\otimes 1\otimes p_{a}^{2}+I_{a}

where
H^{k}
,
k=1,2
, are
cluster-Hamiltonians (defined similarly in their center of mass
frames) and

I_{a}=\sum_{i\in C_{1},\,j\in C_{2}}q_{i}q_{j}|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{-1}.

To expand
I_{a}
we let for
k=1,2

\displaystyle Q_{k}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\in C_{k}}q_{j},\,M_{k}=\sum_{j\in C_{k}}m_{j},

\displaystyle R_{k}

\displaystyle=R_{k}(x)=\sum_{j\in C_{k}}\tfrac{m_{j}}{M_{k}}x_{j},\,\widetilde{Q}_{k}=\widetilde{Q}_{k}(x^{C_{k}})=\sum_{j\in C_{k}}q_{j}(x_{j}-R_{k}),

\displaystyle M

\displaystyle=M_{1}+M_{2},\,R=R_{1}-R_{2},

and we decompose for all
x\in{\mathbf{X}}

\displaystyle x

\displaystyle=x^{C_{1}}+x^{C_{2}}+x_{a},

\displaystyle x^{C_{1}}

\displaystyle=(x_{1}-R_{1},\dots,x_{J}-R_{1},0,\dots,0)\in{\mathbf{X}}^{C_{1}},

\displaystyle x^{C_{2}}

\displaystyle=(0,\dots,0,x_{J+1}-R_{2},\dots,x_{N}-R_{2})\in{\mathbf{X}}^{C_{2}},

\displaystyle x_{a}

\displaystyle=\big(\tfrac{M_{2}}{M}R,\dots,\tfrac{M_{2}}{M}R,-\tfrac{M_{1}}{M}R,\dots,-\tfrac{M_{1}}{M}R\big)\in{\mathbf{X}}_{a}.

Note that indeed the center of charge
\widetilde{Q}_{k}

is a function of
x^{C_{k}}
.

Consequently we can expand for
i\in C_{1}
and
j\in C_{2}

\displaystyle|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{-1}=|R|^{-1}-\tfrac{R}{|R|^{3}}\cdot\big((x_{i}-R_{1})-(x_{j}-R_{2})\big)+{\mathcal{O}}\big(|R|^{-3}\big)|x^{a}|^{2}.

This is in the regime
|R|\to\infty
and

|x_{i}-R_{1}|+|x_{j}-R_{2}|\leq\tfrac{1}{2}|R|
.

Whence in turn we obtain for
|R|\to\infty

\displaystyle I_{a}=Q_{1}Q_{2}|R|^{-1}+\tfrac{R}{|R|^{3}}\cdot\big(Q_{1}\widetilde{Q}_{2}(x^{C_{2}})-Q_{2}\widetilde{Q}_{1}(x^{C_{1}})\big)+{\mathcal{O}}\big(|R|^{-3}\big)|x^{a}|^{2},

(14)

which leads to various cases. We use the notation

\varphi^{k}
,
k=1,2
, to denote a cluster bound state (for
the cluster Hamiltonian
H^{k}
) and
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{k}
to denote the
corresponding cluster inner product. The
effective
potential

V(R):=\langle\varphi^{1}\otimes\varphi^{2},I_{a}\varphi^{1}\otimes\varphi^{2}\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})}.

Case 1

V\approx|R|^{-1}
:
Q_{1}Q_{2}\neq 0
.

Case 2

V\approx|R|^{-2}
:

Subcase 2a

Q_{1}\neq 0
,
Q_{2}=0
and

\langle\varphi^{2},\widetilde{Q}_{2}\varphi^{2}\rangle_{2}\neq 0
.

Subcase 2b

Q_{2}\neq 0
,
Q_{1}=0
and

\langle\varphi^{1},\widetilde{Q}_{1}\varphi^{1}\rangle_{1}\neq 0
.

Case 3

V={\mathcal{O}}\big(|R|^{-3}\big)
:

Subcase 3a

Q_{1}=Q_{2}=0
.

Subcase 3b

Q_{1}\neq 0
,
Q_{2}=0
and

\langle\varphi^{2},\widetilde{Q}_{2}\varphi^{2}\rangle_{2}=0
.

Subcase 3c

Q_{2}\neq 0
,
Q_{1}=0
and

\langle\varphi^{1},\widetilde{Q}_{1}\varphi^{1}\rangle_{1}=0
.

Note that for Subcases 2a, 3a and 3b,
assuming sufficient decay of the cluster bound states, the effective
potential

V(R)=\langle\varphi^{1}\otimes\varphi^{2},I_{a}\varphi^{1}\otimes\varphi^{2}\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})}=Q_{1}\tfrac{R}{|R|^{3}}\cdot\langle\varphi^{2},\widetilde{Q}_{2}\varphi^{2}\rangle_{2}+{\mathcal{O}}\big(|R|^{-3}\big).

(15)

Whence indeed
V\approx|R|^{-2}
at infinity in Subcase
2a, while indeed
V={\mathcal{O}}\big(|R|^{-3}\big)
for Subcases 3a and
3b. We can argue similarly for Subcases 2b and 3c. Note also that
|R|^{-2}
is the
critical decay rate

for threshold analysis, cf.
[
SW
]
. Case 1 is the slowly decaying
case. In Case 1 the potential
V\approx|R|^{-1}
, and
V
is said to be

slowly decaying
. For
Q_{1}Q_{2}<0
and
Q_{1}Q_{2}>0
the one-body
results of
[
FS
]
and
[
Na
,
Ya2
]
will be useful, respectively. In Case 3 the effective
potential is said to be

fastly decaying
and other one-body results/techniques will be
useful, cf. for
example
[
JK
]
.
Case 2 (the critical case) is different and rather ‘rich’.

A detailed analysis of the structure of a class
of generalized eigenfunctions at a two-cluster threshold, possibly a
multiple and/or a
non-simple two-cluster threshold, will be carried out for
physical models in Section
15
. (See (
27
) for
the definition of a ‘two-cluster threshold’.)

From the derivation
it follows that it could happen that the second term
{\mathcal{O}}\big(|R|^{-3}\big)
of
(
15
) actual has homogeneity
-3
at infinity. For
example this happens for Subcase 3a exactly when the moments
\widetilde{R}_{1}:=\langle\varphi^{1},\widetilde{Q}_{1}\varphi^{1}\rangle_{1}\neq 0
and
\widetilde{R}_{2}:=\langle\varphi^{2},\widetilde{Q}_{2}\varphi^{2}\rangle_{2}\neq 0
due to the computation for this case

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}\big(|R|^{-3}\big)=\lvert R\rvert^{-5}\big(\lvert R\rvert^{2}\widetilde{R}_{1}\cdot\widetilde{R}_{2}-3(R\cdot\widetilde{R}_{1})(R\cdot\widetilde{R}_{2})\big)+{\mathcal{O}}\big(|R|^{-4}\big)

If certain ‘moments’
vanish for Subcases 3a and 3b the order of the second term of
(
15
) is of the form
{\mathcal{O}}\big(|R|^{-4}\big)
, cf.
[
JKW
, Appendix A]
. In Chapter

5
we shall obtain leading order resolvent expansions for
Case 3 without distinguishing between whether the homogeneous

-3
term vanishes or not.
In Section
22
we shall study a case, where in
fact the effective potential is (at least) of order

{\mathcal{O}}\big(|R|^{-4}\big)
. In the same section an explicit
calculation of the Hamiltonian is given
in terms of so-called clustered atomic coordinates.

Strictly speaking
the distinction between Cases 2 and 3 as defined above makes best
sense for a simple two-cluster threshold and we will not use this
classification in the non-simple case. Rather in the general possibly non-simple case one
needs the following (slightly) different definition, see Section

15
for further details. Let
\lambda_{a}
be a
non-threshold eigenvalue of the sub-Hamiltonian
H^{a}=H^{1}\otimes 1+1\otimes H^{2}
(more precisely, we will need
\lambda_{a}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{2}
, see (
27
)). Picking an
orthonormal basis
\varphi^{a}_{1},\dots\varphi^{a}_{m}\in L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})
,

m=m_{a}
(being one or possibly bigger),
in the range of the corresponding eigenprojection, the
effective
potential
is the
m\times m
-matrix-valued function in the
variable
R=R_{1}-R_{2}

\displaystyle V(R)_{kl}:=\langle\varphi^{a}_{k},I_{a}\varphi^{a}_{l}\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})}=Q_{1}Q_{2}\delta_{kl}|R|^{-1}+Q_{kl}(\widehat{R})|R|^{-2}+{\mathcal{O}}\big(|R|^{-3}\big).

(16)

Here
\delta_{kl}
is the Kronecker symbol and
\widehat{R}=R/\lvert R\rvert
. In terms of (
16
) the more general
(and correct) classification reads:

Case 1

Q_{1}Q_{2}\neq 0
.

Case 2

Q_{1}Q_{2}=0
and the
matrix-valued function

Q_{a}=\big(Q_{kl}\big)\neq 0
.

Case 3

Q_{1}Q_{2}=0
and the
matrix-valued function

Q_{a}=\big(Q_{kl}\big)=0
.

3
N
-body Schrödinger operators with infinite mass
nuclei

In the case of
M\geq 1
infinite mass
nuclei located at
R_{m}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
m=1,\dots,M
, the Hamiltonian reads

H=-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{2m_{j}}\Delta_{x_{j}}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}V_{ij}(x_{i}-x_{j})+\sum_{1\leq j\leq N,\;1\leq m\leq M}V^{\rm ncl}_{jm}(x_{j}-R_{m}),

(17)

where we impose similar conditions on
V^{\rm ncl}_{jm}
as for
V_{ij}
in Condition
1.4
. The
one-body problem
N=1
is included in
(
17
) (the middle term is absent in that case). The configuration space reads
{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbb{R}}^{nN}
, and we
use the metric
q
as before. The ‘electron-electron’
interaction
V_{ij}(x_{i}-x_{j})
takes as before the form
V_{a}(x^{a})
where
x^{a}=\pi^{a}x
,

a=(ij)
, is the
orthogonal projection of
x
onto an
n
-dimensional
subspace. Similarly the ‘electron-nuclei’
interaction
\sum_{1\leq m\leq M}V^{\rm ncl}_{jm}(x_{j}-R_{m})
takes the form
V_{a}(x^{a})
where again
x^{a}=\pi^{a}x
,

a=a(j)
, is the
orthogonal projection of
x
onto an
n
-dimensional
subspace (let
x^{a}=(0,\dots,0,x_{j},0,\dots,0)
, i.e. all other
coordinates than the
j
’th are put equal to zero). Rather than using the cluster decompositions to label a family
of ‘subspaces of internal motion’
\{{\mathbf{X}}^{a}\}
similar to those
considered in Section
2
we prefer henceforth to appeal to abstract
labeling. Precisely we consider the smallest finite family

\{{\mathbf{X}}^{a}\mid a\in{\mathcal{A}}\}
of
subspaces of
{\mathbf{X}}
which is stable under addition and which contains

\{0\}
and the
n
-dimensional
subspaces discussed above. See Section
4
, and see
[
DG
, Section 5.1]
for a discussion
of the abstract
N
-body problem. On the other hand there is a
concrete description of the index set
{\mathcal{A}}
and
this family
\{{\mathbf{X}}^{a}\mid a\in{\mathcal{A}}\}
which can be useful to have in mind: Consider
a=(C_{1},\dots,C_{p})
where
the sets

C_{q}
are disjoint subsets of
\{1,\dots,N\}
. For
p\geq 2
and
q<p
we have
\#C_{q}\geq 2
and we let
{\mathbf{X}}^{C_{q}}=\{x\in{\mathbf{X}}\mid x_{j}=0\text{ if }j\notin C_{q}\text{ and }\sum_{i\in C_{q}}m_{i}x_{i}=0\}
. Either similarly
{\mathbf{X}}^{C_{p}}=\{x\in{\mathbf{X}}\mid x_{j}=0\text{ if }j\notin C_{p}\text{ and }\sum_{i\in C_{p}}m_{i}x_{i}=0\}
(in that case we have
\#C_{p}\geq 2
) or
{\mathbf{X}}^{C_{p}}=\{x\in{\mathbf{X}}\mid x_{j}=0\text{ if }j\notin C_{p}\}
.
In both cases let
correspondingly
{\mathbf{X}}^{a}={\mathbf{X}}^{C_{1}}\oplus\dots\oplus{\mathbf{X}}^{C_{p}}
. Moreover we
supplement by writing
{\mathbf{X}}^{a_{\min}}=\{0\}
where, for example,
a_{\min}:=\emptyset
. This is a concrete labeling of the family of subspaces of internal motion.

The ordering of subspaces yields an ordering
of the abstract set of indices
{\mathcal{A}}
, by definition
a\subset b\Leftrightarrow{\mathbf{X}}^{a}\subset{\mathbf{X}}^{b}
. We denote

{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{X}}^{a_{\max}}
and
{\mathbf{X}}^{a}+{\mathbf{X}}^{b}={\mathbf{X}}^{a\cup b}
. The orthogonal
complement of
{\mathbf{X}}^{a}
is denoted by
{\mathbf{X}}_{a}
. To have a uniform
language we refer to the indices

a\in{\mathcal{A}}
as ‘cluster decompositions’. The length of a chain of
cluster decompositions
a_{1}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq a_{k}
is the
number
k
. This chain is said to connect
a=a_{1}
and
b=a_{k}
. The
maximal length of all chains connecting a given
a\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\max}\}
and

a_{\max}
is denoted by
\#a
. We define
\#a_{\max}=1
and note
that
\#a_{\min}=N+1
. We say
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
is
k
-cluster if
\#a=k
.

We note the following geometric properties for
N\geq 2
: For all

a,b\in{\mathcal{A}}
with

\#a=2
,
\#b=N
and
b\not\subset a

\displaystyle\ran\big(\pi^{b}\pi^{a}\big)=\{0\}\text{ or }\ran\big(\pi^{b}\pi^{a}\big)=\ran\,\pi^{b},

(18a)

\displaystyle\pi^{b}:{\mathbf{X}}_{a}\to{\mathbf{X}}^{b}\text{ is
bijective}.

(18b)

3.1
Principal example, fixed nuclei

Consider a system of
N

n
-dimensional particles,
n\geq 3
, interacting by Coulomb forces. The
Hamiltonian (
17
) then reads

H=-\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{1}{2m_{j}}\Delta_{x_{j}}+\sum_{1\leq i<j\leq N}q_{i}q_{j}|x_{i}-x_{j}|^{-1}+\sum_{1\leq j\leq N,\,1\leq m\leq M,}q_{j}q_{m}^{\rm ncl}|x_{j}-R_{m}|^{-1},

(19)

where
x_{j}
,
m_{j}
and
q_{j}
denote the position, mass and charge of
the
j
’th ‘electron’, and
R_{m}
and
q_{m}^{\rm ncl}
are the
position and charge of
the
m
’th ‘nucleus’.

Consider the two-cluster decomposition

a=(C)
,
C=\{1,\dots,N-1\}
, meaning
{\mathbf{X}}^{a}=\{x=(x_{1},\dots,x_{N})\in{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbb{R}}^{nN}\mid x_{N}=0\}
. Letting
R=x_{N}
we write
H=H^{1}\otimes 1+1\otimes p_{R}^{2}+I_{a}

where
H^{1}
is the
cluster-Hamiltonian (i.e. the Hamiltonian for the first
N-1
electrons) and

\displaystyle I_{a}=\sum_{1\leq i\leq N-1}q_{i}q_{N}|x_{i}-R|^{-1}+\sum_{1\leq m\leq M,}q_{N}q_{m}^{\rm ncl}|R-R_{m}|^{-1}.

Introducing

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq j\leq N-1}q_{j}+\sum_{1\leq m\leq M}q_{m}^{\rm ncl},

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{Q}(x^{a})=\sum_{1\leq j\leq N-1}q_{j}x_{j},

\displaystyle\widetilde{Q}^{\rm ncl}

\displaystyle=\sum_{1\leq m\leq M}q_{m}^{\rm ncl}R_{m},

the asymptotics of
I_{a}

for
|R|\to\infty
reads

\displaystyle I_{a}=q_{N}Q|R|^{-1}+q_{N}\tfrac{R}{|R|^{3}}\cdot\big(\widetilde{Q}(x^{a})+\widetilde{Q}^{\rm ncl}\big)+{\mathcal{O}}\big(|R|^{-3}\big)\big(1+\lvert x^{a}\rvert^{2}\big).

(20)

For the expectation in a cluster bound state
\varphi=\varphi^{a}(x^{a})
with sufficient
decay we consequently obtain the asymptotics for
|R|\to\infty

\displaystyle\langle\varphi,I_{a}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})}=q_{N}Q|R|^{-1}+q_{N}\tfrac{R}{|R|^{3}}\cdot\big(\langle\varphi,\widetilde{Q}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})}+\widetilde{Q}^{\rm ncl}\big)+{\mathcal{O}}\big(|R|^{-3}\big).

(21)

This leads to various cases.

Case 1

q_{N}Q\neq 0
.

Case 2

q_{N}\langle\varphi,\widetilde{Q}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})}\neq-q_{N}\widetilde{Q}^{\mathrm{ncl}}
and
Q=0
.

Case 3

q_{N}=0
, or
q_{N}\neq 0
,
Q=0
and
\langle\varphi,\widetilde{Q}\varphi\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})}=-\widetilde{Q}^{\mathrm{ncl}}
.

Case 1 is the
slowly decaying case, Case 2
is the critical case and Case 3 is the fastly decaying case. Strictly speaking
this classification makes best
sense for
\varphi
being unique, i.e. for the simple case; in the non-simple case one
needs a slightly different terminology, see Subsection
2.1
and Section
15
.

4
Generalized
N
-body Schrödinger
operators

Motivated by Sections
2
and
3
we discuss the abstract
N
-body problem,
cf.
[
DG
, Section 5.1]
.
Let
{\mathbf{X}}\neq\{0\}
be a real finite dimensional vector space with an inner
product
q
. We consider a finite family
\{{\mathbf{X}}^{a}\mid a\in{\mathcal{A}}\}
of
subspaces of
{\mathbf{X}}^{a}\subset{\mathbf{X}}
which is stable under addition and which contains

\{0\}
and
{\mathbf{X}}
. The ordering of subspaces yields an ordering
of the abstract set of indices
{\mathcal{A}}
,
a\subset b\Leftrightarrow{\mathbf{X}}^{a}\subset{\mathbf{X}}^{b}
. We denote
\{0\}={\mathbf{X}}^{a_{\min}}
,

{\mathbf{X}}={\mathbf{X}}^{a_{\max}}
and
{\mathbf{X}}^{a}+{\mathbf{X}}^{b}={\mathbf{X}}^{a\cup b}
. The orthogonal
complement of
{\mathbf{X}}^{a}
is denoted by
{\mathbf{X}}_{a}
. We refer to the indices

a\in{\mathcal{A}}
as ‘cluster decompositions’. The
length
of a chain of
cluster decompositions
a_{1}\subsetneq\cdots\subsetneq a_{k}
is the
number
k
. This chain is said to connect
a=a_{1}
and
b=a_{k}
. The
maximal length of all chains connecting a given
a\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\max}\}
and

a_{\max}
is denoted by
\#a
. We define
\#a_{\max}=1
and denoting

\#a_{\min}=N+1
we say the family
\{{\mathbf{X}}^{a}\mid a\in{\mathcal{A}}\}
is of
N
-body
type. Note that for the setup of Sections
2
and
3
these examples are of
(N-1)
-body
type and of
N
-body
type, respectively. This terminology might appear slightly misleading for
Section
2
. Henceforth we shall treat the generalized
N
-body framework
only. This would consequently apply to the many-body
framework of Section
2
with
N
there replaced by
N+1
. A cluster
decomposition
a
is said to be
k
-cluster
if
\#a=k
.

Given the above uniform setup of structure of ‘internal subspaces’
we can introduce corresponding generalized Schrödinger
operators. Let
-\Delta^{a}=(p^{a})^{2}
and
-\Delta_{a}=p_{a}^{2}
denote (minus) the
Laplacians on
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})
and
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a})
, respectively. Here

p^{a}=\pi^{a}p
and
p_{a}=\pi_{a}p
denote the internal (i.e. ‘within clusters’) and the
inter-cluster components of the momentum operator
p=-\mathrm{i}\nabla
, respectively.
For all
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}:={\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\min}\}
, we introduce

H^{a}=-\Delta^{a}+V^{a}(x^{a}),\,V^{a}(x^{a})=\sum_{b\subset a}V_{b}(x^{b}),\,H_{a}=H^{a}-\Delta_{a},\,I_{a}(x)=\sum_{b\not\subset a}V_{b}(x^{b}),

where the potentials fulfill the condition below.
We define
H^{a_{\min}}=0
on
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a_{\min}})={\mathbb{C}}
and

H=H^{a_{\max}}
on
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
.

Condition 1.5
.

There exists
\rho>0
such that for all
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
there is given a function
V_{a}:{\mathbf{X}}^{a}\to{\mathbb{R}}

with a splitting
V_{a}=V_{a}^{(1)}+V_{a}^{(2)}
, where

(1)

V_{a}^{(1)}
is smooth and

\partial^{\alpha}_{y}V_{a}^{(1)}(y)={\mathcal{O}}\big(|y|^{-\rho-|\alpha|}\big).

(22)

(2)

V_{a}^{(2)}
is compactly supported and

V_{a}^{(2)}(-\Delta_{y}+1)^{-1}\text{ is compact on
}L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{\dim{\mathbf{X}}^{a}}_{y}).

(23)

Condition
1.5
will be imposed throughout this work
. To
treat local singularities we shall impose an additional condition,
depending on an
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
from a given context. The condition is fulfilled for the
models of Sections
2
and
3
, cf. (
12a
),
(
12b
), (
18a
) and (
18b
).

Consider for a given
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
with

\#a=2
the following properties for
b\not\subset a
:

\displaystyle\ran\big(\pi^{b}\pi^{a}\big)=\{0\}\text{ or }\ran\big(\pi^{b}\pi^{a}\big)=\ran\,\pi^{b},

(24a)

\displaystyle\pi^{b}:{\mathbf{X}}_{a}\to{\mathbf{X}}^{b}\text{ is onto}.

(24b)

We note that the map in
(
24b
) is necessarily injective, and hence bijective if
(
24b
) is fulfilled, see (
50
).

Condition 1.6
.

For all

b\not\subset a
for which the conditions (
24a
) and
(
24b
) are not
fulfilled, the singular part
V^{(2)}_{b}=0
and hence
V_{b}=V^{(1)}_{b}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbf{X}}^{b})
.

If

T
is a self-adjoint operator on a Hilbert space the subsets of
{\mathbb{C}}
,

\displaystyle\sigma(T),\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}(T),\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(T)\text{ and }\sigma_{\mathrm{pp}}(T)

refer to the spectrum, the discrete spectrum, the essential spectrum
and the set of eigenvalues of
T
, respectively.

The operator
H^{a}
is the sub-Hamiltonian associated with the cluster decomposition

a
and
I_{a}
is the sum of all inter-cluster interactions. The detailed
expression of
H^{a}
depends on the choice of coordinates on

{\mathbf{X}}^{a}
.
Let

{\mathcal{T}}={\mathcal{T}}(H)=\cup_{a\in{\mathcal{A}},\#a\geq 2}\;\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{a})

be the set of thresholds of
H
.
The HVZ theorem
[
RS
, Theorem XIII.17]
gives
the bottom of the essential spectrum
\Sigma_{2}:=\inf\sigma_{{\mathrm{ess}}}(H)
of
H
by the formula

\Sigma_{2}=\min_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\max}\}}\inf\sigma(H^{a})=\min_{a\in{\mathcal{A}},\#a=2}\inf\sigma(H^{a}).

(25)

If
N\geq 2
we also introduce

\Sigma_{3}:=\min_{a\in{\mathcal{A}},\#a\geq 3}\inf\sigma(H^{a}).

(26)

Under Condition
1.5
it is known that non-threshold
bound
states decay exponentially
[
FH
]
. It is also well known

[
FH
]
and
[
Pe
]
that under rather general conditions
generalized Schrödinger
operators do
not have
positive eigenvalues and that the negative
eigenvalues can at most
accumulate at the thresholds from below, see also
[
AIIS
]
.

The goal of the present work
is to obtain spectral and scattering properties of
H
near a general
two-cluster
threshold

\lambda_{0}\leq 0
, i.e.

\lambda_{0}\in{\mathcal{T}}_{2}:={\mathcal{T}}\setminus\cup_{a\in{\mathcal{A}},\#a\geq 3}\;\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{a}).

(27)

Note that
\lambda_{0}=0
for
N=1
, while
\lambda_{0}<0
for

N\geq 2
. Note also that
\lambda_{0}\neq\Sigma_{3}
for
N\geq 2
and
that
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{3}
could occur for
N\geq 3
. Since the
case
N=1
has been treated extensively in the literature we assume
throughout our work that
N\geq 2
and therefore
\lambda_{0}<0
. The special case for

N\geq 2
when
\lambda_{0}
is equal to
\Sigma_{2}
and is given as
the eigenvalue of a unique two-cluster sub-Hamiltonian is studied in
[
Wa2
]
for fastly
decaying potentials, meaning
\rho>2
or bigger.

4.1
Spaces and notation

For given Banach (or Fréchet) spaces
X
and
Y
, the space of linear continuous operators
T:X\to Y
is denoted by
{\mathcal{L}}(X,Y)
, and we abbreviate
{\mathcal{L}}(X)={\mathcal{L}}(X,X)
.

Let
H_{s}^{k}
,
k,s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, be the weighted Sobolev space on
{\mathbf{X}}

(or possibly
{\mathbf{X}}_{a}
for any
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\prime}
) equipped with the norm

\displaystyle\lVert u\rVert_{k,s}:=\Big(\int\,\big\lvert\langle x\rangle^{s}(1-\Delta)^{k/2}u\big\rvert^{2}{\mathrm{d}}x\Big)^{1/2},\quad\text{where }\langle x\rangle:=(1+|x|^{2})^{1/2}.

The space
H^{-k}_{-s}
can be identified as the dual space of
H^{k}_{s}

with the usual
L^{2}
inner product used as ‘pairing’. Let

{\mathcal{L}}(k,s;k^{\prime},s^{\prime})={\mathcal{L}}(H^{k}_{s},H^{k^{\prime}}_{s^{\prime}})
.
It will be convenient to regard
H
as an operator in
{\mathcal{L}}(2,0;0,0)
or as an operator in
{\mathcal{L}}(1,0;-1,0)
. We abbreviate

H^{k}=H_{0}^{k}
,
L_{s}^{2}=H^{0}_{s}
, and
H^{k}_{\infty}=\cap_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}H^{k}_{s}
,

H^{k}_{-\infty}=\cup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}H^{k}_{s}
,
L_{\infty}^{2}=\cap_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}\,L_{s}^{2}
and
L_{-\infty}^{2}=\cup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}\,L_{s}^{2}
. Introduce also
H^{k}_{t^{+}}=\cup_{s>t}H^{k}_{s}
,
H^{k}_{t^{-}}=\cap_{s<t}H^{k}_{s}
,
L^{2}_{t^{+}}=\cup_{s>t}L^{2}_{s}
and
L^{2}_{t^{-}}=\cap_{s<t}L^{2}_{s}
for
any
k,t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

We shall also use weighted
Sobolev spaces of
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
-valued functions/distributions on
{\mathbf{X}}

indicated by similar notation, for example
H^{k}=H^{k}({\mathbf{X}};{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
.
For any complex Hilbert spaces
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{1}
we use the notation

{\mathcal{C}}\big({\mathcal{H}}_{1},{\mathcal{H}}_{2}\big)\subset{\mathcal{L}}\big({\mathcal{H}}_{1},{\mathcal{H}}_{2}\big)
for the space of
compact operators
T:{\mathcal{H}}_{1}\to{\mathcal{H}}_{2}
. By standard Sobolev
embedding theory, for all
k_{1}>k_{2}
and
s_{1}>s_{2}
the operator

H^{k_{1}}_{s_{1}}\ni f\to f\in H^{k_{2}}_{s_{2}}
is
compact. Let
H^{1}_{\rm loc}
denote the set of locally
H^{1}

functions, more precisely the set of

v\in L^{2}_{-\infty}
for which
\varphi v\in H^{1}
for all
\varphi\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}
.

Consider balls
B(R)=\{x\in{\mathbf{X}}|\,\lvert x\rvert<R\}
,
R\geq 1
, and
the characteristic functions

\displaystyle F_{0}=F\big(B({R_{0}})\big)\text{ and }F_{\nu+1}=F\big(B({R_{\nu+1}})\setminus B({R_{\nu}})\big),\ R_{\nu}=2^{\nu},\,\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}:={\mathbb{N}}\cup\{0\},

where
F(M)
denotes the sharp characteristic
function of a subset
M\subset{\mathbf{X}}
.
We introduce the Besov spaces
{\mathcal{B}}_{s}={\mathcal{B}}_{s}({\mathbf{X}})
and

{\mathcal{B}}_{s}^{*}={\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s}({\mathbf{X}})=\big({\mathcal{B}}_{s}({\mathbf{X}})\big)^{*}
,
s>0
, as follows.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{s}

\displaystyle=\{\psi\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}\mid\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}_{s}}<\infty\},\quad\|\psi\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{s}}=\sum_{\nu=0}^{\infty}R_{\nu}^{s}\|F_{\nu}\psi\|_{{L^{2}}},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{B}}_{s}^{*}

\displaystyle=\{\psi\in L^{2}_{\mathrm{loc}}\mid\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}_{s}^{*}}<\infty\},\quad\|\psi\|_{{\mathcal{B}}_{s}^{*}}=\sup_{\nu\geq 0}\,R_{\nu}^{-s}\|F_{\nu}\psi\|_{{L^{2}}},

respectively. (Note that indeed
{\mathcal{B}}_{s}^{*}
is the dual space of
{\mathcal{B}}_{s}
.)
We define
{\mathcal{B}}_{s,0}^{*}
to be the closure of
L^{2}
in

{\mathcal{B}}_{s}^{*}
. Note that

\displaystyle u\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s}^{*}\Leftrightarrow u\in L^{2}_{\rm loc}\text{ and }\sup_{R\geq 1}\,R^{-s}\|F\big(B(R)\big)u\|_{L^{2}}<\infty,

and that

\displaystyle u\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s,0}^{*}\Leftrightarrow u\in L^{2}_{\rm loc}\text{ and }\lim_{R\to\infty}\,R^{-s}\|F\big(B(R)\big)u\|_{L^{2}}=0.

Note the following relations between the
standard weighted
L^{2}
spaces and the Besov spaces:

\displaystyle\forall s_{1}>s>0:\quad L^{2}_{s_{1}}\subsetneq{\mathcal{B}}_{s}\subsetneq L^{2}_{s}\subsetneq L^{2}\subsetneq L^{2}_{-s}\subsetneq{\mathcal{B}}_{s,0}^{*}\subsetneq{\mathcal{B}}_{s}^{*}\subsetneq L^{2}_{-s_{1}}.

(28)

We introduce for any
R\geq 1

\displaystyle\chi_{R}(t)=\chi(t/R)\text{ for a real-valued }\chi\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}):\quad\chi(t)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1&\mbox{ for }t\leq 4/3\\
0&\mbox{ for }t\geq 5/3\end{array},\right.

and
\bar{\chi}_{R}=1-\chi_{R}
. We assume
-\chi^{\prime}\geq 0
and that

\sqrt{-\chi^{\prime}}\in C^{\infty}
.

Chapter 2
Reduction to a one-body problem

In this chapter we show that the spectral analysis of a generalized
N
-body
operator near a two-cluster threshold can be reduced to the analysis
of a one-body operator with a non-linear spectral parameter. This is under
Condition
1.5
. Of course such reduction would not be
needed for the one-body problem, however we recall that throughout our work
we impose the condition
N\geq 2
.

The idea of the reduction
goes as follows. For a given two-cluster threshold
\lambda_{0}<0
, denote by
{\mathcal{F}}

the closed subspace in
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
‘spanned by’ bound states of all
possible two-cluster sub-Hamiltonians with eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
. (For a
precise definition/representation in a special case see (
45
)
and the discussion there.) Let
\Pi
be the orthogonal projection from

L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
onto
{\mathcal{F}}
,
\Pi^{\prime}=1-\Pi
and
H^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi^{\prime}
. We study
a Grushin problem for an operator of the form

{H}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}H-z&S\\[7.22743pt]
S^{*}&0\end{array}\right)\,\text{ on }L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})\oplus{\mathcal{H}},

(31)

where the space
{\mathcal{H}}
is some auxiliary
one-body type space,

S:{\mathcal{H}}\to L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
is an appropriately defined operator whose
range coincides with
{\mathcal{F}}
, i.e. the range of the projection
\Pi
, and
S^{*}
is the adjoint of

S
. The choice of
{\mathcal{H}}
and
S
may vary according to various
situations and is to make
H(z)
invertible for
z
near
\lambda_{0}
and
\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0
. If this is realized, we set

{H}(z)^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}E(z)&E_{+}(z)\\[7.22743pt]
E_{-}(z)&E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)\end{array}\right).

(32)

Then the resolvent
R(z)=(H-z)^{-1}
is represented as

R(z)=E(z)-E_{+}(z)E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}E_{-}(z).

(33)

The operator
{H}(z)^{-1}
can be computed in terms of the reduced
resolvent
R^{\prime}(z)=(H^{\prime}-z)^{-1}\Pi^{\prime}
. If
R^{\prime}(z)
has some good
properties near
\lambda_{0}
, the spectral analysis of
H
near
\lambda_{0}
is
then reduced to the analysis of
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}
on
{\mathcal{H}}
.

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)
is a one-body operator with a non-linear dependence
on the spectral parameter
z
. For
example to obtain a resolvent expansion, one can then try to use known methods for one-body operators to study the asymptotics of
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}
as
z\to\lambda_{0}
,
\operatorname{Im}z>0
.

\fancybreak

Below we shall only give detailed analysis in some situations. In
fact, the reductions given below are only useful in the case
\lambda_{0}\not\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{\prime})
. The case
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{\prime})
can
be treated by adding an additional finite dimensional space, as done
in
[
Wa2
, Section 4]
for the lowest threshold. Before we go
into details on various concrete cases we study the reduction
scheme from a more general point of view. As we will see afterwards, the abstract
scheme can be applied to these cases.

5
An abstract reduction scheme

In this section we shall describe an
abstract reduction scheme that will be used many times.

A
bstract framework

Suppose
{\mathcal{H}}
and
{\mathcal{G}}
are given
Hilbert spaces and that
{\mathcal{F}}
is a closed subspace of
{\mathcal{G}}
. Suppose

S:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{F}}\subset{\mathcal{G}}
is a bi-continuous isomorphism (i.e.
S
is
linear, one-to-one, onto
{\mathcal{F}}
and as a map from
{\mathcal{H}}
to
{\mathcal{F}}

bi-continuous). Let
\Pi
denote the orthogonal projection in
{\mathcal{G}}

onto
{\mathcal{F}}
, and let
\Pi^{\prime}=1-\Pi
. Suppose
H
is a self-adjoint
operator on
{\mathcal{G}}
with
\Pi:{\mathcal{D}}(H)\to{\mathcal{D}}(H)
, and that
\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi

and
\Pi H\Pi^{\prime}
(initially defined on the domain
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
of
H
) extend to bounded
operators on
{\mathcal{G}}
. Define
H^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi^{\prime}
with domain
{\mathcal{D}}(H^{\prime})={\mathcal{D}}(H)\cap\ran(\Pi^{\prime})=\Pi^{\prime}\,{\mathcal{D}}(H)
and
H_{\Pi}=\Pi H\Pi

with domain
{\mathcal{D}}(H_{\Pi})={\mathcal{D}}(H)\cap\ran(\Pi)=\Pi\,{\mathcal{D}}(H)
. Then

H^{\prime}
and
H_{\Pi}
are self-adjoint on
\Pi^{\prime}{\mathcal{G}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}
,
respectively (see the proof of Lemma
2.8

3
). Let
R^{\prime}(z)=(H^{\prime}-z)^{-1}\Pi^{\prime}
for
\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0
.

Introduce for all
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0

\displaystyle E(z)

\displaystyle=R^{\prime}(z),

(34a)

\displaystyle E_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=S-R^{\prime}(z)HS,

(34b)

\displaystyle E_{-}(z)

\displaystyle=S^{*}-S^{*}HR^{\prime}(z),

(34c)

\displaystyle E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)

\displaystyle=S^{*}\big(z-H+HR^{\prime}(z)H\big)S.

(34d)

Obviously
E(z)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{G}})
,
E_{+}(z)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{G}})
and
E_{-}(z)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{G}},{\mathcal{H}})
. Also note
that
S^{*}HS=S^{*}H_{\Pi}S
is self-adjoint and
consequently that the operator
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)
of (
34d
) is a closed
operator on
{\mathcal{H}}
with domain
given by

S^{-1}{\mathcal{D}}(H_{\Pi})
.

Proposition 2.1
.

Under the above conditions, for
\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0
:

1)

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{*}=E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\bar{z})
.

2)

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)
is an invertible operator on
{\mathcal{H}}

obeying

\frac{\operatorname{Im}E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)}{\operatorname{Im}z}\geq S^{*}S.

(35)

3)

R(z)=E(z)-E_{+}(z)E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}E_{-}(z).

(36)

Proof.

The identity
1)
is trivial due to the
self-adjointness property stated before the
proposition.

The inequality (
35
) follows from the identity

\operatorname{Im}E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)=\operatorname{Im}z\,S^{*}\big(1+HR^{\prime}(z)^{*}R^{\prime}(z)H\big)S.

(37)

The invertibility property of
2)
follows
from a standard numerical range argument
combining
1)
and (
35
).

Finally the identity (
36
)
follows by an elementary calculation outlined here: Apply
H-z
from
the
left on the right-hand side of the identity and
write for the first term of (
36
)

H\Pi^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}H^{\prime}\Pi^{\prime}+\Pi H\Pi^{\prime}.

(38)

The result is the expression

\Pi^{\prime}+\Pi HR^{\prime}(z)
. For the second term of (
36
) we substitute the
expression (
34b
) and use again (
38
) (to deal with
the second term of (
34b
)). We see that by applying
H-z
to the second term of
(
36
) we obtain an operator taking values in

{\mathcal{F}}
. Consequently we may insert
\Pi=(S^{*})^{-1}S^{*}
to the left
and then combine the factor
S^{*}
with the given calculated expression. This gives a factor
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}

which of course can be omitted on
{\mathcal{H}}=\ran E_{-}(z)
. Then
by substituting the
expression (
34c
) we conclude that the contribution from
the second term of (
36
) is
\Pi-\Pi HR^{\prime}(z)
. Hence the
result of applying
H-z
from the
left on the right-hand side of (
36
) is

\big(\Pi^{\prime}+\Pi HR^{\prime}(z)\big)+\big(\Pi-\Pi HR^{\prime}(z)\big)=1,

which coincides with the
result of applying
H-z
from the
left on the left-hand side of the identity.
∎

Remark 2.2
.

There is an alternative
approach for deriving
the formula (
36
) based on a
certain abstract Grushin problem. For a review of
this method we may refer to
[
SZ
]
, but for sake of completeness of presentation
we outline this alternative
approach here, cf. the beginning of this chapter:

We
consider a Grushin problem for an operator of the form

{H}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}H-z&S\\[7.22743pt]
S^{*}&0\end{array}\right)\text{ on }{\mathcal{G}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}.

(39)

To simplify the notation, we denote still by

(SS^{*})^{-1}=(SS^{*})^{-1}\Pi

the extension of
(SS^{*})^{-1}
to
{\mathcal{G}}
by setting
(SS^{*})^{-1}=0

on
{\mathcal{F}}^{\perp}
.

The invertibility of
SS^{*}
on
{\mathcal{F}}
allows us to show that
{H}(z)
is
invertible on
{\mathcal{G}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}
. In fact, one can explicitly compute its inverse.
Let
T:=(SS^{*})^{-1}S
, and let
{Q}(z)
and
{B}(z)
be defined by

{Q}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}R^{\prime}(z)&T\\
T^{*}&T^{*}(z-H)T\end{array}\right)\text{ and }B(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Pi HR^{\prime}(z)&\Pi^{\prime}HT\\[7.22743pt]
0&0\end{array}\right).

Then one has, at least formally,

{H}(z){Q}(z)=1+{B}(z).

Since
B(z)^{3}=0
the operator
1+B(z)
is invertible and therefore

{H}(z)
has a right inverse. Similarly, one can show that

{H}(z)
has a left inverse. Consequently
{H}(z)
should be invertible
with inverse

{H}(z)^{-1}={Q}(z)(1-B(z)+B(z)^{2}).

(40)

Write
{H}(z)^{-1}
in the form

H(z)^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ll}\widetilde{E}(z)&\widetilde{E}_{+}(z)\\[7.22743pt]
\widetilde{E}_{-}(z)&\widetilde{E}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)\end{array}\right).

(41)

We have the formulas

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}(z)

\displaystyle=R^{\prime}(z),

(42a)

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=T-R^{\prime}(z)HT,

(42b)

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{-}(z)

\displaystyle=T^{*}-T^{*}HR^{\prime}(z),

(42c)

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)

\displaystyle=T^{*}\big(z-H+HR^{\prime}(z)H\big)T,

(42d)

and

R(z)=\widetilde{E}(z)-\widetilde{E}_{+}(z)\widetilde{E}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}\widetilde{E}_{-}(z).

(43)

Now (
36
) follows from (
43
) and
the identity

\displaystyle T=(SS^{*})^{-1}S=S(S^{*}S)^{-1}.

(44)

Remark 2.3
.

Suppose
\lambda_{0}\notin\sigma(H^{\prime})
so that the operators in (
42a
)–(
42d
) have limits
when taking
z\to\lambda=\lambda_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then we learn from the identities

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{-}(\lambda)(H-\lambda)

\displaystyle\subset-\widetilde{E}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda)S^{*},

\displaystyle S\widetilde{E}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle\subset-(H-\lambda)\widetilde{E}_{+}(\lambda),

that

\displaystyle S^{*}:

\displaystyle\ker(H-\lambda)\to\ker\widetilde{E}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda),

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{+}(\lambda):

\displaystyle\ker\widetilde{E}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda)\to\ker(H-\lambda),

respectively.

Combined with the identities

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{+}(\lambda)S^{*}

\displaystyle\supset 1-\widetilde{E}(\lambda)(H-\lambda),

\displaystyle S^{*}\widetilde{E}_{+}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=1,

we then conclude that in fact

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}_{+}(\lambda)S^{*}

\displaystyle=1\text{ on }\ker(H-\lambda),

\displaystyle S^{*}\widetilde{E}_{+}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=1\text{ on }\ker\widetilde{E}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda),

i.e.
S^{*}:\ker(H-\lambda)\to\ker\widetilde{E}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda)
is a linear isomorphism.

Whence, cf. (
44
),
T^{*}:\ker(H-\lambda)\to\ker E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda)
is a linear isomorphism with inverse
E_{+}(\lambda):\ker E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda)\to\ker(H-\lambda)
. We shall refer to the
vector
f=T^{*}\phi\in\ker E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda)
as the

eigentransform
of a given
\phi\in\ker(H-\lambda)
, and the
equation
\phi=E_{+}(\lambda)f
as the
inversion formula
for the
eigentransform of
\phi
. The Hilbert space setting discussed here is
in our application an
L^{2}
setting which will have
extensions to Besov space settings and settings where
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma(H^{\prime})
,
that are incompatible with the framework discussed
here. These concrete extensions will be studied in Chapter
4
.

6
Non-multiple two-cluster threshold case

Assume in this section that
\lambda_{0}<0
is a
non-multiple two-cluster
threshold
in the following sense:

Condition 2.4
.

There exists a unique
a_{0}\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\max}\}
such
that
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{a_{0}})
. This cluster decomposition is
a two-cluster decomposition, i.e.

\#a_{0}=2
. Condition
1.6
is fulfilled for
a=a_{0}
.

Note that we do not here impose that
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{d}}}(H^{a_{0}})
. Nevertheless
\lambda_{0}
is not a threshold for the
Hamiltonian
H^{a_{0}}
, and consequently the corresponding bound states
have exponential decay, cf.
[
FH
,
AIIS
]
.
Note that the multiplicity of
\lambda_{0}
as an eigenvalue of
H^{a_{0}}
, say
m
, can be arbitrary.
The simplest case is
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
(under Condition
2.4

necessarily
m=1
in this case), and it is studied in
[
Wa2
]
for
fastly decaying potentials.
For
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
, we can use the same idea to reduce
H

to a one-body type operator with a non-linear spectral parameter,
although additional complications arise, in particular for
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{3}
.

\fancybreak

Let
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m}\}
be an orthonormal basis of the eigenspace of
H^{a_{0}}
associated with
\lambda_{0}
.
Let
\Pi
be the projection in
{\mathcal{G}}:=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
defined by

\Pi g=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\varphi_{j}\otimes\langle\varphi_{j},g\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a_{0}})},\;g\in{\mathcal{G}}.

(45)

Let
\Pi^{\prime}=1-\Pi
and
H^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi^{\prime}
. Note that
H^{\prime}
is
self-adjoint with domain
{\mathcal{D}}(H^{\prime})={\mathcal{D}}(H)\cap\ran\,\Pi^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}\,{\mathcal{D}}(H)
. In fact
H_{a_{0}}
is reduced by
\Pi^{\prime}
(‘reduced’ in the sense of

[
Ka
, Subsection V.3.9]
) implying in particular that
H_{a_{0}}^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}H_{a_{0}}\Pi^{\prime}
is
self-adjoint. Since
I_{a_{0}}^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}I_{a_{0}}\Pi^{\prime}
is infinitesimally small
relatively to
H_{a_{0}}^{\prime}
it follows from
[
RS
, Theorem X.12]
that
indeed
H^{\prime}
is
self-adjoint. For

\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0
we set

R^{\prime}(z)=(H^{\prime}-z)^{-1}\Pi^{\prime}.

Let
I_{0}=I_{a_{0}}
and abbreviate similarly
p_{0}=p_{a_{0}}
. Since we
have imposed Condition
1.6
with
a=a_{0}
we can
use a ‘free factor’
(|p^{a_{0}}|^{2}+1)^{-1}
(from
\Pi
) to conclude
that

\displaystyle\Pi^{\prime}I_{0}\Pi\in{\mathcal{L}}\big({\mathcal{G}}\big).

(46)

Let
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{a_{0}}:=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}};{\mathbb{C}}^{m})
,
H^{2}_{a_{0}}:=H^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}};{\mathbb{C}}^{m})\subset{\mathcal{H}}_{a_{0}}
and the operator
S:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{G}}
be defined by

S:f=(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})\to Sf=\sum_{j=1}^{m}S_{j}f_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\varphi_{j}(x^{a_{0}})f_{j}(x_{a_{0}}).

(47)

Obviously
S:H^{2}_{a_{0}}\to H^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
and the (
L^{2})
adjoint
S^{*}:H^{2}\to H^{2}_{a_{0}}
. Since
S
in this case is isometric the formulas
(
34a
)–(
34d
) and (
42a
)–(
42d
)
coincide. They read (in terms of the identity matrix
{\textbf{1}}_{m}

of size
m
)

\displaystyle E(z)

\displaystyle=R^{\prime}(z),

(48a)

\displaystyle E_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=(1-R^{\prime}(z)I_{0})S,

(48b)

\displaystyle E_{-}(z)

\displaystyle=S^{*}(1-I_{0}R^{\prime}(z)),

(48c)

\displaystyle E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)

\displaystyle=(z-\lambda_{0})-(|p_{0}|^{2}{\textbf{1}}_{m}+S^{*}{I_{0}}S-S^{*}I_{0}R^{\prime}(z)I_{0}S),

(48d)

which can be used in combination with (
36
), i.e.

R(z)=E(z)-E_{+}(z)E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}E_{-}(z).

(49)

We examine
in the following
some basic properties of
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)
.
Note that
S^{*}{I_{a_{0}}}S
is a matrix-valued potential. Let us
first split
I_{0}=I^{(1)}_{0}+I^{(2)}_{0}=I^{(1)}_{a_{0}}+I^{(2)}_{a_{0}}
in agreement with the
splitting of Condition
1.5
and look at the
contribution from
I^{(1)}_{0}
. Recalling the elementary geometric property

X_{a}\cap X_{b}=\{0\}\text{ if }\#a=2\text{ and }b\not\subset a,

(50)

guaranteeing that

\pi^{b}:{\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}}\to{\mathbf{X}}^{b}
is injective for all
b\not\subset a_{0}
, we obtain by a Taylor
expansion that the leading term
has a scalar leading form. More precisely we obtain by a zero’th
order Taylor
expansion, by using (
81a
) (stated below) and by using the
polynomial decay of the bound states
\varphi_{j}
that

S_{i}^{*}{I^{(1)}_{0}}S_{j}=I^{(1)}_{0}(0+y)\delta_{ij}+{\mathcal{O}}(|y|^{-\rho-1})\text{ for }|y|\to\infty;\quad i,j\leq m.

(51a)

Here the variable
y=x_{a_{0}}
can be thought of as a vector in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
, abbreviating here and henceforth
\dim{\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}}=n
.
We
claim the following analogue of (
51a
) for
I^{(2)}_{0}
:

S_{i}^{*}{I^{(2)}_{0}}S_{j}-I^{(2)}_{0}(0+y)\delta_{ij}\in{\mathcal{C}}\big(H^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}}),L^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}})\big)\text{ for all }s,t\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(51b)

To prove (
51b
) it suffices to
consider the contribution from
V_{b}^{(2)}
for any
b\not\subset a_{0}
under the conditions
(
24a
) and (
24b
), in fact with the second condition of
(
24a
) fulfilled. Due to
(
24b
) the restriction

V_{b}^{(2)}(=V_{b}^{(2)}(y))\in{\mathcal{C}}\big(H_{s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}}),L_{t}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}})\big)
.
It is compactly supported, possibly with singularities. On the other
hand

S_{i}^{*}{V_{b}^{(2)}}S_{j}
is a bounded potential due to the
second condition of
(
24a
), and it remains to show that this potential decays
faster than any negative power
\langle y\rangle^{-s}
. By the compact
support property we can pick
R>1
such that

\displaystyle F(|\pi^{b}\cdot|>R)S_{i}^{*}{V_{b}^{(2)}}S_{j}=S_{i}^{*}{F\big(|\pi^{b}y|>R\big)F\big(|\pi^{b}x^{a_{0}}|\geq\tfrac{1}{2}|\pi^{b}y|\big)V_{b}^{(2)}}S_{j}.

By the polynomial decay of the cluster bound
states the right-hand side
has arbitrary power decay too, showing the desired decay and therefore (
51b
).

We conclude from (
51a
) and (
51b
) that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&S_{i}^{*}I_{0}S_{j}-I^{(1)}_{0}(0+y)\delta_{ij}\in{\mathcal{C}}\big(H^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}}),L^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}})\big)\text{ for }s,t\in{\mathbb{R}},\,t<\rho+1+s,\\
&S_{i}^{*}I_{0}S_{j}-I^{(1)}_{0}(0+y)\delta_{ij}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(H^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}}),L^{2}_{\rho+1+s}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}})\big)\text{ for }s\in{\mathbb{R}}.\end{split}

(52)

It remains to examine the term

S^{*}I_{0}R^{\prime}(z)I_{0}S
in
(
48d
). Note that the two factors of
I_{0}
freely can be
changed to
\Pi I_{0}\Pi^{\prime}
and
\Pi^{\prime}I_{0}\Pi
,
respectively. We can then conveniently
implement the following improvements of (
46
):

\displaystyle\Pi I_{0}\Pi^{\prime},\,\Pi^{\prime}I_{0}\Pi\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}),L^{2}_{\rho+1+s}({\mathbf{X}})\big)\text{ for all }s\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(53)

For these bounds it suffices to consider
\Pi^{\prime}I_{0}\Pi
. The contribution
from
I_{0}^{(1)}
is treated by a Taylor expansion as in
(
51a
) (and by (
81a
)). The contribution from
I_{0}^{(2)}
is treated by
using a ‘free factor’
(|p^{a_{0}}|^{2}+1)^{-1}
(as for
(
46
)) as well as using the above proof of
(
51b
).

We conclude the following representation of the operator

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)
:

\displaystyle E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)

\displaystyle+\big(p^{2}_{0}+I^{(1)}_{0}(\pi_{a_{0}}\cdot)+\lambda_{0}-z\big){\textbf{1}}_{m}=\widetilde{V}-\langle y\rangle^{-\rho-1}\widetilde{K}(z)\langle y\rangle^{-\rho-1};

\displaystyle\widetilde{V}={\mathcal{O}}\big(\langle y\rangle^{-\rho-1}\big),\quad\widetilde{K}(z)={\mathcal{O}}\big(\langle y\rangle^{0}\big);

here the meaning of the
{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
notation is given more
precisely by (
52
) and (
53
), respectively. Note
that
\widetilde{V}
is multiplicative matrix-valued, while

\widetilde{K}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}})
is
non-multiplicative with a
z
-dependence through the appearance of the
factor
R^{\prime}(z)
. Note also that
\langle y\rangle^{\rho}I_{0}^{(1)}(y)
is
bounded. Although the above discussion is based on a natural operator
interpretation of
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)
we shall prefer to use the
corresponding form
interpretation (see Remark
2.13
for details in a
different but similar context).

The representation formula (
49
) is valid for any
z
with

\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0
, but for our purposes it is only useful if one has good properties of

R^{\prime}(z)
for
z
near
\lambda_{0}
. When
\lambda_{0}
is the lowest
threshold, the essential spectrum of
H^{\prime}
is shifted to the right
and the representation (
49
) can be used in the case

\lambda_{0}
is not an eigenvalue of
H^{\prime}
, cf.
[
Wa2
, Lemma 2.1]

(see Lemma
2.10
for an extension). With
this assumption, the reduced resolvent
R^{\prime}(z)
is holomorphic in a small neighbourhood of

\lambda_{0}
, and so are
E_{+}(z),E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)
and
E_{-}(z)
. Moreover
in this case
R^{\prime}(z)
has uniform bounds in weighted
L^{2}
spaces
(see (
87
) for a similar assertion for the multiple case). This is helpful in
the study of asymptotic expansions of
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}
at

\lambda_{0}
and therefore in turn (by (
49
)) for expansions
of
R(z)
near
\lambda_{0}
(see Section

16.2
for the multiple case). In the case
\lambda_{0}
happens to be
an eigenvalue of
H^{\prime}
another reduction is needed.

If
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
,
\lambda_{0}
is in the essential
spectrum of
H^{\prime}
. Under Conditions
1.5
and

2.4
we shall show in Chapter
3
that a limiting absorption principle and microlocal
resolvent estimates hold for
R^{\prime}(z)
with
z
near
\lambda_{0}

provided
\lambda_{0}
is not an eigenvalue of
H^{\prime}
. This leads in this
case to an extension of the ‘eigentransform’ discussed in Remark

2.3
(see Chapter

4
), and then in
turn (
49
) can again be used to analyse the resolvent of

H
near the threshold
\lambda_{0}
(see Section
16.3
). The analysis for
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}

is more complicated than for
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
in that, some more
refined mapping properties of
R^{\prime}(z)
near
\lambda_{0}
are needed,
causing in particular some ‘loss of weight’.

7
Multiple two-cluster threshold,

{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{2}=\{0\}

Consider now the case where there exist at least two two-cluster
decompositions such that the two-cluster threshold
\lambda_{0}(<0)
is an eigenvalue of
the corresponding sub-Hamiltonians. To simplify the presentation, let us only consider in detail the
case where
\lambda_{0}
is double occurring without eigenvalue multiplicity:

Condition 2.5
.

There exist unique
a_{1},a_{2}\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\max}\}
,
a_{1}\neq a_{2}
, such
that
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{a_{j}})
,
j=1,2
. The cluster
decompositions
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are
two-cluster decompositions, i.e.

\#a_{1}=\#a_{2}=2
, and Condition
1.6
is
fulfilled for both of them. The number
\lambda_{0}
is a simple eigenvalue
for both of the operators
H^{a_{1}}
and
H^{a_{2}}
.

Denote

{\mathbf{X}}^{j}={\mathbf{X}}^{a_{j}},\quad{\mathbf{X}}_{j}={\mathbf{X}}_{a_{j}}\quad\text{and}\quad n_{j}=\dim{\mathbf{X}}_{{}_{j}}\quad\text{for}\quad j=1,2,

and similarly for elements
x
of
{\mathbf{X}}^{a_{j}}
and
{\mathbf{X}}_{a_{j}}
,
viz.
x=x^{j}\oplus x_{j}
.
Let

\varphi_{j}
denote the corresponding (real) normalized eigenvector of

H^{a_{j}}
,
j=1,2
. Let

{\mathcal{F}}_{j}=\{g=\varphi_{j}(x^{j})f_{j}(x_{j})\mid f_{j}\in L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n_{j}}_{x_{j}})\},\quad j=1,2.

Note that
{\mathcal{F}}_{j}
is a closed subspace of
{\mathcal{G}}:=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
. Let
\Pi
be the orthogonal
projection in
{\mathcal{G}}
onto
{\mathcal{F}}
, which by definition is the closure of
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}+{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
in
{\mathcal{G}}
. Let
{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})
.

To construct an associated Grushin problem we need to distinguish
between two cases:
a)
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{2}=\{0\}
and b)
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\neq\{0\}
.
Here we shall impose the condition a), i.e.

{\mathcal{F}}_{1}+{\mathcal{F}}_{2}={\mathcal{F}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{2}.

(54)

We believe that this condition is always fulfilled for the finite mass
many-body operators of Section
2
, however we do not have a proof.
A case where it fails for the infinite
mass many-body operators of Section
3
will be discussed in Section
9
. Moreover the general case of b) will be studied there.

Let

S=(S_{1},S_{2}):{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{G}}
be defined by
Sf=S_{1}f_{1}+S_{2}f_{2}

for
f=(f_{1},f_{2})
and with

\displaystyle\begin{split}S_{j}&:L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})\to{\mathcal{G}},\quad f_{j}\to S_{j}f_{j}=\varphi_{j}(x^{j})\otimes f_{j}(x_{j}),\\
S_{j}^{*}&:{\mathcal{G}}\to L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),\quad f\to S_{j}^{*}f=\langle\varphi_{j},f\rangle_{j};\quad j=1,2.\end{split}

(55)

Here
\langle.,.\rangle_{j}
denotes the scalar product in

L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{j})
; the notation
\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle
will be used to denote the scalar product in
{\mathcal{G}}
.
One has

S^{*}S=1+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&s_{12}\\[7.22743pt]
s_{21}&0\end{array}\right)\quad\mbox{ on }{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{2}),

where
s_{ij}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\big),\;i\neq j,
are given by

s_{ij}f_{j}=\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}\otimes f_{j}\rangle_{i}.

In the proof of
Proposition
2.7
stated below we shall use that
s_{ij}\in{\mathcal{C}}\big(L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\big)
,
i\neq j
.
This property
is a consequence of the following lemma.

Lemma 2.6
.

For all
r,t\in{\mathbb{R}}

s_{ij}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{r}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),H^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\big)\cap{\mathcal{L}}\big(H^{-2}_{r}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),L^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\big).

Proof.

Note that (
50
) implies that

\displaystyle\ran(\pi_{b}\pi^{a})=\ran(\pi_{b})\text{ if }\#b=2\text{ and }a\not\subset b.

(56)

We apply (
56
) to
a=a_{i}
and
b=a_{j}
and do integration by
parts (or alternatively change variables) obtaining that

s_{ij}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),H^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\big).

Whence for
|\alpha|\leq 2
we have a formula for

\partial^{\alpha}_{x_{i}}s_{ij}
involving partial derivatives

\partial^{\beta}\varphi_{k}
,
k=1,2
and
|\beta|\leq 2
. By using this
formula, polynomial decay of
\partial^{\beta}\varphi_{k}
and the bound

\displaystyle|x^{i}|+|x^{j}|\geq c|x|

(the latter is a consequence of (
50
)) we
obtain that indeed

s_{ij}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{r}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),H^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\big).

Since
s_{ij}=s_{ji}^{*}
also

s_{ij}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(H^{-2}_{r}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),L^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\big).

∎

Proposition 2.7
.

Suppose (
54
). Then

1.

{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}_{1}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
(i.e.
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}+{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
is closed).

2.

S^{*}=0
on
{\mathcal{F}}^{\perp}
and
S^{*}:{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
is
a bi-continuous isomorphism. Similarly
S:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{F}}
is
a bi-continuous isomorphism, and therefore

SS^{*}:{\mathcal{F}}\to{\mathcal{F}}
is invertible on
{\mathcal{F}}
. One has

S^{*}(SS^{*})^{-1}S=1\text{ on }{\mathcal{H}}.

(57)

3.

S^{*}S
is invertible on
{\mathcal{H}}
and

S(S^{*}S)^{-1}S^{*}=\Pi\text{ on }{\mathcal{G}}.

(58)

Proof.

1
.  For
f\in{\mathcal{F}}_{1}+{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
, one has for some
f_{j}\in L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
,

f=\varphi_{1}\otimes f_{1}+\varphi_{2}\otimes f_{2}.

Since
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{2}=\{0\}
, this decomposition is unique. We claim that
N(f)=\|f_{1}\|+\|f_{2}\|
defines a norm on
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}+{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
which is equivalent with the norm of
{\mathcal{G}}
. Clearly, one has

\|f\|\leq N(f).

Conversely, we want to show the existence of a constant
C>0
such that
N(f)\leq C\|f\|
on
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}+{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
. If this is not true, there would be a sequence
g_{n}\in{\mathcal{F}}_{1}+{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
such that
\|g_{n}\|<1/n
and
N(g_{n})=1
.
Write
g_{n}
as

g_{n}=\varphi_{1}\otimes f_{1,n}+\varphi_{2}\otimes f_{2,n}

with
\|f_{1,n}\|+\|f_{2,n}\|=1
. Without loss of generality, we can assume that
\{f_{k,n}\}
converges weakly to
f_{k}
in
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{k})
as
n\to\infty
. Then, one has

\displaystyle f_{1,n}

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi_{1},g_{n}\rangle_{1}-s_{12}f_{2,n}={\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1})-s_{12}f_{2,n},

\displaystyle f_{2,n}

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi_{2},g_{n}\rangle_{2}-s_{21}f_{1,n}={\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1})-s_{21}f_{1,n}.

Substituting the second equation into the first one, we obtain that

f_{1,n}={\mathcal{O}}(n^{-1})+K_{1}f_{1,n},\;K_{1}=s_{12}s_{21}.

Since
f_{1,n}
is weakly convergent and
K_{1}
is compact,
K_{1}f_{1,n}
is strongly convergent.
This implies that
f_{1,n}
converges to some
f_{1}
in

L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})
. Similarly one shows that
f_{2,n}
converges to
some
f_{2}
in
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})
. Since
g_{n}\to 0
, this implies

\varphi_{1}\otimes f_{1}+\varphi_{2}\otimes f_{2}=0,\quad\|f_{1}\|+\|f_{2}\|=1,

which is impossible since
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{2}=\{0\}
. This proves the equivalence of the norms. It follows that
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}+{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
is closed.

2
.  Let
\widetilde{S}:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{F}}
act as
S
, i.e.
\widetilde{S}f=Sf
for
f\in{\mathcal{H}}
. By the equivalence
of norms shown above we see that
\widetilde{S}
is a bi-continuous
isomorphism. Clearly
S^{*}=0
on
{\mathcal{F}}^{\perp}
. On the other hand we can
identify
(S^{*})_{|{\mathcal{F}}}
as the adjoint of the map
\widetilde{S}
, i.e.
(\widetilde{S})^{*}
, which is a bi-continuous
isomorphism (since
\widetilde{S}
is). In particular
SS^{*}
is
invertible on
{\mathcal{F}}
. As for the last part of
2
we note that
P:=S^{*}(SS^{*})^{-1}S
is bijective on
{\mathcal{H}}
and that it is also a projection,
P^{2}=P
.
Therefore
P=1
, showing (
57
).

3
.  The invertibility of
S^{*}S
on
{\mathcal{H}}

follows from
2
. Letting
Q=S(S^{*}S)^{-1}S^{*}
, we note

Q^{2}=Q,\quad Q^{*}=Q,\quad\ran(Q)=\ran(S)={\mathcal{F}},

showing
Q=\Pi
.

∎

Put
\Pi^{\prime}=1-\Pi
and
H^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi^{\prime}
with domain
{\mathcal{D}}(H^{\prime})={\mathcal{D}}(H)\cap\ran(\Pi^{\prime})=\Pi^{\prime}\,{\mathcal{D}}(H)
. We show below that indeed
\Pi^{\prime}
preserves
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
and that
H^{\prime}
is self-adjoint. Let
\Pi_{j}=S_{j}S_{j}^{*}
and
\Pi^{\prime}_{j}=1-\Pi_{j}
;
j=1,2
. We recall
that Condition
1.6
is imposed for
a=a_{1}
and

a=a_{2}
. As in (
46
) we record that

\displaystyle\Pi_{j}^{\prime}I_{j}\Pi_{j}\in{\mathcal{L}}({{\mathcal{G}}});\,j=1,2.

(59)

Here and henceforth we abbreviate
I_{j}(x)=I_{a_{j}}(x)
;
j=1,2
.

Lemma 2.8
.

Suppose
(
54
).
Then

1.

The operators

\Pi-SS^{*}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big({\mathcal{G}},H^{2}({\mathbf{X}})\big).

(60)

2.

\Pi
preserves
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
, and
\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi
and
\Pi H\Pi^{\prime}
(initially defined on

{\mathcal{D}}(H)
) extend to bounded operators on
{\mathcal{G}}
.

3.

H^{\prime}
is self-adjoint.

Proof.

1
.  We shall use
the identities

\displaystyle\Pi

\displaystyle=S(S^{*}S)^{-1}S^{*}=SS^{*}+S\Big(S^{*}S)^{-1}-1\Big)S^{*},

(61a)

\displaystyle(S^{*}S)^{-1}-1

\displaystyle=-\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&s_{12}\\[7.22743pt]
s_{21}&0\end{array}\right)+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&s_{12}\\[7.22743pt]
s_{21}&0\end{array}\right)(S^{*}S)^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&s_{12}\\[7.22743pt]
s_{21}&0\end{array}\right),

which follow from Proposition
2.7
and the general identity

(A+1)^{-1}-1=-A(A+1)^{-1}=-A+A(A+1)^{-1}A,

respectively.
The statement (
60
) follows from Lemma
2.6
,
(
61a
) and (
61
).

2
.
Writing
SS^{*}=\Pi_{1}+\Pi_{2}
it suffices (due to
1
) to show that
\Pi_{j}
preserves
{\mathcal{D}}(H)
and that
\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi_{j}

extends to a bounded operator on

{\mathcal{G}}
(note that boundedness of
\Pi H\Pi^{\prime}
follows from
boundedness of
\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi
). Since
\Pi_{j}
reduces
H_{a_{j}}
clearly
\Pi_{j}
preserves
{\mathcal{D}}(H)={\mathcal{D}}(H_{a_{j}})
, and since
\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi_{j}=\Pi^{\prime}\Pi^{\prime}_{j}I_{a_{j}}\Pi_{j}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{G}})
, cf. (
59
), we are done.

3
.  Take
g\in{\mathcal{D}}\big((H^{\prime})^{*}\big)\subset\Pi^{\prime}{\mathcal{G}}
. The functional
\Pi^{\prime}{\mathcal{D}}(H)\ni f\to\langle g,H^{\prime}\Pi^{\prime}f\rangle
extends to a bounded functional, so by
(
2
) also
{\mathcal{D}}(H)\ni f\to\langle g,Hf\rangle
extends to a bounded functional. This
shows that
g\in{\mathcal{D}}(H^{*})={\mathcal{D}}(H)
. Whence
g\in{\mathcal{D}}(H^{\prime})
.
∎

Some parts of the previous lemma can be generalized as follows.

Lemma 2.9
.

Suppose (
54
). Then

1.

For all
r,t\in{\mathbb{R}}

\Pi-SS^{*}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{r}({\mathbf{X}}),H^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}})\big)\cap{\mathcal{L}}\big(H^{-2}_{r}({\mathbf{X}}),L^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}})\big).

2.

For all
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
there exist extensions

\langle{x}\rangle^{\rho+1}\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi,\,\langle{x}\rangle^{\rho+1}\Pi H\Pi^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}})\big).

Proof.

For
1
we mimic the proof of Lemma
2.8
.
For
2
it suffices (seen by using
1
) to show that

\langle{x}\rangle^{\rho+1}\big(\Pi^{\prime}_{j}-\Pi_{i}\big)I_{j}\Pi_{j},\,\langle{x}\rangle^{\rho+1}\Pi_{j}I_{j}\big(\Pi^{\prime}_{j}-\Pi_{i}\big)\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}})\big);\,i\neq j.

Next we implement (
24a
) of Condition

1.6
. We obtain, cf. Lemma
2.6
and (
53
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\forall r,t&\in{\mathbb{R}}:\quad\Pi_{i}I_{j}\Pi_{j},\,\Pi_{j}I_{j}\Pi_{i}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{r}({\mathbf{X}}),L^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}})\big)\,(i\neq j),\\
&\langle{x}\rangle^{\rho+1}\Pi^{\prime}_{j}I_{j}\Pi_{j},\,\langle{x}\rangle^{\rho+1}\Pi_{j}I_{j}\Pi^{\prime}_{j}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}})\big).\end{split}

(62)

∎

7.1
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{2}=\{0\}
; the case
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
and

\lambda_{0}\notin\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{\prime})

The case
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
is simpler and there are better mapping properties of
various operators compared to the case

\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
. Consequently we pay special attention to the
former case. We shall show the following extension of
[
Wa2
, Lemma 2.1]
.

Lemma 2.10
.

Suppose Condition
2.5
with
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
and
(
54
). Then there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
such that the essential spectrum of

H^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi^{\prime}
satisfies

\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(H^{\prime})\subset[\lambda_{0}+\epsilon_{0},\infty).

(63)

Proof.

Note

\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}<\inf\sigma({H^{b}}),\text{ for }b\not\in\{a_{1},a_{2},a_{\max}\}.

(64)

In particular we have

\lambda_{0}=\min\sigma_{{\mathrm{d}}}(H^{a_{j}}).

We introduce a
auxiliary Hamiltonians (with inter-cluster momenta
p_{j}=p_{a_{j}}
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\breve{H}=H^{\prime}+p_{1}^{2}\Pi_{1}+p_{2}^{2}\Pi_{2},\\
\;&\widetilde{H}=H-\lambda_{0}\big(\Pi_{1}+\Pi_{2}\big);\\
\,&{\mathcal{D}}(\breve{H})={\mathcal{D}}(\widetilde{H})={\mathcal{D}}(H).\end{split}

(65a)

These operators differ by
H
-compact terms:

\displaystyle\breve{H}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{H}-K_{1}+K_{2};

(65b)

\displaystyle K_{1}

\displaystyle=\Pi H\Pi^{\prime}+\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi+\Pi_{1}I_{1}\Pi_{1}+\Pi_{2}I_{2}\Pi_{2},

\displaystyle K_{2}
