\displaystyle=\Pi_{1}H\Pi_{1}+\Pi_{2}H\Pi_{2}-\Pi H\Pi.

Note that the first and second terms of
K_{1}
are
H
-compact due to Lemma

2.9
. The relative compactness of the third and fourth
terms of
K_{1}
follows from
rewriting
\Pi_{j}I_{j}\Pi_{j}=\varphi_{j}\otimes S_{j}^{*}I_{j}S_{j}\langle\varphi_{j}\rvert

and then invoking the complete analogue of (
52
). As for
K_{2}
we can also use Lemma

2.9
first by replacing the factors of
\Pi
in the
third term by
SS^{*}=\Pi_{1}+\Pi_{2}
and then expanding into four
terms. We are left with considering the sum of cross terms

\Pi_{1}H\Pi_{2}+\Pi_{2}H\Pi_{1}.

By writing

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Pi_{1}H\Pi_{2}&=\Pi_{1}I_{1}\Pi_{2}+H_{a_{1}}\Pi_{1}\Pi_{2},\\
\Pi_{1}\Pi_{2}&=\varphi_{1}\otimes s_{12}\langle\varphi_{2}|,\end{split}

(66)

and then using (
62
) and Lemma

2.6
,
we conclude that the term
\Pi_{1}H\Pi_{2}
is

H
-compact. We
can argue similarly for the term
\Pi_{2}H\Pi_{1}
and then conclude that
also
K_{2}
is

H
-compact.

Next, it follows from the very definition (
65a
) that
{\mathcal{F}}
reduces
\breve{H}
. Whence
\sigma_{\textrm{ess }}(H^{\prime})\subset\sigma_{\textrm{ess }}(\breve{H})
,
and consequently it suffices to show (
63
) with
H^{\prime}
replaced
by
\breve{H}

and hence in turn with
H^{\prime}
replaced
by
\widetilde{H}
(by the compactness shown above).

Consider a family of smooth non-negative functions
\{j_{b}|b\in{\mathcal{A}},\#b=2\}
on
{\mathbf{X}}

obeying that for some
c>0
:

\displaystyle\sum_{\#b=2}j_{b}(x)^{2}

\displaystyle=1,

(67a)

\displaystyle|x^{a}|j_{b}(x)

\displaystyle\geq c|x|j_{b}(x)\text{ for }a\not\subset b\text{ and }|x|\geq 1,

(67b)

\displaystyle|\partial^{\alpha}j_{b}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\alpha}\langle{x}\rangle^{-|\alpha|}\text{ for all }\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\dim{\mathbf{X}}}.

(67c)

We have, using this family of functions,

H=\sum_{\#a=2}j_{a}H_{a}j_{a}+\sum_{\#a=2}I_{a}j_{a}^{2}-\sum_{\#a=2}|\nabla j_{a}|^{2}=\sum_{\#a=2}j_{a}H_{a}j_{a}+K,

(68)

where
K
is
H
-compact.

For
a\neq a_{j}
,
j=1,2
, it follows from (
64
) that
\inf\sigma(H_{a})=\inf\sigma(H^{a})\geq\lambda_{0}+\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}
for some
\epsilon_{0}^{\prime}>0
.
Therefore,

\sum_{\#a=2,a\neq a_{j},j=1,2}j_{a}H_{a}j_{a}\geq(\lambda_{0}+\epsilon_{0}^{\prime})\sum_{\#a=2,a\neq a_{j},j=1,2}j_{a}^{2}.

(69)

Since
\lambda_{0}
is the lowest eigenvalue of
H^{a_{j}}
,
j=1,2
, there exists
\epsilon_{1}>0
such that

\Pi_{j}^{\prime}H_{a_{j}}\Pi_{j}^{\prime}\geq(\lambda_{0}+\epsilon_{1})\Pi^{\prime}_{j}.

Hence for
a=a_{j}
,
j=1,2
,
we obtain (assuming in the last step that
\lambda_{0}+\epsilon_{1}\leq 0
and
using that
p_{j}^{2}\geq 0
)

\displaystyle j_{a_{j}}H_{a_{j}}j_{a_{j}}

\displaystyle\geq(\lambda_{0}+\epsilon_{1})j_{a_{j}}\Pi^{\prime}_{j}j_{a_{j}}+j_{a_{j}}H_{a_{j}}\Pi_{j}j_{a_{j}}

\displaystyle\geq(\lambda_{0}+\epsilon_{1})j_{a_{j}}^{2}+\lambda_{0}j_{a_{j}}\Pi_{j}j_{a_{j}}.

(70)

It is a consequence of (
67a
) and (
67b
) that
the operator
\Pi_{j}j_{a_{j}}^{2}-\Pi_{j}
is
H
-compact. Using (
67c
) it follows by a Taylor
expansion that
[j_{a_{j}},\Pi_{j}]\langle x\rangle
is bounded (cf.
(
135
)). We conclude that also

K^{\prime}_{j}:=\lambda_{0}\big(j_{a_{j}}\Pi_{j}j_{a_{j}}-\Pi_{j}\big)\text{ is }H\text{-compact}.

(71)

We write (
7.1
) as

j_{a_{j}}H_{a_{j}}j_{a_{j}}-\lambda_{0}\Pi_{j}\geq(\lambda_{0}+\epsilon_{1})j_{a_{j}}^{2}+K^{\prime}_{j},\;j=1,2.

(72)

Let
\epsilon_{0}=\min\{\epsilon_{0}^{\prime},\epsilon_{1}\}
.
We then deduce from (
67a
), (
68
), (
69
) and (
72
)
that

\widetilde{H}\geq(\lambda_{0}+\epsilon_{0})+\widetilde{K},

(73)

where
\widetilde{K}=K+K^{\prime}_{1}+K^{\prime}_{2}
.

Since
\widetilde{K}
is
\widetilde{H}
-compact it follows from (
73
) and Weyl’s theorem
[
RS
, Theorem
XIII.14]
that

\sigma_{{\mathrm{ess}}}(\widetilde{H})\subset[\lambda_{0}+\epsilon_{0},\infty).

∎

Remark 2.11
.

By appropriately enlarging the projection
\Pi
as
to include the span of all threshold eigenstates corresponding
to thresholds
\lambda^{a}\in[\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{3}-\epsilon_{0})
for
any given
\epsilon_{0}>0
, one can make sure that
\sigma_{{\mathrm{ess}}}(H^{\prime})\subset[\Sigma_{3}-\epsilon_{0},\infty)
. Here
H^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi^{\prime}
as
before, and the proof is essentially the same as the one of Lemma

2.10
. This trick of ‘subtracting’ all low-energy

2
-cluster channels is limited to energies below
\Sigma_{3}
. We
prefer for simplicity of presentation to treat two-cluster
thresholds
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
on an equal footing not
distinguishing between the cases
\lambda_{0}\in(\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{3})

and
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{3}
. In fact our results would be the same
anyway for the two cases.

Assume from this point that

\lambda_{0}\text{ is not an eigenvalue of }H^{\prime}.

(74)

By Lemma
2.10
we can then chose
\delta>0

such that
\sigma(H^{\prime})\cap\{|z-\lambda_{0}|\leq\delta\}=\emptyset
.
Due to Proposition
2.1

R(z)=E(z)-E_{+}(z)E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}E_{-}(z),

(75)

where the quantities (
34a
)–(
34d
) in
this case are analytic in
\{|z-\lambda_{0}|<\delta\}
.

Let us analyse the form of
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)
. For
f\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
f=\varphi_{1}\otimes f_{1}+\varphi_{2}\otimes f_{2}
and we can write

Hf=\lambda_{0}f+(p_{1}^{2}+I_{1})(\varphi_{1}\otimes f_{1})+(p_{2}^{2}+I_{2})(\varphi_{2}\otimes f_{2}),

recalling
p_{j}=p_{a_{j}}
and
I_{j}=I_{a_{j}}
,
j=1,2
.
Therefore, for
i,j,k\in\{1,2\}

\displaystyle S_{k}^{*}(H-z)S_{k}

\displaystyle=p_{k}^{2}+\lambda_{0}-z+W_{k}(x_{k}),

(76a)

\displaystyle S_{i}^{*}(H-z)S_{j}

\displaystyle=\check{s}_{ij}(z)+W_{ij},

(76b)

\displaystyle S_{i}^{*}HR^{\prime}(z)HS_{j}

\displaystyle=-K_{ij}(z),

(76c)

where

\displaystyle\check{s}_{ij}(z)

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}\otimes(p_{j}^{2}+\lambda_{0}-z)\cdot\rangle_{i},

(77a)

\displaystyle W_{ij}

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi_{i},I_{j}\varphi_{j}\otimes\cdot\rangle_{i},

(77b)

\displaystyle W_{k}(\cdot)=W_{kk}

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi_{k},I_{k}\varphi_{k}\rangle_{k}(\cdot),

(77c)

\displaystyle K_{ij}(z)

\displaystyle=-\langle\varphi_{i},I_{i}R^{\prime}(z)I_{j}(\varphi_{j}\otimes\cdot)\rangle_{i}.

(77d)

Introducing
{\mathcal{H}}^{2}=H^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus H^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})
this yields the
following expression for the operator
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z):{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\to{\mathcal{H}}
.

\displaystyle E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)={}

\displaystyle z-\lambda_{0}

\displaystyle-\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\Delta_{x_{1}}+W_{1}(x_{1})+K_{11}(z)&\check{s}_{12}(z)+W_{12}+K_{12}(z)\\
\check{s}_{21}(z)+W_{21}+K_{21}(z)&-\Delta_{x_{2}}+W_{2}(x_{2})+K_{22}(z)\end{array}\right).

Clearly
\check{s}_{ij}(z)=s_{ij}(\lambda_{0}-z+p_{j}^{2})
for

i\neq j
, and therefore Lemma
2.6
yields that
\check{s}_{ij}(z)
for

i\neq j
is bounded
and in fact polynomially
decreasing (uniformly in
z
near
\lambda_{0}
). Here and henceforth an operator
b_{ij}:L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})\to L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})
is
said to be
polynomially decreasing
if for all
r,t\in{\mathbb{R}}

b_{ij}\in{\mathcal{C}}\big(H^{2}_{r}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),L^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\big)\cap{\mathcal{C}}\big(L^{2}_{r}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),H^{-2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\big).

We note that also the operators
W_{12}
and
W_{21}
are polynomially
decreasing. An operator
B
on
{\mathcal{H}}
is
said to be polynomially decreasing if its entries
b_{ij}:L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})\to L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})
are polynomially
decreasing.

We claim that also
K_{12}(z)
and
K_{21}(z)
are polynomially
decreasing, in fact uniformly in
z
near
\lambda_{0}
. To see this it suffices (by symmetry) to consider

K_{12}(z)
, and it suffices to show that
K_{12}(z)\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{-s}({\mathbf{X}}_{2}),L^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\big)
for any
s\geq 0
. So let us
fix
s\geq 0
. A small consideration using the argument for
(
46
) and the polynomial decay of the cluster
bound states shows, that it suffices to check that

\displaystyle\langle x^{1}\rangle^{-2s}\langle x\rangle^{s}R^{\prime}(z)\langle x\rangle^{s}\langle x^{2}\rangle^{-2s}\text{ is
uniformly bounded near }\lambda_{0}.

(80)

Recall that
\{z||z-\lambda_{0}|\leq\delta\}
is included in the resolvent
set of
H^{\prime}
for a small
\delta>0
, cf. (
74
). We also
record the
following
elementary estimates,

\displaystyle\langle x+y\rangle^{t}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{|t|/2}\langle x\rangle^{|t|}\langle y\rangle^{t},

(81a)

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle

\displaystyle\leq C(\langle x^{1}\rangle+\langle x^{2}\rangle)\leq 2C\langle x^{1}\rangle\langle x^{2}\rangle.

(81b)

In turn, by interpolation and by using (
81b
), the
assertion (
80
) is a consequence of

\displaystyle\langle x^{2}\rangle^{t}R^{\prime}(z)\langle x^{2}\rangle^{-t}\text{ and }\langle x^{1}\rangle^{-t}R^{\prime}(z)\langle x^{1}\rangle^{t}\text{ are
uniformly bounded};\quad t=2s.

(82)

We bound the first expression only.
Representing, using notation of the proof of Lemma
2.10
,

\displaystyle R^{\prime}(z)=\breve{R}(z)\Pi^{\prime},\,\,\breve{R}(z)=(\breve{H}-z)^{-1},

(83)

we first
bound
\langle x^{2}\rangle^{t}\breve{R}(z)\langle x^{2}\rangle^{-t}
.
Whence we want to bound
\langle\kappa x^{2}\rangle^{t}\breve{R}(z)\langle\kappa x^{2}\rangle^{-t}
with

\kappa=1
,
for which it suffices to bound this quantity for any small

\kappa>0
. We show such bound uniformly in
z
near

\lambda_{0}
. As in the proof of Lemma
2.10

\displaystyle\breve{H}=H-\lambda_{0}(\Pi_{1}+\Pi_{2})-K_{1}+K_{2}.

(84)

Letting
\breve{H}_{\kappa,t}=\langle\kappa x^{2}\rangle^{t}\breve{H}\langle\kappa x^{2}\rangle^{-t}
we obtain from (
84
) that

\breve{H}_{\kappa,t}=\breve{H}+{\mathcal{O}}(\kappa)(\breve{H}-\mathrm{i}).

For example, seen by using Taylor
expansion and (
81a
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\kappa x^{2}\rangle^{t}\Pi_{1}\langle\kappa x^{2}\rangle^{-t}-\Pi_{1}&=[\langle\kappa\pi^{2}x\rangle^{t}-\langle\kappa\pi^{2}x_{1}\rangle^{t},\Pi_{1}]\langle\kappa x^{2}\rangle^{-t}\\
&=\kappa{\mathcal{O}}\big(\langle\kappa\pi^{2}x_{1}\rangle^{t-1}\big)\langle\kappa x_{1}\rangle^{-t}\\
&={\mathcal{O}}(\kappa).\end{split}

(85)

We can treat the terms
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
similarly.
Therefore

\displaystyle\big(\breve{H}_{\kappa,t}-z\big)\breve{R}(z)=1+{\mathcal{O}}(\kappa)(\breve{H}-\mathrm{i})\breve{R}(z)

is invertible for

|z-\lambda_{0}|\leq\delta
and for small
\kappa>0
. This shows that
(\breve{H}_{\kappa,t}-z)^{-1}
is uniformly bounded accomplishing our
first goal. The second goal, cf. (
83
), is to
bound
\langle x^{2}\rangle^{t}\Pi^{\prime}\langle x^{2}\rangle^{-t}
, but indeed
\langle x^{2}\rangle^{t}\Pi\langle x^{2}\rangle^{-t}
is bounded due to Lemma
2.9

1
and (
85
). Consequently we have justified
(
82
) and therefore shown that

K_{12}(z)
is polynomially
decreasing uniformly near
\lambda_{0}
.

One can somewhat similarly show that

\langle{x_{j}}\rangle^{\rho_{1}}K_{jj}(z)\langle{x_{j}}\rangle^{\rho_{2}}\text{ is uniformly bounded for on }L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})\text{ for }\rho_{1}+\rho_{2}\leq 2\rho+2.

(86)

In fact we can use a refinement of (
46
) related to
(
53
) and Lemma
2.9

2
. Note here the
Taylor expansion

I^{(1)}_{j}(x)=I^{(1)}_{j}(x_{j})+{\mathcal{O}}(\langle x^{j}\rangle^{\rho+2})\langle x_{j}\rangle^{-\rho-1}
,
cf. (
81a
), which in turn leads to the following bounds for
all
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and for
\rho^{\prime}\in\{\rho_{1},\rho_{2}\}
,

\displaystyle\Pi_{j}\langle{x_{j}}\rangle^{\rho^{\prime}}I_{j}\Pi^{\prime},\quad\Pi^{\prime}I_{j}\langle{x_{j}}\rangle^{\rho^{\prime}}\Pi_{j}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}),L^{2}_{\rho-\rho^{\prime}+1+s}({\mathbf{X}})\big).

We deduce (
86
) by combining these bounds with the following consequence of
(
81a
)
and (
82
),

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle^{s}\breve{R}(z)\Pi^{\prime}\langle x\rangle^{-s}\text{ is bounded for all }s\in{\mathbb{R}}\,\,(\text{uniformly near }\lambda_{0}).

(87)

Due to the above discussion and (
52
) we finally
obtain a simplified version of (
7.1
):

Proposition 2.12
.

Suppose Condition
2.5
with

\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
,
(
54
) and (
74
). As a bounded operator
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z):{\mathcal{H}}^{2}\to{\mathcal{H}}
one then has

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)\equiv z-\lambda_{0}-\left(\begin{array}[]{c !{\kern-7mm} c}-\Delta_{x_{1}}+W_{1}(x_{1})+K_{11}(z)\hfil\lx@intercol\kern-19.91692pt&0\\[5.69054pt]
0\hfil\lx@intercol\kern-19.91692pt&-\Delta_{x_{2}}+W_{2}(x_{2})+K_{22}(z)\end{array}\right),

(88)

where “
\equiv
” means the equality modulo a polynomially decreasing
term which depends holomorphically on
z
near
\lambda_{0}
. We have

\displaystyle W_{j}(x_{j})-I^{(1)}_{j}(x_{j})

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(H^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),L^{2}_{\rho+1+s}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})\big)\text{ for }s\in{\mathbb{R}},

(89a)

\displaystyle W_{j}(x_{j})-I^{(1)}_{j}(x_{j})

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{C}}\big(H^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),L^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})\big)\text{ for }s\in{\mathbb{R}},\,t<\rho+1+s,

(89b)

\displaystyle K_{jj}(z)

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),L^{2}_{2\rho+2+s}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})\big)\text{ for }s\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(89c)

Moreover the operator
K_{jj}(z)
depends holomorphically on
z
,
and the potential
W_{j}
is
p_{j}^{2}
-compact with the singularities of
located in a bounded set.

Remark 2.13
.

We shall prefer a version of
Proposition
2.12
based on forms rather than
operators, although this is not essential. Thus we consider
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)
as an operator
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z):H^{1}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus H^{1}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})\to H^{-1}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus H^{-1}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})
.
Now an operator
b_{ij}:L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})\to L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})
is
said to be
polynomially decreasing
if for all
r,t\in{\mathbb{R}}

b_{ij}\in{\mathcal{C}}\big(H^{1}_{r}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),H^{-1}_{t}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\big).

Also we note that the expansion (
89a
)
should be given the interpretation of being in the space

{\mathcal{L}}\big(H^{1}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),H^{-1}_{\rho+1+s}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})\big)
,
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, rather than in the
stated space (and similarly for (
89b
)). Note for comparison that

I_{j}^{(1)}(x_{j})\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),L^{2}_{\rho+s}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})\big)
,

s\in{\mathbb{R}}
. With these modifications (
88
) still holds.
Moreover it is easy to show by a resolvent equation that the null space
\ker E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
is independent on whether the operator or the
form interpretation of
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
is used.

8
The case
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{\prime})

We discuss briefly the modifications needed in the previous two
sections to treat the case
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{\prime})
.

8.1
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{\prime})
; non-multiple case

For simplicity we assume

m=1
in the setting of Section
6
. The corresponding eigenfunction is denoted
by
\varphi
(rather than
\varphi_{1}
), and we shall use the notation
\langle\varphi|\cdot\rangle_{0}=\langle\varphi,\cdot\rangle_{0}=\langle\varphi,\cdot\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a_{0}})}
. We follow
[
Wa2
]
assuming for simplicity
that
\lambda_{0}
is a simple eigenvalue of
H^{\prime}
and introduce a
corresponding normalized eigenfunction
\psi
,

(H^{\prime}-\lambda_{0})\psi=0
. Let
I_{0}=I_{a_{0}}
.

Now

{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}})\oplus{\mathbb{C}}
, and
S:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{G}}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
is given by

\displaystyle S(f,c)=\varphi\otimes f+c\psi.

We let the orthogonal
projection
SS^{*}
onto the range of this new
S
be denoted by

1-\Pi^{\prime\prime}
(for the
S
in Section
6
the projection is denoted
by
1-\Pi^{\prime}
), and we introduce

H^{\prime\prime}=\Pi^{\prime\prime}H\Pi^{\prime\prime}
on the Hilbert space
\Pi^{\prime\prime}{\mathcal{G}}
. By construction

\lambda_{0}
is not an eigenvalue of
H^{\prime\prime}
. Using
(
34a
)–(
34d
) as before we obtain (
49
) now with

\displaystyle-E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)

\displaystyle=-S^{*}\big(z-H+HR^{\prime\prime}(z)H\big)S

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Big(\lambda_{0}-z+p^{2}_{a_{0}}+\langle\varphi|{I_{0}}|\varphi\otimes\cdot\rangle_{0}-\langle\varphi|I_{0}R^{\prime\prime}(z)I_{0}|\varphi\otimes\cdot\rangle_{0}\Big)&\langle\varphi,I_{0}\psi\rangle_{0}\\[7.22743pt]
\langle I_{0}\psi,\varphi\otimes\cdot\rangle&\lambda_{0}-z\end{array}\right).

Let

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{H}&=H-\lambda_{0}\Pi,\quad\Pi=\varphi\otimes\langle\varphi|\cdot\rangle_{0},\\
\breve{H}&=\widetilde{H}-\breve{K};\\
&\quad\breve{K}=\Pi I_{0}+I_{0}\Pi-\Pi I_{0}\Pi+\lambda_{0}|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|.\end{split}

(90a)

Note that
\breve{K}
is
H
-compact. This
construction is motivated by the following direct sum decomposition.

\breve{H}=H^{\prime\prime}+p^{2}_{a_{0}}\Pi;\;H^{\prime\prime}=\Pi^{\prime\prime}H\Pi^{\prime\prime}.

(90b)

The basic structure of resolvents is given as follows.

\displaystyle\begin{split}R^{\prime\prime}(z)&=\breve{R}(z)-(p_{a_{0}}^{2}-z)^{-1}\Pi-z^{-1}|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|,\\
R^{\prime\prime}(z)&=\Pi^{\prime\prime}\breve{R}(z)\Pi^{\prime\prime}=\Pi^{\prime\prime}\breve{R}(z)=\breve{R}(z)\Pi^{\prime\prime};\quad\breve{R}(z)=(\breve{H}-z)^{-1}.\end{split}

(90c)

We shall prove that
\psi\in H^{2}_{\infty}({\mathbf{X}})
(see Theorem

3.12
), which in turn implies ‘good’ properties
of
\breve{R}(z)
and
\Pi^{\prime\prime}
near
\lambda_{0}
(see Remark
3.21
).

8.2
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{\prime})
; multiple case

We adapt the setting of Section
7
in the case (
74
) of Subsection

7.1
is not
fulfilled and without imposing the condition

\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
as in that subsection. In particular (
54
) is satisfied.

Assume for simplicity
that
\lambda_{0}
is a simple eigenvalue of
H^{\prime}
. We introduce a
corresponding normalized eigenfunction
(H^{\prime}-\lambda_{0})\psi=0
. Now

{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})\oplus{\mathbb{C}}
, and
S:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{G}}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
is given by

\displaystyle S(f_{1},f_{2},c)=\varphi_{1}\otimes f_{1}+\varphi_{2}\otimes f_{2}+c\psi.

We introduce
\Pi^{\prime\prime}
in terms of this
S
as in
the previous subsection and let again
H^{\prime\prime}=\Pi^{\prime\prime}H\Pi^{\prime\prime}
. Note that

\Pi^{\prime\prime}=1-\Pi-|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|=\Pi^{\prime}-|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|

where
\Pi
is given as in Section
7
. We obtain (
49
) now with

\displaystyle E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)=S^{*}\big(z-H+HR^{\prime\prime}(z)H\big)S),

and this operator has a similar representation as in the previous
subsection, now by a
3\times 3
-block representation

(e_{ij})_{i,j\leq 3}
rather than a

2\times 2
-block representation as given there. Here

(e_{ij})_{i,j\leq 2}
is given as in Proposition
2.12
(with

R^{\prime}(z)
replaced by
R^{\prime\prime}(z)
),
e_{33}=z-\lambda_{0}
,
e_{i3}=-\langle\varphi_{i},I_{i}\psi\rangle_{i}
and
e_{3i}=e_{i3}^{*}=\langle e_{3i}|
;
i=1,2
.

The analogue of (
65a
) reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\breve{H}=H^{\prime\prime}+p_{1}^{2}\Pi_{1}+p_{2}^{2}\Pi_{2},\\
\;&\widetilde{H}=H-\lambda_{0}\big(\Pi_{1}+\Pi_{2}\big);\\
\,&{\mathcal{D}}(\breve{H})={\mathcal{D}}(\widetilde{H})={\mathcal{D}}(H).\end{split}

(91a)

These operators differ by
H
-compact terms, cf. (
65b
):

\displaystyle\breve{H}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{H}-K_{1}+K_{2};

(91b)

\displaystyle K_{1}

\displaystyle=\Pi H\Pi^{\prime}+\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi+\Pi_{1}I_{1}\Pi_{1}+\Pi_{2}I_{2}\Pi_{2}+\lambda_{0}|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|,

\displaystyle K_{2}

\displaystyle=\Pi_{1}H\Pi_{1}+\Pi_{2}H\Pi_{2}-\Pi H\Pi.

The basic structure of resolvents is given as follows.

\displaystyle\begin{split}R^{\prime\prime}(z)&=\breve{R}(z)-(p_{1}^{2}\Pi_{1}+p_{2}^{2}\Pi_{2}-z)^{-1}\Pi-z^{-1}|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|,\\
R^{\prime\prime}(z)&=\Pi^{\prime\prime}\breve{R}(z)\Pi^{\prime\prime}=\Pi^{\prime\prime}\breve{R}(z)=\breve{R}(z)\Pi^{\prime\prime};\quad\breve{R}(z)=(\breve{H}-z)^{-1}.\end{split}

(91c)

Again we have good decay properties of
\psi
, cf. Theorem

3.12
and Remark

3.20
, which in turn yields good
properties of
\breve{R}(z)
near
\lambda_{0}
, cf. Remark
3.21
.

9
Multiple two-cluster case,
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\neq\{0\}

It may happen that the condition (
54
) is not satisfied. We
start out by presenting an
example (the only one we know). It is given by an atomic type
2
-body Schrödinger
operator with a third particle of infinite mass fixed at the origin
(fitting into the framework of
N
-body case of Section
3
):

H=\sum_{j=1}^{2}(-\Delta_{x^{j}}+V_{j}(x^{j}))+V_{12}(x^{1}-x^{2}),

(92)

where
x^{j}\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
. Here
x=(x^{1},x^{2})
is used as global coordinates on
{\mathbb{R}}^{2n}
.
Let
\lambda_{0}
be the lowest threshold of
H
. Assume that this threshold is double and is attained by the lowest eigenvalue of
H_{j}

H_{j}=-\Delta_{x^{j}}+V_{j}(x^{j}),\quad j=1,2.

In this case, the condition (
54
) is not satisfied. In fact, let
\varphi_{j}(x^{j})
be the eigenfunction of
H_{j}
associated with
\lambda_{0}
.
Then clearly one has

{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{2}=\mbox{span}\{\varphi_{1}(x^{1})\varphi_{2}(x^{2})\}.

\fancybreak

9.1
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\neq\{0\}
; a general
approach

We discuss a method which is easy to generalize to the case of
an arbitrary multiplicity of the two-cluster threshold
\lambda_{0}
.

We define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}=\big\{f\in L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})\mid f\perp\ker{S^{*}S}\big\},

(93)

where the components of this
S=(S_{i})
are given by
(
55
). Due to Lemma
2.6
the space

\ker{S^{*}S}
is finite dimensional consisting of vectors with
components in
H^{2}_{\infty}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
. By assumption

\dim(\ker{S^{*}S})\geq 1
. Clearly
S:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}_{1}+{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\subset{\mathcal{G}}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
is a continuous
isomorphism. Arguing by contradiction as in the proof of Proposition

2.7
we deduce that in fact
S:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{F}}
is
bi-continuous. In particular
{\mathcal{F}}
is closed in
{\mathcal{G}}
, and we let
correspondingly
\Pi
,
\Pi^{\prime}
,
H^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime}(z)
be given (as in
Section
5
). If we consider
S
as a
map from the bigger space
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})
(as in
(
93
)) the notation
S^{*}g
,
g\in{\mathcal{G}}
, may seem
ambiguous. However this is in fact not the case since then
S^{*}g\perp\ker{S^{*}S}
, so
S^{*}g\in{\mathcal{H}}
. For this reason the conclusion of
Lemmas
2.8
and
2.9
are still valid and
the formula (
7.1
) applies again.

As before we would like to use (
7.1
) in a neighbourhood of

\lambda_{0}
. Let us here assume that
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
and
(
74
). Then of course we can
let
z=\lambda_{0}
in (
7.1
) and obtain formulas as in
Proposition
2.12
and Remark
2.13
. We consider
correspondingly
the operator
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
either as an operator mapping

\big(H^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus H^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})\big)\cap{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
or as an operator mapping

\displaystyle\big(H^{1}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus H^{1}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})\big)\cap{\mathcal{H}}\to\{f\in H^{-1}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus H^{-1}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})\mid f\perp\ker{S^{*}S}\}.

If

\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{\prime})
we need to modify the
construction of
S
and
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
. This is doable along the lines of Subsection

8.2
.
Finally letting
P_{0}
denote the orthogonal projection onto

\ker S^{*}S
in
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})
it is convenient
to study
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})+P_{0}
on
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})

(rather than
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
on
{\mathcal{H}}
). This is because we have
a ‘good
parametrix’ of
{\mathrm{diag}}(h_{1},h_{2})
on

L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})
.

In Section
15
we
shall see what the above ideas amount to in
the setting of the models of physics introduced in Chapter

1
. This will be a general treatment not assuming
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
.

Chapter 3
Spectral analysis of
H^{\prime}
near
\lambda_{0}

In the bulk of this chapter we impose Conditions
1.5

and
2.4
. We shall prove various
N
-body resolvent
estimates for the operator
H^{\prime}

appearing in the Grushin method (or more prescisely for the operator

\breve{H}
defined below). The analysis overlaps
[
AIIS
]
, in
particular it is based on an appropriate Mourre estimate. Sharing the
spirit of
[
AIIS
]
our procedure avoids in any other sense the
‘classical Mourre theory’

[
Mo
,
Je
,
JMP
,
Wa1
]
. The multiple two-cluster case can be treated in
a similar way, although it is notationally more complicated, see
Remarks
3.20
–
3.22
.

Recall from Section
6
the notation
I_{0}=I_{a_{0}}
and
p_{0}=p_{a_{0}}
. We introduce the following modifications of
H
(note the
similarity with (
65a
) and (
65b
)),

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widetilde{H}&=H-\lambda_{0}\Pi,\\
\breve{H}&=\widetilde{H}-\breve{K};\\
&\quad\breve{K}=\Pi I_{0}+I_{0}\Pi-\Pi I_{0}\Pi.\end{split}

(94a)

Note that
\breve{K}
is
H
-compact. This
construction is motivated by the following direct sum decomposition.

\breve{H}=H^{\prime}+p^{2}_{0}\Pi;\;H^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi^{\prime}.

(94b)

The basic structure for resolvents is given as follows.

\displaystyle\begin{split}R^{\prime}(z)&=\breve{R}(z)-(p_{0}^{2}-z)^{-1}\Pi,\\
R^{\prime}(z)&=\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(z)\Pi^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(z)=\breve{R}(z)\Pi^{\prime};\quad\breve{R}(z)=(\breve{H}-z)^{-1}.\end{split}

(95a)

Note that (
95a
) yields ‘good estimates’ of
R^{\prime}(z)

near
\lambda_{0}
provided we can show ‘good estimates’ of
\breve{R}(z)
near
\lambda_{0}
. The goal of this chapter is to prove the
latter, which more or less correspond to (
172
) and Corollary

3.19
, stated as follows: Suppose

\lambda_{0}\notin\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(\breve{H})
. Then there exists the strong
weak-star limits

\displaystyle\swslim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)=\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}({\mathbf{X}}),{\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}^{*}({\mathbf{X}})),

(95b)

and if
\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)f=\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)f
for a given
f\in L^{2}_{s}
for some
s>1/2
, then

\displaystyle\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)f=\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)f\in L^{2}_{s-1}.

(95c)

We shall prove a Mourre estimate for
\breve{H}
near
\lambda_{0}
and
show that
\lambda_{0}
cannot be an accumulating point of eigenvalues
of
\breve{H}
(which are the same as those of
H^{\prime}
). If
\lambda_{0}
is
not an eigenvalue, the limiting absorption principle and some
microlocal estimates hold for the resolvent of
\breve{H}
near

\lambda_{0}
. If
\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue of
\breve{H}
, the
associated eigenfunctions are polynomially decaying. We don’t assume
that
\lambda_{0}<\Sigma_{3}
.

The case of a multiple two-cluster
threshold can be treated similarly and will not be discussed in
detail. See Remark
3.20
for a discussion
and see (
214a
)–(
214c
) for results similar to
(
95a
)–(
95c
).

10
Mourre estimate

We shall use the vector
field constructed by Graf
[
Gr
]
and the associated family
of conjugate operators, cf.
[
Sk1
]
–
[
Sk3
]
and
[
IS1
]
. This vector field satisfies the following
properties, cf.
[
Sk3
, Lemma 4.3]
.

Lemma 3.1
.

There exist on
{\mathbf{X}}
a smooth vector
field
\tilde{\omega}
with symmetric derivative
\tilde{\omega}_{*}
and a
partition of unity
\{\tilde{q}_{a}\}
indexed by
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
and
consisting of smooth functions,
0\leq\tilde{q}_{a}\leq 1
, such
that for some positive constants
r_{1}
and
r_{2}

1.

\tilde{\omega}_{*}(x)\geq\sum_{a}\pi_{a}\tilde{q}_{a}.

2.

\tilde{\omega}^{a}(x)=0\text{ if }|x^{a}|<r_{1}.

3.

|x^{b}|>r_{1}\text{ on }\operatorname{supp}(\tilde{q}_{a})\text{ if }b\not\subset a.

4.

|x^{a}|<r_{2}\text{ on }\operatorname{supp}(\tilde{q}_{a}).

5.

For all
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\dim{\mathbf{X}}}
and

k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
there exist
C\in{\mathbb{R}}
:

|\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\tilde{q}_{a}|+|\partial^{\alpha}_{x}(x\cdot\nabla)^{k}\big(\tilde{\omega}(x)-x\big)|\leq C.

For each
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
there is a similar vector field, denoted by

\tilde{\omega}^{a}
, and from the
construction of these vector fields there is a relationship we are
going to use (see
[
Sk2
, Appendix
A]
for a proof for the model of Section
2.1
, see also
[
Sk6
, Section 5]
).

For any
\delta>0
there exists
\widetilde{R}=\widetilde{R}(\delta)>1
such that for all

a\in{\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle\tilde{\omega}(x)

\displaystyle=x_{a}+\tilde{\omega}^{a}(x^{a})\text{ for all }x\in{\mathbf{Y}},

(96)

\displaystyle{\mathbf{Y}}={\mathbf{Y}}_{a,\delta,\widetilde{R}}

\displaystyle:=\{x\in{\mathbf{X}}\mid|x|>\widetilde{R},\,|x^{b}|>\delta|x|\text{ if }b\not\subset a\}.

Considering rescaled vector fields
\tilde{\omega}^{a}_{R}(x):=R\tilde{\omega}(x^{a}/R)
,

R\geq 1
, obviously a
consequence of (
96
) is the analogous result for the
rescaled fields,

\tilde{\omega}_{R}(x)=x_{a}+\tilde{\omega}^{a}_{R}(x^{a})\text{ for all }x\in{\mathbf{Y}}_{a,\delta,R\widetilde{R}},\;R\geq 1.

(97)

Now, proceeding as in
[
Sk3
]
, we introduce

A_{R}=\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\tilde{\omega}_{R}(x)\cdot p+p\cdot\tilde{\omega}_{R}(x)\big),\;R\geq 1,

and a function
d:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
by

d(\lambda)=\begin{cases}\inf_{\tau\in{\mathcal{T}}(\lambda)}(\lambda-\tau),\;{\mathcal{T}}(\lambda):={\mathcal{T}}\cap\,]-\infty,\lambda]\neq\emptyset,\\
1,\;{\mathcal{T}}(\lambda)=\emptyset.\end{cases}

(98)

These devices enter into the following Mourre estimate
(we refer to
[
PSS
]
for another
N
-body Mourre estimate). We refer to

[
Sk3
, Corollary 4.5]
noting that all inputs needed for the proof are
stated in Lemma
3.1
.

Lemma 3.2
.

For all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\epsilon>0
the
exists
R_{0}\geq 1
such that for all
R\geq R_{0}
there is a neighbourhood

{\mathcal{V}}
of
\lambda
and a compact operator
K
on
L^{2}
such that

f(H)\mathrm{i}[H,A_{R}]f(H)\geq f(H)\{2d(\lambda)-\epsilon-K\}f(H)\text{ for all }\text{ real }f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathcal{V}}).

(99)

We can write
\tilde{\omega}=\nabla r^{2}/2
for some positive smooth
function
r
with
r^{2}-x^{2}
bounded, cf.
[
De3
]
.

A basic ingredient of our procedure is the operator

\displaystyle B=\tfrac{1}{2}\big(\omega(x)\cdot p+p\cdot\omega(x)\big)\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{1},L^{2}),

(100)

where
\omega=\omega_{R}=\tilde{\omega}_{R}/r_{R}
,
r_{R}(x)=Rr(\tfrac{x}{R})
. Note that
\tilde{\omega}_{R}=\nabla r_{R}^{2}/2
, and whence that

{\omega_{R}}=\nabla r_{R}
.
We shall suppress the dependence of the parameter
R
(which
eventually is taken
as a large number, depending on
\lambda
). In particular we shall slightly abuse the
notation writing for example
r
rather than the rescaled
version
r_{R}
. Using the notation
D
for the Heisenberg
derivative
\mathrm{i}[H,\cdot]
we note the
computations
2B={\textbf{D}}r
,
A=r^{1/2}Br^{1/2}
and (formally)

\displaystyle{\textbf{D}}B=r^{-1/2}\big({{\mathbf{D}}}A-2B^{2}\big)r^{-1/2}+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-3}).

(101)

Here the function

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-3})=\tfrac{1}{2}\omega\cdot(\nabla^{2}r)\omega/r^{2}=r^{-3}v(x),

where
v
belongs to the algebra
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}({\mathbf{X}})
of smooth functions on
{\mathbf{X}}

obeying

\forall\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\dim{\mathbf{X}}}\quad\forall k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}:|\partial_{x}^{\alpha}(x\cdot\nabla)^{k}v(x)|\leq C_{\alpha,k}.

Note also that the function

r^{2}-x^{2}\in{\mathcal{F}}
. Obviously
{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbf{X}})\supset{\mathcal{F}}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})
for any
a\neq a_{\min}
. The exact computation of
{{\mathbf{D}}}A
reads

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{D}}}A

\displaystyle=2p\tilde{\omega}_{*}\big(\tfrac{x}{R}\big)p-\big(4R^{2}\big)^{-1}\big(\triangle(\nabla\cdot\tilde{\omega})\big)\big(\tfrac{x}{R}\big)-R\tilde{\omega}\big(\tfrac{x}{R}\big)\cdot\nabla V.

(102)

In particular

\displaystyle{{\mathbf{D}}}A=\mathrm{i}[H,A]=\sum_{|\alpha\leq 2}v_{\alpha}p^{\alpha};\quad v_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{F}},

which make sense as a bounded form on

H^{1}=Q(H)
. Although we define
{\mathbf{D}}A
by (
102
), it can be computed as a strong limit,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{i}[H,A]&=\slim_{t\to 0}t^{-1}\big(H{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tA}-{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tA}H\big)\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{1},H^{-1}).\end{split}

(103)

Similarly the formal
commutator in (
101
) can be computed as a strong
limit in
{\mathcal{L}}(H^{1},H^{-1})
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\textbf{D}}B&=\slim_{t\to 0}t^{-1}\big(H{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tB}-{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tB}H\big)\\
&=r^{-1/2}\big({{\mathbf{D}}}A-2B^{2}\big)r^{-1/2}+r^{-3}v\\
&=\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2}r^{-1}v_{\alpha}p^{\alpha};\quad v_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{F}}.\end{split}

(104)

The assertions (
103
) and (
104
)
are standard results, which follow from mapping properties of the
involved groups and the fact that the formal commutators are
bounded forms on
H^{1}
.

We will need modifications of
A
and
B
in terms of a
parameter
\kappa
. This parameter is needed to control certain multiple commutators. Let

\displaystyle B_{\kappa,R}=B(\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)^{-1},\quad A=A_{\kappa,R}=r^{1/2}B_{\kappa,R}r^{1/2};\quad\kappa\in[0,1].

(105)

We may consider
\widetilde{H}
defined in (
94a
) as a ‘generalized’

N
-body Schrödinger operator. The set of thresholds of
\widetilde{H}
, say denoted by
\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
, coincides with the set
{\mathcal{T}}
of thresholds of
H
except for having one less point. This exception follows from the
identity

\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}\big(\widetilde{H}^{a_{0}}\big)=\Big(\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}\big(H^{a_{0}}\big)\setminus\{\lambda_{0}\}\Big)\cup\{0\};\quad\widetilde{H}^{a_{0}}:=H^{a_{0}}-\lambda_{0}\Pi.

Note in particular that it follows that
\lambda_{0}\not\in\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
. On the other hand there is no simple relationship between the
eigenvalues of
\widetilde{H}
and those of
H
. A similar ‘subtraction’
of a genuine eigenprojection
P
of
H
was employed in
[
AHS
]
in
which case
indeed a
similar relation between
\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}\big(H-\lambda_{0}P\big)
and

\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}\big(H\big)
hold. Let in the following
\tilde{d}:{\mathbb{R}}\to{\mathbb{R}}
refer to the (lower) distance function defined by replacing

{\mathcal{T}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{T}}
in (
98
). Note that
\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})>0
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Suppose Conditions
1.5
and

2.4
. Let
A_{\kappa,R}
and
\breve{H}
be given by
(
105
) and (
94a
), respectively.

For all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
and

\epsilon>0
there exist

R_{0}\geq 1
and
\kappa_{0}\in(0,1]
such for all
R\geq R_{0}
, there exist a neighbourhood
{\mathcal{U}}
of

\lambda
and a compact operator
K
on
L^{2}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}f(\breve{H})&\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{\kappa,R}]f(\breve{H})\\
&\geq f(\breve{H})\{2\tilde{d}(\lambda)-\epsilon-K\}f(\breve{H})\text{ for all
}\kappa\in[0,\kappa_{0}]\text{ and real }f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathcal{U}}).\end{split}

(106)

The meaning of the appearing commutator will be explained in
Subsection
10.1
. However for
\kappa=0
there
is an alternative interpretation to be elaborated on now.
We claim that we can use (
106
) at
\lambda=\lambda_{0}
and for
\kappa=0
to
conclude, that there are at most a finite number of eigenvalues for

H^{\prime}
in a neighbourhood of
\lambda_{0}
. To see this it suffices to
show that the
commutator in (
106
), interpretated as the formal
commutator, can be computed as a strong limit

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{R}]&=\slim_{t\to 0}t^{-1}\big(\breve{H}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tA_{R}}-{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tA_{R}}\breve{H}\big)\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{2},H^{-2}).\end{split}

(107)

Writing
\breve{H}=H-T
,
T:=\lambda_{0}\Pi+\breve{K}
, the part of (
107
) related
to
H
is justified by (
103
). For the part related
to the second term it
suffices to show that the form, say a priori defined on

C_{{\mathrm{c}}}^{\infty}({\mathbf{X}})
, extends as follows.

Lemma 3.4
.

The form
\mathrm{i}{\operatorname{ad}}_{A_{R}}(T):=\mathrm{i}[T,A_{R}]
extends to a bounded form on
H^{2}
. More generally
\mathrm{i}{\operatorname{ad}}_{A_{R}}(T)\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{2},H^{-1})\cap{\mathcal{L}}(H^{1},H^{-2})
.

Proof.

Write the form as

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[\lambda_{0}\Pi+\Pi I_{0}+I_{0}\Pi-\Pi I_{0}\Pi,A_{R}]=T_{1}+\cdots+T_{4}.

We know that
[I_{0},A_{R}]\in{\mathcal{F}}\subset{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2})
. Let us only consider the contribution
[\Pi,A_{R}]I_{0}
(from
T_{2}
) and only show that
[\Pi,A_{R}]I_{0}\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{2},H^{-1}
). For that it suffices to show that
[\Pi,A_{R}]\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2},H^{-1}
).

Let
1=\chi_{1}+\chi_{2}
be a partition of unity on
{\mathbf{X}}
given as
follows. We demand that for
some (small)
\sigma>0
and all
\widecheck{R}\geq 1
:

1.

\chi_{1}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbf{X}})
and for all
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\dim{\mathbf{X}}}
there exists
C\in{\mathbb{R}}
:

|\langle{x}\rangle^{|\alpha|}\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\chi_{1}(x)|\leq C.

2.

\chi_{1}(x)=1
for

x\in\{|x|\geq 2\widecheck{R},\;|x^{a_{0}}|\leq\sigma|x|\}
and

\operatorname{supp}\chi_{1}\subset\{|x|>\widecheck{R},\;|x^{a_{0}}|<2\sigma|x|\}\subset{\mathbf{X}}\setminus\cup_{b\not\subset a_{0}}{\mathbf{X}}_{b}.

Note that
2
implies, referring here to
notation of (
96
) where
\delta=\delta(\sigma)>0
is taken
sufficiently small (independently of
\widecheck{R}
),

\operatorname{supp}\chi_{1}\subset{\mathbf{Y}}_{a_{0},\delta,\widecheck{R}}.

(108)

We write

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[\Pi,A_{R}]=(\chi_{1}+\chi_{2})\mathrm{i}[\Pi,A_{R}](\chi_{1}+\chi_{2})=\chi_{1}\mathrm{i}[\Pi,A_{R}]\chi_{1}+S.

(109)

Now for all sufficiently big values of
\widecheck{R}
(viz.
\widecheck{R}\geq R\widetilde{R}=R\widetilde{R}(\delta)
) we obtain by combining (
97
)
and (
108
) that

\tilde{\omega}_{R}(x)=x_{a_{0}}+\tilde{\omega}^{a_{0}}_{R}(x^{a_{0}})\text{ for all }x\in\operatorname{supp}\chi_{1}.

(110)

The above construction can be done in an
explicit way (including an explicit dependence of
parameter
R
): Let us here and henceforth fix
\widecheck{R}=R\widetilde{R}(\delta)
and choose

\chi_{1}(x)=\bar{\chi}_{\widecheck{R}}(|x|)\Theta(x/|x|)
with

\bar{\chi}_{\widecheck{R}}
specified in (
4.1
) and for a
suitable real-valued smooth function

\Theta
. We record the following improvement of
1
.

1.

For all
\alpha\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}^{\dim{\mathbf{X}}}
and
k\in{\mathbb{N}}

there exists
C\in{\mathbb{R}}

such that for all
R\geq 1
:

|\langle{x}\rangle^{|\alpha|}\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\chi_{1}(x)|+\big|\langle{x}\rangle^{|\alpha|}\partial^{\alpha}_{x}\Big(\tfrac{\langle{x}\rangle^{k}}{\langle{x^{a_{0}}}\rangle^{k}}\bar{\chi}_{2\widecheck{R}}({|x|})\chi_{2}(x)\Big)\big|\leq C.

Since the operator
A_{R}
is a local operator the first term in
(
109
) simplifies due to (
110
) as

\chi_{1}\mathrm{i}[\Pi,A_{R}]\chi_{1}=\chi_{1}\mathrm{i}[\Pi,A_{R}^{a_{0}}]\chi_{1}=\mathrm{i}\chi_{1}\big(\Pi A_{R}^{a_{0}}-A_{R}^{a_{0}}\Pi\big)\chi_{1}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}).

(111)

Now let us look at the term
S
in (
109
). It is given by

S=\chi_{2}\mathrm{i}[\Pi,A_{R}]+\chi_{1}\mathrm{i}[\Pi,A_{R}]\chi_{2}.

(112)

The two terms are treated similarly, so let us only
elaborate on the first term. We write

\displaystyle A_{R}=r_{R}B_{R}-\tfrac{\mathrm{i}}{2}\lvert(\nabla r)(x/R)\rvert^{2},

and then

\displaystyle\begin{split}\chi_{2}[\Pi,A_{R}]&=\chi_{2}\Pi A_{R}-\chi_{2}A_{R}\Pi\\
&=K_{1}B_{R}-B_{R}K_{2}-\mathrm{i}K_{3};\\
&K_{1}=\chi_{2}\Pi r_{R},\\
&K_{2}=r_{R}\chi_{2}\Pi,\\
&K_{3}=\tfrac{1}{2}\chi_{2}\Pi\,\lvert(\nabla r)(x/R)\rvert^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\chi_{2}\lvert(\nabla r)(x/R)\rvert^{2}\,\Pi+\big(\tilde{\omega}_{R}\cdot\nabla\chi_{2}\big)\Pi.\end{split}

(113)

Noting, cf.
1
, that

\displaystyle K_{1},K_{2}\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{-1})\cap{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}),\,K_{3}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2})\text{ and }B_{R}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2},H^{-1}),

(114)

we are done.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

It follows from the technique of the proof that the second order
commutator
{\operatorname{ad}}^{2}_{A_{R}}(T)\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{2},H^{-2})
(and possibly no better for singular potentials). This
suffices for the limiting absorption principle at
\lambda_{0}
,
cf.
[
PSS
]
. However higher commutators do not exist and we need
refined micro-local estimates, which usually require multiple
commutators. (In particular we need bounds with weights in position
space.) Using the

\kappa
-distortions of
A_{R}
and
B_{R}
will allow us to treat multiple
commutators. Note for example that for
\kappa>0

the above proof yields, at least formally, the improved result

{\operatorname{ad}}_{A_{\kappa,R}}(T)\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{1},H^{-1})
. We introduce a calculus of the

\kappa
-distortion of
B
which will be a
major object to study. This is done in Section

11
, and we give a number of applications of
Proposition
3.3
and this calculus in Section
12
.

10.1
Proof of Proposition
3.3

Introduce for
\varepsilon>0
the operators

\displaystyle X_{\varepsilon}=X_{\varepsilon,R}=r/(1+\varepsilon r),\quad A_{\varepsilon,\kappa,R}=X^{1/2}_{\varepsilon}B_{\kappa,R}X_{\varepsilon}^{1/2};\quad\kappa\in[0,1].

(115)

Note that

A_{\varepsilon,\kappa,R}\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{2},H^{1})
. Whence
\mathrm{i}[H,A_{\varepsilon,\kappa,R}]
is a well-defined
form on
H^{2}
. We

define
the commutators
\mathrm{i}[H,A_{\kappa,R}]
,
\mathrm{i}[\widetilde{H},A_{\kappa,R}]
and
\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{\kappa,R}]
as strong weak-star limits in
{\mathcal{L}}(H^{2},H^{-2})
in
the following way. For technical reasons to be explained after the
definitions, this works for small enough values of
\kappa
only. Let for
each of the operators
H^{\#}=H,\,\widetilde{H}
or
\breve{H}

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[H^{\#},A_{\kappa,R}]=\swslim_{\varepsilon\to 0_{+}}\mathrm{i}[H^{\#},A_{\varepsilon,\kappa,R}].

(116)

It is easy to see that for
\kappa=0
, these limiting
forms exist and coincide with our previous computations, cf.
(
107
) and Lemma
3.4
. The case of
\kappa>0

requires an elaboration to be given in Lemma
3.6
.

An immediate virtue of
(
116
) is the validity of the virial theorem. Thus for
example,
(
116
) combined with Proposition
3.3
yields
that eigenvalues of
\breve{H}
cannot accumulate at
\lambda_{0}
. Note
that this assertion does not require
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
for which
case in
fact
\lambda_{0}\notin\sigma_{\mathrm{ess}}(\breve{H})
, cf.
[
Wa2
, Lemma
2.1]
or the proof of Lemma
2.10
. Proposition

3.3
will be a crucial tool for us only for

\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
. At the level of proofs, the
reader will see similarities of the proofs of Lemma

2.10
and Proposition

3.3
.

We are
going to use that for
\kappa>0

\displaystyle B_{\kappa,R}=\tfrac{1}{2\kappa}\big((\kappa B+\mathrm{i})^{-1}+(\kappa B-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\big).

(117)

Note that it follows from Mourre theory,
[
Mo
]
, that
(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1}

preserve
H^{2}
provided
\kappa
is small enough. We need a
uniform bound in
\kappa
and
R
. Thus, more precisely, we claim that
there exists
\kappa^{\prime}_{0}\in(0,1]
such that for all

\kappa\in[0,\kappa^{\prime}_{0}]
and all
R\geq 1
the space
H^{2}
is preserved,
and in fact

\displaystyle\sup_{R\geq 1,\,\kappa\leq\kappa^{\prime}_{0}}\,\lVert(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1}\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}(H^{2})}<\infty.

(118a)

We may take this constant as
\kappa_{0}^{\prime}=\min\{(2C)^{-1},1\}
, where

\displaystyle C=\sup_{R\geq 1}\,\lVert[H_{0},B](H_{0}+1)^{-1}\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2})}<\infty.

(118b)

Note that the
R
-dependence of
B
is through
\omega_{R}(x)=(\nabla r)(x/R)
, which is bounded along
with all derivatives. Whence the above finiteness claims
hold. We also note that for any
R\geq 1

\displaystyle\slim_{\kappa\to 0}(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1}=\mp\mathrm{i}\text{ in }{\mathcal{L}}(H^{2}).

(118c)

Clearly there are similar properties as (
118a
) and
(
118c
) with
H^{2}
replaced by
L^{2}_{s}
for any

s\geq 0
. For (
118a
) in that case,
\kappa^{\prime}_{0}
can be
an arbitrary positive number (it does
not need to be small); see
the beginning of the proof of Lemma
3.9
.

Lemma 3.6
.

The limits
\mathrm{i}[H^{\#},A_{\kappa,R}]
, with
H^{\#}=H,\,\widetilde{H}
or
\breve{H}
, are
well-defined bounded form on
H^{2}
for
\kappa\in[0,\kappa^{\prime}_{0}]
. More generally
\mathrm{i}[H^{\#},A_{\kappa,R}]\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{2},H^{-1})\cap{\mathcal{L}}(H^{1},H^{-2})
.

Moreover for each of the above three forms

\displaystyle\exists C>0\,\forall R\geq 1\,\forall\kappa\in[0,\kappa^{\prime}_{0}]:\quad\big\lVert\mathrm{i}[H^{\#},A_{\kappa,R}]-\mathrm{i}[H^{\#},A_{R}]\big\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}(H^{2},H^{-2})}\leq C\kappa.

(119)

Proof.

We need to examine the case
\kappa>0
. We start by examining the quantity
\mathrm{i}[H,A_{\kappa,R}]
.

We calculate using (
104
), (
117
) and (
118a
)

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\Big[H,A_{\varepsilon,\kappa,R}\Big]

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}X_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\Big[H,B_{\kappa,R}\Big]X_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}+2\operatorname{Re}\Big(\mathrm{i}\Big[H,X_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\Big]B_{\kappa,R}X_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\Big)

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{\pm}X_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1}\mathrm{i}\Big[H,B\Big](\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1}X_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}+2\operatorname{Re}\Big(\operatorname{Re}{\big(X_{\varepsilon}^{-1/2}(\nabla X_{\varepsilon})\cdot p\big)}B_{\kappa,R}X_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\Big)

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2,\,\pm}X_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1})r^{-1}v_{\alpha}p^{\alpha}(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1}X_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}

\displaystyle+2\operatorname{Re}\Big(r^{-1/4}(1+\varepsilon r)^{-3/4}B_{R}(1+\varepsilon r)^{-3/4}r^{-1/4}B_{\kappa,R}X_{\varepsilon}^{1/2}\Big);\quad v_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{F}}.

We can let
\varepsilon\to 0
, yielding the
existence of the desired limit in the
H^{2}
-form sense. The result is

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\Big[H,A_{\kappa,R}\Big]

\displaystyle=-\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{|\alpha|\leq 2,\,\pm}r^{1/2}(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1})r^{-1}v_{\alpha}p^{\alpha}(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1}r^{1/2}

\displaystyle+2\operatorname{Re}\Big(r^{-1/4}B_{R}r^{-1/4}B_{\kappa,R}r^{1/2}\Big);\quad v_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{F}}.

This expression is obviously in
{\mathcal{L}}(H^{2},H^{-1})\cap{\mathcal{L}}(H^{1},H^{-2})
(in fact in
{\mathcal{L}}(H^{1},H^{-1})
in this case).

Next we move multiplication operators to the middle as to
become sandwiched by
(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1}
. Thereby we pick up errors
of order
{\mathcal{O}}(\kappa)
in
{\mathcal{L}}(H^{2},H^{-2})
uniformly in
R\geq 1
,
cf. (
118a
) and the remark after (
118c
). This
means that we only have to consider

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\sum_{\pm}(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1}\mathrm{i}\big[H,A\big](\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1},

(120)

where
\mathrm{i}\big[H,A\big]
is given by (
102
).
But

\displaystyle(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1}\pm\mathrm{i}=\pm\mathrm{i}\kappa(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1}B,

(121)

which give an extra error
{\mathcal{O}}(\kappa)\text{ in
}{\mathcal{L}}(H^{2},H^{-2})
when removing the factors
(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1}
in (
120
) one by one. This proves (
119
) in the case
H^{\#}=H
.

For the commutators
\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{\kappa,R}]
and
\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{\kappa,R}]
we proceed similarly and by using in addition the proof of Lemma

3.4
. Note that in the case of
H^{\#}=\breve{H}
the
topology in (
119
) is the strongest Sobolev space
topology we can use for singular
potentials by this method, cf. Remark
3.5
.
∎

Lemma 3.7
.

For all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\epsilon>0
there
exist
R_{0}\geq 1
such for all
R\geq R_{0}
,
there exist a neighbourhood
{\mathcal{V}}
of
\lambda
and a compact
operator
\widetilde{K}
on
L^{2}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}f(\widetilde{H})&\mathrm{i}[\widetilde{H},A_{R}]f(\widetilde{H})\\
&\geq f(\widetilde{H})\{2\tilde{d}(\lambda)-\epsilon-\widetilde{K}\}f(\widetilde{H})\text{ for all
real }f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({{\mathcal{V}}}).\end{split}

(122)

Proof.

We divide the proof into several steps.

I
.  We note that for any
product

P_{1}\cdots P_{m}

of factors
P_{l}
given either as
P_{l}=\langle x^{a_{0}}\rangle
or
P_{l}=A_{R}^{a_{0}}
we have

P_{1}\cdots P_{m}\Pi\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a_{0}})\big).

(123)

In fact since
\langle x^{a_{0}}\rangle^{m}(H^{a_{0}}+\mathrm{i})^{m}\Pi\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a_{0}})\big)
the result follows from the property

P_{1}\cdots P_{m}(H^{a_{0}}+\mathrm{i})^{-m}\langle x^{a_{0}}\rangle^{-m}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a_{0}})\big),

which in turn follows from repeated commutation and
the property
A_{R}^{a_{0}}\langle x^{a_{0}}\rangle^{-1}(H^{a_{0}}+\mathrm{i})^{-1}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a_{0}})\big)
.

II
.
There is a complete analogue of
Lemma
3.2
with the triple
\big(H,\mathrm{i}[H,A_{R}],d\big)
replaced
by
\big(\widetilde{H},\mathrm{i}[H,A_{R}],\tilde{d}\big)
. It reads conveniently as follows.

For all
\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\epsilon>0
there
exists
R_{0}\geq 1
such for all
R\geq R_{0}
,
there exist a bounded neighbourhood
{\mathcal{V}}
of
\lambda
and a compact
operator
\widetilde{K}_{1}
on
L^{2}
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}f(\widetilde{H})&\mathrm{i}[{H},A_{R}]f(\widetilde{H})\\
&\geq f(\widetilde{{H}})\{2\tilde{d}(\lambda)-\epsilon/2-\widetilde{K}_{1}\}f(\widetilde{H})\text{ for all
real }f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathcal{V}}).\end{split}

(124)

This statement follows by mimicking the proofs of
[
Sk3
, Corollaries
4.2 and 4.5]
. One comment is due since the ‘potential’
\Pi

is not local: Expanding the operator
T(\delta^{\prime})
appearing in

[
Sk3
, (4.16)]
one ‘new term’ is given by
T:=[\Pi,j_{b}]
; here

j_{b}
is a partition function with similar properties as the ones in (
67a
)–(
67c
). We need
to show that
T
is
\widetilde{H}
-compact. If
a_{0}\not\subset b
indeed

\Pi j_{b}
and
j_{b}\Pi
, and hence also
T
, are
H
-compact, cf. Step
I
. If on the other
hand
a_{0}\subset b
we have
a_{0}=b
and we can write
T=-\sum_{d\neq b}[\Pi,j_{d}]
. Since for any such term we have
a_{0}\not\subset d

the previous argument yields that
[\Pi,j_{d}]
is
H
-compact.

III
.  Recalling the definition (
94a
) it remains to
show the following
estimate in terms of the set
{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{V}}(R)
from (
124
).

There exists a compact operator
\widetilde{K}_{2}=\widetilde{K}_{2}(R)

such that

1_{{\mathcal{V}}}(\widetilde{H})\mathrm{i}[-\lambda_{0}\Pi,A_{R}]1_{{\mathcal{V}}}(\widetilde{H})\geq-\epsilon/2-\widetilde{K}_{2}.

(125)

Clearly the combination of (
124
) and (
125
) yields
(
122
) with
this neighbourhood
{\mathcal{V}}
and with

\widetilde{K}=\widetilde{K}_{1}+\widetilde{K}_{2}
.
To show (
125
) we decompose as in the proof of Lemma

3.4
writing

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\Big[\Pi,A\Big]

\displaystyle=(\chi_{1}+\chi_{2})\mathrm{i}\Big[\Pi,A\Big](\chi_{1}+\chi_{2})=\chi_{1}\mathrm{i}\Big[\Pi,A_{R}^{a_{0}}\Big]\chi_{1}+S.

Since

\displaystyle\lVert\Pi A_{R}^{a_{0}}-A_{R}^{a_{0}}\Pi\rVert\to 0\text{ for }R\to\infty,

cf. Lemma
3.1
2
and Step

I
, the first
term conforms with (
125
).

It remains to consider
S
, which is given by (
112
). We
decompose as in (
113
) (treating again only the first term of

S
). The operators
K_{1}
,
K_{2}
and
K_{3}
are not only bounded (as
stated in (
114
)) but also

\langle p\rangle
-compact. Since
{\mathcal{V}}
is bounded (
125
) follows.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

Let

\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\epsilon>0
be given. We need to specify
R_{0}\geq 1
,
\kappa_{0}\in(0,\kappa_{0}^{\prime}]
and for
R\geq R_{0}
a neighbourhood
{\mathcal{U}}(R)
of
\lambda

and a compact
K(R)
such that (
106
) holds.

I
.
First we use
Lemma
3.7
to obtain the statement
(
122
) with the factors of
f(\widetilde{H})
replaced by the factors of
f(\breve{H}
). More precisely we use the statement with
\epsilon
replaced by

\epsilon/2
allowing us to pick corresponding
R_{0}\geq 1
and for
R\geq R_{0}
a neighbourhood
{\mathcal{V}}(R)
of
\lambda

and a compact
\widetilde{K}(R)
such that (
122
)
holds. Now take for any such set
{\mathcal{V}}(R)
any smaller
neighbourhood of
\lambda
, denoted by
{\mathcal{U}}(R)
, with compact
closure contained in the interior of

{\mathcal{V}}(R)
. We can pick a real function
\tilde{f}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathcal{V}}(R))
such that
\tilde{f}=1
on
{\mathcal{U}}(R)

and then estimate as follows. Note that
\breve{K}_{0}=\breve{K}_{0}(R):=\big(H_{0}+1\big)\big(\tilde{f}(\breve{H})-\tilde{f}(\widetilde{H})\big)
is compact
(cf. (
130
) given below) and that
\mathrm{i}[\widetilde{H},A_{R}]\in{{\mathcal{L}}(H^{2},H^{-2})}
(cf. Lemma
3.4
).
Writing then

\displaystyle\breve{K}_{1}:=

\displaystyle\tilde{f}(\breve{H})\mathrm{i}[\widetilde{H},A_{R}]\tilde{f}(\breve{H})-\tilde{f}(\widetilde{H})\mathrm{i}[\widetilde{H},A_{R}]\tilde{f}(\widetilde{H})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\breve{K}_{0}^{*}\breve{T}+\widetilde{T}^{*}\breve{K}_{0};

\displaystyle\quad\breve{T}=\big(H_{0}+1\big)^{-1}\mathrm{i}[\widetilde{H},A_{R}]\tilde{f}(\breve{H}),

\displaystyle\quad\widetilde{T}=\big(H_{0}+1\big)^{-1}\mathrm{i}[\widetilde{H},A_{R}]\tilde{f}(\widetilde{H}),

we conclude that
\breve{K}_{1}
is
compact. Note also that

\displaystyle\breve{K}_{2}(R):=\big(2\tilde{d}(\lambda)-\epsilon/2\big)\big(\tilde{f}(\breve{H})^{2}-\tilde{f}(\widetilde{H})^{2}\big)

is compact.

In conclusion we take
R_{0}\geq 1
,
{\mathcal{U}}(R)
as described above and

\displaystyle\breve{K}_{3}(R):=\tilde{f}(\widetilde{H})\widetilde{K}\tilde{f}(\widetilde{H})-\breve{K}_{1}+\breve{K}_{2};\quad R\geq R_{0},

yielding

\displaystyle f(\breve{H})

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[\widetilde{H},A_{R}]f(\breve{H})

\displaystyle\geq f(\breve{H})\{2\tilde{d}(\lambda)-\epsilon/2-\breve{K_{3}}\}f(\breve{H})\text{ for all
real }f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({{\mathcal{U}}(R)}).

II
.
We note that

\displaystyle\breve{K}_{4}(R):=1_{U(R)}(\breve{H})\mathrm{i}[\breve{K},A_{R}]1_{U(R)}(\breve{H})\text{ is compact}.

We subtract these terms in the previous estimate,
yielding

\displaystyle f(\breve{H})

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{R}]f(\breve{H})

\displaystyle\geq f(\breve{H})\{2\tilde{d}(\lambda)-\epsilon/2-{K}\}f(\breve{H})\text{ for all
real }f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({{\mathcal{U}}(R)}),

where
K=K(R)=\breve{K}_{3}+\breve{K}_{4}
.

III
.
We invoke (
119
), estimating

\displaystyle 1_{U(R)}(\breve{H})\big(\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{\kappa,R}]-\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{R}]\big)1_{U(R)}(\breve{H})\geq-\epsilon/2,

for all small enough
\kappa
. In combination with the
previous estimate this estimate yields (
106
).
∎

11
Multiple commutators and calculus

It is convenient to introduce a concept of ‘order’ for certain classes
of linear operators
T
on
L^{2}
, cf.
[
GIS
]
. It is based on the
following elementary result, cf. Lemma
3.9
.

\displaystyle\forall\kappa\in(0,1],\;\forall R\geq 1:\quad{B_{\kappa,R}}\,L^{2}_{\infty}\subset L^{2}_{\infty}.

(126)

For operators
S,T
on
L^{2}
we define (formally) multiple commutators by

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ad}}^{0}_{S}(T)=T\text{ and }{\operatorname{ad}}^{k}_{S}(T)=[{\operatorname{ad}}^{k-1}_{S}(T),S]\text{ for }k\in{\mathbb{N}}.

Let
X
be multiplication by
r=r_{R}
on
L^{2}
, and
recall that
\kappa_{0}^{\prime}\in(0,1]
is introduced in the discussion of
(
118a
) and (
118b
).

Definition 3.8
.

Let
R\geq 1
be given.
An operator
T
on
L^{2}

is
of
\kappa
-order
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
if

1.

{\mathcal{D}}(T),{\mathcal{D}}(T^{*})\supset L^{2}_{\infty}
,

2.

T
and
T^{*}
leave
L^{2}_{\infty}
invariant,

3.

\forall\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0}^{\prime}]\;\forall s\in{\mathbb{R}}\,\;\forall k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}:\quad X^{s-k-t}{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa,R}}^{k}(T)X^{-s}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}).

For any operator
T
of
\kappa
-order
t
the adjoint

T^{*}
also has
\kappa
-order
t
, and we write
T,T^{*}={\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t})
. The class of such operators is denoted by

{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t})
. It is readily seen that

X^{t}={\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t})
, cf. (
132
) and (
133
)
stated below.
This is related to the fact that
{\operatorname{ad}}_{B}^{k}(X^{k})\in{\mathcal{F}}
for

k\in{\mathbb{N}}
, where the algebra
{\mathcal{F}}
is introduced in the discussion of (
101
). By the Leipniz rule, if
S
and
T
are of
\kappa
-order
s
and

t
, respectively, then
ST
is of order

s+t
. We do not keep
track of neither the
\kappa
- nor the

R
-dependence, however it is important for our applications of the
above
concept of ‘order’ that
\kappa=0

is
not included
in
3
. We abbreviate

\breve{R}(z)=(\breve{H}-z)^{-1}
and

R_{\kappa}(z)=(B_{\kappa}-z)^{-1}=(B_{\kappa,R}-z)^{-1}
.

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
R\geq 1
be given big enough. Then for any
f\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
,
g\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
,
v\in{\mathcal{F}}
,
|\alpha|\leq 2
,
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
and polynomials
P_{1},P_{2}
in

A^{a_{0}}
and the components of
x^{a_{0}}
and
p^{a_{0}}
, such that the total
number of components of
p^{a_{0}}
is at most two for
P_{1}
as well as
for
P_{2}
, the operators

\displaystyle g(B_{\kappa}),\,v,\,P_{1}\Pi P_{2},\,I_{0}(p^{2}+1)^{-1},\,p^{\alpha}\breve{R}(z),\,I_{0}\Pi\breve{R}(z),\,f(\breve{H})\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0}).

(127)

Proof.

We prove the bounds one by one. For simplicity of presentation the
above list is not stated as complete as possible. In the proof we will see that a
certain algebra
\breve{\mathcal{B}}\subset{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0})
; this
algebra contains a few
operators not listed above for which the assertion that
they are contained in
{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0})
will also be useful
later.

By repeated commutation we obtain for the
regularization
X_{\varepsilon}
of (
115
) that for any
s\geq 0
,
\varepsilon>0

and
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle\begin{split}X_{\varepsilon}^{s}(B-z)^{-1}=\sum^{[s]}_{k=0}&(-1)^{k}(B-z)^{-k-1}{\operatorname{ad}}_{B}^{k}(X_{\varepsilon}^{s})\\
&+(-1)^{[s]+1}(B-z)^{-[s]-1}{\operatorname{ad}}_{B}^{[s]+1}(X_{\varepsilon}^{s})(B-z)^{-1}.\end{split}

(128)

We multiply by
X^{-s}
from the right and take
\varepsilon\to 0
observing that the
resulting right-hand side is explicitly
in
{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2})
. Note that the operator

{\operatorname{ad}}_{B}^{[s]+1}(X^{s})\in{\mathcal{F}}
, and therefore it is a bounded
multiplication operator. We can
argue similarly for
s<0
. In particular we conclude
(
126
) (using (
117
)) as well as a boundedness result remarked before
Lemma
3.6
. In fact it follows that

\displaystyle(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0}).

(129)

g(B_{\kappa})
; 1st proof
.
In terms of an almost analytic
extension
\tilde{g}
of a given
g\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
(‘absorbed’ in a measure
\mu=\mu_{g}
and writing
z=u+\mathrm{i}v
)

\displaystyle g(B)={\tfrac{1}{\pi}}\int_{{{\mathbb{C}}}}{\left(\bar{\partial}\tilde{g}\right)}{\left(z\right)}{\left(B-z\right)}^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}u{\mathrm{d}}v=\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}(B-z)^{-1}{\mathrm{d}}\mu(z).

(130)

Let for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{G}}_{t}=\{g\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})|\,\forall k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}:\,\lvert g^{(k)}(x)\rvert\leq C_{k}\langle x\rangle^{t-k}\}.

(131)

It is well-known that (
130
) is valid in fact for
g\in{\mathcal{G}}_{t}
with
t<0
(for any self-adjoint operator
B
).

Now for any given
g\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
we write
g(B_{\kappa})=g_{\kappa}(B)+g(0)
with
g_{\kappa}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{-1}
.
By using (
130
)
for
g_{\kappa}

we conclude by using (
128
) with
\varepsilon=0

(and its adjoint version)
that

g(B_{\kappa})=g_{\kappa}(B)+g(0)\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0})
.

g(B_{\kappa})
; 2nd proof
.  Since
B_{\kappa,R}
is
bounded we can write

g(B_{\kappa,R})=f(B_{\kappa,R})
for some
f\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
and then apply (
130
). For

s\geq 0
(treating only this case in
3
)
we expand as in (
128
)

\displaystyle X^{s}R_{\kappa}(z)=\sum^{[s]}_{k=0}(-1)^{k}R_{\kappa}(z)^{k+1}{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}^{k}(X^{s})+(-1)^{[s]+1}R_{\kappa}(z)^{[s]+1}{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}^{[s]+1}(X^{s})R_{\kappa}(z).

It suffices to show that

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}^{k}(X^{s})X^{k-s},k=0,\dots,[s],\text{ and }{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}^{[s]+1}(X^{s})\text{ are bounded.}

(132)

Using (
117
) and (
129
) we compute for
k=1,\dots,[s]+1

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}^{k}(X^{s})&=\sum_{\sigma=(\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{k})\in\{-1,1\}^{k}}T_{\sigma}v_{\sigma}X^{s-k}T_{\sigma};\\
T_{\sigma}&=\prod^{k}_{j=1}(\kappa B+\sigma_{j}\mathrm{i})^{-1},\quad v_{\sigma}\in{\mathcal{F}}.\end{split}

(133)

Clearly this shows that
{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}^{[s]+1}(X^{s})
is
bounded.
For
k\leq[s]
we obtain the
first part of (
132
) by using that
X^{s-k}T_{\sigma}X^{k-s}
is
bounded, cf. (
129
). Consequently we are done.

v
.  We compute similarly (using the same notation)

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}^{k}(v)=\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}^{k}}T_{\sigma}v_{\sigma}X^{-k}T_{\sigma}.

We conclude
that
v\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0})
using again (
129
).

P_{1}\Pi P_{2}
.  We let
T
be of the form
T=P_{1}\Pi P_{2}
and compute

\displaystyle{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}(T)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\sigma\in\{-1,1\}}(\kappa B+\sigma\mathrm{i})^{-1}[T,B](\kappa B+\sigma\mathrm{i})^{-1},

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[T,B]

\displaystyle=r^{-1/2}\mathrm{i}[T,A]r^{-1/2}+2\operatorname{Re}\Big(\mathrm{i}\Big[T,r^{-1/2}\Big]Ar^{-1/2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}r^{-1/2}\chi_{1}\big(TA^{a_{0}}-A^{a_{0}}T\big)\chi_{1}r^{-1/2}+T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3};

\displaystyle T_{1}=r^{-1/2}\chi_{2}\mathrm{i}[T,r^{1/2}Br^{1/2}]r^{-1/2},

\displaystyle T_{2}=r^{-1/2}\chi_{1}\mathrm{i}[T,r^{1/2}Br^{1/2}]\chi_{2}r^{-1/2},

\displaystyle T_{3}=2\operatorname{Re}\Big(\mathrm{i}\Big[T,r^{-1/2}\Big]r^{1/2}B\Big).

We need to compute higher order commutators as well, so we need to
iterate this computation. We will demonstrate a self-similar structure
which will allow us to control higher order commutators. To this end
we introduce the graded
algebra
{\mathcal{B}}=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
, where
{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
is
given by linear combinations of any products of

\displaystyle X^{-s}\,\,(s\in{\mathbb{R}}),v\in{\mathcal{F}},(\kappa B\pm\mathrm{i})^{-1},T,P_{\sigma}^{\alpha},(P_{\sigma}^{\alpha})^{*};\quad P^{\alpha}_{\sigma}:=p^{\alpha}T_{\sigma}(p^{2}+1)^{-1}\,\,(|\alpha|\leq 2).

Here
T
is any operator of the form
T=P_{1}\Pi P_{2}
and
T_{\sigma}

is given by the last equation of (
133
) for
k\in{\mathbb{N}}
while
T_{\sigma}:=I

for
k=0
, and for any such product the total sum of appearing
exponents
s\in{\mathbb{R}}

is given by
k
. We claim that

\displaystyle\forall S\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k}:\quad{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}(S)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k+1}.

(134)

From this property it follows that

{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}^{k}(S)\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
for any
S\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
(in particular for
T
), and we readily see that the
powers of
X
can be redistributed as we want, i.e. we obtain that

{\mathcal{B}}\subset{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0})
, in particular
T\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0})
follows.

To show (
134
) it suffices to consider
k=0
and then in
turn only the operator
T=P_{1}\Pi P_{2}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
. So
we consider the above computation of the commutator with
B_{\kappa}
.
The first term is clearly in
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
since

TA^{a_{0}}
and
A^{a_{0}}T
have the same form as
T
and the two outer
factors of
r^{-1/2}
together provides us with the extra factor

X^{-1}
required for the class
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
.

For the term

T_{1}
and
T_{2}
we also ‘undo’ the commutation. Whence

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}T_{1}=r^{-1/2}\chi_{2}Tr^{1/2}B-B\chi_{2}r^{1/2}Tr^{-1/2}+[B,\chi_{2}]r^{1/2}Tr^{-1/2}.

We use (
121
) to combine the factors of
B
with the factors
of
(\kappa B+\sigma\mathrm{i})^{-1}
. The remaining terms can for any

s\in{\mathbb{R}}
be written as

X^{-s}v_{1}\widecheck{T}v_{2}X^{-s}
, where
v_{1},v_{2}\in{\mathcal{F}}
and

\widecheck{T}
has the same form as
T
(cf.
1
in the
proof of Lemma
3.4
). In particular we get the extra
factor
X^{-1}
by choosing
s=1/2
. We can argue similarly for
T_{2}
.

For the term

T_{3}
we note the following general formula. Let
g\in{\mathcal{G}}_{t}
for
any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
consider the commutation formula for the composition
g(r)
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{i}\big[{\Pi,g(r)}\big]&=\Pi h(x)-h(x)\Pi,\\
h(x)&=\mathrm{i}\int_{0}^{1}\,{\mathrm{d}}t\,g^{\prime}(r(tx^{a}+x_{a}))\int_{0}^{t}x^{a}\cdot(\nabla^{2}r)\big((sx^{a}+x_{a})\big)x^{a}\,{\mathrm{d}}s.\end{split}

(135)

Here we used two zero’th order Taylor expansions, the
fact that
[{\Pi,g(r(x_{a}))}]=0
and the property
\nabla r\big(x_{a}\big)\cdot x^{a}=0
, cf. Lemma
3.1
2
.

Applied to
g(r)=r^{-1/2}
we conclude that

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\big[{T,r^{-1/2}}\big]=Th(x)-h(x)T,

(136)

with
h
given by (
135
). We can write

h=\langle x^{a_{0}}\rangle^{7/2}r^{-3/2}v
, where
v\in{\mathcal{F}}
.

This representation can
be refined using the formula

\displaystyle r\nabla^{2}r=\nabla^{2}r^{2}/2-\lvert\nabla r\rangle\langle\nabla r\rvert\in{\mathcal{F}}.

(137)

In fact
it follows from (
135
) and (
137
) that
(
136
) holds with

\displaystyle h=\langle x^{a_{0}}\rangle^{9/2}r^{-5/2}v,\text{ where }v\in{\mathcal{F}}.

(138)

Thus intuitively the commutator is two inverse powers of
X
better.
Obviously this
is a general property for commutation by
g(r)
for
g\in{\mathcal{G}}_{t}
, and
this comes in handy
later, see the proof
of Lemma
3.10
.
In any case by the appearance of at least one extra power of
X^{-1}
and
(
121
) we see that also
T_{3}
contributes by a term in
{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
.

For an application in the next step of the proof let us note that

\displaystyle S_{1}:=(p^{2}+1)\Pi(p^{2}+1)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}\text{ and }S_{2}:=(p^{2}+1)[\Pi,B_{\kappa}](p^{2}+1)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}.

(139)

Obviously
S_{1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
, and therefore the second assertion follows from
(
134
) and

\displaystyle S_{2}=[S_{1},B_{\kappa}]-[p^{2},B_{\kappa}](p^{2}+1)^{-1}S_{1}+S_{1}[p^{2},B_{\kappa}](p^{2}+1)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}.

I_{0}(p^{2}+1)^{-1}
and
p^{\alpha}T_{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{j}}\breve{R}(z),\,p^{\alpha}T_{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{j}}\Pi\breve{R}(z);\,|\alpha|\leq 2,\,j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
.  We add these operators as well
as the adjoint expressions to the list of generators of algebra
{\mathcal{B}}
and denote
the corresponding algebra by
\breve{\mathcal{B}}=\sum_{k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
, where as
before the total sum of appearing
exponents
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
in any term of an element of
\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{k}

is given by
k
. We claim that

\displaystyle\forall S\in\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{k}:\quad{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}(S)\in\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{k+1}.

(140)

From this property it follows that

{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}^{k}(S)\in\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
for any
S\in\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
(in particular for the enlisted operators and their adjoint expressions), and again we can redistribute the
powers of
X
as we want. Whence we obtain that

\breve{\mathcal{B}}\subset{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0})
, in particular the fourth,
fifth and sixth operators
listed in (
127
) are all in

{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0})
as claimed.
In conclusion it suffices to show
(
140
), and therefore in turn (
140
) with
k=0
.
As for the first term we note that
[I_{0},B]\in X^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}
. Whence it suffices to consider the other
terms.
For the second term
S=p^{\alpha}T_{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{j}}\breve{R}(z)=p^{\alpha}T_{\sigma}\breve{R}(z)
we compute

\displaystyle[S,B_{\kappa}]

\displaystyle=[p^{\alpha},B_{\kappa}]T_{\sigma}\breve{R}(z)-p^{\alpha}T_{\sigma}\breve{R}(z)[\breve{H},B_{\kappa}]\breve{R}(z)

\displaystyle=T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}+T_{4};

\displaystyle T_{1}

\displaystyle=-\sum_{\sigma^{\prime}\in\{-1,1\}}(\kappa B+\sigma^{\prime}\mathrm{i})^{-1}[p^{\alpha},B]T_{\sigma_{1},\dots,\sigma_{j},\sigma^{\prime}}\breve{R}(z),

\displaystyle T_{2}

\displaystyle=-p^{\alpha}T_{\sigma}\breve{R}(z)[H,B_{\kappa}]\breve{R}(z),

\displaystyle T_{3}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{0}p^{\alpha}T_{\sigma}\breve{R}(z)[\Pi,B_{\kappa}]\breve{R}(z),

\displaystyle T_{4}

\displaystyle=p^{\alpha}T_{\sigma}\breve{R}(z)[\breve{K},B_{\kappa}]\breve{R}(z).

Since
[p^{\alpha},B]\in X^{-1}\sum_{|\beta|\leq 2}v_{\beta}p^{\beta}

where
v_{\beta}\in{\mathcal{F}}
, the operator
T_{1}\in\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
. Thanks to
(
104
) we conclude that also
T_{2}\in\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
. Since

\Pi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
and therefore
[\Pi,B_{\kappa}]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
(due to
(
134
)) also
T_{3}\in\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
. For
T_{4}
we
combine the facts that

\displaystyle I_{0}(p^{2}+1)^{-1},(p^{2}+1)\breve{R}(z)\in\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{0}

and (
139
). Thus, for
example (with
S_{2}
given in (
139
))

\displaystyle[I_{0}\Pi,B_{\kappa}]\breve{R}(z)-[I_{0},B_{\kappa}]\Pi\breve{R}(z)=\big(I_{0}(p^{2}+1)^{-1}\big)S_{2}\big((p^{2}+1)\breve{R}(z)\big)\in\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{1}.

Clearly the second term to the left is in
\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
too,
completing our treatment of one of the three terms of
\breve{K}
. This
argument also works for the other terms of
\breve{K}
.

Finally for the third term
S=p^{\alpha}T_{\sigma}\Pi\breve{R}(z)
we write

\displaystyle S=P_{\sigma}^{\alpha}S_{1}\big((p^{2}+1)\breve{R}(z)\big).

Since
P_{\sigma}^{\alpha},S_{1}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}
, cf.
(
139
),
[P^{\alpha}_{\sigma}S_{1},B_{\kappa}]\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1}\subset\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
, and since
[(p^{2}+1)\breve{R}(z),B_{\kappa}]\in\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
(as proved
above), it follows that
indeed
[S,B_{\kappa}]\in\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{1}
as we want.

f(\breve{H})
.  We use (
130
) with
B
replaced
by
\breve{H}
. We have proven that

R(z)={\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0})
for any fixed non-real
z
. Whence we can compute any number of commutators with

B_{\kappa}
, redistribute powers of
X
as we want, and then estimate
to see that indeed
f(\breve{H})\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0})
. Note that
the bounds
\lVert\breve{R}(z)^{m}\rVert\leq|\operatorname{Im}z|^{-m}
come in naturally estimating
integrals like (
130
) and in fact appear
‘harmless’ in combination with the appearing
measure
\mu
.
∎

11.1
Computing a commutator

We are interested in computing commutators
\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},P]
where

P
in all relevant cases has the form

\displaystyle P=f(\breve{H})h(r)g(B_{\kappa})h(r)f(\breve{H});\quad f\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}),\,h,g\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}}),\text{ real-valued}.

(We suppress the dependence of
R
.) Let
\check{f}(\lambda)=\lambda\tilde{f}(\lambda)
for any real-valued
\tilde{f}\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
chosen such that
\tilde{f}=1
on
the support of
f
. Let
\widecheck{H}=\check{f}(\breve{H})
and denote the
corresponding Heisenberg derivative
\mathrm{i}[\widecheck{H},\cdot]
by

{\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}}
, in particular
\mathrm{i}[\widecheck{H},P]={\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}}P
.
We are interested in specific choices of
h
and
g
,
and for those choices
P
has a
\kappa
-order, say
2t
.
More precisely we impose the condition
h\in{\mathcal{G}}_{t}
, cf. (
131
), and conclude that
the composed function
h(r)
has
\kappa
-order
t
, so that indeed
P
has a

\kappa
-order
2t
(note that due to
Lemma
3.9
the
\kappa
-order of
g(B_{\kappa})
and

\widecheck{H}
are
zero). This implies that
the commutator
{\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}}P
has order
2t
, possibly (and
indeed) smaller. We need to
compute the order more exactly along with the leading order term.

Lemma 3.10
.

Let
R\geq 1
be given big enough and suppose
h\in{\mathcal{G}}_{t}
. Then

\displaystyle\begin{split}&{\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}}P-L_{1}-L_{2}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-2});\\
&L_{1}=4f(\breve{H})\operatorname{Re}\big((\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)h^{\prime}(r)B_{\kappa}g(B_{\kappa})h(r)\big)f(\breve{H}),\\
&L_{2}=f(\breve{H})h(r)\operatorname{Re}\big(g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa}){\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}B_{\kappa}}\big)h(r)f(\breve{H}).\end{split}

(141a)

The operators
L_{1},L_{2}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-1})
, so in particular
{\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}}P\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-1})
.

Suppose in addition that
g^{\prime}\geq 0
and that

(g^{\prime})^{1/2}\in C^{\infty}
. Then
L_{2}
can be replaced by any of the
following expressions
L_{3},L_{4},L_{5}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-1})
for which
\textrm{Com}=f(\breve{H})\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{\kappa}]f(\breve{H})
is defined in agreement with Lemma
3.6
.

\displaystyle L_{3}

\displaystyle=h(r)\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}f(\breve{H})\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},B_{\kappa}]f(\breve{H})\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}h(r),

(141b)

\displaystyle\begin{split}L_{4}&=h(r)\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}X^{-1/2}\textrm{Com}\,X^{-1/2}\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}h(r)\\
&-2h(r)\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}X^{-1/2}f(\breve{H})(\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)B_{\kappa}^{2}f(\breve{H})X^{-1/2}\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}h(r),\end{split}

(141c)

\displaystyle\begin{split}L_{5}&=h(r)X^{-1/2}\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}\textrm{Com}\,\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}X^{-1/2}h(r)\\
&-2h(r)X^{-1/2}\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}f(\breve{H})(\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)B_{\kappa}^{2}f(\breve{H})\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}X^{-1/2}h(r).\end{split}

(141d)

Proof.

We write

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}}P&=L^{\prime}_{1}+L^{\prime}_{2};\\
L^{\prime}_{1}&=2f(\breve{H})\operatorname{Re}\Big(\big(\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}h(r)\big)g(B_{\kappa})h(r)\Big)f(\breve{H}),\\
L^{\prime}_{2}&=f(\breve{H})h(r)\big({\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}g(B_{\kappa})}\big)h(r)f(\breve{H}).\end{split}

(142)

By (
130
)

\displaystyle\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}h(r)

\displaystyle=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}\breve{R}(z)\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},h(r)]\breve{R}(z){\mathrm{d}}\mu_{\check{f}}(z);

(143a)

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},h(r)]

\displaystyle=2\operatorname{Re}\big(h^{\prime}(r)B\big)-\lambda_{0}\mathrm{i}[\Pi,h(r)]-\mathrm{i}[\breve{K},h(r)].

(143b)

We insert (
143b
) into (
143a
) obtaining
then three terms, say
T_{1},T_{2},T_{3}
. We can write (seen by commutation)

\displaystyle T_{1}=2\check{f}^{\prime}(\breve{H})h^{\prime}(r)B+O_{\kappa}(X^{t-2})\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t-1}).

The terms
T_{2}
and
T_{3}
are treated using
(
135
) and (
137
) and Lemma
3.9

(cf. the proof of discussion after
(
137
)) to obtain

\displaystyle T_{2},\,T_{3}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t-2}).

Next we insert the resulting formula

\displaystyle{\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}}h=2\check{f}^{\prime}(\breve{H})h^{\prime}(r)B+{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t-2})\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t-1})

(144)

into the expression
L^{\prime}_{1}
, and we see that

L^{\prime}_{1}=L_{1}+{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-2})
(note that
f\check{f}^{\prime}=f
). Clearly

L_{1}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-1})
.

As for
L^{\prime}_{2}
we can assume that
g\in C_{\mathrm{c}}^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
(since
B_{\kappa}
is bounded we can truncate
g
outside

\sigma(B_{\kappa})
) and use (
130
) again

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}g(B_{\kappa})&=-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}R_{\kappa}(z)\big(\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}B_{\kappa}\big)R_{\kappa}(z){\mathrm{d}}\mu_{g}(z)\\
&=g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}B_{\kappa}+\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}R_{\kappa}(z)^{2}[{\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}B_{\kappa}},B_{\kappa}]R_{\kappa}(z){\mathrm{d}}\mu_{g}(z)\\
&=g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}B_{\kappa}+{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{-2})\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{-1});\end{split}

(145)

for the last identity we used Lemma
3.9
(and its
proof). Obviously the first term to the right can be replaced by its
real part. We
conclude that
L^{\prime}_{2}=L_{2}+{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-2})
. Whence
L^{\prime}_{1}+L^{\prime}_{2}=L_{1}+L_{2}+{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-2})
, showing
(
141a
). Clearly

L_{2}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-1})
.

Next to show that
L_{3}-L_{2}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-2})
under the extra
conditions on
g
we first note that the truncation of
g
outside

\sigma(B_{\kappa})
, to make
it compactly supported, obviously applies to
(g^{\prime})^{1/2}
as well making again
(
130
) applicable. Whence we can
symmetrize
L_{2}
, so that it suffices to show that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\bar{L}_{2}-\bar{L}_{3}&\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-2});\\
\bar{L}_{2}&=f(\breve{H})h(r)\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}\big({\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}B_{\kappa}}\big)\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}h(r)f(\breve{H}),\\
\bar{L}_{3}&=h(r)\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}f(\breve{H})\big({\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}B_{\kappa}}\big)f(\breve{H})\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}h(r).\end{split}

(146)

(Note that
L_{3}=\bar{L}_{3}
.) Using (
144
) and
(
145
) with
\check{f}
replaced by
f
we see that

\displaystyle[f(\breve{H}),h(r)]\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t-1}),\,[f(\breve{H}),\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})}]\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{-1}),

which shows (
146
).
By this proof we see
that
L_{3}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-1})
. Alternatively we may easily check
the latter property directly. (We can verify similarly that
L_{4},L_{5}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-1})
.)

To show that
L_{4}-L_{3}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-2})
it suffices to show
that

\displaystyle\begin{split}X^{1/2}f(\breve{H})&\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},B_{\kappa}]f(\breve{H})X^{1/2}-f(\breve{H})\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{\kappa}]f(\breve{H})\\
&+2f(\breve{H})(\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)B_{\kappa}^{2}f(\breve{H})\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{-1}).\end{split}

(147)

For the first term we substitute

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},B_{\kappa}]=\mathrm{i}[H,B_{\kappa}]-\lambda_{0}\mathrm{i}[\Pi,B_{\kappa}]-\mathrm{i}[\breve{K},B_{\kappa}].

As we saw in the last part of the proof of Lemma

3.9
each of these commutators contributes by an operator in

\breve{\mathcal{B}}\subset{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0})
. This remains valid if we
replace the term by

\displaystyle f(\breve{H})X^{1/2}\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},B_{\kappa}]X^{1/2}f(\breve{H}),

and indeed the error (given by commuting the factors of
X^{1/2}

and
f(\breve{H})
) contributes by an operator in

{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{-1})
. On the other hand

\displaystyle f(\breve{H})\Big(X^{1/2}

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},B_{\kappa}]X^{1/2}-\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{\kappa}]+2(\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)B_{\kappa}^{2}\Big)f(\breve{H})

\displaystyle=f(\breve{H})\Big(-2\operatorname{Re}\big(\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},X^{1/2}]B_{\kappa}X^{1/2}\big)+2(\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)B_{\kappa}^{2}\Big)f(\breve{H})

\displaystyle=f(\breve{H})\Big(-\operatorname{Re}\big(X^{-1/2}2BB_{\kappa}X^{1/2}\big)+2(\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)B_{\kappa}^{2}\Big)f(\breve{H})+O_{\kappa}(X^{-1})

\displaystyle\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{-1}).

Thus (
147
) is proven.

Noting that

\textrm{Com}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0})
and

\big[\sqrt{g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})},X^{-1/2}\big]\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{-3/2})
it follows
that
L_{5}-L_{4}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-2})
, which finishes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.11
.

The formula (
145
) is an example of the following general commutator
expansion formula for operators in
{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t})
, cf.
[
GIS
]
. Thus for
given
S\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t})
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
g\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}})
and

K\geq 1

\displaystyle\begin{split}[S,g(&B_{\kappa})]\\
=&-\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}R_{\kappa}(z){\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}(S)R_{\kappa}(z){\mathrm{d}}\mu_{g}(z)\\
=&\sum^{K}_{k=1}\tfrac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k!}g^{(k)}(B_{\kappa}){\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}^{k}(S)+R(K);\\
&R(K)=(-1)^{K+1}\int_{{\mathbb{C}}}R_{\kappa}(z)^{K+1}{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}^{K+1}(S)R_{\kappa}(z){\mathrm{d}}\mu_{g}(z)\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t-K-1}).\end{split}

(148)

We will need better control of the error terms of Lemma

3.10
in the application in Subsection

12.3
. Here we explain a procedure for establishing this. As noted before the function
g

in the first part of the lemma may be taken compactly
supported as required for
(
148
).
A principal goal is to refine the right-hand side of the statement

\displaystyle{\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}}P-L_{1}-L_{5}=(L^{\prime}_{1}-L_{1})+(L_{2}^{\prime}-L_{5})\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-2}).

Due to (
144
) we can
write

\displaystyle\begin{split}L^{\prime}_{1}&=4f(\breve{H})\operatorname{Re}{\big(h^{\prime}(r)Bg(B_{\kappa})h(r)\big)}f(\breve{H})\\
