&+f(\breve{H})\operatorname{Re}{\big({\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t-2})g(B_{\kappa})h(r)\big)}f(\breve{H})\\
&=L_{1}+f(\breve{H})\operatorname{Re}{\big({\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t-2})g(B_{\kappa})h(r)\big)}f(\breve{H}).\end{split}

(149)

For
L^{\prime}_{2}
we apply (
148
) to
S=\check{f}(\breve{H})
,
yielding (for
K\geq 2
)

\displaystyle\begin{split}L^{\prime}_{2}&=f(\breve{H})h(r)\big({\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}g(B_{\kappa})}\big)h(r)f(\breve{H})\\
&=\mathrm{i}\sum^{K}_{k=1}\tfrac{(-1)^{k-1}}{k!}\,f(\breve{H})h(r)g^{(k)}(B_{\kappa}){\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa}}^{k}(\check{f}(\breve{H}))h(r)f(\breve{H})+O_{\kappa}(X^{2t-K-1})\\
&=f(\breve{H})h(r)g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})\big(\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}B_{\kappa}\big)f(\breve{H}))h(r)f(\breve{H})+\sum^{K}_{k=2}S_{k}+O_{\kappa}(X^{2t-K-1}).\end{split}

(150)

The terms
S_{k}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2t-k})
, and they have an explicit form
suitable for the induction argument of Subsection
12.3
.
A consequence of (
150
) is the refined formula

\displaystyle\begin{split}L^{\prime}_{2}=L_{2}+\sum^{K}_{k=2}\operatorname{Re}(S_{k})+O_{\kappa}(X^{2t-K-1}).\end{split}

(151)

11.1.1
Smooth sign function

In some of our applications of Lemma
3.10

the function
g
will be a ‘smooth sign function’
\zeta_{\epsilon}
, cf.
[
AIIS
]
. It is
constructed in terms of a cut-off function
\eta_{\epsilon}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
with special properties: The parameter
\epsilon>0
is
considered small, and we define
\eta_{\epsilon}(b)=\tfrac{1}{\epsilon}\eta(\tfrac{b}{\epsilon})
, where
\eta^{\prime}(b)>0
for
|b|<1
,

\eta(b)=0
for
b\leq-1
and
\eta(b)=1
for
b\geq 1
. We can
choose
\eta
such that
\eta^{\prime}
is even,
\sqrt{\eta},\sqrt{\eta^{\prime}}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
and for some
c>0

\displaystyle\eta^{\prime}(b)\geq c\,\eta(b)\text{ for }b\in(-1,1/2].

(152)

The optimal choice of such
c
is not important for us
since we will only need (
152
) in the following disguised
form: For any
\tilde{c}>0
and all
\epsilon
small enough
(
\epsilon^{2}\leq\tfrac{2}{3}c\tilde{c}
suffices)

\displaystyle(\tfrac{\epsilon}{2}-b)\eta_{\epsilon}(b)\leq\tilde{c}\,\eta^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(b)\text{ for all }b\in{\mathbb{R}}.

(153a)

Note also that since
\eta_{\epsilon}^{\prime}
is even

\displaystyle 1=\epsilon\eta_{\epsilon}(b)+\epsilon\eta_{\epsilon}(-b).

(153b)

Let
\zeta_{\epsilon}(b)=\eta_{\epsilon}(b)-\eta_{\epsilon}(-b)
.

12
Positive commutator estimates

We shall prove properties of possibly existing eigenfunctions of

\breve{H}
at
\lambda_{0}
. In the case where they dont exist we shall
prove various resolvent estimates of
\breve{H}
near
\lambda_{0}
. Our
analysis is based on Lemma
3.10
and positive commutator
methods of
[
IS2
]
,
[
AIIS
]
and
[
GIS
]
. For convenience we
abbreviate in this section the Besov spaces with index
s=1/2
as

{\mathcal{B}}:={\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}
,
{\mathcal{B}}^{*}:={\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{1/2}

{{\mathcal{B}}}_{0}^{*}:={\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{1/2,0}
. (Note however that the same notation is
used with a slightly different meaning in Subsection
12.2
.)

12.1
A Rellich type theorem

As a first application
we show the following result, largely mimicking
[
IS2
]
.

Theorem 3.12
.

Every
generalized eigenfunction in
{{\mathcal{B}}}_{0}^{*}
of
\breve{H}
at

\lambda_{0}
, or at any sufficiently nearby real
\lambda^{\prime}_{0}
, is in
L^{2}_{\infty}
.

Proof.

We shall use the Mourre estimate (
106
) with

\lambda=\lambda_{0}
, say with the positive number
\epsilon=\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})
and
R=R_{0}\geq 1

fixed sufficiently big. In agreement with the statement
\kappa_{0}>0

is fixed too (small), and we can freely use the estimate for

\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0}]
and for a fixed (small) neighbourhood
{\mathcal{U}}
of

\lambda_{0}
and a fixed compact
K
. Whence we have freedom to
choose
\kappa>0
very small whenever conveniently in the proof
(note that
{\mathcal{U}}
and
K
do not depend on
\kappa
). Now suppose

\phi\in{{\mathcal{B}}}_{0}^{*}
and
(\breve{H}-\lambda^{\prime}_{0})\phi=0
with

\lvert\lambda_{0}-\lambda_{0}^{\prime}\rvert
small, then we can write

\phi=f(H)\phi
where
f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathcal{U}})
,
f(\lambda^{\prime}_{0})=1

and
f

is real.

Let
y_{\varepsilon}(r)=\tfrac{1}{1+\varepsilon r}
and

x_{\varepsilon}(r)=ry_{\varepsilon}(r)
for
\varepsilon\in[0,1]
,
and let
X_{\varepsilon}
and
Y_{\varepsilon}
be the operators of
multiplication by
x_{\varepsilon}
and
y_{\varepsilon}
, respectively
(this notation is consistent with (
115
)). These
quantities are henceforth used with
R=R_{0}
only. We note that

X:=X_{0}
agrees with the notation used in Section
11
and that
X_{\varepsilon}=XY_{\varepsilon}
. Note also
that
\nabla x_{\varepsilon}(r)=y_{\varepsilon}^{2}\omega
, whence for
example
\mathrm{i}[H,X_{\varepsilon}]={2Y_{\varepsilon}}B{Y_{\varepsilon}}
.

It is convenient to introduce
the following terminology for families

(T_{\varepsilon})_{0<\varepsilon\leq 1}\subset{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t})
of
operators on
L^{2}
,
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We say
(T_{\varepsilon})
is
uniformly of
\kappa
-order
t
if

\displaystyle\forall\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0}^{\prime}]\;\forall s\in{\mathbb{R}}\,\;\forall k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}:\quad\sup_{\varepsilon\in(0,1]}\lVert X^{s-k-t}{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa,R_{0}}}^{k}(T_{\varepsilon})X^{-s}\rVert<\infty.

We shall allow ourselves to write

T_{\varepsilon}={\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(X^{t})
and
T_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(X^{t})
to symbolize that the operator
T_{\varepsilon}
is member of
a family of operators
(T_{\varepsilon})
which is uniformly of
\kappa
-order
t
. If
(S_{\varepsilon})
and

(T_{\varepsilon})
are uniformly of
\kappa
-order
s
and

t
, respectively, then
(S_{\varepsilon}T_{\varepsilon})
is uniformly
of
\kappa
-order
s+t
.

I
.  We show
that
\phi\in L^{2}_{-1/2}
.
Fix any
\delta\in(0,1/2)
. We shall consider the ‘propagation observable’

\displaystyle P_{\varepsilon}=f(\breve{H})X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}\zeta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}f(\breve{H}),\,\varepsilon\in(0,1].

Clearly
P_{\varepsilon}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(X^{2\delta})
. The positive parameter
\epsilon

used
here will be
fixed shortly, small enough. Note that
X_{\varepsilon}
and
P_{\varepsilon}
are bounded due to the appearance of the
factor
y_{\varepsilon}
. Eventually this factor will be removed by
letting
\varepsilon\to 0
.
More precisely we shall demonstrate some ‘essential positivity’
of
\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},P_{\varepsilon}]
persisting in
the

\varepsilon\to 0
limit. For any
n\in{\mathbb{N}}
the function

\phi_{n}=\chi_{n}(r)\phi\in H^{1}
(cf. the notation (
4.1
)),
(\breve{H}-\lambda^{\prime}_{0})\phi_{n}=[\breve{H},\chi_{n}]\phi
and whence the expectation (we use in general
the notation
\langle T\rangle_{\phi}=\langle\phi,T\phi\rangle
)

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},P_{\varepsilon}]\rangle_{\phi_{n}}=-2\operatorname{Re}\langle\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},\chi_{n}]P_{\varepsilon}\chi_{n}\rangle_{\phi}.

(154a)

Since
\phi\in{{\mathcal{B}}}_{0}^{*}
the term to the right vanishes as

n\to\infty
. Writing
\breve{H}=H-\lambda_{0}\Pi-\breve{K}
this
claim is obvious for the contribution from
H
. For the other terms
the commutator is effectively of order
X^{-2}
,
cf. (
135
) and the subsequent discussion, whence their contributions vanish too as

n\to\infty
.
It remains to study the left-hand side of (
154a
) in this
limit.

Let

S_{\varepsilon}={Y^{2}_{\varepsilon}}X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta-1}\left(\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(X^{\delta-1})\right)
and

\theta_{\epsilon}=\sqrt{\eta^{\prime}_{\epsilon}}
. We compute using Lemma
3.10

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},P_{\varepsilon}]\rangle_{\phi_{n}}

\displaystyle=\langle\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}P_{\varepsilon}\rangle_{\phi_{n}}=\langle L_{1}+L_{5}+{\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(X^{2\delta-2})\rangle_{\phi_{n}};

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle=4\delta f(\breve{H})\operatorname{Re}\big((\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)S_{\varepsilon}B_{\kappa}\zeta_{\varepsilon}(B_{\kappa})X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}\big)f(\breve{H}),

\displaystyle X^{1/2}X_{\varepsilon}^{-\delta}L_{5}X_{\varepsilon}^{-\delta}X^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\theta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})f(\breve{H})\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{\kappa}]f(\breve{H})\theta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})

\displaystyle+\theta_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa})f(\breve{H})\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{\kappa}]f(\breve{H})\theta_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa})

\displaystyle-2\theta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})f(\breve{H})(\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)B_{\kappa}^{2}f(\breve{H})\theta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})

\displaystyle-2\theta_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa})f(\breve{H})(\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)B_{\kappa}^{2}f(\breve{H})\theta_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa}).

Noting the essential positivity of the term containing the factor

\kappa^{2}B^{2}
we obtain after symmetrizing

\displaystyle L_{1}\geq 4\delta f(\breve{H})Y_{\varepsilon}X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta-1/2}B_{\kappa}\zeta_{\varepsilon}(B_{\kappa})X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta-1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}f(\breve{H})+O_{\textrm{unf}}(X^{2\delta-2}).

(154b)

Using the Mourre estimate of the
form discussed in the beginning of the proof and factors of
\tilde{f}(\breve{H})

(the latter can be inserted for free to bound

\kappa^{2}B^{2}
), we can estimate for
\kappa>0
sufficiently small

\displaystyle L_{5}

\displaystyle\geq X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}X^{-1/2}\theta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})f(\breve{H})\big(\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})-K\big)f(\breve{H})\theta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})X^{-1/2}X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}

\displaystyle+X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}X^{-1/2}\theta_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa})f(\breve{H})\big(\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})-K\big)f(\breve{H})\theta_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa})X^{-1/2}X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}

\displaystyle-4\epsilon^{2}f(\breve{H})X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}X^{-1/2}\Big(\eta^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})+\eta^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa})\Big)X^{-1/2}X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}f(\breve{H})+{\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(X^{2\delta-2}).

We shall require that
\epsilon>0
is so small that

8\epsilon^{2}<\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})
, implying

\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})-4\epsilon^{2}>\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})/2
.
We shall use (
153a
) with
\tilde{c}=\tfrac{\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})}{8\delta}
and
any possibly smaller

\epsilon
, henceforth considered fixed.
Now we fix a big
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
such that (with this

\epsilon
)

\displaystyle\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})-4\epsilon^{2}-\|K-\chi_{m}K\chi_{m}\|\geq\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})/2,

and note that the contribution from
the operator
\chi_{m}K\chi_{m}
is in
{\mathfrak{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(X^{2\delta-2})
.

Then we estimate

\displaystyle L_{5}

\displaystyle\geq 2^{-1}\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})f(\breve{H})X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}X^{-1/2}\Big(\eta^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})+\eta^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa})\Big)X^{-1/2}X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta}f(\breve{H})+{\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(X^{2\delta-2})

\displaystyle\geq 4\delta\tilde{c}f(\breve{H})Y_{\varepsilon}X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta-1/2}\Big(\eta^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})+\eta^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa})\Big)X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta-1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}f(\breve{H})+{\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(X^{2\delta-2}).

We conclude the
following lower
bound by combining this bound with (
154b
) and by using
(
153a
) and (
153b
),

\displaystyle L_{1}+L_{5}

\displaystyle\geq 2\delta\epsilon f(\breve{H})Y_{\varepsilon}X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta-1/2}\big(\eta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})+\eta_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa})\big){X_{\varepsilon}}^{\delta-1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}f(\breve{H})+{\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(X^{2\delta-2})

\displaystyle=2\delta f(\breve{H})X_{\varepsilon}^{2\delta-1}Y^{2}_{\varepsilon}f(\breve{H})+{\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(X^{2\delta-2}).

Whence we obtain from these
arguments the uniform bound

\displaystyle\|X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta-1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}\phi\|^{2}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\|X_{\varepsilon}^{\delta-1/2}Y_{\varepsilon}f(\breve{H})\phi_{n}\|^{2}\leq C\|X^{\delta-1}\phi\|^{2}.

(155)

By letting
\varepsilon\to 0
in (
155
) it follows that

\phi\in L^{2}_{\delta-1/2}\subset L^{2}_{-1/2}
.

II
.  We show that

\phi\in L_{\infty}^{2}
by a bootstrap argument. So suppose we have shown that

\phi\in L^{2}_{(m-1)/2-1/2}
for an
m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We did show this for

m=0
in Step
I
. Then we come to the
conclusion that
\phi\in L^{2}_{m/2-1/2}
by repeating the previous
procedure using now the observable

\displaystyle P=P_{\varepsilon}=f(\breve{H})X_{\varepsilon}^{m/2}\zeta_{\epsilon}(B_{k})X_{\varepsilon}^{m/2}f(\breve{H}),\,\varepsilon>0,

leading to the bound

\displaystyle\|Y_{\varepsilon}X_{\varepsilon}^{m/2-1/2}\phi\|^{2}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\|Y_{\varepsilon}X_{\varepsilon}^{m/2-1/2}f(\breve{H})\phi_{n}\|^{2}\leq C\|X^{(m-1)/2-1/2}\phi\|^{2}.

(156)

By letting
\varepsilon\to 0
we deduce that
\phi\in L^{2}_{m/2-1/2}
.

∎

12.2
LAP bound

We show a Besov space limiting
absorption principle bound, largely mimicking
[
AIIS
]
.

Theorem 3.13
.

Suppose
\lambda_{0}
is not an eigenvalue
of
\breve{H}
. Then there exist a neighbourhood
I\subset{\mathbb{R}}
of

\lambda_{0}
and
C>0
such that for all for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
with
\operatorname{Re}z\in I
and all
\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle\lVert\breve{R}(z)\psi\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}^{*}}\leq C\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}}.

(157)

To prove this bound we shall use the following weight-functions parametrized by

\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and defined on
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}

\displaystyle\Theta=\Theta_{\nu}(r)=1-\big(1+r/2^{\nu}\big)^{-1}.

Noting the formula for derivatives

\displaystyle\Theta^{(k)}=(-1)^{k-1}k!2^{-k\nu}\big(1+r/2^{\nu}\big)^{-1-k};\quad k\geq 1,

we obtain the bounds

\displaystyle\begin{split}&0<\Theta\leq\min\{1,r/2^{\nu}\},\\
&0<(-1)^{k-1}\Theta^{(k)}\leq k(-1)^{k}r^{-1}\Theta^{(k-1)}\leq k!r^{-k}\Theta;\quad k\geq 2.\end{split}

(158)

Below we will consider the composition
\Theta=\Theta_{\nu}(r_{R})
given
in terms of the parameter
R
appearing in Proposition

3.3
. In fact we shall apply Proposition

3.3
in essentially the same way as done in the beginning of
the proof of
Theorem
3.12
, in particular for
R=R_{0}
only. Since
\lambda_{0}\notin\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(\breve{H})
we can now
take
K=0
and
therefore replace
2\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})-\epsilon-K
by
\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})
in (
106
).
More precisely we use the Mourre estimate (
106
) with
\epsilon=\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})/2>0
and
R=R_{0}\geq 1

fixed sufficiently big. In agreement with this assertion,

\kappa_{0}>0

is fixed too (small, in particular
\kappa_{0}\leq\kappa_{0}^{\prime}
), and we can freely use the estimate for

\kappa\in(0,\kappa_{0}]
, for a fixed (small) open neighbourhood
{\mathcal{U}}
of

\lambda_{0}
and in fact with
K=0
, leaving us with the lower
bound
\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})
as claimed above. By the virial
theorem
\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(\breve{H})\cap{\mathcal{U}}=\emptyset
. We fix a
compact
neighbourhood
I\subset{\mathcal{U}}
of
\lambda_{0}
such that there are no
nonzero generalized eigenfunctions of
\breve{H}
in

{{\mathcal{B}}}_{0}^{*}
at any
point of
I
, which is doable thanks to Theorem
3.12
. As we will
see this
I
works in Theorem
3.13
. Choose a real-valued

f\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathcal{U}})
such that
f=1
on a
neighbourhood of
I
.

Below we
use the notation
{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
for the Besov spaces given in terms
of
r_{R_{0}}
(rather than in terms of
\lvert x\rvert
as before), the latter written for short
r=r_{R_{0}}
. Of course the
spaces
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathcal{B}}(r)
and
{\mathcal{B}}(\lvert x\rvert)
coincide and similarly
for the adjoint spaces (allowing us to
change the meaning of the notation in (
157
)).
We could use
X
for multiplication by

r
as in the previous subsection, however we find it more convenient to use the notation
r

only,
even though mostly it will be the operator of multiplication by

r
. We will assign the notation
{\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{t})
a different meaning (although very related) than
{\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(X^{t})
used in the previous subsection.
More precisely we introduce
the following terminology for families
(T_{\nu})_{\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}
of
operators on
L^{2}
.

We say
(T_{\nu})\subset{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{t})
is
uniformly of

\kappa
-order
t
(for
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
) if

\displaystyle\forall\kappa

\displaystyle\in(0,\kappa_{0}^{\prime}]\;\forall s\in{\mathbb{R}}\,\;\forall k\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}:

\displaystyle\sup_{\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}\lVert r^{s-k-t}{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa,R_{0}}}^{k}(T_{\nu})r^{-s}\rVert+\sup_{\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}2^{\nu/2}\lVert r^{s-k-t-1/2}{\operatorname{ad}}_{B_{\kappa,R_{0}}}^{k}(T_{\nu})r^{-s}\rVert<\infty.

For the elements
T_{\nu}
in such a family
(T_{\nu})
we write

T_{\nu}={\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{t})
and
T_{\nu}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{t})
. Note that for example

\Theta^{1/2}
, considered as the composed function
\Theta_{\nu}^{1/2}(r(\cdot))
, is uniformly of
\kappa
-order
0
, cf. (
158
). If
T_{\nu}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{t})

and
S
is any of the operators listed in
Lemma
3.9
(with
R=R_{0}
), then
(ST_{\nu})
and
(T_{\nu}S)

are also uniformly of
\kappa
-order

t
. More generally for any

T_{\nu}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{t})
and
S\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{s})

the operators
ST_{\nu},T_{\nu}S\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{s+t})
.
Similarly, if
S_{\nu}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{s})
and
T_{\nu}\in{\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{t})
then
S_{\nu}T_{\nu},T_{\nu}S_{\nu}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{s+t})
.

Lemma 3.14
.

There exists
C>0
and such that for all

z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
with
\operatorname{Re}z\in I
and for
all
\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}
and
\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle\|\Theta^{\prime}{}^{1/2}\phi\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigl(\|\phi\|_{{\mathcal{B}}^{*}}\|\psi\|_{{\mathcal{B}}}+\lVert{\psi}\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{2}+2^{-\nu/2}\|r^{-3/4}\phi\|^{2}\bigr),

(159a)

\displaystyle\lVert\phi\rVert^{2}_{{\mathcal{B}}^{*}}

\displaystyle\leq C\bigl(\lVert{\psi}\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{2}+\lVert r^{-3/4}\phi\rVert^{2}\bigr);

(159b)

here

\Theta=\Theta_{\nu}(r(\cdot))
and
\phi=\breve{R}(z)\psi
.

Proof.

The bound
(
159b
) follows from
(
159a
) by taking supremum over
\nu\geq 0
. So
it suffices to show (
159a
).

We consider
P_{\nu}=f(\breve{H})\Theta^{1/2}\zeta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})\Theta^{1/2}f(\breve{H})
,
where
B_{\kappa}
and
\zeta_{\epsilon}
are given as in
the previous subsection and
f
is the function introduced above. Note that

P_{\nu}={\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{0})
. As before we have the freedom to choose

\kappa>0
sufficiently small, and again also the parameter
\epsilon>0
will be
fixed sufficiently small.

Let

\theta_{\epsilon}=\sqrt{\eta^{\prime}_{\epsilon}}
. We compute using Lemma
3.10

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},P_{\nu}]\rangle_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\langle\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}P_{\nu}\rangle_{\phi}=\langle L_{1}+L_{5}+{\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{-2})\rangle_{\phi};

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle=2f(\breve{H})\operatorname{Re}\big((\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)\tfrac{\Theta^{\prime}}{\Theta^{1/2}}B_{\kappa}\zeta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})\Theta^{1/2}\big)f(\breve{H}),

\displaystyle r^{1/2}\Theta^{-1/2}L_{5}\Theta^{-1/2}r^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\theta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})f(\breve{H})\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{\kappa}]f(\breve{H})\theta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})

\displaystyle+\theta_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa})f(\breve{H})\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{\kappa}]f(\breve{H})\theta_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa})

\displaystyle-2\theta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})f(\breve{H})(\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)B_{\kappa}^{2}f(\breve{H})\theta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})

\displaystyle-2\theta_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa})f(\breve{H})(\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)B_{\kappa}^{2}f(\breve{H})\theta_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa}).

Noting the essential positivity of the term containing the factor

\kappa^{2}B^{2}
we obtain after symmetrizing

\displaystyle L_{1}\geq 2f(\breve{H})\Theta^{\prime 1/2}B_{\kappa}\zeta_{\varepsilon}(B_{\kappa})\Theta^{\prime 1/2}f(\breve{H})+O_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{-2}).

(160)

Using the Mourre estimate of the
form discussed in the beginning of the proof and factors of
\tilde{f}(\breve{H})

(the latter can be inserted for free to bound

\kappa^{2}B^{2}
), we can estimate for
\kappa>0
sufficiently small

\displaystyle L_{5}\geq(\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})-4\epsilon^{2})f(\breve{H})\Theta^{1/2}r^{-1/2}\Big(\eta^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})+\eta^{\prime}_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa})\Big)r^{-1/2}\Theta^{1/2}f(\breve{H})+{\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{-2}).

We shall require that
\epsilon>0
is so small that

8\epsilon^{2}<\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})
, implying

\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})-4\epsilon^{2}\geq\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})/2
.
We shall use (
153a
) with
\tilde{c}={\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})}/{4}
and
any possibly smaller

\epsilon
, henceforth considered fixed. Thus
by combining the above bound with (
160
) and using the bound
r\Theta^{\prime}\leq\Theta
we finally
obtain the
following lower
bound

\displaystyle L_{1}+L_{5}

\displaystyle\geq\epsilon f(\breve{H})\Theta^{\prime 1/2}\big(\eta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})+\eta_{\epsilon}(-B_{\kappa})\big)\Theta^{\prime 1/2}f(\breve{H})+{\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{-2})

\displaystyle=f(\breve{H})\Theta^{\prime}f(\breve{H})+{\mathcal{O}}_{\textrm{unf}}(r^{-2}).

Note also the trivial bound
{\langle\Theta^{\prime}\rangle_{(1-f(\breve{H}))\phi}}\leq C\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{2}
(using that
\operatorname{Re}z\in I
)
leading finally to the bound

\displaystyle\begin{split}\|\Theta^{\prime 1/2}\phi\|^{2}\leq 2\langle\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},P_{\nu}]\rangle_{\phi}+C\bigl(2^{-\nu/2}\lVert r^{-3/4}\phi\rVert^{2}+\lVert{\psi}\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}}^{2}\bigr).\end{split}

(161)

On the other hand

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},P_{\nu}]\rangle_{\phi}&=\mathrm{i}\langle\psi,P_{\nu}\phi\rangle-\mathrm{i}\langle P_{\nu}\phi,\psi\rangle+2(\operatorname{Im}z)\langle P_{\nu}\rangle_{\phi}\\
&\leq 2\lVert P_{\nu}\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}})}\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}}\lVert\phi\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}^{*}}+2\lVert P_{\nu}\rVert\lvert\operatorname{Im}{\langle\breve{H}-z\rangle_{\phi}}\rvert\\
&\leq 2(\lVert P_{\nu}\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}})}+\lVert P_{\nu}\rVert)\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}}\lVert\phi\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}^{*}}\\
&\leq C\lVert\phi\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}^{*}}\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}}.\end{split}

(162)

The combination of
(
161
) and (
162
) yields
(
159a
).
∎

Proof of Theorem
3.13
.

Let
I
be
given as in Lemma
3.14
.
Suppose by contradiction that
z_{n}\to\lambda_{0}^{\prime}\in I
,

\lVert\psi_{n}\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}}\to 0
and
\lVert\phi_{n}\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}^{*}}=1
where

\phi_{n}=\breve{R}(z_{n})\psi_{n}
(here
{\mathcal{B}}
and
{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
are defined in terms of

r=r_{R_{0}}
).
Fix
s\in(1/2,3/4)
. We can assume that there exists
\wslim_{n\to\infty}\phi_{n}=:\phi\in L^{2}_{-s}
.
By local compactness and an energy estimate we easily see (using
the notation (
4.1
) and
\chi_{m}=\chi_{m}(r)
) that

\displaystyle\forall m\in{\mathbb{N}}:\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}\chi_{m}\phi_{n}=\chi_{m}\phi\text{ in }H^{1}.

Moreover by (
159a
)

\displaystyle\exists C>0\,\forall m\in{\mathbb{N}}\,\forall\nu\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}:\quad\|\Theta^{\prime}{}^{1/2}\chi_{m}\phi\|^{2}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\Theta^{\prime}{}^{1/2}\chi_{m}\phi_{n}\|^{2}\leq C2^{-\nu/2}.

From this bound we learn that

\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{0}^{*}
, and since also

(\breve{H}-\lambda^{\prime}_{0})\phi=0
we obtain that

\phi=0
(cf. Theorem
3.12
).
By local compactness it then follows that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\phi_{n}=\phi=0\text{ in }L^{2}_{-3/4}
,
and by (
159b
) we then deduce that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\lVert\phi_{n}\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}^{*}}^{2}=0

contradicting the assumption that
\lVert\phi_{n}\rVert_{{\mathcal{B}}^{*}}=1

for all
n
.
∎

12.3
Microlocal bounds and LAP

We prove microlocal bounds of the
resolvent
\breve{R}(z)
, largely mimicking
[
GIS
]
and
[
AIIS
]
. As in
[
GIS
]
we then obtain similar bounds of powers
of the
resolvent, and this implies LAP (the limiting absorption principle) for
\breve{H}
near
\lambda_{0}
.

We assume
\lambda_{0}\notin\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(\breve{H})
. Since we are not going to need the sharpest version of these microlocal
bounds which use the optimal constant in Proposition

3.3
we will simplify the presentation and proceed
exactly as in
the beginning of the previous subsection, not to be repeated. (The
sharper version is given by replacing the constant
\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})/2
in Lemma
3.15
by any positive number
less than
\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})
.) With the
given
neighbourhood
I\ni\lambda_{0}
we define
I_{\pm}=\{z|\,\operatorname{Re}z\in I,\,\pm\operatorname{Im}z>0\}
and
I_{\mathbb{C}}=I_{+}\cup I_{-}
.

We start by showing a version of the first step of an induction
procedure of

[
GIS
]
, cf.
[
AIIS
]
. Let for all
\sigma>0
the notation
{\mathcal{G}}_{-}^{\sigma}
and

{\mathcal{G}}_{+}^{\sigma}
signify
the classes of real functions
g\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})

with support in
(-\infty,\sigma)
and
(-\sigma,\infty)
, respectively.

Lemma 3.15
.

Under the above conditions let

\tilde{\sigma}>0
be given by

\tilde{\sigma}^{2}=\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})/2
and let
\sigma\in(0,{\tilde{\sigma}})
.
There exists
\check{\kappa}_{0}\in(0,\kappa_{0}]
such that the
following bounds hold for any

\chi(\pm\cdot<\sigma)\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\pm}^{\sigma}
and
t\in(0,1/2)
.

\displaystyle\forall\kappa\in(0,\check{\kappa}_{0}]\,\exists C>0\,\forall z\in I_{\pm}:\quad\lVert\chi(\pm B_{\kappa}<\sigma)\breve{R}(z)\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{1-t},L^{2}_{-t})}\leq C.

(163)

Proof.

Let

t\in(0,1/2)
and
\delta=1/2-t
. Let
z\in I_{+}
,
\psi\in L^{2}_{1-t}
and
\phi=\breve{R}(z)\psi
(we only consider
I_{+}
). We introduce for
\epsilon>0

(and with
R=R_{0}
) the following operator
P
of
\kappa
-order

2\delta
,

\displaystyle P=f(\breve{H})X^{\delta}g_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})X^{\delta}f(\breve{H});\quad g_{\epsilon}(b)=-(\sigma+2\epsilon-b)^{2\delta}\eta^{2}_{\epsilon}(\sigma-b).

(164)

We compute

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}(\sigma+2\epsilon-b)^{2t}g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(b)=\delta\eta^{2}_{\epsilon}(\sigma-b)+(\sigma+2\epsilon-b)(\eta_{\epsilon}\eta^{\prime}_{\epsilon})(\sigma-b),

(165)

and noting that
g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}\geq 0
and
(g_{\epsilon}^{\prime})^{1/2}\in C^{\infty}
we
see that all of
Lemma
3.10
applies.
Letting

\theta_{\epsilon}=\sqrt{g_{\epsilon}^{\prime}}
we
read off

\displaystyle\langle\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},P]\rangle_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\langle\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}P\rangle_{\phi}=\langle L_{1}+L_{5}+{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2\delta-2})\rangle_{\phi};

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle=4\delta f(\breve{H})\operatorname{Re}\big((\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1))X^{\delta-1}B_{\kappa}g(B_{\kappa})X^{\delta}\big)f(\breve{H}),

\displaystyle X^{t}L_{5}X^{t}

\displaystyle=\theta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})f(\breve{H})\mathrm{i}[\breve{H},A_{\kappa}]f(\breve{H})\theta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})

\displaystyle-2\theta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})f(\breve{H})(\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)B_{\kappa}^{2}f(\breve{H})\theta_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa}).

Letting

T=(\sigma+2\epsilon-B_{\kappa})^{-t}\eta_{\epsilon}(\sigma-B_{\kappa})X^{-t}f(\breve{H})
we can estimate

\displaystyle L_{1}

\displaystyle\geq 4\delta f(\breve{H})X^{-t}B_{\kappa}g(B_{\kappa})X^{-t}f(\breve{H})+{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2\delta-2})

\displaystyle\geq 4\delta T^{*}\big(B^{2}_{\kappa}-(\sigma+\epsilon)(\sigma+2\epsilon)\big)T+{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2\delta-2}).

To bound
L_{5}
we first fix
\check{\kappa}_{0}\in(0,\kappa_{0}]
such with

C^{\prime}=\lVert\tilde{f}(\breve{H})B^{4}\tilde{f}(\breve{H})\rVert
and with an arbitrary
sufficiently small
\epsilon^{\prime}>0
, the constant

\displaystyle c^{\prime}=\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})-2(\sigma+\epsilon^{\prime})(\sigma+2\epsilon^{\prime})-2{\check{\kappa}}_{0}^{2}C^{\prime}\text{ is positive}.

(166)

Then for all
\kappa\in(0,\check{\kappa}_{0}]
and

\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon^{\prime}]
the second term on the right-hand side of
(
165
) contributes by a non-negative term and we obtain

\displaystyle L_{5}

\displaystyle\geq X^{-t}f(\breve{H})\big(\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})-2\kappa^{2}C^{\prime}-2B_{\kappa}^{2}\big)g^{\prime}(B_{\kappa})f(\breve{H})X^{-t}+{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2\delta-2})

\displaystyle\geq 2\delta T^{*}\big(\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})-2\kappa^{2}C^{\prime}-2B_{\kappa}^{2}\big)T+{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2\delta-2}).

Observing the cancellation of the terms containing
B_{\kappa}^{2}
these bounds lead to the lower bounds

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}P&\geq 2\delta T^{*}\big(-2(\sigma+\epsilon)(\sigma+2\epsilon)+\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})-2\kappa^{2}C^{\prime}\big)T+{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2\delta-2})\\
&\geq 2\delta c^{\prime}T^{*}T+{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2\delta-2});\quad\kappa\in(0,\check{\kappa}_{0}],\,\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon^{\prime}].\end{split}

(167)

Next, introducing

S=\eta_{\epsilon}(\sigma-B_{\kappa})X^{-t}
and using the fact
that

B_{\kappa}
is bounded we obtain

\displaystyle\lVert Sf(\breve{H})\phi\rVert\leq C_{1}\lVert T\phi\rVert.

Using the notation
\|\cdot\|_{s}=\|\cdot\|_{L^{2}_{s}}
we
also note that

\displaystyle\lVert S(1-f(\breve{H})\phi\rVert\leq C_{2}\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\delta-1/2}}.

We conclude that

\displaystyle\lVert S\phi\rVert^{2}\leq C_{3}\big(\lVert T\phi\rVert^{2}+\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\delta-1/2}}^{2}\big).

By combining this bound with (
167
) we obtain

\displaystyle c\|S\phi\|^{2}\leq\langle\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}P\rangle_{\phi}+C_{4}\bigl(\lVert\phi\rVert_{{\delta-1}}^{2}+\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\delta-1/2}}^{2}\bigr);\quad{c}=2\delta c^{\prime}/C_{3}.

On the other hand for any
\varepsilon>0

\displaystyle\langle\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}P\rangle_{\phi}

\displaystyle=\,\mathrm{i}\langle\psi,P\phi\rangle-\mathrm{i}\langle P\phi,\psi\rangle+2(\operatorname{Im}z)\langle P\rangle_{\phi}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\big(\lVert S\phi\rVert+\lVert\phi\rVert_{{\delta-3/2}}\big)\,\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\delta+1/2}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\varepsilon\big(\lVert S\phi\rVert^{2}+\lVert\phi\rVert_{{\delta-3/2}}^{2}\big)+C\varepsilon^{-1}\lVert\psi\rVert^{2}_{{\delta+1/2}}.

We choose
\varepsilon=c/(2C)
, yielding

\displaystyle\tfrac{c}{2}\|S\phi\|^{2}\leq C_{5}\bigl(\lVert\phi\rVert_{{\delta-1}}^{2}+\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\delta+1/2}}^{2}\bigr).

Finally we invoke Theorem
3.13
and conclude
(after a commutation)
that for all
\kappa\in(0,\check{\kappa}_{0}]
and
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon^{\prime}]

\displaystyle\|\eta_{\epsilon}(\sigma-B_{\kappa})\phi\|_{{\delta-1/2}}^{2}\leq C_{6}\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\delta+1/2}}^{2}.

(168)

This finishes the proof since for any given function

\chi=\chi(\cdot<\sigma)
, we can write
\chi=\epsilon\chi\eta_{\epsilon}(\sigma-\cdot)
for a small enough
\epsilon
and then for any
\kappa\in(0,\check{\kappa}_{0}]
bound

\displaystyle\|\chi(B_{\kappa}<\sigma)\phi\|_{{\delta-1/2}}\leq C_{7}\|\eta_{\epsilon}(\sigma-B_{\kappa})\phi\|_{{\delta-1/2}}\leq C_{8}\lVert\psi\rVert_{{\delta+1/2}}.

∎

Proposition 3.16
.

Under the conditions of Lemma

3.15
the same assertion holds for arbitrary

t<1/2
(i.e. the constraint
t>0
of the lemma is removed).

Proof.

Let
J_{m}=\tfrac{1}{4}\big([2,3)-m\big);\,m\in{\mathbb{N}}
. We saw in
the previous proof that

\check{\kappa}_{0}=\check{\kappa}_{0}(\sigma)\in(0,\kappa_{0}]
chosen in agreement with (
166
)
works for any given
\sigma\in(0,\tilde{\sigma})
, and in particular we
obtained (
163
) with
t\in J_{1}
. We fix
\check{\kappa}_{0}=\check{\kappa}_{0}(\sigma)
this way along
with the other positive constant
\epsilon^{\prime}=\epsilon^{\prime}(\sigma)
of
(
166
) and
proceed to show by induction the assertion
\underline{q(m)}
:

\displaystyle\forall\sigma\in(0,\tilde{\sigma})\,\forall t\in J_{m}\,\forall g\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\pm}^{\sigma}\,\forall\kappa\in(0,\check{\kappa}_{0}(\sigma)]:

\displaystyle\quad\sup_{z\in I_{\pm}}\quad\lVert g(B_{\kappa})\breve{R}(z)\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{1-t},L^{2}_{-t})}<\infty.

Since we have shown
q(1)
we can assume

q(m-1)
for a given
m\geq 2
is known, and then it remains to verify
q(m)
.

Let
t\in J_{m}
and
\kappa\in(0,\check{\kappa}_{0}]
be
given, and introduce again
\delta=1/2-t
. Let
z\in I_{+}
,
\psi\in L^{2}_{1-t}
and
\phi=\breve{R}(z)\psi
(we consider only
I_{+}
). We
consider again (
164
) for
\epsilon\in(0,\epsilon^{\prime}]
. It
suffices to show (
168
) for any such
\epsilon
by the
argument at the end of the proof of Lemma
3.15
. For
that we use the same scheme of proof as before, however since now
possibly
\delta-1\geq-1/2
we can not use Theorem
3.13
in
the same way. Rather we need to combine the commutator expansion formula
(
148
) with Lemma
3.10
which will allow us to
use the induction hypothesis in combination with Theorem

3.13
. This is already discussed in Remark
3.11
.

First we look at the contribution to
\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}P
from

L^{\prime}_{1}-L_{1}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2\delta-2})
of (
149
), i.e. we
look at

\displaystyle Q_{2\delta-2}=f(\breve{H})\operatorname{Re}{\Big({\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{\delta-2})g_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})X^{\delta}\Big)}f(\breve{H}).

By commutator expansion we see that with
\check{g}_{\epsilon}=\eta_{\epsilon}(\sigma+2\epsilon-\cdot)

\displaystyle\begin{split}Q_{2\delta-2}&=\check{g}_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})Q_{2\delta-2}\check{g}_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})+{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{-2})\\
&=\check{g}_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})X^{-t-1/2}{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{0})X^{-t-1/2}\check{g}_{\epsilon}(B_{\kappa})+{\mathcal{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{-2}).\end{split}

(169)

Whence the induction hypothesis combined with Theorem

3.13
works upon taking

\epsilon>0
sufficiently small.

Next we look at the contribution to
\widecheck{\mathbf{D}}P
from

L^{\prime}_{2}-L_{5}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2\delta-2})
. As in the proof of Lemma

3.10
we split

\displaystyle L^{\prime}_{2}-L_{5}=(L^{\prime}_{2}-L_{2})-(L_{3}-L_{2})-(L_{4}-L_{3})-(L_{5}-L_{4})=\sum^{4}_{j=1}T_{j}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2\delta-2}).

By (
151
) the term
T_{1}
can be represented as
Q_{2\delta-2}
in (
169
), so we can argue in
the same way for this term. By inspection of the proof of Lemma

3.10
we see that there are similar expansions for

T_{j}\in{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2\delta-2})
,
j=2,3,4
, which allows us to
treat these terms in the same way.

Finally using the auxiliary operators
S
and
T
of the proof of Lemma

3.15
we can mimic the last part of the proof using again the localization argument above to
treat lower order terms, in particular various terms in
{\mathfrak{O}}_{\kappa}(X^{2\delta-2})
. We obtain
q(m)
.
∎

By the same method we can prove a two-sided estimate.

Proposition 3.17
.

Let
\tilde{\sigma}>0
be given by

\tilde{\sigma}^{2}=\tilde{d}(\lambda_{0})/2
and let
\sigma\in(0,{\tilde{\sigma}})
.
There exists
\check{\kappa}_{0}>0
such that the
following bounds hold for any
s>0
and for any pair

g_{\pm}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\pm}^{\sigma}
such that

\sup\operatorname{supp}g_{-}<\inf\operatorname{supp}g_{+}
.

\displaystyle\forall\kappa\in(0,\check{\kappa}_{0}]\,\exists C>0\,\forall z\in I_{+}:\quad\lVert g_{-}(B_{\kappa})\breve{R}(z)g_{+}(B_{\kappa})\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{-s},L^{2}_{s})}\leq C.

(170)

As in
[
GIS
]
the assertions Theorem
3.13
and Propositions
3.16
and

3.17
combine algebraically yielding bounds (including
microlocal ones) of
powers of the resolvent. In particular the following bounds follow,
which in turn implies LAP.

Theorem 3.18
.

Suppose
\lambda_{0}
is not an eigenvalue
of
\breve{H}
.
Then

\displaystyle\forall k\in{\mathbb{N}}\,\forall s<1/2\,\exists C>0\,\forall z\in I_{\mathbb{C}}:\quad\lVert\breve{R}(z)^{k}\rVert_{{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{k-s},L^{2}_{s-k})}\leq C.

(171)

In particular for any
t>1/2
the limits
\breve{R}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\breve{R}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)

exist in
{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{t},L^{2}_{-t})
uniformly in
\lambda\in I
. Moreover
there exists the strong weak-star limits

\displaystyle\swslim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\breve{R}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)=\breve{R}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{*});\quad\lambda\in I.

(172)

We will need the following application, see (
214c
).

Corollary 3.19
.

Suppose
\lambda_{0}
is not an eigenvalue
of
\breve{H}
,
s>1/2
and that
f\in L^{2}_{s}
is given
such that
\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)f=\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)f
. Then

\displaystyle\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)f=\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)f\in L^{2}_{s-1}.

(173)

Proof.

The result follows from Proposition
3.16
and
Theorem
3.18
by using a suitable decomposition
1=g_{-}(B_{\kappa})+g_{+}(B_{\kappa})
,
g_{\pm}\in{\mathcal{G}}_{\pm}^{\sigma}
.
∎

Remark 3.20
.

In the multiple case, here discussed with the assumptions of
Section
7

only (in particular with (
54
) imposed), we need the analogues of
(
172
) and Corollary
3.19
,
cf. (
214a
)–(
214c
). We argue by commenting
on the necessary modifications that the same methods of proof work
with a minimum of extra complication.

First note that the operator
\breve{K}
of (
94a
) needs to
be replaced
\breve{K}=K_{1}-K_{2}
with
K_{1}
and
K_{2}
given by
(
65b
). To simplify the form of this operator
\breve{K}

we introduce the notation

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}_{-\infty,\infty}=\cap_{r,t\in{\mathbb{R}}}\,{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{r}({\mathbf{X}}),L^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}})\big).

(174)

Now using (
62
), Lemmas
2.6
and
2.9

as in the proof of Lemma
2.10
we see
that

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\breve{K}-\breve{K}_{1}-\breve{K}_{2}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{-\infty,\infty};\\
&\breve{K}_{j}=\Pi_{j}I_{j}+I_{j}\Pi_{j}-\Pi_{j}I_{j}\Pi_{j},\quad j=1,2.\end{split}

(175)

Note for example that

\displaystyle\Pi-\Pi_{1}-\Pi_{2}\in(1-\Delta)^{-1}{\mathcal{L}}^{2}_{-\infty,\infty}\subset{\mathcal{L}}^{2}_{-\infty,\infty}.

Using (
175
) we can prove Proposition

3.3
by mimicking the proof for the non-multiple
case. In the first step of the proof of Lemma
3.7
we
have the given properties for
a_{j}
and
\Pi_{j}
,
j=1,2
, rather than
for
a_{0}
and
\Pi
. This is all we need to repeat the proof. In Lemma

3.9
we need a similar replacement (in particular
the polynomial factors in
P_{1}\Pi_{j}P_{2}
are polynomials in quantities
defined for

a_{j}
rather than for
a_{0}
). In the proof of Lemma
3.9

we can obviously add the class of operators in the intersection of
all operators of finite
\kappa
-order to the classes
\breve{\mathcal{B}}_{k}
. Note that
{\mathcal{L}}_{-\infty,\infty}
is included in this way, and
we can repeat the proof. The applications of Subsection

11.1
and the present section are the same as before.

Remarks 3.21
.

1.

We discuss the extension to the case

\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{\prime})
. We shall use Section
8
and Remark

3.20
. Note that

\lambda_{0}\notin\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{\prime\prime})
and that Theorem

3.12
implies that the
L^{2}
-eigenfunctions of
H^{\prime}
at

\lambda_{0}
are in
L^{2}_{\infty}
(note also that the dimension of the
corresponding
eigenspace is finite, cf. Proposition

3.3
). Under the assumptions of Section

8
the ’extra term’

\lambda_{0}|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
of (
90a
) and
(
91b
) is consequently in
{\mathcal{L}}_{-\infty,\infty}
. We can
therefore prove Proposition
3.3
with the operator
\breve{H}
of Section
8
(cf. Remark

3.20
) and obtain the corresponding
resolvent bounds of
\breve{R}
from Subsections
12.2

and
12.3
. In particular Corollary

3.19
also holds for the operators

\breve{H}
and
\breve{R}
of Section
8
in the case

\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{\prime})
.

2.

We shall in Chapter

5
use variations of this
construction. These are given by applying the theory of the present
chapter to
H
replaced by
H_{\sigma}:=H-\sigma\Pi_{H}
,
\sigma>0

small, where
\Pi_{H}
is the orthogonal projection onto the
eigenspace of
H
corresponding to
\lambda_{0}
(assuming that

\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue). In the context of Chapter

5
this projection has finite rank, and
if all
L^{2}
-eigenfunctions are in
L^{2}_{\infty}
the results of the present
chapter generalize easily. However we do not have this good decay of
the eigenfunctions for the cases considered in Chapter

5
. Assuming ‘sufficient decay’, see for
example (
417
) and (
175
), the results of the
present chapter applies to some degree (sufficiently well for
Chapter
5
). In particular, to be
concrete, there is the following version of (parts of) Theorem
3.18

and Corollary
3.19
. Suppose

\displaystyle\ran\Pi_{H}\subset H^{2}_{t}\text{ for some }t\in(1,3/2),

(176)

and suppose
\lambda_{0}
is not an eigenvalue
of
\breve{H}_{\sigma}
and that
f\in L^{2}_{s}
for some
s\in(1/2,t-1/2)
. Then there
exist limits
\breve{R}_{\sigma}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)f,\breve{R}_{\sigma}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)f\in L^{2}_{(-1/2)^{-}}
. If in addition
\breve{R}_{\sigma}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)f=\breve{R}_{\sigma}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)f
, then

\displaystyle\begin{split}\breve{R}_{\sigma}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)f=\breve{R}_{\sigma}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)f\in L^{2}_{s-1}.\end{split}

(177)

These results follow by straightforward modifications of the previous proofs. Note that all terms
in the calculus involving the perturbation
-\sigma\Pi_{H}
are regarded
as ‘errors’, in fact we can use Lemma

3.10
and freely interchange
f({\breve{H}})
and
\check{f}({\breve{H}})
by
f({\breve{H}}_{\sigma})
and
\check{f}({\breve{H}}_{\sigma})
, respectively, and similarly for the Mourre estimate
Proposition
3.3
. To obtain (
177
) under the
given hypothesis and given limitations on
s
and

t
we need Lemma
3.15
for
\breve{R}_{\sigma}
, and
the interested reader may check that the errors from the indicated
substitutions are harmless when mimicking the proof of the lemma.

In Section
18
a version of
(
177
) is needed, but only for a
s
arbitrarily close
1/2
.

Remark 3.22
.

The case when (
54
) fails was discussed in Section

9
. One can then
obtain the same results as the ones of Remarks

3.20
and
3.21
(depending on whether
\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue
of
H^{\prime}
or not, respectively). Using Subsection
9.1
we can again invoke the formulas (
65b
) and
(
91b
) for
{\breve{H}}
(in the respectively cases) and in fact
argue as before, obtaining the results.

Chapter 4
Rellich type theorems

We investigate the structure of eigenfunctions and resonances
states at a two-cluster threshold under Condition
1.5

and (relevant part of) Condition
1.6
. This structure
depends strongly on decay properties of effective potentials. In
Section
13
we treat the lowest threshold case which
allows for somewhat stronger assertions than above this threshold. In
Section
14
we study the latter case which demands a more
careful examination of the ’eigentransform’ of Remark

2.3
. In Section
15
we study the
physical models from Sections
2

and
3
by applying previously discussed methods, in
particular we shall apply most of Section
14
.

We are going to treat the multiple two-cluster case in detail using
for the lowest threshold
Proposition
2.12
. The non-multiple two-cluster case can be treated
similarly, it is notationally simpler of course, and we do not pay
special attention to this case. The non-simple cases can be treated similarly. Only in
Section
15
we do a complete study covering all
cases, however for the
physical models only.

Under the conditions of Proposition
2.12
(used in Section

14
without
the lowest threshold condition) it is convenient to consider operators as quadratic
forms defined on the first order Sobolev spaces, cf. Remark

2.13
. For
k,s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, let
H^{k}_{s}

denote the weighted Sobolev space of order
k
with position-space
weight
\langle{x}\rangle^{s}
(as defined in Subsection
4.1
) and

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{H}}_{s,t}^{k,l}&=H^{k}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus H^{l}_{t}({\mathbf{X}}_{2}),\quad{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{s}={\mathcal{H}}^{k,k}_{s,s},\quad{\mathcal{H}}^{k}={\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{0},\quad{\mathcal{H}}_{s}={\mathcal{H}}^{0}_{s},\quad{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}^{0}_{0},\\
&{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{\infty}=\cap_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{s},\quad{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{-\infty}=\cup_{s\in{\mathbb{R}}}{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{s},\quad{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{s^{+}}=\cup_{t>s}{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{t},\quad{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{s^{-}}=\cap_{t<s}{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{t}.\end{split}

(178)

We may write the operator
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
of
Proposition
2.12
as

\displaystyle E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})=-(p_{1}^{2}+W_{1})\oplus(p_{2}^{2}+W_{2})-V.

(179)

This is for the lowest threshold only, and the operator
V
is a non-local
symmetric potential of order
{\mathcal{O}}(|x|^{-2-2\rho})
, see Proposition

2.12
and Remark
2.13
. A modification of
(
179
) for higher thresholds will be discussed in Section

14
, and the ’exceptional
cases’ of Sections
8
and

9
will be discussed in
Remarks
4.9
and

4.16
. We will shortly introduce a slightly different
decomposition (with corresponding different notation).

13
The case
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}

For
\rho<2
there are no results for the one-body problem in
general. Thus for example it is not known if zero can be an eigenvalue
of infinite multiplicity. However if either the potential is negative
or positive at infinity like
-|x|^{-\rho}
or
|x|^{-\rho}
,
respectively, then it is known that zero is at most an eigenvalue of
finite multiplicity (in fact it is not an eigenvalue in the negative
case). In this section we impose the conditions of Proposition

2.12
. In Subsections

13.2
and

13.3
we are going to use the
proposition under additional sign conditions
on the effective potentials
W_{1}
and
W_{2}
. In Subsection

13.4
the effective potentials are assumed to be homogeneous of degree
-2
to leading
order, which is a border line case with a rich structure. The case

\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
is divided into similar cases, to be treated in Section

14
. In both cases we need extensions of Remark

2.3
, to be treated in Subsections
13.1
and
14.1
, respectively.

13.1
Extended eigentransform for
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}

Recall from Remark
2.3
the
eigentransform and the
corresponding inversion formula:

\displaystyle f=T^{*}u\text{ and }u={E}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f=Sf-\breve{R}(\lambda_{0})\Pi^{\prime}HSf.

(180)

We can apply these relations to
L^{2}
-eigenfunctions as well as to generalized
eigenfunctions. In this extended sense the following result holds.

Lemma 4.1
.

For any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
the relations
(
180
) obey

\displaystyle u\in H^{1}_{s}(=H^{1}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}))\text{ and }(H-\lambda_{0})u=0\Leftrightarrow f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{s}\text{ and }{E}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0.

(181)

Proof.

Let
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
be given.
First we extend the boundedness result (
87
), now
claiming that

\displaystyle\breve{R}(z)\Pi^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{s},H^{2}_{s})\subset{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{s},H^{1}_{s})\quad(\text{in particular for }z=\lambda_{0}).

Noting that
\langle x\rangle^{s}(-\Delta-\breve{H})\langle x\rangle^{-s}

is
\epsilon
-bounded relatively to
-\Delta
the result follows by
a standard computation using (
87
).
By (
61
), Lemmas
2.6
,
2.9

2
and the polynomial decay of the threshold bound
states it then follows that

\displaystyle S^{*},T^{*}\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{1}_{s},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{s})\text{ and }\big(1-\breve{R}(\lambda_{0})\Pi^{\prime}H\big)S\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{s},H^{1}_{s}).

(182)

In particular (
181
) holds.
∎

The above proof is rather simple due to the fact that

\lambda_{0}\notin\sigma(H^{\prime})
. In Subsection
14.1
we derive a rather detailed version of the lemma when
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
. In this case the
eigentransform and the corresponding inversion formula need a more
subtle treatment. Moreover we remark that by ellipticity of the
equation on the left-hand side of (
181
) we can replace
the requirement
u\in H^{1}_{s}
by
u\in L^{2}_{s}
or
u\in H^{2}_{s}

without changing the content of this side of (
181
). A similar comment is
due for the right-hand side of (
181
), cf. the last part
of Remark
2.13
.

13.2
Negative slowly decaying effective
potentials

Suppose
\rho<2
and that
I_{j}(x_{j})=I_{j}(x)_{x^{j}=0}
,
j=1,2
, fulfill the
following condition, cf.
[
FS
]
:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists R\geq 0&\quad\exists\epsilon>0\quad\forall y\in{\mathbf{X}}_{j}\text{ with }|y|\geq R:\\
&I_{j}(y)\leq-\epsilon\langle y\rangle^{-\rho}\text{ and }-2I_{j}(y)-y\cdot\nabla I_{j}(y)\geq\epsilon\langle{y}\rangle^{-\rho}.\end{split}

(183)

We can for each
j
write the potential

W_{j}(x_{j})=\widehat{W}_{j}(x_{j})+B_{j}(x_{j})
, where
B_{j}
is polynomially
decreasing and
\widehat{W}_{j}
is smooth with

\partial^{\alpha}_{y}\widehat{W}_{j}={\mathcal{O}}(\langle{y}\rangle^{-\rho-|\alpha|})
and fulfills

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists\epsilon>0\quad\forall y\in{\mathbf{X}}_{j}:\\
&\widehat{W}_{j}(y)\leq-\epsilon\langle y\rangle^{-\rho}\text{ and }-2\widehat{W}_{j}(y)-y\cdot\nabla\widehat{W}_{j}(y)\geq\epsilon\langle{y}\rangle^{-\rho}.\end{split}

(184)

To see this we split each
V_{b}
in the definition of

I_{j}
as
V_{b}=V_{b}^{(1)}+V_{b}^{(2)}
, where the second term is
compactly supported. We note that

\langle V_{b}^{(2)}\rangle_{\varphi_{j}}(x_{j})=\langle\varphi_{j},V_{b}^{(2)}(\cdot+x_{j})\varphi_{j}\rangle_{j}

is polynomially decreasing. This can be proven in the same way as we
proved (
52
). It remains to consider the contribution
from
V_{b}^{(1)}
. Now the potential

A(x_{j})=\langle V_{b}^{(1)}\rangle_{\varphi_{j}}(x_{j})-V_{b}^{(1)}(x_{j})
is
C^{\infty}
. We note that
A(y)={\mathcal{O}}(\langle{y}\rangle^{-\rho-1})
follows
in the same way as we proved (
51a
). In a similar fashion one checks that
also
y\cdot\nabla A(y)={\mathcal{O}}(\langle{y}\rangle^{-\rho-1})
, and due to these properties we are lead
to consider
V_{b}^{(1)}(x_{j})
, or rather
I^{(1)}_{j}(x_{j})=\sum_{b}V_{b}^{(1)}(x_{j})
. Since
we know the property (
183
) for this sum we conclude a
similar property for
\sum_{b}\langle V_{b}^{(1)}\rangle_{\varphi_{j}}
, and we can add a suitable compactly supported potential
to have the bounds fulfilled for
R=0
(and some
\epsilon>0
), cf.
[
FS
]
. This leads to
a
\widehat{W}_{j}
fulfilling (
184
).

For convenience we use below the notation
w_{j}
for the potential
\widehat{W}_{j}
. Introducing the operators
h_{j}=p_{j}^{2}+w_{j}
,
j=1,2
we write
(
179
) as

\displaystyle-E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})=h_{1}\oplus h_{2}+v.

(185)

Note that
v-V
is polynomially
decreasing. The operators
h_{1}
and
h_{2}

have a number of microlocal properties as
stated in
[
FS
, Theorems 4.1 and 4.2]
. In particular there is a
limiting absorption principle at zero: Let
s_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}+\tfrac{\rho}{4}
. Then for any
s>s_{0}
there exist the norm-limits

\displaystyle r_{j}(0\pm\mathrm{i}0)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}r_{j}(\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{-1}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),H^{1}_{-s}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})),

where
r_{j}(z)=(h_{j}-z)^{-1}
. Moreover it is known from
[
Sk4
]
that

\displaystyle r_{j}(0\pm\mathrm{i}0)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}},{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}^{*}),

(186a)

stated in terms of the Besov spaces introduced in Subsection
4.1
. We are going to use yet another property.

Suppose
r_{j}(0+\mathrm{i}0)f=r_{j}(0-\mathrm{i}0)f
for a given
f\in L^{2}_{t}
for
some
t>s_{0}
, then

\displaystyle r_{j}(0+\mathrm{i}0)f=r_{j}(0-\mathrm{i}0)f\in L^{2}_{s}\text{ for any }s<t-2s_{0},

cf.
[
FS
, Theorems 4.1 (ii) and (iii)]
.
In fact one can strenghten the proof in
[
FS
]
and obtain the
following statement:

Suppose

r_{j}(0+\mathrm{i}0)f=r_{j}(0-\mathrm{i}0)f
for a given
f\in L^{2}_{t}
for
some
t>s_{0}
, then

\displaystyle r_{j}(0+\mathrm{i}0)f=r_{j}(0-\mathrm{i}0)f\in L^{2}_{t-2s_{0}}.

(186b)

This statement is similar to
Corollary
3.19
, and it can be proved by
improved versions of some resolvent bounds from
[
FS
]
which are
one-body threshold
analogues of the bounds of
Proposition
3.16
.

Theorem 4.2
.

Under the conditions of
Proposition
2.12
and (
183
) for
j=1,2
,

\displaystyle\dim\ker(H-\lambda_{0})<\infty.

If
\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0},0}^{*}({\mathbf{X}})
solves the distributional equation

(H-\lambda_{0})\phi=0
, then
\phi\in L_{\infty}^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
.

Proof.

I
.  By Remarks
2.3
and
2.13

\displaystyle\dim\ker(H-\lambda_{0})=\dim\ker E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}).

Note that we have chosen the form interpretation of
the appearing operators in agreement with Remark
2.13
, but
that the operator interpretation of Remark
2.3

amount to the same spaces, cf. Remark
2.13
. Let
R^{\pm}_{{\mathrm{diag}}}=r_{1}(0\pm\mathrm{i}0)\oplus(r_{2}(0\pm\mathrm{i}0)
and suppose
f\in\ker E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
.
We write the equations
R^{\pm}_{{\mathrm{diag}}}E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
as

\displaystyle(1+K^{\pm})f=0,\quad K^{\pm}=R^{\pm}_{{\mathrm{diag}}}v,

(187)

where
1f
arises by writing
f=(f_{1},f_{2})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
and using (to be shown below) that

\displaystyle r_{j}(0\pm\mathrm{i}0)h_{j}f_{j}=f_{j}.

(188)

We note that

\displaystyle K^{\pm}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})\text{
for }s\in(s_{0},2\rho+2-s_{0}).

(189)

Since
K^{\pm}
is compact (on any such space) it follows from
Fredholm theory that
\dim\ker E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})<\infty.

II
.  We prove the following more general version of
(
188
): Suppose
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0},0}^{*}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
and that
the distribution

h_{j}f_{j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
, then (
188
) holds and
the function
f_{j}\in H^{1}_{-s}
for any
s>s_{0}
(for
the problem above
h_{j}f_{j}\in L^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})\subset{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})

for any
s
as in
(
189
)). To see this we let
\chi_{R}(x)=\chi(|x|/R)
,
R\geq 1
, be
given in
agreement with (
4.1
). We consider
\chi_{R}
as a multiplication operator on

{\mathbf{X}}_{j}
. Now

r_{j}(0\pm\mathrm{i}0)h_{j}\chi_{R}f_{j}=\chi_{R}f_{j}
for all
R>1
. On the other
hand

\displaystyle h_{j}\chi_{R}f_{j}=\chi_{R}h_{j}f_{j}-(\Delta\chi_{R})f_{j}-2(\nabla\chi_{R})\cdot\nabla f_{j}.

For the last term

\displaystyle\begin{split}&R^{2}\|(\nabla\chi_{R})\cdot\nabla f_{j}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})}\\
&\leq C_{1}\langle p^{2}\rangle_{\chi_{2R}\bar{\chi}_{R/2}f_{j}}\\
&\leq C_{2}\big(\operatorname{Re}\langle\chi^{2}_{2R}\bar{\chi}^{2}_{R/2}f_{j},h_{j}f_{j}\rangle+R^{-\rho}\|\chi_{4R}f_{j}\|^{2}\big)\\
&=C_{2}\big({\mathcal{O}}(R^{0})+R^{-\rho}o(R^{2s_{0}})\big)\\
&=o(R^{1-\rho/2}),\end{split}

(190)

yielding

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\,R^{s_{0}}\|(\nabla\chi_{R})\cdot\nabla f_{j}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})}=0.

For the middle term

\displaystyle R^{s_{0}}\|(\Delta\chi_{R})f_{j}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})}\leq CR^{2s_{0}-2}\big(R^{-s_{0}}\|\chi_{2R}f_{j}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})}\big),

yielding

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\,R^{s_{0}}\|(\Delta\chi_{R})f_{j}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})}=0.

These computations lead to

\displaystyle f_{j}=\wslim_{R\to\infty}\,\chi_{R}f_{j}=\wslim_{R\to\infty}\,r_{j}(0\pm\mathrm{i}0)h_{j}\chi_{R}f_{j}=r_{j}(0\pm\mathrm{i}0)h_{j}f_{j}\text{ in }{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),

and therefore (
188
) holds for
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0},0}^{*}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
obeying

h_{j}f_{j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
.

III
.  Using again the ’eigentransform’ of Remark

2.3
it is readily seen that any
\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0},0}^{*}({\mathbf{X}})
solving the
distributional equation
(H-\lambda_{0})\phi=0
corresponds to a
(distributional) solution
f=(f_{1},f_{2})
,
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0

with
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0},0}^{*}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
obeying

h_{j}f_{j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
, cf. Lemma
4.1
. By the previous steps we then conclude
that (
187
) is fulfilled. In particular

K^{+}f=K^{-}f
. Conversely using that
v
is symmetric
we
obtain that

\displaystyle 0=\operatorname{Im}\langle{f,vf}\rangle=-\operatorname{Im}\langle{R^{+}_{{\mathrm{diag}}}vf,vf}\rangle

(191)

for any solution to
(1+K^{+})f=0
, where
f=(f_{1},f_{2})

obeys
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}^{*}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
,
j=1,2
. Whence also
(1+K^{-})f=0
for any such

f
. (Similarly a solution to
(1+K^{-})f=0
is also a solution to

(I+K^{+})f=0
.) Moreover
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
in the
distributional sense.
If
(1+K^{+})f=0
and
(1+K^{-})f=0
where
f=(f_{1},f_{2})

and
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}^{*}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
better decay is obtained by invoking
(
186b
). Explicitly we may fix
s
as in (
189
)
and starting with the input
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{0}_{-s}

conclude that
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{0}_{-s+2\kappa}
, where

\kappa=\rho+1-s_{0}
. By iterating this argument we conclude that
f_{j}\in L_{\infty}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
. Next, by the ‘inversion formula’ of
Remark
2.3
any such

f
corresponds to a

\displaystyle\phi\in\ker(H-\lambda_{0})\cap L_{\infty}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}),

see Lemma
4.1
. In particular, cf. the remark at the beginning of the paragraph, this is valid for any
\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0},0}^{*}({\mathbf{X}})
solving

(H-\lambda_{0})\phi=0
.

∎

13.3
Positive slowly decaying effective
potentials

Suppose
\rho<2
and
that
I_{j}(x_{j})=I_{j}(x)|_{x^{j}=0}
,
j=1,2
, fulfill the following condition:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists R\geq 0\quad\exists\epsilon>0\quad\exists\bar{\rho}\in[\rho,\tfrac{2}{3}(1+\rho))&\quad\forall y\in{\mathbf{X}}_{j}\text{ with }|y|\geq R:\\
&I_{j}(y)\geq\epsilon\langle y\rangle^{-\bar{\rho}}.\end{split}

(192)

We write for each
j
the potential

W_{j}(x_{j})=\widehat{W}_{j}(x_{j})+B_{j}(x_{j})
, where
B_{j}
is polynomially
decreasing and
\widehat{W}_{j}
is smooth with

\partial^{\alpha}_{y}\widehat{W}_{j}={\mathcal{O}}(\langle{y}\rangle^{-\rho-|\alpha|})
and fulfills

\exists\epsilon>0\quad\exists\bar{\rho}\in[\rho,\tfrac{2}{3}(1+\rho))\quad\forall y\in{\mathbf{X}}_{j}:\quad\widehat{W}_{j}(y)\geq\epsilon\langle y\rangle^{-\bar{\rho}}.

(193)

This is seen by arguments as in Subsection

13.2
(using that
\bar{\rho}<1+\rho
). Again
we shall use
the notation
w_{j}
for the potential
\widehat{W}_{j}
, introduce the operators
h_{j}=p_{j}^{2}+w_{j}
,
j=1,2
and write
(
179
) as (
185
).

We introduce the symbols
s_{j}=s_{j}(y,\xi)=(\xi_{j}^{2}+w_{j}(y))^{-1}
. Note
that
s_{j}
belongs to the Hörmander class

S\big(\langle y\rangle^{\bar{\rho}}\langle\xi\rangle^{-2},g\big)
,
where the metric is given by

\displaystyle g(v)=\langle y\rangle^{2(\bar{\rho}-\rho-1)}\,v_{y}^{2}+\langle y\rangle^{\bar{\rho}}\,v_{\xi}^{2},\quad v=(v_{y},v_{\xi}),

and note that the corresponding Weyl calculus has ‘Planck
constant’ of size

\displaystyle\langle y\rangle^{(\bar{\rho}-\rho-1)}\langle y\rangle^{\bar{\rho}/2}=\langle y\rangle^{-t};\,t=1+\rho-3\bar{\rho}/2.

Next we may construct a parametrix to infinite order for

h_{j}={\operatorname{Op\!^{w}\!}}(\xi^{2}_{j}+w_{j}(y))
following a standard
procedure. However for the theorem below we only need the first step which amounts to letting
the parametrix be given by
r^{0}_{j}={\operatorname{Op\!^{w}\!}}(s_{j})
. We compute
r^{0}_{j}h_{j}=1+A_{j}
for
some
A_{j}={\operatorname{Op\!^{w}\!}}(a_{j})
with
a_{j}\in S(\langle y\rangle^{-t}\langle\xi\rangle^{-1},g)
.

Theorem 4.3
.

Under the conditions of
Proposition
2.12
and (
192
) for
j=1,2
,

\displaystyle\dim\ker(H-\lambda_{0})<\infty.

If
\phi\in L_{-\infty}^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
(or alternatively
\phi\in H^{1}_{-\infty}({\mathbf{X}})
) solves the distributional equation

(H-\lambda_{0})\phi=0
, then
\phi\in L_{\infty}^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
.

Proof.

I
.  By Remark
2.3

\displaystyle\dim\ker(H-\lambda_{0})=\dim\ker E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}).

Suppose
(H-\lambda_{0})\phi=0
for
\phi\in H^{1}_{s}({\mathbf{X}})
for some
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
(possibly nonzero). Due to Lemma

4.1
the eigentransform
f=T^{*}\phi\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{s}
and
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
.
Let
R=r^{0}_{1}\oplus r^{0}_{2}
and
A=A_{1}\oplus A_{2}
. Then we write the equation
RE_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
for
any
f\in L^{2}_{s}
as

\displaystyle(1+A+K)f=0,\quad K=Rv,

(194)

where we use (
185
) and that for the components
of
f=(f_{1},f_{2})

\displaystyle r^{0}_{j}(0)h_{j}f_{j}=(1+A_{j})f_{j}.

We note that
A+K\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{s})
.
It then follows from Fredholm theory that

f
belongs to a finite-dimensional subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{s}
. This
dimension is an upper bound of the dimension of the
set of
functions
\phi\in H^{1}_{s}({\mathbf{X}})
solving
(H-\lambda_{0})\phi=0
(seen by the inversion
formula). In particular the
latter space is finite-dimensional. The first statement of the theorem follows by this
argument for
s=0
.

II
.  By an iteration procedure using (
194
),
cf. the proof of Theorem
4.2
, it follows
that any (zero-energy) generalized eigenfunction
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-\infty}

must belong to
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\infty}
. Note that we can deduce that

f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{s+t}
given that
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{s+(k-1)t}
, where

t=1+\rho-3\bar{\rho}/2
. Since there is no limit on
k(\in{\mathbb{N}})
used
for this argument, indeed this conclusion comes out by
iteration. Consequently, thanks to Lemma
4.1
, the
generalized eigenfunctions of
H
at
\lambda_{0}

in
H^{1}_{-\infty}({\mathbf{X}})
are all in
L^{2}_{\infty}({\mathbf{X}})
.
∎

The equation (
194
) might have ‘spurious’
solutions, i.e. solutions not corresponding to eigenfunctions of

H
. We can cure this ‘deficiency’ by showing that the exact inverse

h_{j}^{-1}
,
j=1,2
, in fact is a pseudodifferential operator. We do this
below. In particular
h_{j}^{-1}
has nice commutation proporties
with power-type weights in the configuration space. Such properties
were proved by Yafaev
[
Ya2
]
, and we are in fact going to use

[
Ya2
]
. More precisely we are going to use the assertion

\displaystyle\forall a,b\geq 0,a-b\geq\bar{\rho}:\quad B=\slim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\,\langle y\rangle^{-a}(h_{j}+\epsilon)^{-1}\langle y\rangle^{b}\text{ exists}.

(195)

Here we note that

B_{\epsilon}=\langle y\rangle^{-a}(h_{j}+\epsilon)^{-1}\langle y\rangle^{b}
is bounded uniformly in
small positive

\epsilon
, which follows by keeping track of constants in the
proof of
[
Ya2
, Theorem 1]
. Since
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}B_{\epsilon}u
exists if

u=\langle y\rangle^{-b}h_{j}v
with
v\in{\mathcal{D}}(h_{j})
and the set of such
u
’s
is dense in

L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
, indeed (
195
) follows (in
fact
Bu=\langle y\rangle^{-a}h_{j}^{-1}\langle y\rangle^{b}u
for any
u
of this form).

Lemma 4.4
.

The operators

\slim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\,\langle y\rangle^{-\bar{\rho}}(h_{j}+\epsilon)^{-1}
,
j=1,2
, (extending
\langle y\rangle^{-\bar{\rho}}h_{j}^{-1}
) are pseudodifferential
operators with symbol
in
S\big(\langle\xi\rangle^{-2},g\big)
.

Proof.

Recall
r^{0}_{j}h_{j}=1+A_{j}
, where
A_{j}
has symbol
a_{j}\in S(\langle y\rangle^{-t}\langle\xi\rangle^{-1},g)
.
If
-1\notin\sigma(A_{j})

\displaystyle(h_{j}+\epsilon)^{-1}=(1+A_{j})^{-1}r^{0}_{j}-\epsilon(1+A_{j})^{-1}r^{0}_{j}(h_{j}+\epsilon)^{-1},

yielding
\slim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\,\langle y\rangle^{-\bar{\rho}}(h_{j}+\epsilon)^{-1}=\langle y\rangle^{-\bar{\rho}}(1+A_{j})^{-1}r^{0}_{j}
.
(Whence formally

h_{j}^{-1}=(1+A_{j})^{-1}r^{0}_{j}
.) Using the Neumann series to
expand
(1+A_{j})^{-1}
and Beal’s criterion,
cf.
[
FS
, Subsection 4.2]
,
we conclude that indeed
\langle y\rangle^{-\bar{\rho}}(1+A_{j})^{-1}r^{0}_{j}
has symbol in

S\big(\langle\xi\rangle^{-2},g\big)
completing the proof in this case.

To treat the general case we let
\chi_{1}(r)=\chi(r<1)
and
\chi_{2}(r)=\chi(r>1)
form a quadratic
partition of unity of smooth non-negative functions on
{\mathbb{R}}
,

\chi_{1}(r)^{2}+\chi_{2}(r)^{2}=1
, such that
\chi_{1}
is supported
in
(-\infty,2)
and
\chi_{1}(r)=1
for

r<1
. We introduce for
l>1
the functions

\chi_{i,l}(r):=\chi_{i}(r/l)
;
i=1,2
. Let

\displaystyle R_{l}=\oplus_{j=1}^{2}\big(\chi_{1,l}(r_{j})h_{j}^{-1}\chi_{1,l}(r_{j})+\chi_{2,l}(r_{j})r^{0}_{j}\chi_{2,l}(r_{j})\big)=\oplus_{j=1}^{2}r_{j,l}.

Here
\chi_{1,l}(r_{j})h_{j}^{-1}\chi_{1,l}(r_{j}):=\slim_{\epsilon\searrow 0}\,\chi_{1,l}(r_{j})(h_{j}+\epsilon)^{-1}\chi_{1,l}(r_{j})
. Using
(
195
) and Beal’s criterion we deduce that

\chi_{1,l}(r_{j})h_{j}^{-1}\chi_{1,l}(r_{j})
and therefore also
r_{j,l}
are pseudodifferential operators with symbol
in
S\big(\langle y\rangle^{\bar{\rho}}\langle\xi\rangle^{-2},g\big)
. Next we write

r_{j,l}h_{j}=1+A_{j,l}
and note (as above) that

A_{j,l}
has symbol
a_{j,l}\in S(\langle y\rangle^{-t}\langle\xi\rangle^{-1},g)
.
We also observe that

\displaystyle\lVert\chi_{2,l}(r_{j})A_{j}\chi_{2,l}(r_{j})\rVert\to 0\text{ for }l\to\infty.

Using this and a little computation it follows that

\displaystyle\lVert A_{j,l}\rVert\to 0\text{ for }l\to\infty.

In particular it
follows that
-1\notin\sigma(A_{j,l})
for
l
taken big
enough. Consequently for any such big number

\langle y\rangle^{-\bar{\rho}}h_{j}^{-1}=\langle y\rangle^{-\bar{\rho}}(1+A_{j,l})^{-1}r_{j,l}
has symbol
in
S\big(\langle\xi\rangle^{-2},g\big)
.
∎

We note that Lemma
4.4
can be used to
replace (
194
) by the cleaner assertion

\displaystyle(1+\widetilde{K})f=0;\quad\widetilde{K}=\widetilde{R}v,\quad\widetilde{R}=h^{-1}_{1}\oplus h^{-1}_{1},

(196)

of course given an appropriate interpretation.

13.4
Homogeneous degree
-2
effective
potentials

Suppose

\rho\geq 1/2
, the conditions of
Proposition
2.12
and that
W_{j}=W_{j}(x_{j})
,
j=1,2
, fulfill the
following condition (recall that in general
W_{j}
is bounded outside
a bounded set):

For
j=1,2
there exists a real continuous
function
q_{j}=q_{j}(y)
on the unit sphere
{\mathbb{S}}_{j}
of
{\mathbf{X}}_{j}
and a
bounded potential
B_{j}(x_{j})={\mathcal{O}}(\langle{x_{j}}\rangle^{-3})
on
{\mathbf{X}}_{j}
such that

\exists R>0\quad\forall y=r\theta\in X_{j}\text{ with }|y|=r\geq R:W_{j}(y)=\tfrac{q_{j}(\theta)}{r^{2}}+B_{j}(y).

(197)

This condition may arise by a Taylor expansion of
I_{j}(x)
,
j=1,2
,
as follows: Decompose

\displaystyle\sum_{b\not\subset a_{j}}V_{b}^{(1)}(x^{b})=\sum_{b\not\subset a_{j}}V_{b}^{(1)}((x_{j})^{b})+\sum_{b\not\subset a_{j}}(x^{j})^{b}\cdot\nabla V_{b}^{(1)}((x_{j})^{b})+{\mathcal{O}}(\langle{x_{j}}\rangle^{-\rho-2}).

Assume now that the first term vanishes
identically and that the last term contributes to
W_{j}
by a term
of order
{\mathcal{O}}(\langle{x_{j}}\rangle^{-3})
(valid for

\rho\geq 1
of course). The middle term contributes to
W_{j}
by

\sum_{b\not\subset a_{j}}k^{b}_{j}\cdot\nabla V_{b}^{(1)}((x_{j})^{b})
, where

k_{j}=\langle\varphi_{j},x^{j}\varphi_{j}\rangle_{j}
, so the content of
(
197
) would in this case be the condition

\displaystyle\sum_{b\not\subset a_{j}}k^{b}_{j}\cdot\nabla V_{b}^{(1)}((x_{j})^{b})=\tfrac{q_{j}(\theta)}{r^{2}}+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-3}).

This has relevance with nonzero
q_{j}
’s in a certain
case for systems with Coulomb interactions, see
Subsections
2.1
and
3.1
. The case
q_{j}=0
,
j=1,2
is of course included in
(
197
), and the relevance of this case for the physics
models is also explained there.

For simplicity of presentation let us in the following assume

R=1
. For
n_{j}=\dim{\mathbf{X}}_{j}\geq 2
the spectrum of the (minus)
Laplace-Beltrami operator
-\Delta_{\theta_{j}}
on the unit sphere

{{{\mathbb{S}}_{j}}}\subset{\mathbf{X}}_{j}
is known to be
\{l(l+n_{j}-2)\mid l\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}\}
. For
our problem it is relevant to study the spectrum of

-\Delta_{\theta_{j}}+q_{j}
. For
n_{j}=1
we define

-\Delta_{\theta_{j}}=0
, so in this case the spectrum of

-\Delta_{\theta_{j}}+q_{j}
is
\{q_{j}(-1),q_{j}(1)\}
. In any dimension
it is convenient to use the following parametrization: Write each

\mu\in\sigma(-\Delta_{\theta_{j}}+q_{j})
as

\displaystyle\mu=\nu^{2}-\tfrac{(n_{j}-2)^{2}}{4},

(198)

where by convention
\nu\geq 0
if
\mu\geq-\tfrac{(n_{j}-2)^{2}}{4}
, and
\mathrm{i}\nu>0
if
\mu<-\tfrac{(n_{j}-2)^{2}}{4}
. Then the collection of such numbers
\nu
is denoted by

\sigma_{j}
. Let
P_{j,\nu}
,
\nu\in\sigma_{j}
, denote
the orthogonal projection onto the corresponding eigenspace. For
convenience we omit in the following
the subscript
j
. Letting
\zeta
denote a corresponding eigenvector, the
eigenvalue problem

\displaystyle(-\Delta+\tfrac{q_{j}(\theta)}{r^{2}})\big(r^{\tfrac{1-n_{j}}{2}}u(r)\big)\otimes\zeta(\theta)=0

reduces to the Euler equation

\displaystyle-u^{\prime\prime}(r)+\tfrac{\nu^{2}-1/4}{r^{2}}u(r)=0.

(199)

Consider now for any
\nu\geq 0
or
\mathrm{i}\nu>0
the Dirichlet
problem

\displaystyle u\text{ fulfills }\eqref{eq:ode}\text{ for }r\geq 1,\text{ and }u(1)=0.

(200)

The
regular solution
is

\displaystyle\phi_{\nu}(r)=\begin{cases}\tfrac{r^{1/2+\nu}-r^{1/2-\nu}}{2\nu}&\text{ for }\nu\neq 0,\\
r^{1/2}\ln r&\text{ for }\nu=0\end{cases};

note that indeed
\phi_{\nu}(1)=0
. The
outgoing
solution
to (
199
) (also defined for
r\geq 1
) is

\displaystyle\psi_{\nu}(r)=r^{1/2-\nu};

note that indeed this is ‘outgoing’ for
\nu
complex. These two solutions form a fundamental system, and we can define a Green’s function by

\displaystyle R_{\nu}(r,r^{\prime})=\phi_{\nu}(r_{<})\psi_{\nu}(r_{>});\quad r_{<}=\min\{r,r^{\prime}\},\,r_{>}=\max\{r,r^{\prime}\}.

Its formal adjoint
R^{*}_{\nu}(r,r^{\prime})=\phi_{\nu}(r_{<})\overline{\psi_{\nu}(r_{>})}

is also a Green’s function.
This means that for all sufficiently decaying functions
v=v(r)

\displaystyle-u^{\prime\prime}(r)+\tfrac{\nu^{2}-1/4}{r^{2}}u(r)=v(r),\text{ where }u(r)=\int_{1}^{\infty}R_{\nu}(r,r^{\prime})v(r^{\prime})\,{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime},

and similarly for
R^{*}_{\nu}
.

Let
\chi_{1}(r)=\chi(r<8)
and
\chi_{2}(r)=\chi(r>8)
form a quadratic
partition of unity of smooth non-negative functions on
{\mathbb{R}}
,

\chi_{1}(r)^{2}+\chi_{2}(r)^{2}=1
, such that
\chi_{1}
is supported
in
(-\infty,8)
and
\chi_{1}(r)=1
for

r<4
.

Let
w_{j}=W_{j}
,

h_{j}=p_{j}^{2}+w_{j}
and
v=V
(this notation conforms with the previous
subsections). We easily check that

v\in{\mathcal{L}}\big({\mathcal{H}}^{0}_{s},{\mathcal{H}}^{0}_{3+s}\big)\text{ for }s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, cf. (
89c
).
Let

\displaystyle\begin{split}&G_{+}=\oplus_{j=1}^{2}\Big(\chi_{1}(r_{j})(h_{j}-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\chi_{1}(r_{j})+\sum_{\nu\in\sigma_{j}}\big(\chi_{2}(r_{j})r_{j}^{\tfrac{1-n_{j}}{2}}R_{j,\nu}r_{j}^{\tfrac{n_{j}-1}{2}}\chi_{2}(r_{j})\big)\otimes P_{j,\nu}\Big),\\
&G^{*}_{+}=\oplus_{j=1}^{2}\Big(\chi_{1}(r_{j})(h_{j}+\mathrm{i})^{-1}\chi_{1}(r_{j})+\sum_{\nu\in\sigma_{j}}\big(\chi_{2}(r_{j})r^{\tfrac{1-n_{j}}{2}}R^{*}_{j,\nu}r^{\tfrac{n_{j}-1}{2}}\chi_{2}(r_{j})\big)\otimes P_{j,\nu}\Big),\end{split}

(201)

where
r_{j}=|x_{j}|
and
R_{j,\nu}
has a formal interpretation as an
(unbounded) operator on
L_{I}^{2}:=L^{2}(I;{\mathrm{d}}r)
,
I:=[1,\infty)
. Phrased
differently the powers of
r
are isometrically identifying the
spaces
L_{I}^{2}

and
L^{2}(I;r^{n_{j}-1}{\mathrm{d}}r)
; note that the latter
space appears naturally for tensor decompositions in spherical
coordinates. The operators
G_{+}
and
G^{*}_{+}
are parametrices for

-E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
in the sense of the following lemma. Of
course these operators are not the only options for a parametrix
construction. Under stronger conditions, we consider in fact a simpler
parametrix in Section
16.2
.

Introduce also the numbers

\displaystyle\nu_{0}=\min_{j\in\{1,2\}}\min_{\nu\in\sigma_{j}}\operatorname{Re}\nu,\quad\quad s_{0}=1+\nu_{0}.

(202)

In the fastly decaying case,
q_{j}=0
for
j=1,2
, these numbers are
explicit dimensional depending constants, cf. (
244
). For
example
\nu_{0}=\tfrac{1}{2}
for
n_{1}=n_{2}=3
in this case (to be
considered in Section
16.2
).

Lemma 4.5
.

1.

The operators

\displaystyle G_{+},G^{*}_{+}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{s^{\prime}},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})\text{ for }s,s^{\prime}>1-\nu_{0}\text{ with }s+s^{\prime}>2,

(203)

\displaystyle K^{*}_{+}:=-E^{*}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})G^{*}_{+}-1\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{s})\text{ for
any }s\in(1-\nu_{0},2+\nu_{0});

(204)

here
E^{*}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})=E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
is given the
distributional meaning, and we define

K^{+}=(K^{*}_{+})^{*}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})
. Explicitly

\displaystyle\begin{split}K^{+}&=G_{+}v+\oplus_{j=1}^{2}\Big(\chi_{1}(h_{j}-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\big(\mathrm{i}\chi_{1}+[\chi_{1},p_{j}^{2}]\big)\\
&+\sum_{\nu\in\sigma_{j}}\Big(\big(\chi_{2}r^{\tfrac{1-n_{j}}{2}}R_{j,\nu}r^{\tfrac{n_{j}-1}{2}}\chi_{2}\big)\otimes P_{j,\nu}\Big)B_{j}\\
&+\sum_{\nu\in\sigma_{j}}\big(\chi_{2}r^{\tfrac{1-n_{j}}{2}}R_{j,\nu}r^{\tfrac{n_{j}-1}{2}}[\chi_{2},p_{j}^{2}]\big)\otimes P_{j,\nu}\Big).\end{split}

(205)

2.

K^{+}f=-G_{+}E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f-f
for every

f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s_{0}}
such that

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{s}
for some
s>1-\nu_{0}
.

3.

For any
s>1
the operator

G_{+}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{s},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})
is injective, and

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}G_{+}=\tfrac{G_{+}-G^{*}_{+}}{2\mathrm{i}}\geq\oplus_{j=1}^{2}\big(\chi_{1}(h_{j}+\mathrm{i})^{-1}(h_{j}-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\big)\chi_{1}\geq 0.

(206)

Proof.

I
.  Let
s,s^{\prime}
be given as in (
203
). We show that

r^{-s}R_{j,\nu}r^{-s^{\prime}}
is bounded on
L_{I}^{2}
with a bound
independent of
\nu\in\sigma_{j}
. The Hilbert-Schmidt criterion
works. We note the bounds (here for
\nu\neq 0
only; the case

\nu=0
is simpler)

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}r|\nu|^{-2}r^{1+2\operatorname{Re}\nu-2s}\int_{r}^{\infty}r^{\prime\,(1-2\operatorname{Re}\nu-2s^{\prime})}{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime}\leq C|\nu|^{-2}(s^{\prime}+\operatorname{Re}\nu-1)^{-1},

\displaystyle\int_{1}^{\infty}{\mathrm{d}}r|\nu|^{-2}r^{1-2\operatorname{Re}\nu-2s}\int_{1}^{r}r^{\prime\,(1+2\operatorname{Re}\nu-2s^{\prime})}{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime}

\displaystyle\leq C|\nu|^{-2}\begin{cases}(s+\operatorname{Re}\nu-1)^{-1}(s^{\prime}-\operatorname{Re}\nu-1)^{-1}\quad\text{
for }s^{\prime}>\operatorname{Re}\nu+1,\\
(\operatorname{Re}\nu+1-s^{\prime})^{-1}\quad\text{
for }s^{\prime}<\operatorname{Re}\nu+1,\\
\int_{1}^{\infty}r^{1-2\operatorname{Re}\nu-2s}\ln r\,{\mathrm{d}}r\quad\text{ for }s^{\prime}=\operatorname{Re}\nu+1,\end{cases}.

This gives in particular the operator bound
{\mathcal{O}}(|\nu|^{-3/2})
for

\operatorname{Re}\nu\to\infty
. Similarly
\partial_{r}r^{1-s}R_{j,\nu}r^{-s^{\prime}}

is bounded with the bound
{\mathcal{O}}(|\nu|^{-1/2})
. This leads to

\displaystyle\big(-\Delta_{j}+\tfrac{q_{j}(\theta)}{r^{2}}\big)\Big(\big(\chi_{2}r^{\tfrac{1-n_{j}}{2}}R_{j,\nu}r^{\tfrac{n_{j}-1}{2}}\chi_{2}\big)\otimes P_{j,\nu}\Big)

\displaystyle=\big([p_{j}^{2},\chi_{2}]r^{\tfrac{1-n_{j}}{2}}R_{j,\nu}r^{\tfrac{n_{j}-1}{2}}\chi_{2}\big)\otimes P_{j,\nu}+\chi_{2}^{2}\otimes P_{j,\nu}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{s^{\prime}},L^{2}_{-s}),

where the first term is bounded by

{\mathcal{O}}(|\nu|^{-1/2})
. By using the
ellipticity of

-\Delta_{j}+\tfrac{q_{j}(\theta)}{r^{2}}
and interpolation we then
obtain that

\displaystyle\big(\chi_{2}r^{\tfrac{1-n_{j}}{2}}R_{j,\nu}r^{\tfrac{n_{j}-1}{2}}\chi_{2}\big)\otimes P_{j,\nu}\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{-1}_{s^{\prime}},H^{1}_{-s})

with the bound
{\mathcal{O}}(|\nu|^{0})
.

Since the infinite sum defining
G_{+}
is an orthogonal direct sum
decomposition (and

\chi_{1}(h_{j}-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\chi_{1}\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{-1}_{s^{\prime}},H^{1}_{-s})
, obviously) we conclude (
203
).

II
.  The compactness assertion (
204
) follows by
first computing
K^{*}_{+}
, estimating as in Step
I
(for a good choice
of parameters) and then invoking compactness on ‘each

\nu
-sector’ (obtained by the Hilbert-Schmidt criterion) and the
{\mathcal{O}}(|\nu|^{-1/2})
bound (also
from I). The requirement
s\in(1-\nu_{0},2+\nu_{0})
is dictated by
the Hilbert-Schmidt criterion. More precisely fixing any such

s
we need
to find
s^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that, most importantly,

r^{-s^{\prime}}R^{*}_{j,\nu}r^{-s}
and
r^{s-3}R^{*}_{j,\nu}r^{-s}
are
bounded on
L_{I}^{2}
. Due to (
203
) it suffices to have

\displaystyle s^{\prime}>1-\nu_{0},\,s+s^{\prime}>2\text{ and }s-3+s^{\prime}\leq 0.

These requirements are fulfilled with
s^{\prime}=3-s
, and we
can
use that
\lVert r^{s-3}R^{*}_{j,\nu}r^{-s}\rVert\to 0
for
\nu\to\infty
. We
have shown
1
.

III
.  We show
2
by mimicking the proof of
Theorem
4.2
. For any given

f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s_{0}}
with
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{s}
for
some
s>1-\nu_{0}
we apply
G_{+}
to
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f
and do
an integration by parts. More precisely we compute as follows for
any
g\in{\mathcal{H}}^{0}_{\infty}
using the smooth cut-off argument
of Step
II
of the proof of Theorem
4.2

(the integration by parts) in the third step below:

\displaystyle\langle g,G_{+}E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle G^{*}_{+}g,E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f\rangle=\lim_{R\to\infty}\langle G^{*}_{+}g,\chi_{R}E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle-K^{*}_{+}g-g,f\rangle=\langle g,-K^{+}f-f\rangle,

yielding
2
by a density argument. This
argument uses conveniently that
vf\in{\mathcal{H}}_{(3-s_{0})^{-}}^{-1}
,
which in turn follows from (
87
).

IV
.  For the assertion (
206
) it suffices to show
that
\operatorname{Im}R_{j,\nu}\geq 0
. For
\nu\geq 0
the kernel of

R_{j,\nu}
is real and symmetric, whence
\operatorname{Im}R_{j,\nu}=0
in
that case. If
\nu=-\mathrm{i}\sigma
where
\sigma>0
the kernel is

\displaystyle\big(\operatorname{Im}R_{j,\nu}\big)(r,r^{\prime})=\sigma\phi_{\nu}(r_{<})\phi_{\nu}(r_{>}),

and since
\phi_{\nu}
is real, it then follows that
\operatorname{Im}R_{j,\nu}\geq 0
also in that case.

If
G_{+}f=0
,
f=(f_{1},f_{2})
, then (
206
) yields

\chi_{1}f=0
. Whence

\displaystyle\Big(\chi_{2}r^{\tfrac{1-n_{j}}{2}}R_{j,\nu}r^{\tfrac{n_{j}-1}{2}}\Big)\otimes P_{j,\nu}\chi_{2}f_{j}=0\text{ for all }\nu\in\sigma_{j}.

Since
R_{j,\nu}(r,r^{\prime})
is a Green’s function it follows
from these formulas that also
\chi_{2}f=0
. Therefore in turn
f=0
, and

3
is shown.
∎

Remark
.

The space
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s_{0}}
in
2
is not
optimal for the assertion. It suffices to require that
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0},0}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
,
j=1,2
. On the other hand if one uses a
slightly smaller space than below in the case
s_{0}=1

to define
the notion of a resonance, viz.
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(-1)^{+}}=\cup_{s>-1}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{s}

rather than
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-1}
, one avoids the special treatment
of the case
s_{0}=1
in
Theorem
4.7

3
and
4
.

Definition 4.6
.

1.

\lambda_{0}
is called a resonance of
H
if
the equation
(H-\lambda_{0})u=0
admits a solution

u\in H^{1}_{-s_{0}}\setminus H^{1}
. Such solution is a resonance
state of
H
. The
multiplicity of the resonance
\lambda_{0}
is defined as the
dimension, say denoted by
n_{\mathrm{res}}
, of the quotient space

\displaystyle\ker(H-\lambda_{0})_{|H^{1}_{-s_{0}}}/\ker(H-\lambda_{0})_{|H^{1}}.

2.

0
is called a resonance of

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
if the equation
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0

has a solution
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s_{0}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
. Such solution is a resonance
state of
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
. The multiplicity of the
resonance
0
is defined as the dimension of the quotient space

\displaystyle\ker E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})_{|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s_{0}}}/\ker E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})_{|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}.

It follows from Lemma
4.1
(applied with
s=s_{0}
and
s=0
)
that
\lambda_{0}
is a resonance
(resp., an eigenvalue) of
H
if and only if
0
is a resonance
(resp., an eigenvalue) of
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
and their
multiplicities are the same.

We introduce for
j=1,2
,

\sigma_{j,k}=\sigma_{j}\setminus(k,\infty)\text{ and }\sigma^{+}_{j,k}=\sigma_{j}\cap(0,k];\quad k\in{\mathbb{N}}.

We take an orthonormal basis in
\ran P_{j,\nu}
for each

\nu\in\sigma_{j}
, say

\zeta_{j,\nu}^{(1)},\dots,\zeta_{j,\nu}^{(n_{j,\nu})}
. The set
of resonances is partially determined by the set
\sigma^{+}_{j,1}
, as
the following result shows.

Theorem 4.7
.

Suppose the conditions of
Proposition
2.12
and (
197
) for
j=1,2
.

1.

The dimension of the space of vectors

u\in H^{1}_{-s_{0}}
solving
(H-\lambda_{0})u=0
is finite.

If a vector
u\in H^{1}_{-s_{0}}
obeys
(H-\lambda_{0})u=0

then
f=T^{*}u\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s_{0}}
and
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
,
and conversely, if
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s_{0}}
obeys
