E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
then

u={E}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f\in H^{1}_{-s_{0}}
and

(H-\lambda_{0})u=0
.

2.

A vector
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s_{0}}
obeys

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
if and only if
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(\nu_{0}-1)^{-}}
and
f\in\ker(1+K^{+})
; here

K^{+}
is given by (
205
) (as an operator on

{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
for any
s\in(1-\nu_{0},2+\nu_{0})
).

3.

Suppose
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s_{0}}
and

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
. In the case
s_{0}=1
suppose in addition
that
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
for some
s<1
. Then the components of
f=(f_{1},f_{2})

can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}-f_{j}(r_{j}\theta_{j})&=\sum_{\nu\in\sigma_{j,1}}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{j,\nu}}l_{j,\nu,k}(f){r_{j}^{\frac{2-n_{j}}{2}-\nu}}\chi_{2}(r_{j}){\zeta_{j,\nu}^{(k)}(\theta_{j})}+g_{j},\\
&\text{ where }g_{j}\in L^{2}\text{ and }\\
l_{j,\nu,k}(f)&=\big\langle\phi_{\nu}(|y_{j}|)|y_{j}|^{-\frac{n_{j}-1}{2}}\otimes\zeta_{j,\nu}^{(k)},\tilde{f}_{j}(y_{j})\big\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathrm{d}}y_{j})};\\
\tilde{f}_{j}&=\chi_{2}\big(vf\big)_{j}+\chi_{2}B_{j}f_{j}+[\chi_{2},p_{j}^{2}]f_{j}\,(\in L^{2}_{3-s_{0}}).\end{split}

(207)

Here

\displaystyle l_{j,\nu,k}(f)=0\text{ for }\mathrm{i}\nu\geq 0.

(208)

In particular

\displaystyle\begin{split}f\in{\mathcal{H}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\forall&j=1,2,\,\nu\in\sigma^{+}_{j,1},\,k=1,\dots,n_{j,\nu}:\\
&\quad l_{j,\nu,k}(f)=0.\end{split}

(209)

4.

Suppose
u\in H^{1}_{-s_{0}}
and

(H-\lambda_{0})u=0
. In the case
s_{0}=1
suppose in addition
that
u\in H^{1}_{-s}
for some
s<1
. Then

\displaystyle\begin{split}u\in L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\forall&j=1,2,\,\nu\in\sigma^{+}_{j,1},\,k=1,\dots,n_{j,\nu}:\\
&\quad l_{j,\nu,k}(T^{*}u)=0.\end{split}

(210)

In particular
if
s_{0}\neq 1
, then the multiplicity
n_{\mathrm{res}}
of the resonance of

H
at
\lambda_{0}
(if existing) is bounded by

\displaystyle n_{\mathrm{res}}\leq\sum^{2}_{j=1}\,\,\sum_{\nu\in\sigma^{+}_{j,1}}\,n_{j,\nu}.

(211)

For
s_{0}=1
the bound (
211
) is valid provided the left-hand side is
replaced by the dimension of the quotient space
\ker(H-\lambda_{0})_{|H^{1}_{(-1)^{+}}}/\ker(H-\lambda_{0})_{|H^{1}}
.

Proof.

I
.  For the second part of
1
we refer to
Lemma
4.1
. To show the first part of
1

we use this correspondance and study the equation

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
with
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s_{0}}
. By Lemma

4.5

2
we can write

-f=G_{+}E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f+K^{+}f=K^{+}f
, and we recall that

K^{+}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})
for any
s\in(1-\nu_{0},2+\nu_{0})
. In
particular
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s^{\prime}}
,
K^{+}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s^{\prime}})
and

f+K^{+}f=0
for
s^{\prime}=3/2+\nu_{0}
. Whence it follows from Fredholm theory
that the dimension of the space of functions
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s_{0}}
solving

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
is finite. We have shown
1
.

II
.  To show the ‘only if part’ of
2
we
consider any
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s_{0}}\cap\ker(1+K^{+})
. Due to Step

I
it suffices to show that
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(\nu_{0}-1)^{-}}
. This property is trivially fulfilled for
s_{0}=1
. If
s_{0}>1
we
argue as follows. By using (
205
) we conclude that

f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s_{1}}
for any
s_{1}>\max\{s_{0}-1,1-\nu_{0}\}
, and we are
done if
s_{0}\leq 2-\nu_{0}
. In general we pick the smallest

k\in{\mathbb{N}}
such
s_{0}-k\leq 1-\nu_{0}
and note that after
k-1

iterations of the above argument we get
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1,}_{-s_{k}}
for any

s_{k}>\max\{s_{0}-k,1-\nu_{0}\}=1-\nu_{0}
.

To show the ‘if part’ of
2
suppose

f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(\nu_{0}-1)^{-}}\cap\ker(1+K^{+})
. We need to show that

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
. By an
explicit calculation using (
205
) it follows that

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=-E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})K^{+}f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{s}
for all

s<2+\nu_{0}
, in particular for some
s>1-\nu_{0}
. Whence by Lemma

4.5

2
we have

-G_{+}E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=f+K^{+}f=0
. Invoking Lemma

4.5

3
we then conclude that
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
.

III
.  We show (
207
) for any given

f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s_{0}}
obeying
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
. If

\nu_{0}=0
by assumption

f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
for some
s<1
. If
\nu_{0}>0
we know from Step
II
that

f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
for all
s>1-\nu_{0}
. Whence

\displaystyle\text{for some }s<1:\quad f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}.

(212)

Since
v
and
B_{j}
appearing in the definition of
l_{j,\nu,k}(f)

in (
207
) are of order
r^{-3}
, we conclude
that

\displaystyle\text{for some }s<1\text{ and }j=1,2:\quad\tilde{f}_{j}\in L^{2}_{3-s}.

(213)

On the other hand for
\nu\in\sigma_{j,1}
the vector

\chi_{2}(|y_{j}|)\phi_{\nu}(|y_{j}|)|y_{j}|^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}\otimes\zeta_{j,\nu}^{(k)}\in H^{1}_{(-2)^{-}}
, so
l_{j,\nu,k}(f)
is well-defined.
As we saw in the proof of Lemma
4.5
the operator

r^{-s}R_{j,\nu}r^{-s^{\prime}}
is bounded on
L_{I}^{2}
with a bound
independent of
\nu\in\sigma_{j}
provided

s,s^{\prime}>1-\operatorname{Re}\nu\text{ and }s+s^{\prime}>2
. In particular if
\nu>1
we
can take
s=0
and conclude boundedness of
R_{j,\nu}r^{-s^{\prime}}
for
any
s^{\prime}>2
. In combination with (
213
) we conclude that
the terms in the expansion of
-K_{+}f
corresponding to
\nu>1
sum up
to a vector in
L^{2}
.

It remains to consider the contributions from

\nu\in\sigma_{j,1}
. We examine the term in
K_{+}f
corresponding to
any fixed
j
,
\nu\in\sigma_{j,1}
and
k=1,\dots,n_{j,\nu}
. Write it as

\displaystyle\Big\langle\zeta_{j,\nu}^{(k)},\Big(\chi_{2}r^{\tfrac{1-n_{j}}{2}}R_{j,\nu}r^{\tfrac{n_{j}-1}{2}}\tilde{f}_{j}\Big)(r_{j},\cdot)\Big\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbb{S}}_{j})}\otimes{\zeta_{j,\nu}^{(k)}}

\displaystyle=r_{j}^{\tfrac{1-n_{j}}{2}}\chi_{2}(r_{j})\Big(l_{j,\nu,k}(f)\psi_{\nu}(r_{j})+f_{j,\nu,k}(r_{j})\Big)\otimes{\zeta_{j,\nu}^{(k)}},

where

\displaystyle f_{j,\nu,k}(r)

\displaystyle=\phi_{\nu}(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}\psi_{\nu}(r^{\prime})\big\langle\zeta_{j,\nu}^{(k)},r^{\prime\tfrac{n_{j}-1}{2}}\tilde{f}_{j}(r^{\prime},\cdot)\big\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbb{S}}_{j})}\,{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime}

\displaystyle-\psi_{\nu}(r)\int_{r}^{\infty}\phi_{\nu}(r^{\prime})\big\langle\zeta_{j,\nu}^{(k)},r^{\prime\tfrac{n_{j}-1}{2}}\tilde{f}_{j}(r^{\prime},\cdot)\big\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbb{S}}_{j})}\,{\mathrm{d}}r^{\prime}.

Using the Cauchy Schwarz inequality and (
213
)
we obtain that

\displaystyle\text{for }r\geq 1\text{ and for some }s<1:|f_{j,\nu,k}(r)|\leq Cr^{s-3/2}.

In particular we see that the function
g
given by
g(r\theta)=r^{\tfrac{1-n_{j}}{2}}\chi_{2}(r)f_{j,\nu,k}(r){\zeta_{j,\nu}^{(k)}}(\theta)
is in
L^{2}
.

We have shown (
207
). Obviously (
208
) and
(
209
) are consequences of (
207
) and
(
212
).

IV
.  As for
4
we note that
(
210
) follows from
1
,
3
and Lemma
4.1
,
and that the remaining statements of
4
follow
from (
210
).

∎

Remark 4.8
.

In this section we imposed the conditions of
Proposition
2.12
and considered three different cases of
asymptotics of the effective inter-cluster interaction
W_{j}
,

j=1,2
. For symplicity we did not consider cases where these
asymptotics mix. Due to the diagonal structure exhibited in
Proposition
2.12
it is easy to see that the diagonal
parametrix construction works in such cases too. Thus for example it
could be the case where
W_{1}
is determined by a
I_{1}
fulfilling
(
183
) while
W_{2}
fulfills (
197
). Then
the first diagonal element of the parametrix should be the one in
(
186a
) (taken with plus) and the second element should be
the operator
G_{+}
appearing in Lemma
4.5
. By using an
analogue of (
191
) it follows by a similar iteration
scheme as the ones used before that the resonance states of

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
(defined similarly in this case) have a
similar structure as in Theorem
4.7

3
and now constitute a subspace of

{\mathcal{H}}^{1,1}_{\infty,-s_{0}}
, where
s_{0}
is defined as in
(
202
) in terms of the (assumed) asymptotics
of
W_{2}
.

Remark 4.9
.

Since we imposed the conditions of
Proposition
2.12
we did not treat the exceptional cases
discussed in Sections
8
and

9
. This is an additional
technical issue that can be treated essentially by the same
parametric construction as the one discussed above. We omit the
details, referring the reader to Section
15
for a
treatment of models of physics.

14
The case
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}

Here we investigate the case where
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
. We need a replacement

R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})\to R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)
in the definition of

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
in Proposition
2.12
(or rather in Remark

2.13
), giving rise to the notation

E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
. For that (and for other purposes) we need various statements from Chapter
3
. The basic structure of the resolvent is

\displaystyle\begin{split}R^{\prime}(z)&=\breve{R}(z)-(p_{1}^{2}\Pi_{1}+p_{2}^{2}\Pi_{2}-z)^{-1}\Pi,\\
R^{\prime}(z)&=\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(z)\Pi^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(z)=\breve{R}(z)\Pi^{\prime};\quad\breve{R}(z)=(\breve{H}-z)^{-1}.\end{split}

(214a)

We shall use the following properties that are parallel to
(
186a
) and (
186b
). See Corollary

3.19
for the non-multiple two-cluster case and
Remark
3.20
for the present multiple case. There exists the strong
weak-star limits

\displaystyle\swslim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)=\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}({\mathbf{X}}),{\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}^{*}({\mathbf{X}})),

(214b)

and if
\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)f=\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)f
for a given
f\in L^{2}_{t}
for some
t>1/2
, then

\displaystyle\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)f=\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)f\in L^{2}_{t-1}.

(214c)

We shall use the operators
h_{j}=p_{j}^{2}+w_{j}
,
j=1,2
, from Subsections

13.2
–
13.4
. Recall
that
w_{j}-W_{j}
are real polynomially
decreasing potentials with varying meaning
reflecting the different hypotheses (
183
),
(
192
) and (
197
), respectively. In this section
we modify each of the three subsections to cover the case
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
.
For each case we write (
179
) as

\displaystyle-E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})=h_{1}\oplus h_{2}+v^{\pm}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1},{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}),

(215)

where the only new feature is that the previous
v
is
replaced by operators
v^{\pm}
defined in terms of the two
limits
R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)
, respectively. As before they are
non-local, but now no longer symmetric. Due to
to (
214b
) they are roughly of order
{\mathcal{O}}(|x|^{-1-2\rho})
, and
a priori they do
not
have ‘good commutation properties’ when commuting with
multiplication operators (which is in contrast to the lowest threshold
setup of Proposition

2.12
). More
precisely we need the following version of (
7.1
) and (
88
)

-E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})\equiv h_{1}\oplus h_{2}+(K^{\pm}_{ij}(\lambda_{0}))_{i,j\leq 2},

(216)

where the difference is not only polynomially
decreasing, but in fact also symmetric (to be used in (
218
)).
Due to (
214b
) there are bounds

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{ij}(\lambda_{0})

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),L^{2}_{t}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\big)\text{ for all }s>-1/2-\rho\text{ and }t<1/2+\rho,

(217)

and we note that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mp\operatorname{Im}v^{\pm}&=\mp\operatorname{Im}(K^{\pm}_{ij}(\lambda_{0}))_{i,j\leq 2}\geq 0,\\
\mp\operatorname{Im}\sum_{i,j\leq 2}\langle{f_{i},K^{\pm}_{ij}(\lambda_{0})f_{j}}\rangle&=\pm\langle{F,\operatorname{Im}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)F}\rangle;\\
\quad&F:=\sum_{i\leq 2}\,F_{i},\quad F_{i}=I_{i}\varphi_{i}\otimes f_{i}.\end{split}

(218)

14.1
Extended eigentransform for
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}

Since the operator
\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)\Pi^{\prime}
cannot be expected to
preserve weighted spaces for
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
(as in (
87
))
we need to examine carefully the ‘eigentransform’ of Remark

2.3
, cf. Lemma
4.1
.

First we note the following modification of
(
182
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}E^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda_{0})&=\big(1-\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0))\Pi^{\prime}(H-\lambda_{0})\big)S\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{s},H^{1}_{t})\\
&\text{ provided }s>-1/2-\rho,\,t<-1/2\text{ and }t\leq s,\end{split}

(219)

cf. the proof of Lemma
4.1
. We shall use a
Besov space version of (
219
), in fact we shall need (cf. the proof of Lemma
4.1
) that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle-\Delta\rangle^{1/2}\breve{R}&(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)\Pi^{\prime}(H-\lambda_{0})S\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{1/2}({\mathbf{X}}));\\
&\quad{\mathcal{B}}:={\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus{\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}({\mathbf{X}}_{2}).\end{split}

(220)

The following Lemma
4.10
holds for the cases
treated in Subsections

13.2
–
13.4
,
in fact the setting is that of Proposition
2.12
(now with

\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
). We introduce
s^{\prime}_{0}=1/2+\rho^{\prime}/4
, where

0<\rho^{\prime}<4\rho
,
\rho^{\prime}\leq 2
and

\displaystyle\lvert W_{j}(y)\rvert\leq C\langle y\rangle^{-\rho^{\prime}}\text{ for }|y|\geq R;\quad R\text{
large}.

(221)

If
\rho=1
(our main interest) the condition
simplify as (
221
) for some
\rho^{\prime}\leq 2
, and in that
case we would choose
\rho^{\prime}=1
and
\rho^{\prime}=2
in Subsections

14.2
and
14.4
,
respectively. On the other hand Subsection
14.3
does not involve
(
221
) and Lemma
4.10
directly, but rather a
version of Lemma
4.10
stated below as Lemma

4.11
. We regard Lemmas
4.10
and
4.11
as
substitutes for Lemma
4.1
. Recall the notation
{\mathcal{H}}_{s}=L^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus L^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})
.

Lemma 4.10
.

Suppose the conditions of
Proposition
2.12
(except for assuming now

\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
). Let

s\leq s^{\prime}_{0}
and
t\in(0,s^{\prime}_{0}]
.

1.

The map
T^{*}:{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
, where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}:=

\displaystyle\{u\in L^{2}_{-s}({\mathbf{X}})\mid(H-\lambda_{0})u=0,\,\,\Pi^{\prime}u\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{1/2,0}({\mathbf{X}})\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}:=

\displaystyle\{f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}\mid E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0\},

is a well-defined linear isomorphism with inverse
E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0}):{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}
.

2.

The spaces
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
and
\{f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}\mid E^{-}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0\}

coincide, and

E^{-}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f=E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f
for all
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
.

3.

The map

T^{*}:\ker(H-\lambda_{0})\to\ker E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})_{|{\mathcal{H}}}
is a
well-defined linear isomorphism with inverse
E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})
.

4.

The map
T^{*}:{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-t,0}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-t,0}
, where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-t,0}:=

\displaystyle\{u\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{t,0}({\mathbf{X}})\mid(H-\lambda_{0})u=0,\,\,\Pi^{\prime}u\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{1/2,0}({\mathbf{X}})\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-t,0}:=

\displaystyle\{f\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{t,0}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{t,0}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})\mid E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0\},

is a well-defined linear isomorphism with inverse
E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0}):{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-t,0}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-t,0}
.

5.

The spaces
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-t,0}
and
\{f\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{t,0}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{t,0}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})\mid E^{-}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0\}
coincide, and

E^{-}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f=E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f
for all
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-t,0}
.

6.

There exists
\sigma<0
such that for any real function

\chi_{\sigma}\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})

which is supported in
(-\infty,\sigma/2)
and which is
1
on

(-\infty,\sigma)
,
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s^{\prime}_{0},0}
and the two spaces

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{G}},\pm}_{-s^{\prime}_{0},\pm\sigma}:=\{u\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s^{\prime}_{0},0}({\mathbf{X}})\mid(H-\lambda_{0})u=0,\,\,\Pi^{\prime}u

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{1/2}({\mathbf{X}}),

\displaystyle\,\,\chi_{\sigma}(\pm B_{R_{0}})\Pi^{\prime}u

\displaystyle\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{1/2,0}({\mathbf{X}})\}

all coincide. (Here
B=B_{R_{0}}
is given by (
100
).)

Proof.

I
.
Let
u\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}
be given. Then

f=T^{*}u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}

follows easily, so to show that
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
it remains to show
that
E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
. Note that

\Pi^{\prime}(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi u\in{\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}({\mathbf{X}})
, cf.
Lemma
2.9
, so that

R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi u
is a well-defined
element of
{\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}^{*}({\mathbf{X}})
. We calculate using this fact,
(
44
) and (
61a
)

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f

\displaystyle=S^{*}(\lambda_{0}-H)ST^{*}u+S^{*}HR^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda_{0})ST^{*}u

\displaystyle=S^{*}(\lambda_{0}-H)\Pi u+S^{*}HR^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi u

\displaystyle=S^{*}(\lambda_{0}-H)\Pi u+S^{*}(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi u

\displaystyle=S^{*}(\lambda_{0}-H)\Pi u+\lim_{R\to\infty}S^{*}(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi\chi_{R}u;

here
\chi_{R}(x)=\chi(|x|/R)
,
R\geq 1
, is given in
agreement with (
4.1
). We calculate the second term as follows using the
weak-star topology on
{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
where
{\mathcal{B}}={\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus{\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}S^{*}(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi\chi_{R}u

\displaystyle=-\wslim_{R\to\infty}S^{*}(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi^{\prime}\chi_{R}u

\displaystyle\quad+\wslim_{R\to\infty}S^{*}(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda_{0})\chi_{R}u

\displaystyle=\wslim_{R\to\infty}S^{*}(\lambda_{0}-H)\Pi^{\prime}\chi_{R}u

\displaystyle\quad+\wslim_{R\to\infty}S^{*}(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)[H-\lambda_{0},\chi_{R}]u

\displaystyle=S^{*}(\lambda_{0}-H)\Pi^{\prime}u

\displaystyle\quad+\wslim_{R\to\infty}S^{*}(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)[H-\lambda_{0},\chi_{R}]u.

Suppose we can show that the second term

\displaystyle\wslim_{R\to\infty}S^{*}(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)[H-\lambda_{0},\chi_{R}]u=0.

(222)

Then we obtain from the above computations the desired
result

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f

\displaystyle=S^{*}(\lambda_{0}-H)\Pi u+S^{*}(\lambda_{0}-H)\Pi^{\prime}u

\displaystyle=S^{*}(\lambda_{0}-H)u

\displaystyle=0.

So it remains to show (
222
). We note that

\displaystyle-[(H-\lambda_{0}),\chi_{R}]u=2\nabla\cdot(\nabla\chi_{R})\,u-(\Delta\chi_{R})u

and that for all the components
\nabla_{k}
of
\nabla
,
the operator
\nabla_{k}
combines with the operator to the left
in (
222
), and for this combination
we
may use (
220
). Keeping this ‘effective boundedness’ in mind
we treat the first term by substituting

\displaystyle u=\Pi u+\Pi^{\prime}u=\Pi_{1}u+\Pi_{2}u+(\Pi-SS^{*})u+\Pi^{\prime}u.

For
j=1,2

\displaystyle\Pi^{\prime}\nabla\cdot(\nabla\chi_{R})\Pi_{j}u=\Pi^{\prime}\nabla_{x_{j}}\cdot(\nabla\chi_{R})_{|x^{j}=0}\Pi_{j}u+{\mathcal{O}}(R^{-2})\Pi u=0+{\mathcal{O}}(R^{-2})u,

(223)

and therefore the first and second terms do not contribute in the
limit. The third term is treated by Lemma
2.9
.
Similarly

\displaystyle\Pi^{\prime}\nabla\cdot(\nabla\chi_{R})\Pi^{\prime}u={\mathcal{O}}(R^{-1})\Pi^{\prime}u

does not contribute in the limit,
and obviously the term
(\Delta\chi_{R})u={\mathcal{O}}(R^{-2})u
does not neither. We conclude (
222
).

II
.  First we note that
E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f
has a
well-defined distributional meaning for

f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
so that

{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
is well-defined. We show that

E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
if and only if
E^{-}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
.
Suppose
E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
. First we show that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\langle{f_{j},\check{h}_{j}f_{j}}\rangle=0;\quad\check{h}_{j}=-\Delta_{j}+W_{j},\,j=1,2.

(224)

Note that
\check{h}_{j}f_{j}\in L^{2}_{s}
for any
s<1/2+\rho
,

f_{j}\in L^{2}_{-s^{\prime}_{0}}
and that
s^{\prime}_{0}<1/2+\rho
, so that

\langle{f_{j},\check{h}_{j}f_{j}}\rangle
is well-defined. We compute

\displaystyle\chi_{R}\check{h}_{j}f_{j}=\check{h}_{j}\chi_{R}f_{j}+(\Delta\chi_{R})f_{j}+2(\nabla\chi_{R})\cdot\nabla f_{j},

and then

\displaystyle\operatorname{Im}\langle{f_{j},\check{h}_{j}f_{j}}\rangle=\lim_{R\to\infty}\operatorname{Im}\langle{\chi_{R}f_{j},\chi_{R}\check{h}_{j}f_{j}}\rangle=2\lim_{R\to\infty}\operatorname{Im}\langle{\chi_{R}f_{j},(\nabla\chi_{R})\cdot\nabla f_{j}}\rangle

Next we estimate as in (
190
)

\displaystyle R^{2}\|(\nabla\chi_{R})\cdot\nabla f_{j}\|^{2}_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\langle p^{2}\rangle_{\chi_{2R}\bar{\chi}_{R/2}f_{j}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\big(\operatorname{Re}\langle\chi^{2}_{2R}\bar{\chi}^{2}_{R/2}f_{j},\check{h}_{j}f_{j}\rangle+R^{-\rho^{\prime}}\|\chi_{4R}f_{j}\|^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=C_{2}\big(o(R^{0})+R^{-\rho^{\prime}}o(R^{2s^{\prime}_{0}})\big)

\displaystyle=o(R^{1-\rho^{\prime}/2}),

yielding

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\,R^{s^{\prime}_{0}}\|(\nabla\chi_{R})\cdot\nabla f_{j}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})}=0.

Moreover

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\,R^{-s^{\prime}_{0}}\|\chi_{R}f_{j}\|_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})}=0.

Whence we obtain (
224
) by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.

Next we compute by using (
218
) and (
224
) that

\displaystyle\begin{split}0=\operatorname{Im}\langle{f,E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f}\rangle&=\operatorname{Im}\langle{F,R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)F}\rangle;\\
\quad&F=\sum_{i\leq 2}F_{i},\quad F_{i}=I_{i}\varphi_{i}\otimes f_{i}.\end{split}

(225)

Whence also
\operatorname{Im}\langle{F,R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)F}\rangle=0
and we learn that
R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)F=R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)F
, and therefore that also
E^{-}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
. We can
argue similarly for the other implication.

III
.  Suppose
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
. We need to show that

u:=E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}
and that
T^{*}u=f
. Clearly

Sf\in L^{2}_{-s}({\mathbf{X}})
. We noted in Step
II
that

R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)F=R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)F
where
F
is specified in
(
225
). This means that
u=E^{-}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f
, and using
(
214a
)–(
214c
) we then conclude that

u\in L^{2}_{-s}({\mathbf{X}})
and
\Pi^{\prime}u\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{1/2,0}({\mathbf{X}})
. To show that
u\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}
it
remains to show that
(H-\lambda_{0})u=0
. We calculate

\displaystyle(H-\lambda_{0})u

\displaystyle=(H-\lambda_{0})Sf-(H-\lambda_{0})R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)HSf

\displaystyle=(H-\lambda_{0})Sf-\Pi^{\prime}(H-\lambda_{0})R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda_{0})Sf

\displaystyle\quad-\Pi(H-\lambda_{0})R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda_{0})Sf

\displaystyle=(H-\lambda_{0})Sf-\Pi^{\prime}(H-\lambda_{0})Sf-\Pi HR^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)HSf

\displaystyle=\Pi(H-\lambda_{0})Sf-\Pi HR^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)HSf.

We conclude that
(H-\lambda_{0})u\in\Pi L^{2}_{-s}({\mathbf{X}})
(since

\Delta f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
) and that

\displaystyle S^{*}(H-\lambda_{0})u=-E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0,

and therefore (since

S^{*}
maps injectively on the space
\Pi L^{2}_{-s}({\mathbf{X}})
) that
indeed
(H-\lambda_{0})u=0
.

Next we compute

\displaystyle T^{*}E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f

\displaystyle=T^{*}Sf-T^{*}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda_{0})Sf

\displaystyle=f-0\quad(\text{since }T^{*}\Pi^{\prime}=0)

\displaystyle=f.

IV
.  Let
u\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}
and note that we showed in Step

I
that
f:=T^{*}u\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
. Using parts of the proof we show
that
u=E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f
as follows.

\displaystyle E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})T^{*}u

\displaystyle=ST^{*}u-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda_{0})ST^{*}u

\displaystyle=\Pi u-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi u

\displaystyle=\Pi u-\lim_{R\to\infty}\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi\chi_{R}u

\displaystyle=\Pi u+\Pi^{\prime}u

\displaystyle=u.

This finishes the proof of
1
and

2
, and

3
is a special case of
1
. The statements

4
and
5
are proved verbatim as
1
and

2
.

V
.  Obviously
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s^{\prime}_{0},0}\subset{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{G}},\pm}_{-s^{\prime}_{0},\pm\sigma}
. We choose
\sigma<0
such that

\displaystyle\bar{\chi}_{\sigma}(B_{R_{0}})\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}({\mathbf{X}}),{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{1/2,0}({\mathbf{X}}));\quad\bar{\chi}_{\sigma}(B_{R_{0}})=\big(1-\chi_{\sigma}(B_{R_{0}})\big),

cf. Lemma
3.15
.
We show the opposite inclusion for
{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{G}},+}_{-s^{\prime}_{0},\sigma}
only. So let
u\in{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{G}},+}_{-s^{\prime}_{0},\sigma}
be
given. Then we can mimic Step
I
and see that also in this case

f=T^{*}u\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s^{\prime}_{0},0}
. Note that for showing (
222
)
we can use the above bound to estimate

\displaystyle\wslim_{R\to\infty}S^{*}(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)[H-\lambda_{0},\chi_{R}]u

\displaystyle=-2\wslim_{R\to\infty}S^{*}(H-\lambda_{0})\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)\Pi^{\prime}\nabla\cdot(\nabla\chi_{R})\Big(\chi_{\sigma}(B_{R_{0}})+\bar{\chi}_{\sigma}(B_{R_{0}})\Big)\Pi^{\prime}u

\displaystyle=0+0

\displaystyle=0.

We use the same argument mimicking Step
IV
,
cf.
4
. So indeed
u\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s^{\prime}_{0},0}
, and we have
shown that
{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{G}},+}_{-s^{\prime}_{0},\sigma}={\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s^{\prime}_{0},0}
.
∎

In the regime
s\in(s^{\prime}_{0},1/2+\rho)
we can define
{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}_{-s}

as in Lemma
4.10

1
, however we are
not able to conclude Lemma
4.10

2
is
this case. This leads to the following definition

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}:=\{f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}|\quad E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=E^{-}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0\}\quad\text{for}\quad s<1/2+\rho,

which is consistent with Lemma

4.10
. Obviously we can also extend the definition
of

{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}
given in Lemma

4.10

1
to any real
s
.

Lemma 4.11
.

Suppose the conditions of
Proposition
2.12
(except for assuming now

\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
). Suppose

s\leq 3/2
and
s<1/2+\rho
.
Then the map

\displaystyle T^{*}:{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}

is a well-defined linear isomorphism with inverse
E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0}):{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}
(defined by (
219
)). Moreover
E^{-}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f=E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f
for all
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
.

Proof.

We can assume (
221
) and that
s\in(s^{\prime}_{0},1/2+\rho)
,
in particular that
s>1/2
.
It follows from Step
I

of the proof of Lemma

4.10
that
T^{*}
maps into

{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
. Obviously
E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})
maps into

L^{2}_{-s}({\mathbf{X}})
, but to show
that
E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})
in fact maps into
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}
we cannot use
(
225
), however the following substitute works:

\displaystyle\begin{split}0=\operatorname{Im}\langle{f,E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f-E^{-}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f}\rangle&=2\operatorname{Im}\langle{F,R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)F}\rangle;\\
\quad&F=\sum_{i\leq 2}F_{i},\quad F_{i}=I_{i}\varphi_{i}\otimes f_{i}.\end{split}

(226)

This shows that
R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)F=R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)F
for all
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
, and therefore that
\Pi^{\prime}E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{1/2,0}({\mathbf{X}})
and
E^{-}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f=E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f
for all
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
. Next we use Step
III

to see that also

(H-\lambda_{0})E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f=0
showing that indeed

E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})
maps into
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}
.

Finally it follows from the last part of Step
III
and of Step
IV
that

T^{*}
and
E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})
are mutually inverses, as we want.
∎

14.2
Negative slowly decaying effective
potentials

We aim at proving a version of
Theorem

4.2
in the setting of Subsection

13.2
(except that now

\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
). In particular
\rho<2
and we can use Lemma

4.10
with
s_{0}^{\prime}=s_{0}=1/2+\rho/4
(corresponding to
taking
\rho=\rho^{\prime}
).

Theorem 4.12
.

Under the conditions of
Proposition
2.12
(except for assuming now

\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
) and (
183
) for
j=1,2

\displaystyle\dim\ker(H-\lambda_{0})<\infty.

Moreover
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s_{0},0}\subset H^{1}_{\infty}
.

Proof.

We basically mimic the proof of Theorem

4.2
using Lemma
4.10
as
a substitute for Lemma
4.1
.

Note that Lemma

4.10

3
implies that

\displaystyle\dim\ker(H-\lambda_{0})=\dim\ker E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}),

(227)

and that the map

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda_{0}):\ker E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})\to\ker(H-\lambda_{0})

(228)

is an isomorphism.

We shall use the notation
h_{1}
,
h_{2}
and

R^{\pm}_{{\mathrm{diag}}}=r_{1}(0\pm\mathrm{i}0)\oplus(r_{2}(0\pm\mathrm{i}0)
from the proof of Theorem

4.2
. Suppose
f\in\ker E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
. Then we write the equations

R^{\pm}_{{\mathrm{diag}}}E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
as

\displaystyle(1+K^{\pm})f=0,\quad K^{\pm}=R^{\pm}_{{\mathrm{diag}}}v^{\pm},

(229)

where
1f
arises by writing
f=(f_{1},f_{2})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
and using that

\displaystyle r_{j}(0\pm\mathrm{i}0)h_{j}f_{j}=f_{j}.

(230)

Note that it is shown in Step
II
of the proof of
Theorem
4.2
that generally, if
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0},0}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})

obeys
h_{j}f_{j}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
, then (
230
)
holds. In particular (
230
) holds for
f\in\ker E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
.

Next (
217
)
and (
186a
) imply that

\displaystyle K^{\pm}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})\text{
for }s\in(s_{0},1/2+\rho).

(231)

Since
K^{\pm}
is compact (on any such space) it follows from
Fredholm theory that
\dim\ker E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})<\infty
,
proving the first assertion of the theorem.

By Lemma
4.10
any given

\phi\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s_{0},0}
corresponds to the vector

f=(f_{1},f_{2})=T^{*}\phi\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s_{0},0}
(obeying
\phi=E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f=E^{-}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f
). Note also that
h_{j}f_{j}\in L_{s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})\subset{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
for any
s
given as
in (
231
), so by the assertion in Step
II
of the proof of
Theorem
4.2

we can conclude that (
229
) is
fulfilled. In particular
K^{+}f=K^{-}f
.

A converse assertion is true. We first observe using (
218
), that

\operatorname{Im}v^{+}\leq 0
. Since
\operatorname{Im}R^{+}_{{\mathrm{diag}}}\geq 0
the equation

\displaystyle 0=\operatorname{Im}\langle{f,v^{+}f}\rangle+\operatorname{Im}\langle{R^{+}_{{\mathrm{diag}}}v^{+}f,v^{+}f}\rangle

(232)

for any solution to
(1+K^{+})f=0
, where

f=(f_{1},f_{2})\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})
, then yields that

v^{-}f=v^{+}f
and that also
(1+K^{-})f=0
for any such

f
. (Similarly a solution to
(1+K^{-})f=0
is also a solution to

(I+K^{+})f=0
.)
Moreover
E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
in the
distributional sense indeed yielding a converse statement.

More
importantly we can improve the decay of any
f=(f_{1},f_{2})\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{2})
solving
(1+K^{+})f=0

(and therefore also
(1+K^{-})f=0
) by invoking repeatedly (
186b
) and (
214c
):
Since
f_{j}\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
,
j=1,2
, and
v^{-}f=v^{+}f
(as
noted above) we
deduce from (
214c
)
that
v^{+}f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{t}
for any

t<1+2\rho-s_{0}
. Next using (
186b
) (in combination with
(
232
)) we deduce
that
f=-K^{+}f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\epsilon-s_{0}}
for any

\epsilon<\epsilon_{0}
, where

\epsilon_{0}=1+2\rho-2s_{0}=\tfrac{3}{2}\rho
. Repeating this
argument, say
k
times, yields
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{t-s_{0}}
for any

t<\epsilon_{0}k
, and therefore that

f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\infty}
.

We have shown that for any given
\phi\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s_{0},0}
the
vector

f=(f_{1},f_{2})=T^{*}\phi\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\infty}
and therefore, due to Lemma

4.10

1
,
indeed
\phi=E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f\in H^{1}_{\infty}
.
∎

14.3
Positive slowly decaying effective
potentials

We aim at proving a version of Theorem

4.3
in the setting of Subsection

13.3

(except that now
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
).

Theorem 4.13
.

Under the conditions of
Proposition
2.12
and (
192
) for
j=1,2
,

\displaystyle\dim\ker(H-\lambda_{0})<\infty.

Moreover
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}\subset H^{1}_{\infty}
for and real
s
such
that
s\leq 3/2
and
s<1/2+\rho
.

Proof.

Due to Lemma
4.11
we need to study
the space
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
for any such
s
.
Using the notation
\widetilde{R}=h_{1}^{-1}\oplus h_{2}^{-1}
of (
196
) as well as the notation (
215
), we write the equation
\widetilde{R}E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
for
any
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
as

\displaystyle(1+\widetilde{K})f=0;\quad\widetilde{K}=\widetilde{R}v^{+}.

(233)

Due to Lemma

4.4
we can easily check that
\widetilde{K}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})
.
It then follows from Fredholm theory that

f
belongs to a finite-dimensional subspace of
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
,
yielding in particular the first statement of the theorem by
taking

s=0
.

Finally we show

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\infty},

(234)

using an
iteration procedure as in the proof of Theorem

4.12
. Note that due to Lemma

4.11
we can use (
214c
) and
(
233
) to improve the decay. We deduce that

f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s+\bar{t}}
given that
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s+(k-1)\bar{t}}

where

\bar{t}:=1+2\rho-\bar{\rho}
. Since there is no limit on
k(\in{\mathbb{N}})
used for
this argument, indeed (
234
) follows by iteration. By Lemma

4.11
and (
234
) it follows that
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}\subset H^{1}_{\infty}
.
∎

14.4
Homogeneous degree
-2
effective
potentials

We aim at proving a version of
Theorem
4.7
in a setting similar to that of
Subsection
13.4
. Now of course

\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
, and having the physics examples in mind we demand

\rho=1
rather than
\rho\geq 1/2
as before. It is easy to see that our
theory is void if
\rho=1/2
is kept. On the other hand there is something to say in
the case
\rho\in(1/2,1)
, but for simplicity of presentation we
leave this case out. It turns out that the proof of
Theorem
4.7
works again with only minor
modifications, in particular we will need Lemma

4.10

2
with
s^{\prime}_{0}=1
.

First we examine how Lemma
4.5
can be modified for

\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
. Due to restricted mapping proporties of
v^{+}

we can only obtain the following weakened result (by the same
proof). Let
s_{1}=\max\{1-\nu_{0},1/2\}
, and recall that

s_{0}=1+\nu_{0}
in agreement with (
202
).

Lemma 4.14
.

1.

The operators

\displaystyle G_{+},G^{*}_{+}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{s^{\prime}},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})\text{ for }s,s^{\prime}>1-\nu_{0}\text{ with }s+s^{\prime}>2,

(235)

\displaystyle K^{*}_{+}:=-E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})^{*}G^{*}_{+}-1\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{s})\text{ for
}s\in(s_{1},3/2);

(236)

here
E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})^{*}
is given the
distributional meaning, and we define

K^{+}=(K^{*}_{+})^{*}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})
. Explicitly

\displaystyle\begin{split}K^{+}&=G_{+}v^{+}+\oplus_{j=1}^{2}\Big(\chi_{1}(h_{j}-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\big(\mathrm{i}\chi_{1}+[\chi_{1},p_{j}^{2}]\big)\\
&+\sum_{\nu\in\sigma_{j}}\Big(\big(\chi_{2}r^{\tfrac{1-n_{j}}{2}}R_{j,\nu}r^{\tfrac{n_{j}-1}{2}}\chi_{2}\big)\otimes P_{j,\nu}\Big)B_{j}\\
&+\sum_{\nu\in\sigma_{j}}\big(\chi_{2}r^{\tfrac{1-n_{j}}{2}}R_{j,\nu}r^{\tfrac{n_{j}-1}{2}}[\chi_{2},p_{j}^{2}]\big)\otimes P_{j,\nu}\Big).\end{split}

(237)

2.

K^{+}f=-G_{+}E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f-f
for every

f\in\cup_{s<3/2}\,{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-\min\{s,s_{0}\}}
with

E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{(1-\nu_{0})^{+}}
.

3.

For any
s>1
the operator

G_{+}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{s},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})
is injective.

Using Lemmas
4.10
and
4.14
(as
substitutes for Lemma
4.5
) and Lemma

4.11
(as a substitute for Lemma
4.1
)
we can show the following weakened version of Theorem

4.7
. We mimic the proof, yielding
immediately the second assertion of Theorem

4.15

1
(stated below) from
Lemma
4.11
and then in turn the first one from
Lemma
4.14

2
. The results
2
and

3
follow partly as before. Note however that Lemma

4.10

2
is used for the second
implication of
2
. Clearly
4
follows from

1
,
3
and Lemma
4.11
.

Theorem 4.15
.

Suppose the conditions of
Proposition
2.12
and (
197
) for

j=1,2
. Suppose
s<3/2
and
s\leq s_{0}
.

1.

The dimension of the space

{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{G}}}_{-s}\subset H^{1}_{-s}
is finite.

If a vector
u\in{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{G}}}_{-s}
,
then
f=T^{*}u\in{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}_{-s}\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
.
Conversely if
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}_{-s}
, then

u={E}^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{G}}}_{-s}
and
v^{+}f=v^{-}f
.

2.

If
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}_{-s}
, then
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(\nu_{0}-1)^{-}}\text{ and }f\in\ker(1+K^{+})
; here

K^{+}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-t})
is given by (
237
) and

t\in(s_{1},3/2)
is arbitrary.

Conversely if
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-1}\text{ and }f\in\ker(1+K^{+})
, then
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}_{-1}
(in particular
E^{-}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f=E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f)
.

3.

Suppose
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{H}}}_{-s}
. In the case

s_{0}=1
suppose in addition
that
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-t}
for some
t<1
. Then the components of
f=(f_{1},f_{2})

can be decomposed as

\displaystyle\begin{split}-f_{j}(r_{j}\theta_{j})&=\sum_{\nu\in\sigma_{j,1}}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{j,\nu}}l_{j,\nu,k}(f){r_{j}^{\frac{2-n_{j}}{2}-\nu}}\chi_{2}(r_{j}){\zeta_{j,\nu}^{(k)}(\theta_{j})}+g_{j},\\
&\text{ where }g_{j}\in L^{2}\text{ and }\\
l_{j,\nu,k}(f)&=\big\langle\phi_{\nu}(|y_{j}|)|y_{j}|^{-\frac{n_{j}-1}{2}}\otimes\zeta_{j,\nu}^{(k)},\tilde{f}_{j}(y_{j})\big\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathrm{d}}y_{j})};\\
\tilde{f}_{j}&=\chi_{2}\big(v^{+}f\big)_{j}+\chi_{2}B_{j}f_{j}+[\chi_{2},p_{j}^{2}]f_{j}.\end{split}

(238)

Here

\displaystyle l_{j,\nu,k}(f)=0\text{ for }\mathrm{i}\nu\geq 0.

(239)

In particular

\displaystyle\begin{split}f\in{\mathcal{H}}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\forall&j=1,2,\,\nu\in\sigma^{+}_{j,1},\,k=1,\dots,n_{j,\nu}:\\
&\quad l_{j,\nu,k}(f)=0.\end{split}

(240)

4.

Suppose
u\in{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{G}}}_{-s}
. In the case

s_{0}=1
suppose in addition
that
u\in H^{1}_{-t}
for some
t<1
. Then

\displaystyle\begin{split}u\in L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\forall&j=1,2,\,\nu\in\sigma^{+}_{j,1},\,k=1,\dots,n_{j,\nu}:\\
&\quad l_{j,\nu,k}(T^{*}u)=0.\end{split}

(241)

In particular
if
s_{0}\neq 1
, then

\displaystyle\dim\Big({\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{G}}}_{-1}/\ker(H-\lambda_{0})_{|{H^{1}}}\Big)\leq\sum^{2}_{j=1}\,\,\sum_{\nu\in\sigma^{+}_{j,1}}\,n_{j,\nu}.

(242)

For
s_{0}=1
the bound (
242
) is valid provided that

{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{G}}}_{-1}
to the left is
replaced by
{\mathcal{E}}^{{\mathcal{G}}}_{-1}\cap H^{1}_{(-1)^{+}}
.

Remark 4.16
.

We note that Remarks
4.8
and
4.9

apply equally well for the case
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
.

15
Models of physics

In this section
we treat the Coulombic potential models of Subsections
2.1

and
3.1

(with
N\geq 3
and
N\geq 2
, respectively) and demonstrate
consequences of the previous sections for these models for any given
two-cluster threshold
\lambda_{0}<0
. For convenience
we shall not distinguish between the cases
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
and

\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
as done before. Whence we will be concerned with
generalizing Section
14
only (note that the case

\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
can be considered as a special case of Section

14
although
stronger results are presented in Section

13
). As in Sections
2
and
3
we shall not consider models
with spin included (see however Remark
4.19
).

We consider a two-cluster threshold
\lambda_{0}
for the models of Sections
2
and
3
, grouping the set of thresholds
a
for which
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{a})
into
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
(all depending
on
\lambda_{0}
) for which

{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
:

the effective
inter-cluster interaction is to leading order attractive Coulombic,

{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
:

the effective
inter-cluster interaction is to leading order repulsive Coulombic,

{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
:

the effective
inter-cluster interaction is
{\mathcal{O}}(|x_{a}|^{-2})
.

Note that this distinction
does not depend on choices of corresponding sub-Hamiltonian
bound states
\varphi^{a}
(i.e. channels); it is determined by charges only
(cf. Case 1 introduced independently in both of the above
sections).

Let for
a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}:={\mathcal{A}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
(again we
suppress the dependence of
\lambda_{0}
) the operator
P^{a}
be the corresponding orthogonal projection onto

\ker(H^{a}-\lambda_{0})
in

L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})
and let
m_{a}
be the dimension of this
space. Obviously
\Pi^{a}:=P^{a}\otimes 1
projects onto
the span of functions of the form
\varphi^{a}\otimes f_{a}
,

\varphi^{a}\in\ker(H^{a}-\lambda_{0})
, in
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
. We identify
\ran P^{a}
, say spanned by an
orthonormal basis

\varphi^{a}_{1},\dots\varphi^{a}_{m_{a}}
, with
{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}}
(using the
basis), and similarly

\displaystyle L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}})

\displaystyle\simeq\oplus_{m\leq m_{a}}\,L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a})\ni\oplus_{m\leq m_{a}}f_{a,m}=f_{a}

\displaystyle\simeq S_{a}f_{a}:=\sum_{m\leq m_{a}}\varphi^{a}_{m}\otimes f_{a,m}\in\ran\Pi^{a}.

Considering for the moment only
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
we write

W_{a}:=S^{*}_{a}I_{a}S_{a}=Q_{a}|x_{a}|^{-2}+B_{a}
, where
Q_{a}
is a
m_{a}\times m_{a}
matrix-valued function depending only on

\theta=\hat{x}_{a}=|x_{a}|^{-1}x_{a}
while

B_{a}=B_{a}(x_{a})={\mathcal{O}}(|x_{a}|^{-3})
. The ‘right generalization’ to the case
m_{a}>1
of the
distinction between Cases 2) and 3) (discussed primarily for

m_{a}=1
in both of Sections
2
and
3
) is to let Case 2) correspond to
Q_{a}\neq 0
and let Case 3) correspond to
Q_{a}=0
, respectively.

Let

{\mathbb{S}}_{a}={\mathbb{S}}_{a}^{n-1}
,
n=\dim{\mathbf{X}}_{a}
, denote
the unit sphere in
{\mathbf{X}}_{a}
. We use spherical coordinates on
{\mathbf{X}}_{a}
and the
Laplace-Beltrami operator
\Delta_{\theta}
to
express the Laplacian
p_{a}^{2}
on
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}})
. The
operator
-\Delta_{\theta}+Q_{a}
on
L^{2}({\mathbb{S}}_{a},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}})
has discrete
spectrum.
As in (
198
) we parametrize its eigenvalues
in terms of a parameter
\nu
the collection of which is denoted
be
\sigma_{a}
. More precisely consider for each
\mu\in\sigma(-\Delta_{\theta}+Q_{a})
the equation
\mu=\nu^{2}-\tfrac{(n-2)^{2}}{4}
,
where by convention
\nu\geq 0
if
\mu\geq-\tfrac{(n-2)^{2}}{4}
, and
\mathrm{i}\nu>0
if
\mu<-\tfrac{(n-2)^{2}}{4}
. Then the collection of such numbers
\nu
is denoted by

\sigma_{a}
. The orthogonal projection onto the corresponding
eigenspace is denoted by
P_{a,\nu}
.
We take an orthonormal basis in
\ran P_{a,\nu}
for each

\nu\in\sigma_{a}
, say

\zeta_{a,\nu}^{(1)},\dots,\zeta_{a,\nu}^{(n_{a,\nu})}
.
Let

\displaystyle\sigma_{a,1}

\displaystyle=\sigma_{a}\setminus(1,\infty),\quad\sigma^{+}_{a,1}=\sigma_{a}\cap(0,1],

\displaystyle\nu_{a}

\displaystyle=\min_{\nu\in\sigma_{a}}\operatorname{Re}\nu,\quad s_{a}=1+\nu_{a},\quad d_{a}=\sum_{\nu\in\sigma^{+}_{a,1}}n_{a,\nu}.

Now for the physics models we have the following result,
recalling the
notation
L^{2}_{t^{+}}=L^{2}_{t^{+}}({\mathbf{X}})=\cup_{s>t}L^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}})
for
any real
t
.

Theorem 4.17
.

1.

The space of locally
H^{1}
solutions to

(H-\lambda_{0})u=0
in

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}\Pi^{a}L^{2}_{-3/4}+\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\Pi^{a}L^{2}_{(-3/2)^{+}}+\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}}\Pi^{a}L^{2}_{(-\min\{3/2,\,s_{a}\})^{+}}+L^{2}_{-1/2},

say denoted by
\mathcal{E}
,
has finite dimension.

If
{\mathcal{A}}_{3}=\emptyset
, then
{\mathcal{E}}\subset H^{1}_{\infty}
.

2.

The number

\displaystyle\dim\big({\mathcal{E}}/\ker(H-\lambda_{0})_{|{H^{1}}}\big)\leq\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}}d_{a}.

3.

There exist linear functionals
\check{l}_{a,\nu,k}:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
defined for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
,
\nu\in\sigma^{+}_{a,1}
and
k=1,\dots,n_{a,\nu}
, such that for any
u\in{\mathcal{E}}

\displaystyle u\in L^{2}\Leftrightarrow\check{l}_{a,\nu,k}(u)=0\text{ for
all such }a,\nu\text{ and }k.

Any
u\in{\mathcal{E}}
fulfills the asymptotics

\displaystyle u-\sum_{a,\nu,k,m}\check{l}_{a,\nu,k}(u)\varphi^{a}_{m}\otimes\zeta_{a,\nu,m}^{(k)}\otimes{\lvert x_{a}\rvert^{\frac{2-n}{2}-\nu}}F(\lvert x_{a}\rvert>1)\in L^{2}.

(243)

(Here
\zeta_{a,\nu,\cdot}^{(k)}
labels the
m_{a}
coordinates of
\zeta_{a,\nu}^{(k)}
.)

For those
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
for which
s_{a}\in(1,3/2)
we can replace

L^{2}_{(-\min\{3/2,\,s_{a}\})^{+}}
by
L^{2}_{-s_{a}}
in the definition of

{\mathcal{E}}
. If
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
we
can to some extent use a bigger space of generalized eigenfunctions
than the one considered in
1
, cf. Theorems

4.3
and

4.7
(not to be elaborated on).

Before giving
details of proofs we discuss the structure of
(
243
) in the case the angular-depending potential

Q_{a}
vanishes (for
a given
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
). Note
that for
m_{a}=1
this corresponds to Case 3 of Sections
2

and
3
. If
Q_{a}=0
the set
\sigma^{+}_{a,1}
is given
explicitly as

\sigma^{+}_{a,1}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}\{\frac{1}{2}\},&n=3,\\
\{1\},&n=4,\\
\emptyset,&n\geq 5.\\
\end{array}\right.

(244)

Moreover for
n=3
and
n=4
the total multiplicity of the single
point in
\sigma^{+}_{a,1}
is
m_{a}

(corresponding to constant
eigenfunctions
\zeta_{a,\nu}^{(k)}
,
k=1,\dots,m_{a}
). In particular we see that if

Q_{a}=0
for all
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
, then the space
{\mathcal{E}}
simplifies as the
space
of generalized eigenfunctions in

\displaystyle\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}\Pi^{a}L^{2}_{-3/4}+\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{3}}\Pi^{a}L^{2}_{(-3/2)^{+}}+L^{2}_{-1/2},

and we see that
\lambda_{0}
can be a ‘resonance’ only
for
n=3
or
n=4
and then with multiplicity at most
\Sigma_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}}\,m_{a}
.

The proof Theorem

4.17
relies strongly on Section

14
. Clearly the assertion
2
is a
consequence of

3
. A
complication of the proof of the assertions
1
and
(
243
) is the possible existence of exceptional cases
discussed previously, partly in remarks. In fact the definition of the
functionals
\check{l}_{a,\nu,k}
depends on the such cases. We shall
first give the proof of Theorem
4.17
in the ‘generic’
case and then outline the proof for exceptional cases.

Proof of Theorem
4.17
in the ‘generic’
case.

We consider the
non-exceptional case. This amounts to proving the theorem under two
separate additional conditions. We
introduce

{\mathcal{G}}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{a}=\Pi^{a}{\mathcal{G}}
,
a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}
.

The first assumption is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}:=\sum_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}{\mathcal{F}}_{a}=\sum_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}\oplus{\mathcal{F}}_{a}.

(245a)

Let
\Pi
be the orthogonal projection in
{\mathcal{G}}
onto

{\mathcal{F}}
(with (
245a
)
{\mathcal{F}}
is closed, cf. Proposition
2.7
). The second assumption is

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}\notin\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{\prime});\quad H^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi^{\prime},\,\Pi^{\prime}=1-\Pi.

(245b)

Under (
245a
) and (
245b
) we let

{\mathcal{H}}=\sum_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{a}
where

{\mathcal{H}}_{a}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}})=\oplus_{m\leq m_{a}}\,L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a})
, and we let

S=(S_{a}):{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{F}}\subset{\mathcal{G}}
be given by

\displaystyle\oplus_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}\,f_{a}\to\sum_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}\,S_{a}f_{a};\quad f_{a}=\sum_{m\leq m_{a}}\oplus f_{a,m},\quad S_{a}f_{a}=\sum_{m\leq m_{a}}\varphi^{a}_{m}\otimes f_{a,m}.

Letting
T=(SS^{*})^{-1}S
we note that
\Pi=ST^{*}
.

In agreement with (
34d
) and (
216
) we
consider two operators
E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
, specified by the entries

\displaystyle\begin{split}-E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})_{ab}=S^{*}_{a}(p_{b}^{2}+I_{b})S_{b}+K^{\pm}_{ab}(\lambda_{0})\equiv\delta_{ab}\check{h}_{a}+K^{\pm}_{ab}(\lambda_{0});\quad a,b\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}},\end{split}

(246)

as operators from
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}=\sum_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}\oplus H^{1}({\mathbf{X}}_{a},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}})
to
{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}=\sum_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\oplus H^{-1}({\mathbf{X}}_{a},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}})
. Here
\check{h}_{a}=-\Delta_{a}+S^{*}_{a}I_{a}S_{a}
and

K^{\pm}_{ab}(\lambda_{0})=-S^{*}_{a}I_{a}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)I_{b}S_{b}
. As in (
216
) the difference
is symmetric and polynomially
decreasing. The latter notion is defined as in Remark

2.13
, i.e.
(b_{ab})
is
polynomially
decreasing
if
b_{ab}\in{\mathcal{C}}(H_{r}^{1}({\mathbf{X}}_{b},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{b}}),H_{t}^{-1}({\mathbf{X}}_{a},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}}))
for all

r,t\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

We note that

\displaystyle K^{\pm}_{ab}(\lambda_{0})\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{-s}({\mathbf{X}}_{b},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{b}}),L^{2}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{a},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}}))\text{
for all }s<3/2.

(247a)

For
a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}
the effective
inter-cluster interaction
W_{a}=S^{*}_{a}I_{a}S_{a}
obeys

\displaystyle\begin{split}W_{a}&=C_{a}|x_{a}|^{-1}1_{{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}}}+{\mathcal{O}}(|x_{a}|^{-2}),\\
&\text{ where }C_{a}\neq 0\text{ if and only if }a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{2}.\end{split}

(247b)

Recall also

\displaystyle\forall a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}:\quad W_{a}=|x_{a}|^{-2}Q_{a}(\hat{x}_{a})+B_{a}(x_{a}),\quad B_{a}={\mathcal{O}}(|x_{a}|^{-3}).

(247c)

Write

{\mathcal{A}}_{3}=\{a_{1},\dots,a_{\bar{l}}\}
(assuming
{\mathcal{A}}_{3}\neq\emptyset
) and let
{\mathcal{T}}
denote the set of vectors
\bar{t}=(t_{1},\dots,t_{\bar{l}})\in{\mathbb{R}}^{\bar{l}}

such that
t_{l}>-\min\{3/2,\,s_{a_{l}}\}
for all
l\leq\bar{l}
. We
introduce for
r\geq-3/4
,
s>-3/2
and
\bar{t}\in{\mathcal{T}}
the spaces

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{a,s}^{k}

\displaystyle=H_{s}^{k}({\mathbf{X}}_{a},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}}),\quad k\in{\mathbb{R}},\quad a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{r,s,\bar{t}}^{k}

\displaystyle=\oplus_{b\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}\,{\mathcal{H}}_{b,r}^{k}\bigoplus\oplus_{b\in{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\,{\mathcal{H}}_{b,s}^{k}\bigoplus\oplus_{l\leq\bar{l}}{\mathcal{H}}_{a_{l},t_{l}}^{k},\quad k\in{\mathbb{R}},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{r,s,\bar{t}}^{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\{f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{r,s,\bar{t}}^{1}\,|\quad E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=E^{-}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0\},

where
E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})

is given by (
246
). A similar notation may be used if

{\mathcal{A}}_{3}=\emptyset
, for example
{\mathcal{E}}_{r,s}^{\mathcal{H}}
, however we
prefer to keep the uniform notation
{\mathcal{E}}_{r,s,\bar{t}}^{\mathcal{H}}
not
distinguising between whether one (or two) of the sets
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
,

{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
is (are) empty or, possibly, all three sets are non-empty. Let

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}=\cup_{s>-3/2,\,\bar{t}\in{\mathcal{T}}}\quad{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-3/4,s,\bar{t}}.

(248)

Let
E^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda_{0})\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}},{\mathcal{E}})
be given by

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f=Sf-\sum_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)I_{a}S_{a}f_{a}.

(249)

By mimicking the proof of Lemma
4.11
we can show
that indeed
E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0}):{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
and that, considered as such
a map, it is a linear isomorphism with inverse
T^{*}:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}
. Note that as before we need the property that
E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f=E^{-}_{+}(\lambda_{0})f

for any
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}
.

Next we adapt the parametrix construction of Section

14
(combining the three different cases
treated there) and convert the equation
E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
,
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}
,
to an equation of the form
(1+K^{+})f=0
. We can then show by iteration that

f
belongs to a suitable space (independent of
f
) on which
K^{+}
is
compact,
yielding
1
.

To carry this out in more details, let us define

R^{+}=\oplus_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\,r_{a}
where
r_{a}
is the following parametrix (depending on the three cases): For
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{2}

we take
r_{a}
diagonal in
{\mathcal{H}}_{a}
,
r_{a}=\oplus_{m\leq m_{a}}\,r_{a,m}
. For
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
and
m\leq m_{a}
we let

r_{a,m}=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0_{+}}(h_{a}-\mathrm{i}\epsilon)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{-1}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{a}),H^{1}_{-s}({\mathbf{X}}_{a})),\,s>3/4,

where
h_{a}=p_{a}^{2}+w_{a}
is constructed as in Subsection

13.2
. For
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
we let

r_{a}=\oplus_{m\leq m_{a}}\,r_{a,m}
, where
r_{a,m}=h_{a}^{-1}
with

h_{a}=p_{a}^{2}+w_{a}
been constructed as in Subsection

13.3
. Note that in this case

\langle\cdot\rangle^{-1}h_{a}^{-1}
is a bounded pseudodifferential operator,
cf. Lemma
4.4
. Finally we let for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}

the operator
r_{a}=G_{a+}
, where
G_{a+}
is constructed as in Subsection

13.4
, i.e.

\displaystyle G_{a+}=\chi_{1}(\check{h}_{a}-\mathrm{i})^{-1}\chi_{1}+\sum_{\nu\in\sigma_{a}}\big(\chi_{2}\,\lvert\cdot\rvert^{\tfrac{1-n}{2}}R_{a,\nu}\lvert\cdot\rvert^{\tfrac{n-1}{2}}\,\chi_{2}\big)\otimes P_{a,\nu}.

(250)

Here
\check{h}_{a}=p_{a}^{2}+W_{a}
, where
W_{a}=S^{*}_{a}I_{a}S_{a}=|\cdot|^{-2}Q_{a}+{\mathcal{O}}(|\cdot|^{-3})
acting as a (matrix-valued)
multiplication operator on
{\mathcal{H}}_{a}
. The quantities
Q_{a}
,

\sigma_{a}
and
P_{a,\nu}
are introduced before Theorem

4.17
, and the operator
R_{a,\nu}
is the
one-dimensional Green’s function studied in Subsection

13.4
. While the parametrix
r_{a}
for

a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
is in some sense taken as an exact inverse, the
operator
r_{a}=G_{a+}
chosen for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
is less accurate. This is
explicitly seen in (
237
) in which the second up to the
fourth terms represent ‘errors’ not appearing for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
.

Next we write the equation
-R^{+}E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
for
f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}
as
(1+K^{+})f=0
, where integration by parts is used to
produce the term
1f
. This is done separately for the components of

f
by the arguments of Subsections

14.2
–
14.4
. By
(
246
) there are terms in
K^{+}
involving polynomially
decreasing factors and there are also terms of the form

r_{a}K^{\pm}_{ab}(\lambda_{0})
. Effectively
K^{\pm}_{ab}(\lambda_{0})
is of
order
-3
when applied to an
f
given as above. For
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}

there are additional terms as discussed above,
cf. (
237
). In any case it follows that the weighted orders
of the components of
K^{+}f
tend to be at least
1/2
power better (as
determined by (
247a
)–(
247c
)) than the apriory
orders of the components of
f(=-K^{+}f)
(at this point, note also
(
230
) and its application (
186b
)). However
there is an exception for this general rule due to the restriction
s>1-\nu_{a}
imposed by mapping properties of
G_{a+}
,
cf. (
235
). Note for example that to prove
\sum_{b}r_{a}K^{\pm}_{ab}(\lambda_{0})f_{b}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{a,-s}^{1}
for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
we need
at least
s>1-\nu_{a}
. This is the only restriction for an iteration
argument. Actually we infer from one iteration (i.e. just from the
properties of
K^{+}f
) that for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
the component
f_{a}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{a,-1/4}^{1}
, that for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
the component
f_{a}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{a,-1/2}^{1}
, while
f_{a}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{a,\epsilon-1}^{1}
for some positive

\epsilon
for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
(noting though that the latter property is
an assumption if
\nu_{a}=0
). With one more iteration we then obtain
that
f_{a}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{a,0}^{1}
for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
. If

{\mathcal{A}}_{3}=\emptyset
continued iteration leads to
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{\infty}

(with Lemma
4.11
this proves the very last part
of
1
). In particular we can
conclude that

\displaystyle f\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}\Rightarrow f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{*}^{1}:={\mathcal{H}}_{-1,-1,\bar{t}}^{1},

where all coordinates of

\bar{t}
are taken to be
-4/3
. Since
K^{+}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}}_{*}^{1})
we have shown
1
.

It remains to prove
3
. We only need
to construct linear functionals
\check{l}_{a,\nu,k}:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathbb{C}}
such
that (
243
) is fulfilled for any
u\in{\mathcal{E}}
. For given

u\in{\mathcal{E}}
we let
f=T^{*}u
and need to examine the components
f_{a}
of

f
that might not be in
{\mathcal{H}}_{a}
. As we have seen this amounts to
looking at
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
only in which case we know the analogue of
(
212
) that
f_{a}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{a,-s}^{1}
for some
s<1
. Looking
at
-f_{a}=(K^{+}f)_{a}
for such
a
we need the first
equation of (
246
) written as

\displaystyle\begin{split}-E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})_{ab}=S^{*}_{a}(p_{b}^{2}+I_{b})S_{b}+K^{\pm}_{ab}(\lambda_{0})=\delta_{ab}\check{h}_{a}+v_{ab}^{+};\quad\check{h}_{a}=p_{a}^{2}+W_{a}.\end{split}

(251)

Now, by using (
250
) and the analogue of
(
237
), we obtain by mimicking Step
III
of the proof of
Theorem
4.7
that

\displaystyle\begin{split}-f_{a}(\lvert x_{a}\rvert\theta_{a})&=\sum_{\nu\in\sigma_{a,1}}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{a,\nu}}l_{a,\nu,k}(f){\lvert x_{a}\rvert^{\frac{2-n}{2}-\nu}}\chi_{2}(\lvert x_{a}\rvert){\zeta_{a,\nu}^{(k)}(\theta_{a})}+g_{a},\\
&\text{ where }g_{a}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{a}\text{ and }\\
l_{a,\nu,k}(f)&=\big\langle\phi_{\nu}(|y_{a}|)|y_{a}|^{-\frac{n-1}{2}}\otimes\zeta_{a,\nu}^{(k)},\tilde{f}_{a}(y_{a})\big\rangle_{{\mathcal{H}}_{a}};\\
\tilde{f}_{a}&=\chi_{2}\sum_{b\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}v^{+}_{ab}f_{b}+\chi_{2}B_{a}f_{a}+[\chi_{2},p_{a}^{2}]f_{a}.\end{split}

(252)

Note that
\tilde{f}_{a}\in{\mathcal{H}}_{a,3-s}^{0}
for some

s<1
, cf. (
213
), yielding in particular that
l_{j,\nu,k}(f)
is well-defined. Next we deduce from
(
252
) that

\displaystyle l_{j,\nu,k}(f)=0\text{ for }\mathrm{i}\nu\geq 0.

(253)

Clearly (
252
) and (
253
), in
combination with (
249
) and the adapted version of
Lemma
4.11
(stated after (
249
)), lead to
(
243
) with
\check{l}_{a,\nu,k}
given by

\displaystyle\check{l}_{a,\nu,k}(u)=-l_{a,\nu,k}(T^{*}u);\quad u\in{\mathcal{E}},\,a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3},\,\nu\in\sigma^{+}_{a,1},\,k=1,\dots,n_{a,\nu}.

∎

Proof of Theorem
4.17
in the ‘general’
case, a sketch.

We outline a proof of the theorem without the two
additional conditions (
245a
) and (
245b
) imposed. We shall implement Remarks

4.9
and
4.16
using the previous
notation as much as possible.

First, if (
245a
) is not fulfilled, we infer again that
the space

\sum_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}{\mathcal{F}}_{a}
is closed by considering as
in Subsection
9
the
’restriction’ of
S
to

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{0}:=\Big\{f\in\sum_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{a}\mid f\perp\ker\big(S_{ab}\big)_{a,b\in\widetilde{A}}\Big\},\,S_{ab}=S_{a}^{*}S_{b},

(254)

where as before
{\mathcal{H}}_{a}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}})
. Then

S=(S_{a}):{\mathcal{H}}_{0}\to{\mathcal{F}}_{0}:=\sum_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{F}}_{a}\subset{\mathcal{G}}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
is a continuous
isomorphism. In particular indeed
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
is closed, and we let
\Pi

denote the orthogonal projection onto
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
in
{\mathcal{G}}
. The quantities

\Pi^{\prime}
,
H^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime}(z)
are introduced as in previous sections (with

{\mathcal{F}}_{0}
playing the role of
{\mathcal{F}}
).

Next we would like to consider limits
R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)
which is
doable under the condition (
245b
). However we shall
allow, and here consider, the exceptional case where (
245b
)
is not fulfilled, and we need a version of Section
8
to treat this case. By a version of Theorem

3.12
, cf. Remarks

3.20
,
3.21
and
3.22
,
\lambda_{0}
has
finite multiplicity as an eigenvalue of
H^{\prime}
with eigenfunctions in

H^{2}_{\infty}
.

To simplify the presentation let us make the same
assumption as in Subsection
8

that this eigenspace is given as
{\mathrm{span}}\{\psi\}
,

\lVert\psi\rVert=1
. We introduce then
{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}_{0}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}
and
S:{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{F}}_{0}\oplus{\mathrm{span}}\{\psi\}
by

\displaystyle S\tilde{f}=\sum_{a}\,S_{a}f_{a}+c\psi,\quad\tilde{f}=\big((f_{a}),c\big).

We let
\Pi^{\prime\prime}
denote the orthogonal projection onto the orthogonal
complement
of
{\mathcal{F}}
in

{\mathcal{G}}
, and let
H^{\prime\prime}:=\Pi^{\prime\prime}H\Pi^{\prime\prime}
and
R^{\prime\prime}(z)=(H^{\prime\prime}-z)^{-1}
. Note that

\Pi^{\prime\prime}=1-\Pi-|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|=\Pi^{\prime}-|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|
. Recall then (
34d
)

\displaystyle E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)=S^{*}\big(z-H+HR^{\prime\prime}(z)H\big)S.

(255)

Letting
M=\#\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}+1
this operator has a
M\times M
-block representation

(e_{ij})_{i,j\leq M}
. Here (listing the elements
a\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}

as
a_{1},\dots,a_{M-1}
)
e_{MM}=z-\lambda_{0}
,
e_{iM}=-S^{*}_{a_{i}}H\psi=-S^{*}_{a_{i}}I_{a_{i}}\psi
and
e_{Mi}=e_{iM}^{*}=\langle e_{Mi}|
for

i\leq M-1
, while for
i,j\leq M-1
we set
e_{ij}=S_{a_{i}}^{*}\big(z-H+HR^{\prime\prime}(z)H\big)S_{a_{j}}
.

The analogue of (
91a
) reads

\displaystyle\begin{split}&\breve{H}=H^{\prime\prime}+\sum_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\,p_{a}^{2}\Pi^{a},\\
\;&\widetilde{H}=H-\lambda_{0}\sum_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\Pi^{a};\\
\,&{\mathcal{D}}(\breve{H})={\mathcal{D}}(\widetilde{H})={\mathcal{D}}(H).\end{split}

(256a)

These operators differ by
H
-compact terms, cf. (
91b
),

\displaystyle\breve{H}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{H}-K_{1}+K_{2};

(256b)

\displaystyle K_{1}

\displaystyle=\Pi H\Pi^{\prime}+\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi+\sum_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\Pi^{a}I_{a}\Pi^{a}+\lambda_{0}|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|,

\displaystyle K_{2}

\displaystyle=\sum_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\Pi^{a}H\Pi^{a}-\Pi H\Pi,

and the basic structure of resolvents is given as

\displaystyle\begin{split}R^{\prime\prime}(z)&=\breve{R}(z)-\Big(\sum_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\,\,p_{a}^{2}\Pi^{a}-z\Big)^{-1}\Pi-z^{-1}|\psi\rangle\langle\psi|,\\
R^{\prime\prime}(z)&=\Pi^{\prime\prime}\breve{R}(z)\Pi^{\prime\prime}=\Pi^{\prime\prime}\breve{R}(z)=\breve{R}(z)\Pi^{\prime\prime};\quad\breve{R}(z)=(\breve{H}-z)^{-1}.\end{split}

(256c)

Due to the good properties of
\psi
we can make sense to

\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)
and then in turn to
R^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)
. In particular we can consider (
249
) with

f
replaced by
\tilde{f}:=\big((f_{a}),c\big)\in{\mathcal{H}}
and
R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)
by
R^{\prime\prime}((\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0))
, i.e.

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda_{0})\tilde{f}=S\tilde{f}-\sum_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}R^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)I_{a}S_{a}f_{a}.

(257a)

Similarly, by taking limits in (
255
), we obtain

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})=S^{*}\big(\lambda_{0}-H+HR^{\prime\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)H\big)S=(e_{ij})_{i,j\leq M,\,z=\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0}.

(257b)

The previous definition of
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}
given in
(
248
) needs to be modified as
follows. We introduce (for parameters given as before)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{r,s,\bar{t}}^{k,0}

\displaystyle=\Big\{f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{r,s,\bar{t}}^{k}\,\mid f\perp\ker\big(S_{ab}\big)_{a,b\in\widetilde{A}}\Big\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{r,s,\bar{t}}^{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\{\tilde{f}=(f,c)\,\mid f\in{\mathcal{H}}_{r,s,\bar{t}}^{1,0},\,c\in{\mathbb{C}},\,E^{+}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})\tilde{f}=E^{-}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})\tilde{f}=0\},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}

\displaystyle=\cup_{s>-3/2,\,\bar{t}\in{\mathcal{T}}}\quad{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}_{-3/4,s,\bar{t}}.

As
before we can now check that
E^{+}_{+}(\lambda_{0}):{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
and that, considered as such
a map, it is a linear isomorphism with inverse
T^{*}=S^{*}(SS^{*})^{-1}:{\mathcal{E}}\to{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{H}}
.

Next we consider the space

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}=\Big(\sum_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{a}\Big)\oplus{\mathbb{C}}.

Clearly
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}\oplus\ker\big(S_{ab}\big)_{a,b\in\widetilde{A}}
, and
E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})

acts only on the first component. Let us introduce
\widetilde{E}^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})=E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})+P_{0}
, where
P_{0}
denotes the orthogonal projection onto

\ker\big(S_{ab}\big)_{a,b\in\widetilde{A}}
in
\sum_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{a}
. We can consider
P_{0}
as acting on the second
component of
\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}
and hence write
\widetilde{E}^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})=E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})\oplus 1
. Clearly

\ker\widetilde{E}^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})=\ker E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
, and
similar relations hold in weighted Sobolov spaces. We are lead to
consider
\widetilde{E}^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})=E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})\oplus 1

on spaces of the form

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{r,s,\bar{t}}=\Big({\mathcal{H}}_{r,s,\bar{t}}^{k,0}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}\Big)\oplus\ker\big(S_{ab}\big)_{a,b\in\widetilde{A}}={\mathcal{H}}_{r,s,\bar{t}}^{k}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}.

With the same restrictions on the parameters we can prove
a
version of Lemma
4.11
(using the same proof), and
by applying
the parametrix construction
\oplus_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\,r_{a}
of the previous proof of the theorem (the
regular case) extended as
R_{{\mathrm{diag}}}^{+}=\big(\oplus_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\,r_{a}\big)\oplus 1_{\mathbb{C}}
to the equation

\displaystyle\widetilde{E}^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})\tilde{f}=0,\quad\tilde{f}:=\big(f,c\big)=\Big(\big(P^{\prime}_{0}f,c\big),P_{0}f\Big)\in\widetilde{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{r,s,\bar{t}},\,\,P_{0}^{\prime}=1-P_{0},

we arrive again at Fredholm theory. More precisely, using that the
operator
E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})\oplus 0
is realized concretely
by the expression on the right-hand side of (
257b
), we arrive at the
equation

\displaystyle 0=R_{{\mathrm{diag}}}^{+}\big(P_{0}f+(\widetilde{E}^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})\tilde{f}-P_{0}f)\big)=-(1+\widetilde{K}^{+})\tilde{f}

for a ‘nice’ operator
\widetilde{K}^{+}
. Indeed
using the specific form of this
slightly modified new
K^{+}
we can proceed as
before. We omit the details.
∎

Remark 4.18
.

If
{\mathcal{A}}_{3}=\emptyset
we can
write the above operator as
\widetilde{K}^{+}=R_{{\mathrm{diag}}}^{+}\tilde{v}^{+}
,
where
\operatorname{Im}\tilde{v}^{+}\leq 0
, however
in general the form of
\widetilde{K}^{+}
is more
complicated. Moreover the structure of the set of solutions to the equations

(1+K^{+})f=0
or
(1+\widetilde{K}^{+})\tilde{f}
=0 studied in the above proofs
appears ‘cleanest’ in this case in the sense that ‘spurious’ solutions, i.e. resonances
states,
do not occur. On the other hand if
{\mathcal{A}}_{3}\neq\emptyset
resonances
states could occur, and for this reason
our theory of resolvent expansion at the two-cluster threshold is
more complicated for
{\mathcal{A}}_{3}\neq\emptyset
. See Chapter

5
for a systematic study applicable to the fastly decaying
case for which
{\mathcal{A}}={\mathcal{A}}_{3}
and the effective
inter-cluster interaction is
{\mathcal{O}}(|x_{a}|^{-3})
for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}
.
Resolvent expansion at the two-cluster threshold for some non-fastly decaying
cases is treated
in Chapter
6
, see Remark
6.1
.

Remark 4.19
.

We did not consider particles with spin in Theorem

4.17
. However the theorem applies to
cases with spin because this basically amount to restricting the
operator
H
to suitable subspaces. However this is under the
assumption that
\lambda_{0}
is an (un-restricted) two-cluster
threshold. It could be that
\lambda_{0}
is a threshold not of this
type but in the restricted sense is a two-cluster
threshold. To treat such situation one would need a different
procedure, although it could be that a somewhat similar
treatment would work. Thus for example, to indicate a possible
preliminary step, an obvious procedure for modifying
S

for fermions would be to use an anti-symmetrization of

S_{a}f_{a}=\varphi^{a}\otimes f_{a}
to bring this vector into the
fermionic subspace of
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
.

Chapter 5
Resolvent asymptotics near a two-cluster
threshold

This chapter is devoted to the asymptotics of the resolvent near an
arbitrary two-cluster threshold
\lambda_{0}
for the
N
-body
Schrödinger operator
H
. As discussed in Chapter
4
, for physics models, the effective potential may decay like

{\mathcal{O}}(\lvert x_{a}\rvert^{-1})
(slowly decaying case),
{\mathcal{O}}(\lvert x_{a}\rvert^{-2})
(critically decaying case) or
{\mathcal{O}}(\lvert x_{a}\rvert^{-3})
(fastly decaying case).
We only study two cases: 1) the effective potential is fastly decaying; 2) the effective potential is slowly decaying and positive outside a compact set.
The main difference between these two cases is that threshold resonance may appear in the first one and it is absent in the second one. For fastly decay effective potentials, we only study in full details two situations:
i)
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
is a double
two-cluster threshold (the case
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
is a non-multiple two-cluster
threshold is easier and already studied in
[
Wa2
]
); ii)
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}

is a non-multiple two-cluster threshold of
H
.
We calculate the leading term of resolvent expansions according to cases the threshold is an eigenvalue or/and a resonance. For positively slowly decreasing effective potentials we only study the lowest threshold and prove Gevrey estimates in exponentially weighted spaces for the remainder in the resolvent expansion (cf. (
[
AW
,
Wa6
]
) for one-body operators). Our study will have applications for the physics models of Sections
2
and
3
. Parts of our study easily
generalize to cover interesting cases for the physics models
of Sections
2

and
3
; our results will be stated with sketched proofs.

This chapter is organized as follows. In Section
16
we study the asymptotic expansion of the
resolvent
R(z)=(H-z)^{-1}
for rapidly decreasing effective potentials. For simplicity we assume that the intercluster spaces are of dimension three and analyze the notion of two-cluster threshold resonances in Subsection
16.1
. We give some normalization conditions for resonance
states according to spectral nature of the two-cluster threshold. These conditions are useful, they allow to explicitly compute some constants in physically interesting models (cf. Chapter 6).
The resolvent expansions are given in Subsection
16.2
for
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
(when
\Sigma_{2}
is a double two-cluster threshold) and in Subsection
16.3
for
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
. In Section
17

we study the case where the effective potential is positive outside
a compact set and slowly decaying at infinity. In this case threshold resonance is absent and we prove one-term resolvent expansions with Gevrey estimates on the remainder at the lowest threshold
\Sigma_{2}
. These results may be used to show sub-exponential time-decay of local energies. In
Section
18
we study the
combination of the previous sections for the physics models
of Sections
2

and
3
.

16
Rapidly decaying effective potentials

16.1
Two-cluster threshold resonances

Let
\lambda_{0}\in{\mathcal{T}}
be a two-cluster threshold of
H
. We analyze
in this subsection spectral properties of
H
at
\lambda_{0}
, assuming
the effective potential obtained through the Grushin method decays
sufficiently fastly. In addition, we assume the intercluster configuration
{\mathbf{X}}_{a}
is of dimension three for two-cluster decompositions
a
such that
\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue of
H^{a}
.

16.1.1
The case
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}

Consider first the case

\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
is a double two-cluster threshold in the sense
of Condition
2.5
.

(258)

Whence the lowest threshold
\Sigma_{2}
is a two-cluster threshold of
H
and an eigenvalue of exactly two sub-Hamiltonians
H^{a_{j}}
,
j=1,2
. Here of course
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
are two-cluster
decompositions of the
N
-body system.
The above condition implies that
\Sigma_{2}<0
, however the
requirement of Condition
2.5

that
\lambda_{0}(=\Sigma_{2})
be a simple eigenvalue
for both
H^{a_{1}}
and
H^{a_{2}}
is automatically fulfilled. It is
also a part of Condition
2.5
that that Condition

1.6
are fulfilled for
a=a_{1}
and
a=a_{2}
. In agreement with Section

7
, let

\varphi_{j}
denote a corresponding (real) normalized eigenvector of

H^{a_{j}}
,
j=1,2
.
Denote

{\mathbf{X}}^{j}={\mathbf{X}}^{a_{j}},\quad{\mathbf{X}}_{j}={\mathbf{X}}_{a_{j}},\quad j=1,2,

and
x^{j}
(resp.,
x_{j}
) variables in
{\mathbf{X}}^{j}
(resp., in
{\mathbf{X}}_{j}
).
Let

{\mathcal{F}}_{j}=\{g=\varphi_{j}(x^{j})f_{j}(x_{j})\mid f_{j}\in L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})\},\quad j=1,2.

{\mathcal{F}}_{j}
is a closed subspace of
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
.
Assume, cf. (
54
),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{2}=\{0\}\quad\mbox{ and }n_{j}:=\dim{\mathbf{X}}_{j}=3\,\text{ for }\,j=1,2.

Recall from Proposition
2.7
that
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}_{1}+{\mathcal{F}}_{2}
is a closed subspace in
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
.
Let
\Pi
be the orthogonal projection from
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
onto
{\mathcal{F}}

and
\Pi^{\prime}=1-\Pi
. We borrow the notation (
178
) and
denote, in particular,

{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{s}=H^{k}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus H^{k}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{2}),\quad{\mathcal{H}}^{k}={\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{0},\quad{\mathcal{H}}_{s}={\mathcal{H}}^{0}_{s},\quad{\mathcal{H}}={\mathcal{H}}^{0}_{0}.

Let
{\mathcal{L}}(k,s;k^{\prime},s^{\prime})={\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{s},{\mathcal{H}}^{k^{\prime}}_{s^{\prime}})
,
cf. Subsection
4.1
.

Let

S=(S_{1},S_{2}):{\mathcal{H}}\to L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
be defined by
Sf=S_{1}f_{1}+S_{2}f_{2}

for
f=(f_{1},f_{2})
and with

S_{j}:L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})\to L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}),\quad f_{j}\to S_{j}f_{j}=\varphi_{j}(x^{j})\otimes f_{j}(x_{j}).

Then
S_{j}^{*}:L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})\to L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
is given by
S_{j}^{*}:\,f\to S_{j}^{*}f=\langle\varphi_{j},f\rangle_{j}
, for
j=1,2.
Here
\langle.,.\rangle_{j}
denotes the scalar product in

L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{j})
; the notation
\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle
will be used to denote
the scalar product in
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
(or in
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
).
One has

S^{*}S=1+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&s_{12}\\[7.22743pt]
s_{21}&0\end{array}\right)\quad\mbox{ on }{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{1})\oplus L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{2}),

where
s_{ij}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j}),L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{i})\big),\;i\neq j,
are
given by
s_{ij}f_{j}=\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}\otimes f_{j}\rangle_{i}
.

Introduce for
z\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0

\displaystyle E(z)

\displaystyle=R^{\prime}(z),

\displaystyle E_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=S-R^{\prime}(z)HS,

\displaystyle E_{-}(z)

\displaystyle=S^{*}-S^{*}HR^{\prime}(z),

\displaystyle E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)

\displaystyle=S^{*}\big(z-H+HR^{\prime}(z)H\big)S.

Then (recalling from Section

7
)

R(z)=E(z)-E_{+}(z)E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}E_{-}(z).

(259)

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z):{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{H}}
is computed in (
7.1
):

\displaystyle E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)={}

\displaystyle z-\lambda_{0}

\displaystyle-\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\Delta_{x_{1}}+W_{1}(x_{1})+K_{11}(z)&\check{s}_{12}(z)+W_{12}+K_{12}(z)\\
\check{s}_{21}(z)+W_{21}+K_{21}(z)&-\Delta_{x_{2}}+W_{2}(x_{2})+K_{22}(z)\end{array}\right),

where

\displaystyle\check{s}_{ij}(z)

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi_{i},\varphi_{j}\otimes(\lambda_{0}-z-\Delta_{x_{j}})\cdot\rangle_{i},

\displaystyle W_{ij}

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi_{i},I_{j}\varphi_{j}\otimes\cdot\rangle_{i},

\displaystyle W_{k}(\cdot)

\displaystyle=W_{kk}=\langle\varphi_{k},I_{k}\varphi_{k}\rangle_{k}(\cdot),

\displaystyle K_{ij}(z)

\displaystyle=-\langle\varphi_{i},I_{i}R^{\prime}(z)I_{j}(\varphi_{j}\otimes\cdot)\rangle_{i}.

Assume now
\lambda_{0}\not\in\sigma_{\mathrm{d}}(H^{\prime})
. Then
by Lemma
2.10

R^{\prime}(z)
is holomorphic in
z
for
z
near

\lambda_{0}
and
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
is well-defined. To simplify
notation, denote
P=-E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})
and decompose this
operator as
P=P_{0}+U
, where

\displaystyle P_{0}

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}P_{1,0}&0\\
0&P_{2,0}\\
\end{array}\right):=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}-\Delta_{x_{1}}&0\\
0&-\Delta_{x_{2}}\\
\end{array}\right),

\displaystyle U

\displaystyle=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}U_{11}&U_{12}\\
U_{21}&U_{22}\\
\end{array}\right):=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}W_{1}&W_{12}+\check{s}_{12}(\lambda_{0})\\
W_{21}+\check{s}_{21}(\lambda_{0})&W_{2}\\
\end{array}\right)+K(\lambda_{0}).

We impose the condition
\rho>\tfrac{1}{2}
(where
\rho
is given in (
22
)) and
(
197
) with
q_{j}(\theta)=0
for
j=1,2
, and hence
(cf. Proposition
2.12
) that at least for
\rho_{0}=3

\displaystyle U:{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\rho_{0}-s}\quad\mbox{ is
continuous for any $s\in[0,\rho_{0}]$}.

(264)

This leads us finally to impose the following set of conditions
(including the previous ones):

\displaystyle\dim{\mathbf{X}}_{1}=\dim{\mathbf{X}}_{2}=3,\quad{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{2}=\{0\}\,\text{ and }\,\lambda_{0}\not\in\sigma(H^{\prime}),

(265a)

\displaystyle\rho>\tfrac{1}{2}\,\text{ and }\,{\mathrm{diag}}(W_{1},W_{2})\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1},{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{\rho_{0}})\mbox{ for some }\,\rho_{0}\geq 3,

(265b)

\displaystyle 2+2\rho\geq\rho_{0}.

(265c)

Actually (
265c
) is imposed for Section

16.2
only; it is a convenient
assumption relevant for the case
\rho_{0}>3
. Clearly (
264
)
is fulfilled (in fact the operator
U(z)

of Section

16.2
fulfills

U(z)\in{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s;-1,\rho_{0}-s)
for
0\leq s\leq\rho_{0}
uniformly
for
z
near zero, generalizing (
264
)). The conditions (
265a
) and
(
265b
) will be imposed
throughout the section.

Recalling the notation
H^{1}_{t^{-}}=\cap_{s<t}H^{1}_{s}
and
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{t^{-}}=\cap_{s<t}{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{s}
for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
, (
265a
) and
(
265b
) lead to the
following effective version of Definition

4.6
, see Theorems
4.7
and

4.15
(and (
244
)).

Definition 5.1
.

1.

\lambda_{0}
is a
resonance of
H
if the
equation
Hu=\lambda_{0}u
admits a solution

u\in H^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}\setminus H^{1}
. The multiplicity
of the resonance
\lambda_{0}
