is defined as the dimension of the quotient
space

\ker(H-\lambda_{0})_{|H^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}}/\ker(H-\lambda_{0})_{|H^{1}}
.

2.

0
is a
resonance of
P
if the equation

Pv=0
has a solution
v\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
. The multiplicity of the resonance zero is defined as
the dimension of the quotient space

\ker P_{|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}}/\ker P_{|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}
.

Similarly we can define (although this will not be needed) a notion of zero-resonance for
\widetilde{P}=(S^{*}S)^{-1}P(S^{*}S)^{-1}
. Since

(S^{*}S)^{-1}
and
S^{*}S
are
continuous on
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{s}
for any
s
, zero is a
resonance of
\widetilde{P}
if and only if it is a resonance of
P
and
their multiplicities are the same. Following
[
Ne
,
JK
]
we
distinguish between four cases for the threshold
\lambda_{0}
according to its spectral
nature as follows.

Case 0

A regular point
:
\lambda_{0}
(
0
, resp.) is
neither an eigenvalue nor a resonance of
H
(
P
, resp.).

Case 1

An exceptional point of the first kind
:

\lambda_{0}
(
0
, resp.) is a resonance but not an eigenvalue of
H

(
P
, resp.).

Case 2

An exceptional point of the second kind
:

\lambda_{0}
(
0
, resp.) is an eigenvalue but not a resonance of
H

(
P
, resp.).

Case 3

An exceptional point of the third kind
:

\lambda_{0}
(
0
, resp.) is simultaneously an eigenvalue and a resonance of

H
(
P
, resp.).

From Lemma
4.1
we deduce the following result.

Lemma 5.2
.

\lambda_{0}
is a resonance (resp., an eigenvalue) of
H
if and only
if zero is a resonance (resp., an eigenvalue) of
P
and their
multiplicities are the same.

We need the following simplified version of
Theorem

4.7
.

Theorem 5.3
.

Assume
the conditions (
265a
) and
(
265b
).

1.

Suppose
v=(v_{1},v_{2})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}
and
Pv=0
. Then one has

v_{j}(x_{j})=-\frac{\langle 1,U_{j1}v_{1}+U_{j2}v_{2}\rangle}{4\pi|x_{j}|}+w_{j}\text{ for }|x_{j}|\geq 1,

(266)

where

w_{j}\in L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{j})
;

j=1,2
.
Moreover

v\in{\mathcal{H}}\Longleftrightarrow\langle 1,U_{j1}v_{1}+U_{j2}v_{2}\rangle=0;\quad j=1,2.

(267)

2.

Suppose
u\in H^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}

is a solution to the equation

(H-\lambda_{0})u=0
. Let
v=T^{*}u\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}
.
Then
u
is an eigenfunction of

H
at
\lambda_{0}
if and only if

\langle 1,U_{j1}v_{1}+U_{j2}v_{2}\rangle=0;\quad j=1,2.

(268)

Proof.

Since
n_{j}=3
and we assume that

q_{j}(\theta)=0
for
j=1,2
, in Theorem

4.7

the sets
\sigma_{j,1}=\{\tfrac{1}{2}\}
. Whence by this theorem we need to
consider the functionals
l_{j,\tfrac{1}{2},1}
applied to
v\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}
solving

Pv=0
. Since
q_{j}=0
,

\displaystyle\sqrt{4\pi}\,l_{j,\tfrac{1}{2},1}(v)=\big\langle\phi_{\frac{1}{2}}(|y_{j}|)|y_{j}|^{-1},\Delta_{y_{j}}(\chi_{2}v_{j})(y_{j})\big\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathrm{d}}y_{j})}.

By
integrating by parts in the
integral to
the right of this formula we
verify that

\displaystyle\sqrt{4\pi}\,l_{j,\tfrac{1}{2},1}(v)=\big\langle 1,\Delta_{y_{j}}(\chi_{2}v_{j})(y_{j})\big\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathrm{d}}y_{j})}=\big\langle 1,\Delta_{y_{j}}v_{j}(y_{j})\big\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathrm{d}}y_{j})}=\langle 1,U_{j1}v_{1}+U_{j2}v_{2}\rangle,

showing
(
266
). Trivially
(
267
) is a consequence
of (
266
). The above
computation and Theorem

4.7

4
yield
2
.
∎

Remark 5.4
.

Although this was not used above let us note that the equation

Pv=0
for
v=(v_{1},v_{2})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}

(equivalently) reads

\displaystyle v=-G_{0}Uv,

(269)

where
G_{0}:=\slim_{z\to 0,\,z\not\in\,[0,\infty)}(P_{0}-z)^{-1}
is
computed as

G_{0}=G_{0,1}\oplus G_{0,2}
with
G_{0,j}
specified by its integral kernel

G_{0,j}(x_{j},y_{j})=\frac{1}{4\pi|x_{j}-y_{j}|};\quad j=1,2.

This assertion follows from an integration by parts argument as in
the proof of Lemma
4.5

2
. We
note that the operators

\langle x\rangle^{-s}G_{0,j}\langle x\rangle^{-s^{\prime}}

are Hilbert-Schmidt operators for

s,s^{\prime}>\tfrac{1}{2}
and
s+s^{\prime}>2
, cf.
[
JK
, Lemmas 2.2]
. The
asymptotics (
266
) may alternatively be
derived from (
269
).

The condition
\langle{1,U_{j1}v_{1}+U_{j2}v_{2}}\rangle=0
for
j=1,2
is
equivalent to the condition
\langle{U^{*}c,v}\rangle=0
for all
c\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
. Since
Hu=\lambda_{0}u
if and only if
Pv=0
with
v=T^{*}u
, the following result is an immediate consequence of Theorem
5.3
.

Corollary 5.5
.

Assume conditions of Theorem

5.3
. Let
u\in H^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}
be a solution to the equation
Hu=\lambda_{0}u
. Then
u
is an
L^{2}
-eigenfunction of
H
if and only if

\langle{TU^{*}c,u}\rangle=0\quad\mbox{ for any }c\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}.

(270)

The condition (
270
) can be rewritten as the system of
equations

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{X}}\varphi_{1}I_{1}udx+\langle 1,\check{s}_{12}(T^{*}u)_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=0,

(271a)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{X}}\varphi_{2}I_{2}udx+\langle 1,\check{s}_{21}(T^{*}u)_{1}\rangle

\displaystyle=0.

(271b)

In fact, using
u=Sv-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})HSv
with
v=T^{*}u
and
writing

Sv=\varphi_{1}v_{1}+\varphi_{2}v_{2}
, one has

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{X}}\varphi_{1}I_{1}udx

\displaystyle=\langle I_{1}\varphi_{1},Sv-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})HSv\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle 1,(W_{1}+K_{11}(\lambda_{0}))v_{1}\rangle+\langle 1,(W_{12}+K_{12}(\lambda_{0}))v_{2}\rangle,

showing, by using
(
270
) with
c=(1,0)
, that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{X}}\varphi_{1}I_{1}udx+\langle 1,\check{s}_{12}v_{2}\rangle=\langle 1,(UT^{*}u)_{1}\rangle=0.

Similarly (
271b
) follows from the computation

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{X}}\varphi_{2}I_{2}udx+\langle 1,\check{s}_{21}v_{1}\rangle=\langle 1,(UT^{*}u)_{2}\rangle=0.

By the same computations we see that
(
271a
) and (
271b
) imply (
270
).

For
s\in(\frac{1}{2},\frac{3}{2})
we
set

{\mathcal{K}}=\ker(1+G_{0}U)\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}.

(272)

This space
{\mathcal{K}}

coincides with the subspace

\{v\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}|\,Pv=0\}
and is of
finite dimension. By Theorem
5.3
, if zero is a resonance of

P
, its multiplicity is at most two.
Next we introduce the constant functions

\displaystyle g_{j}(x_{i})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\text{ for }x_{i}\in{}{\mathbf{X}}_{i};\quad i=1,2.

(273a)

Set
\mathfrak{g}_{1}=(g_{1},0)
and
\mathfrak{g}_{2}=(0,g_{2})
.
For
v\in{\mathcal{K}}
, define
c(v)=(c_{1}(v),c_{2}(v))\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
by

\displaystyle c_{i}(v)=\langle{\mathfrak{g_{i}},Uv}\rangle=\langle{g_{i},U_{i1}v_{1}+U_{i2}v_{2}}\rangle;\quad i=1,2.

(273b)

Theorem
5.3
implies that
v\in{\mathcal{K}}
is an eigenfunction of
P
if and only if
c(v)=0
. In case zero is a resonance of
P
, we use the following
normalization of resonance states:

•

If zero resonance is simple, we denote by
\psi_{1}\in{\mathcal{K}}
a resonance state
such that

|c(\psi_{1})|=1.

(274)

•

If zero resonance is double, we denote by
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
two
resonance states in
{\mathcal{K}}
such that

c(\psi_{1})=(1,0)\text{ and }c(\psi_{2})=(0,1),

(275)

respectively.

If
\lambda_{0}
is a resonance of
H
, let
\kappa
denote its multiplicity. Then
\kappa\in\{1,2\}
and
the corresponding normalization condition for resonance states
u_{j}=(1-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})H)S\psi_{j}
,
1\leq j\leq\kappa
, of
H
reads

\displaystyle|\langle{TU^{*}\mathfrak{g}_{1},u_{1}}\rangle|^{2}+|\langle{TU^{*}\mathfrak{g}_{2},u_{1}}\rangle|^{2}=1,

\displaystyle\quad\mbox{ if $\kappa=1$},

(276)

\displaystyle\langle{TU^{*}\mathfrak{g}_{i},u_{j}}\rangle=\delta_{ij}\quad\mbox{for $1\leq i,j\leq 2$},

\displaystyle\quad\mbox{ if $\kappa=2$}.

(277)

With this choice of resonance functions we show in
Section
16.2
, see Theorem

5.14
, that if
\Sigma_{2}
is a resonance of multiplicity
\kappa
but not an eigenvalue of
H
, then

R(z)=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z-\lambda_{0}}}\left(\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}{}\langle{u_{j},\cdot}\rangle u_{j}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z-\lambda_{0}|^{\epsilon})\right)

(278)

in
{\mathcal{L}}(-1,s;1,-s)
,
s>1
, for
z
near
\lambda_{0}
and
z-\lambda_{0}\not\in[0,\infty)
.

The case where
\Sigma_{2}
is an eigenvalue (and possibly a resonance
as well) is also
studied, see Theorem

5.14
when
\rho_{0}>3
. For
\rho_{0}=3
this requires an a priori weak decay
property of the corresponding eigenfunctions, see Theorem

5.16
. On the other hand with this additional assumption in fact
we obtain the expansion of the resolvent up to second
order. Expansions to higher orders usually require strong decay of the
potential, see
[
JK
,
Wa2
]
. In particular in our setting this would
mean that
\rho_{0}
should be sufficiently big. We shall not pursue
this direction, but rather mainly restrict our study to the case

\rho_{0}=3
which indeed has
relevance for the physics models, see discussions in Sections
2
and
3
.

16.1.2
The case
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}

In this Subsection we consider the case

\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
is a non-multiple two-cluster threshold in the sense
of Condition
2.4
.

(279)

This means there exists a unique

a_{0}\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\max}\}
such that
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{a_{0}})\setminus\{\Sigma_{2}\}
, and this cluster
decomposition is a two-cluster decomposition obeying Condition

1.6
. As in Section
6
we let
m
denote the multiplicity of
eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
of
H^{a_{0}}
and
\{\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m}\}
be an orthonormal basis of the associated eigenspace.
Let
\Pi
be the projection in
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
defined by

\Pi g=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\varphi_{j}\otimes\langle\varphi_{j},g\rangle_{L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a_{0}})},\;g\in L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}).

(280)

Put
\Pi^{\prime}=1-\Pi
,
H^{\prime}=\Pi^{\prime}H\Pi^{\prime}
and
R^{\prime}(z)=(H^{\prime}-z)^{-1}\Pi^{\prime}
. Let in this Subsection

{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}};{\mathbb{C}}^{m}),\quad{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{s}=H^{k}_{s}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}};{\mathbb{C}}^{m}),\quad{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{s^{-}}=\cap_{t<s}{\mathcal{H}}^{k}_{t};\quad k,s\in{\mathbb{R}}.

The scalar product in
{\mathcal{H}}
concerns only the
x_{a_{0}}
-variable and
will be denoted as
\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{0}
, but we shall allow
ourselves (slightly abusively) to use the same notation for the inner product on
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}})
. Define
S:{\mathcal{H}}\to L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
by

S:f=(f_{1},\dots,f_{m})\to Sf=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\varphi_{j}(x^{a_{0}})f_{j}(x_{a_{0}}).

(281)

From Section
6
we have the representation formula for the resolvent,

R(z)=E(z)-E_{+}(z)E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}E_{-}(z)\,\text{ for }\,\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0,

(282)

where

\displaystyle E(z)

\displaystyle=R^{\prime}(z),

\displaystyle E_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=(1-R^{\prime}(z)I_{0})S,

\displaystyle E_{-}(z)

\displaystyle=S^{*}\big(1-I_{0}R^{\prime}(z)\big),

\displaystyle E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)

\displaystyle=(z-\lambda_{0})-(P_{0}+S^{*}{I_{0}}S-S^{*}I_{0}R^{\prime}(z)I_{0}S),

where
P_{0}=-\Delta_{x_{a_{0}}}{\textbf{1}}_{m}
with
{\textbf{1}}_{m}

being the identity matrix of size
m
and
I_{0}=\sum_{b\not\subset a}V_{b}(x^{b})
. We assume
(
285a
) stated below. Then one has the following limiting absorption principle for
H^{\prime}
,

\forall{s>\tfrac{1}{2}}:\quad\|\langle{x}\rangle^{-s}R^{\prime}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\langle{x}\rangle^{-s}\|\leq C_{s}\text{ for }\lambda\text{ near }\lambda_{0}.

(283)

This follows from Theorem
3.13
for
\breve{R}(z)
, recalling
R^{\prime}(z)=\breve{R}(z)\Pi^{\prime}
. Decompose
P^{\pm}=-E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)
as

P^{\pm}=P_{0}+U^{\pm},

(284a)

where

U^{\pm}=S^{*}{I_{0}}S-S^{*}I_{0}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)I_{0}S.

(284b)

In addition to (
279
) we assume that

\displaystyle\dim{\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}}=3\,\text{ and }\,\lambda_{0}\not\in\sigma_{\mathrm{pp}}(H^{\prime}),

(285a)

\displaystyle\rho\geq{1}\,\text{ and }\,S^{*}{I_{0}}S\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}_{-\rho{{}_{1}}}^{1},{\mathcal{H}}_{\rho{{}_{1}}}^{-1})\mbox{ with }\,\rho_{1}\geq\tfrac{3}{2},

(285b)

\displaystyle\rho\geq\rho_{1}-\tfrac{1}{2}.

(285c)

Recall that
\rho>0
is the rate of decay of the pair potentials as
specified by (
22
).
The condition (
285c
) will be convenient (although not
essential) in Section

16.3
; it is not used in the present
section. (Note that with (
285c
) the operator
U(z)
of Section

16.3
fulfills
U(z)\in{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s;-1,s)
for
s<\rho_{1}
uniformly in
z
near zero with
\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0
.) Imposing only (
285b
) obviously the property
(
285c
) is valid for

\rho_{1}=\tfrac{3}{2}
. This weak version of (
285c
) will be very useful.

According to discussions in Section 2.2, (
283
) and the condition (
285b
) imply

U^{\pm}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s},{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{s})\,\text{ for }\,s<\tfrac{3}{2}.

(286)

The free resolvent
r_{0}(z)=(P_{0}-z)^{-1}
can be expanded in appropriately weighted spaces as

r_{0}(z)=G_{0}+z^{\frac{1}{2}}G_{1}+zG_{2}+\cdots,

(287)

see (
352
),
and
K^{\pm}=G_{0}U^{\pm}
are compact operators on
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
for

s\in(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2})
.
Using the integral kernel of
G_{0}
, one deduces that

\ker_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}}(1+K^{\pm})\subset{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}
. Set
{\mathcal{K}}=\ker_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}}(1+K^{+})
for

s\in(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2})
. Let for any
s<\tfrac{3}{2}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}=\{u\in L^{2}_{-s}({\mathbf{X}})|\,(H-\lambda_{0})u=0,\,\Pi^{\prime}u\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{1/2,0}({\mathbf{X}})\}.

Theorem 5.6
.

Assume conditions (
279
),
(
285a
) and (
285b
), and let
s\in(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2})
.

1.

For any
v\in{\mathcal{K}}

R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)I_{0}Sv=R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)I_{0}Sv\quad\mbox{ and }\quad U^{+}v=U^{-}v\in{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{(2\rho{{}_{1}}-1/2)^{-}}}.

(288)

Moreover

{\mathcal{K}}=\ker_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}}(1+K^{-})=\ker_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}}(P^{+})=\ker_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}}(P^{-}).

(289)

2.

For any
v=(v_{1},\cdots,v_{m})\in{\mathcal{K}}
and
k=1,\dots,m

v_{k}(x_{a_{0}})=-\frac{\langle{1,U^{\pm}_{k1}v_{1}+\cdots+U^{\pm}_{km}v_{m}}\rangle_{0}}{4\pi|x_{a_{0}}|}+w_{k}(x_{a_{0}})\text{ for }|x_{a_{0}}|\geq{1},

(290)

where
w=(w_{1},\cdots,w_{m})\in{\mathcal{H}}
.

3.

Let
v\in{\mathcal{K}}
. Then
v\in{\mathcal{H}}
if and only if

\forall c\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}:\quad\langle{c,U^{\pm}v}\rangle_{0}=0.

(291)

Here
c\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
is considered as an element of
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
,
s>\frac{3}{2}
.

4.

u\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}
if and only if
u=(S-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)I_{0}S)v

for some
v\in{\mathcal{K}}
. In this case
v
is uniquely given by
v=S^{*}u\in{\mathcal{K}}
.

5.

u\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}\cap L^{2}{({\mathbf{X}})}
if and only if
v=S^{*}u\in{\mathcal{K}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}
.

Proof.

We may argue essentially as in Section
14.4
, in
particular using Lemmas

4.10
and
4.11
, although the
setup is different there (being a multiple two-cluster case). The
parametrix
G_{0}
is of course simpler to use than the more general construction
used in Section
14.4
, but the resulting null space

\ker_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}}(1+K^{+})
is
independent of the specific choice of
parametrix to define
K^{+}
, cf. Remark
5.4
. Accepting
a detour to Section
14.4
the identification of
functionals would follow like in the proof of Theorem

5.3
; alternatively
2
follows from the
asymptotics of
G_{0}
, cf. Remark
5.4
. ∎

We are lead to define the following effective version of the notion of threshold resonances.

Definition 5.7
.

1.

\lambda_{0}
is a
resonance of
H
if the
equation
Hu=\lambda_{0}u
admits a solution in

{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{(-1/2)^{-}}:=\cap_{s>1/2}\,{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s}

which is not in
H^{1}
. The multiplicity
of the resonance
\lambda_{0}
is defined as the dimension of the quotient
space

{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{(-1/2)^{-}}/\ker(H-\lambda_{0})_{|H^{1}}
.

2.

0
is a
resonance of
P^{\pm}
if the equation

P^{+}v=0
(and therefore
P^{-}v=0
, and vice versa) has a solution
v\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}\setminus{\mathcal{H}}^{1}
. The multiplicity of the resonance zero is defined as
the dimension of the quotient space

\ker P^{+}_{|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}}/\ker P^{+}_{|{\mathcal{H}}^{1}}
.

Note the consequence of Theorem

5.6
that if zero is a resonance of
P^{\pm}
then its multiplicity
does not exceed
m
. As in Subsection
16.1.1
we
can, based on those definitions, introduce Cases
0-3
, and
Theorem

5.6
then shows that the threshold spectral properties of
H
at
\lambda_{0}
are determined by those of
P^{\pm}
at zero.
In fact, completely parallel to the case of the lowest threshold

\Sigma_{2}
,
\lambda_{0}
is a regular point (resp., an exceptional
point of the first kind, the second kind, the third kind) of
H
if
and only if zero is a regular point (resp., an exceptional point of the first kind, the second kind, the third kind) of
P^{\pm}
.

Assume zero is a resonance of
P^{\pm}
. Then the quotient space

{\mathcal{K}}/{\mathcal{H}}:={\mathcal{K}}/({\mathcal{K}}\cap{\mathcal{H}})
has dimension
\kappa=\dim{\mathcal{K}}/{\mathcal{H}}\geq 1
. We call
\kappa
the multiplicity of the zero resonance of

P^{\pm}
. Let
\mu=\dim{\mathcal{K}}
.
For
\phi=(v_{1},\cdots,v_{m})\in{\mathcal{K}}
, define
c(\phi)\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
by

\displaystyle c(\phi)=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\big(\langle{1,U^{\pm}_{11}v_{1}+\cdots+U^{\pm}_{1m}v_{m}}\rangle_{0},\cdots,\langle{1,U^{\pm}_{m1}v_{1}+\cdots+U^{\pm}_{mm}v_{m}}\rangle_{0}\big).

(292)

Theorem
5.6

2
shows that
\phi\in{\mathcal{K}}
is
a resonance state of
P^{\pm}
if and only if
c(\phi)\neq 0
. Clearly

c(\cdot)
is a linear action on
{\mathcal{K}}
. It follows
that a family of resonance states
\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{k}\}
of
P^{\pm}
is linearly independent in
{\mathcal{K}}/{\mathcal{H}}
if and only if the family of vectors
\{c(\psi_{1}),\dots,c(\psi_{k})\}
is linearly independent in
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
.

Proposition 5.8
.

1.

Assume zero is a resonance of
P^{\pm}
with multiplicity
\kappa
. Then
there exists a basis

\{\psi_{1},\cdots,\psi_{\kappa}\}
of
{\mathcal{K}}/{\mathcal{H}}
such that

\displaystyle(c(\psi_{i}),c(\psi_{j}))=\delta_{ij},\quad i,j=1,\cdots,\kappa,

(293)

where
(\cdot,\cdot)
is the scalar product of
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
.

2.

Assume zero is a resonance but not an
eigenvalue of
P^{\pm}
(i.e. that
\kappa=\mu
). Then the operator
Q
defined by

Q=\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{\psi_{j},\cdot}\rangle\psi_{j}:{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{s}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s},s>\tfrac{1}{2},

(294)

is independent of the choice of basis
\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{\kappa}\}
of
{\mathcal{K}}
verifying (
293
): If
\{\psi^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}\}
is another basis of
{\mathcal{K}}
verifying (
293
), then one has

\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{\psi_{j},\cdot}\rangle\psi_{j}=\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{\psi^{\prime}_{j},\cdot}\rangle\psi^{\prime}_{j}.

Proof.

1
.  Let
\Phi=\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{\kappa}\}
be a basis of
{\mathcal{K}}/{\mathcal{H}}
.
Then the rank of the matrix
C(\Phi)=(c(\phi_{1}),\dots,c(\phi_{\kappa}))\in{\mathcal{M}}_{m\times\kappa}
is equal to
\kappa
, where

c(\phi_{j})
is considered as the
j
’th column of
C(\Phi)
.
Consequently
C(\Phi)^{*}C(\Phi)
is positive definite.
Let
M_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\kappa\times\kappa}({\mathbb{C}})
be the positive definite
Hermitian matrix obeying
M_{0}^{2}=(C(\Phi)^{*}C(\Phi))^{-1}
.
Set
M_{0}=(m_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq\kappa}
and define

\psi_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{\kappa}m_{ki}\phi_{k},\quad i=1,\cdots,\kappa.

(295)

Then
\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{\kappa}\}
is also a basis of
{\mathcal{K}}/{\mathcal{H}}
.

Let
C(\Psi)
be the matrix defined in the same way as
C(\Phi)
with
\Phi=\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{\kappa}\}
replaced by

\Psi=\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{\kappa}\}
. Then

c(\psi_{j})=\sum_{k=1}^{\kappa}m_{kj}c(\phi_{k});\quad j=1,\cdots,\kappa.

This shows
C(\Psi)=C(\Phi)M_{0}
and

C(\Psi)^{*}C(\Psi)=M_{0}C(\Phi)^{*}C(\Phi)M_{0}=1\text{ in }{\mathcal{M}}_{\kappa\times\kappa}({\mathbb{C}}).

It follows that
\Psi=\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{\kappa}\}
is a basis of
{\mathcal{K}}/{\mathcal{H}}
verifying the normalization condition (
293
).

2
.
Let
t_{ij}\in{\mathbb{C}}
,
1\leq i,j\leq\kappa
, be such that

\psi^{\prime}_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}t_{ij}\psi_{j},\quad i=1,\cdots,\kappa.

Then
c(\psi^{\prime}_{i})=\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}t_{ij}c(\psi_{j})
in
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
.
The condition
(c(\psi^{\prime}_{i}),c(\psi^{\prime}_{j}))=\delta_{ij}
becomes

\displaystyle(c(\psi^{\prime}_{i}),c(\psi^{\prime}_{j}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{l,m=1}^{\kappa}\overline{t_{il}}t_{jm}(c(\psi_{l}),c(\psi_{m}))

\displaystyle=\sum_{l=1}^{\kappa}\overline{t_{il}}t_{jl}=\delta_{ij}

for
i,j=1,\dots,\kappa
. This means that if both
\{\psi_{1},\dots,\psi_{\kappa}\}
and
\{\psi^{\prime}_{1},\dots,\psi^{\prime}_{\kappa}\}
satisfy (
293
), the matrix
T=(t_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq\kappa}
is unitary. We obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}\langle{\psi^{\prime}_{i},\cdot}\rangle\psi^{\prime}_{i}=\sum_{l,m=1}^{\kappa}\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{\kappa}\overline{t_{il}}t_{im}\Big)\langle{\psi_{l},\cdot}\rangle\psi_{m}=\sum_{l,m=1}^{\kappa}\delta_{lm}\langle{\psi_{l},\cdot}\rangle\psi_{m}=\sum_{l=1}^{\kappa}\langle{\psi_{l},\cdot}\rangle\psi_{l}.

∎

The normalization condition of the resonance
states (
293
) will be used to compute the leading term of the
resolvent
R(z)=(H-z)^{-1}
for
z
near
\lambda_{0}
and
\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0
in the case
\lambda_{0}
is a resonance but not an eigenvalue of

H
. The normalization condition (
274
) can be regarded as a special case of (
293
),
and Proposition
5.8

2
is also valid for the
case
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
is a double two-cluster threshold.

A main result on the resolvent expansion in Subsection

16.3
is for the case where
\lambda_{0}
is
not an eigenvalue of
H
, see Theorem
5.24
, Case 0 and Case 1.
However the case where
\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue (and possibly a resonance
as well) is also studied, cf. Subsection

16.1.1
. For the physical models we then need an
additional weak decay property of the corresponding
L^{2}
-eigenfunctions, see
Theorem
5.26
. On the other hand with this additional
assumption we obtain the expansion of the resolvent up to
second order.

16.2
Resolvent asymptotics near the lowest
threshold

In this subsection, we keep the conditions and the notation of
Subsection
16.1.1
, in particular,
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
is a double two-cluster threshold.
We want to study the asymptotics of the resolvent
R(z)=(H-z)^{-1}
near
\Sigma_{2}
, using
the formula (
259
) for
R(z)
.
We let
P(z)=-E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+z)
and decompose
P(z)
as

P(z)=P_{0}+U(z)-z\text{ and }U(z)=U+zU_{1}+z^{2}U_{2}(z)

(296a)

with
P_{0}
and
U
defined by (
263
) and (
263
), respectively, and

\displaystyle U_{1}

\displaystyle=-\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\otimes\cdot\rangle_{1},\\
\langle{\varphi_{2},\varphi_{1}\otimes\cdot}\rangle_{2}&0\end{array}\right)-K_{1}(\lambda_{0}),

\displaystyle U_{2}(z)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{j=2}^{\infty}z^{j-2}K_{j}(\lambda_{0}),

(296d)

\displaystyle K_{j}(\lambda_{0})

\displaystyle=(\langle{\varphi_{l},I_{l}(R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}))^{j+1}I_{m}\varphi_{m}\cdot}\rangle)_{1\leq l,m\leq 2};\quad j\in{\mathbb{N}}.

(296e)

Since
K(\lambda_{0}+z)
is holomorphic in
z
near zero and continuous from
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-1-\rho}
to
{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{1+\rho}
with
\rho>0
given by (
22
), the
above power series converges in the space
{\mathcal{L}}(1,-1-\rho;-1,1+\rho)
for example. Note that

P(z)=P-z(1-U_{1})+z^{2}U_{2}(z);\quad P=P_{0}+U.

(297)

Differently from one-body Schrödinger operators, cf.
[
JK
]
,
P(z)
is an operator
pencil depending on the spectral parameter in a non-linear way. The
following result is important for the existence of an asymptotic
expansion of
P(z)^{-1}
as
z\to 0
in the case
0
is an
eigenvalue of
P
.

Lemma 5.9
.

1-U_{1}
is positive definite on
{\mathcal{H}}
.

Proof.

Note firstly that
K_{1}(\lambda_{0})\geq 0
. In fact, for
f=(f_{1},f_{2})\in{\mathcal{H}}
, since
R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})
is a bounded self-adjoint operator,

\langle{f,K_{1}(\lambda_{0})f}\rangle=\sum_{i,j}\langle{\varphi_{i}\otimes f_{i},I_{i}(R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}))^{2}I_{j}\varphi_{j}\otimes f_{j}}\rangle=\|R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})F\|^{2}\geq 0,

where
F=\sum_{j=1}^{2}I_{j}\varphi_{j}\otimes f_{j}
. One can check that

1+\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\langle\varphi_{1},\varphi_{2}\otimes\cdot\rangle_{1}\\
\langle{\varphi_{2},\varphi_{1}\otimes\cdot}\rangle_{2}&0\end{array}\right)\geq 0.

In fact, cf. the proof of Proposition
2.7
, its expectation
value on
f
is given by

\|f_{1}\|_{1}^{2}+\|f_{2}\|_{2}^{2}+2\operatorname{Re}\langle{\varphi_{1}\otimes f_{1},\varphi_{2}\otimes f_{2}}\rangle=\|\varphi_{1}\otimes f_{1}+\varphi_{2}\otimes f_{2}\|^{2}.

It follows that

\langle{(1-U_{1})f,f}\rangle=\|\varphi_{1}\otimes f_{1}+\varphi_{2}\otimes f_{2}\|^{2}+\|R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})F\|^{2}\geq 0.

(298)

Therefore,
1-U_{1}
is non-negative on
{\mathcal{H}}
and
f\in\ker(1-U_{1})
if and only if

\varphi_{1}\otimes f_{1}+\varphi_{2}\otimes f_{2}=0\mbox{ and }R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})F=0.

In particular, since we assume (
265a
),
\ker(1-U_{1})
is reduced to
\{0\}
and
1-U_{1}
is positive definite on
{\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

Set

r_{0}(z)=(P_{0}-z)^{-1};\quad z\not\in[0,\infty).

(299)

To obtain the asymptotic expansion of
P(z)^{-1}
for
z\not\in[0,\infty)
and
z
near zero, we first use the resolvent equation

P(z)^{-1}=W(z)^{-1}r_{0}(z)\text{ where }W(z):=1+r_{0}(z)U(z).

(300)

Next we shall apply the Grushin method to study the factor

W(z)^{-1}
.

Let
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
s>N+\frac{1}{2}
. Since
\dim{\mathbf{X}}_{j}=3
, the free resolvent
r_{0}(z)
can be expanded in

{\mathcal{L}}(-1,s;1,-s)
as

r_{0}(z)=G_{0}+\sqrt{z}G_{1}+\cdots+z^{\frac{N}{2}}G_{N}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\frac{N}{2}+{\epsilon}}),

(301)

for some
{\epsilon}>0
depending on
N
and
s
, where
G_{i}=\textrm{diag}(G_{i,1},G_{i,2})
for
i\leq N
are diagonal matrices which can be
calculated explicitly, cf.
[
JK
, Lemma 2.3]
. In particular the integral kernel of
G_{i,j}
for
i=0,1
and
j=1,2
are given by

\displaystyle G_{0,j}(x_{j},y_{j})=\frac{1}{4\pi|x_{j}-y_{j}|},\quad G_{1,j}(x_{j},y_{j})=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{4\pi};\quad j=1,2.

(302)

We also recall, cf.
[
JK
, Lemmas 2.1–2.3]
, that
r_{0}(z)\in{\mathcal{L}}(-1,s^{\prime};1,-s)
if
s,s^{\prime}>1/2
and
s+s^{\prime}>2

with a Hölder continuous dependence in
z
at
z=0
. Hence

\displaystyle r_{0}(z)-G_{0}={\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{{\epsilon}})\in{\mathcal{L}}(-1,s^{\prime};1,-s)\text{ for
}s,s^{\prime}>\tfrac{1}{2}\text{ and }s+s^{\prime}>2.

(303)

We consider now expansions of the operator
W(z)
using the
expansions (
296a
), (
301
) and
(
303
).

Lemma 5.10
.

Under the conditions
(
265a
)–(
265c
) the following expansions in
z
(with
z\not\in[0,\infty)
) hold in terms of the quantities

W_{0}=1+G_{0}U,\quad W_{1}=G_{1}U,\quad W_{2}=G_{2}U+G_{0}U_{1},

and for some (small) positive number

\epsilon
(depending on given parameters
s
and
s^{\prime}
).

1.

For
s>\frac{1}{2}
,
s\geq s^{\prime}
and
\rho_{0}-s^{\prime}>\max\{\frac{1}{2},2-s\}

W(z)=W_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon})\in{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s^{\prime};1,-s).

(304a)

2.

For
s>\frac{3}{2}
,
s\geq s^{\prime}
and
\rho_{0}-s^{\prime}>\frac{3}{2}

W(z)=W_{0}+\sqrt{z}W_{1}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon})\in{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s^{\prime};1,-s).

(304b)

3.

For
s>\frac{5}{2}
,
s\geq s^{\prime}
and
\rho_{0}-s^{\prime}>\frac{5}{2}

W(z)=W_{0}+\sqrt{z}W_{1}+zW_{2}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+\epsilon})\in{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s^{\prime};1,-s).

(304c)

The assertion
1
follows from (
303
), while
2
and
3
follow from
the bounds
(
301
) with
N=1
and
N=2
, respectively. In all cases we
use (
296a
) as well.

Higher order asymptotic expansions can also be established under
stronger decay assumptions on the effective potentials.

From the identity
\langle{-U\cdot,\cdot}\rangle=\langle{P_{0}\cdot,\cdot}\rangle
on
{\mathcal{K}}=\ker(1+G_{0}U)
and an
integration by parts (cf. (
190
)) it follows that
\langle{-U\cdot,\cdot}\rangle
is a
positive quadratic form on
{\mathcal{K}}
. Let

\mu=\dim{\mathcal{K}},

and let
\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{\mu}\}
be a basis of
{\mathcal{K}}
orthonormalized with respect to
\langle{-U\cdot,\cdot}\rangle
:

\langle{-U\phi_{i},\phi_{j}}\rangle=\delta_{ij}.

This normalization is a
technical tool from
[
Wa2
]

which in general does not conform with (
274
) and
(
275
).
We make the convention that if zero is a resonance of
P
with
multiplicity
\kappa
,
\phi_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq\kappa
are resonance
states and
\phi_{j}
for
\kappa<j\leq\mu

(for
\kappa<\mu
only of course) are eigenstates of
P
.

In order to obtain the expansion of
W(z)^{-1}
, consider the Grushin problem

{\mathcal{W}}(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}W(z)&{\mathcal{S}}\\[7.22743pt]
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}&0\end{array}\right)\;:\;{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}\times{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu},

where
s\in(\frac{1}{2},\rho_{0}-\frac{1}{2})
,
{\mathcal{S}}:{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
is defined by

{\mathcal{S}}c=\sum_{j=1}^{\mu}c_{j}\phi_{j},\;\;c=(c_{1},\dots,c_{\mu})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu},

and

{\mathcal{S}}^{*}f=(\langle{-U\phi_{1},f}\rangle\dots,\langle{-U\phi_{\mu},f}\rangle),\quad f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}.

Note that
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
can be regarded as the formal adjoint of
{\mathcal{S}}
with
respect to the form
\langle{-U\cdot,\cdot}\rangle
on
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
(it is not
the Hilbert space adjoint). Define for
s
as above the map
Q:{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
by

Qf=\sum_{j=1}^{\mu}\langle{-U\phi_{j},f}\rangle\phi_{j}.

Then,

{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}=Q\mbox{ on ${\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}$ and }{\mathcal{S}}^{*}{\mathcal{S}}=1\mbox{ on }{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu},

in particular
Q
is a projection in
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
.

One can then prove (see
[
JK
,
Wa2
]
) that

{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}={\mathcal{K}}\oplus\ran(1+G_{0}U),

(305)

and that
Q
projects onto
{\mathcal{K}}

relatively to the direct sum decomposition (
305
), in particular
\ker Q=\ran(1+G_{0}U)
.
Then of course
Q^{\prime}=1-Q
is the projection from
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
onto
\ran(1+G_{0}U)
relatively to (
305
). It follows
readily that

Q^{\prime}(1+G_{0}U)Q^{\prime}
is bijective on
\ran(1+G_{0}U)
. Since
\ran(1+G_{0}U)

is closed the operator

D_{0}:=(Q^{\prime}(1+G_{0}U)Q^{\prime})^{-1}Q^{\prime}

(306)

exists and is continuous on
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
. By an argument
of perturbation based on (
304a
) it follows that for
|z|
small
enough
Q^{\prime}W(z)Q^{\prime}
is invertible on
\ran(1+G_{0}U)
with continuous inverse. Let

D(z)=(Q^{\prime}W(z)Q^{\prime})^{-1}Q^{\prime}.

The following expansions hold in
{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s;1,-s)
under the specified
conditions and with

\displaystyle D_{0}=(Q^{\prime}W_{0}Q^{\prime})^{-1}Q^{\prime},\quad D_{1}=-D_{0}W_{1}D_{0},\quad D_{2}=-D_{0}W_{2}D_{0}{+D_{0}W_{1}D_{0}W_{1}D_{0}}.

\displaystyle D(z)

\displaystyle=D_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon}),\mbox{ if }\tfrac{1}{2}<s<\rho_{0}-\tfrac{1}{2}.

(307a)

\displaystyle D(z)

\displaystyle=D_{0}+\sqrt{z}D_{1}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon}),\mbox{ if }\tfrac{3}{2}<s<\rho_{0}-\tfrac{3}{2}.

(307b)

\displaystyle D(z)

\displaystyle=D_{0}+\sqrt{z}D_{1}+zD_{2}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+\epsilon}),\mbox{ if }\tfrac{5}{2}<s<\rho_{0}-\tfrac{5}{2}.

(307c)

Note that if
{\mathcal{K}}=\{0\}
, we have
Q^{\prime}=1
and
D(z)=W(z)^{-1}
and
the asymptotic expansion of
W(z)^{-1}
is given by the one of
D(z)
.
In the following we treat the case
{\mathcal{K}}\neq\{0\}
. The assertions
(
307b
) and (
307c
) are not needed for leading term
expansions which is our main interest.

Using the operator
D(z)
, we can compute the inverse of
{\mathcal{W}}(z)
as

{\mathcal{W}}(z)^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\mathcal{E}}(z)&{\mathcal{E}}_{+}(z)\\[7.22743pt]
{\mathcal{E}}_{-}(z)&{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)\end{array}\right),

(308)

where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(z)=D(z),\quad{\mathcal{E}}_{+}(z)={\mathcal{S}}-D(z)W(z){\mathcal{S}},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-}(z)={\mathcal{S}}^{*}-{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W(z)D(z),\quad{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)={\mathcal{S}}^{*}(-W(z)+W(z)D(z)W(z)){\mathcal{S}}.

One obtains from (
308
) a representation of the inverse of
W(z)
,

W(z)^{-1}={\mathcal{E}}(z)-{\mathcal{E}}_{+}(z){\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}_{-}(z).

(309)

{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm}(z)
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)
can be expanded similarly as
D(z)
,
that is

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,0}+\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,1}+z{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,2}+\dots

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{-+,0}+\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}+z{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}+\cdots

More precisely one has the following result.

Lemma 5.11
.

Assume (
265a
)–(
265c
).

1.

One has in
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathbb{C}}^{\mu},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})
(for the
+
case) or
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s},{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu})
(for the
-
case):

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon}),\mbox{ if }s>\tfrac{1}{2}.

(310a)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,0}+\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,1}+{\mathcal{O}}(|{z}|^{\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}),\mbox{ if }s>\tfrac{3}{2}.

(310b)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,0}+\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,1}+z{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,2}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}}),\mbox{ if }s>\tfrac{5}{2}\text{ and }\rho_{0}>4.

(310c)

Here

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{+,0}={\mathcal{S}},\quad

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-,0}={\mathcal{S}}^{*},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{+,1}=-D_{0}W_{1}{\mathcal{S}},\quad

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-,1}=-{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W_{1}D_{0},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{+,2}=-(D_{0}W_{2}+D_{1}W_{1}){\mathcal{S}},\quad

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-,2}=-{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(W_{2}D_{0}+W_{1}D_{1}).

2.

One has in
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mu\times\mu}({\mathbb{C}})
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}+{\mathcal{O}}(|{z}|^{\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}).

(311a)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}+z{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}}),\mbox{ if }\rho_{0}>3.

(311b)

Here

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W_{1}{\mathcal{S}},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(W_{2}-W_{1}D_{0}W_{1}){\mathcal{S}}.

Proof.

1
.  Recall that
{\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathbb{C}}^{\mu},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s^{\prime}})
for any
s^{\prime}>\frac{1}{2}
. Since
W_{0}{\mathcal{S}}=0
, using (
304a
)-(
304c
), the following
expansions in
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathbb{C}}^{\mu};{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})
hold.

\displaystyle\forall s>\tfrac{1}{2}:\quad W(z){\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{{\epsilon}}){\mathcal{S}}.

(313a)

\displaystyle\forall s>\tfrac{3}{2}:\quad W(z){\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{z}W_{1}{\mathcal{S}}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}).

(313b)

\displaystyle\forall s>\tfrac{5}{2}:\quad W(z){\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle=(\sqrt{z}W_{1}+zW_{2}){\mathcal{S}}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}}),\quad\mbox{ if }\rho_{0}>3.

(313c)

The expansions for
{\mathcal{E}}_{+}(z)
follows from (
307a
), (
307b
)
and (
313a
)-(
313c
). The results for
{\mathcal{E}}_{-}(z)
can be proved in a similar way.

2
.  For (
311a
) we use (
304a
),
(
304b
) and (
307a
). Note that indeed since

W_{0}{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}^{*}W_{0}=0
and
D(z):{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
is
uniformly bounded for any
s\in(\tfrac{1}{2},\rho_{0}-\tfrac{1}{2})
, one obtains

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W(z){\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W_{1}{\mathcal{S}}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}),

(314a)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W(z)D(z)W(z){\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}).

(314b)

Clearly (
311a
) follows from
(
314a
) and (
314b
).

For (
311b
) we use (
304b
), (
304c
) and
(
307a
). Note that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W(z){\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W_{1}{\mathcal{S}}+z{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W_{2}{\mathcal{S}}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}}),

(315a)

\displaystyle\begin{split}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W(z)D(z)W(z){\mathcal{S}}&=z{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W_{1}D(z)W_{1}{\mathcal{S}}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}})\\
&=z{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W_{1}D_{0}W_{1}{\mathcal{S}}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}}).\end{split}

(315b)

Clearly
(
311b
) follows from (
315a
) and (
315b
).
∎

If zero is an eigenvalue but not a resonance, then
\phi_{j}\in L^{2}
for all
j
and

{\mathcal{S}}
is continuous from
{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu}
to
{\mathcal{H}}
, and by Theorem

5.3
the composition
W_{1}{\mathcal{S}}=0
.

In the case
\rho_{0}>3
the asymptotics (
311b
)
then amounts to the statement

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)=-z{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W_{2}{\mathcal{S}}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}}).

(316)

For
\rho_{0}=3
the right hand sides of (
311b
) and (
316
) make
sense since
{\mathcal{S}}
maps to
{\mathcal{H}}
, however we dont know if
these asymptotics still hold in that case. In fact we only know the following
weaker (and too poor) assertion for
\rho_{0}=3
,

\displaystyle\quad\forall\epsilon>0:\quad{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)={\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1-{\epsilon}}).

(317)

To show (
317
) we apply (
304c
) with

s^{\prime}=0
and
s\in(\frac{5}{2},3]
and conclude that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W(z){\mathcal{S}}=z{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W_{2}{\mathcal{S}}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}})={\mathcal{O}}(|z|).

Next by (
304b
),

W(z){\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{O}}(|{z}|^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon})

in
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathbb{C}}^{\mu},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})
for any
s>\frac{3}{2}
and with
\epsilon=\epsilon(s)>0
. We apply (
307a
) with
an
s
taken close to
\frac{3}{2}
. Then we argue that for
any small
\delta>0

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W(z)={\mathcal{O}}(|{z}|^{\frac{1}{2}-\kappa})\text{ in }{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}.{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu}),\,\kappa:=(s-\tfrac{3}{2}+\delta)/2,

(318)

Given (
318
) it follows that

{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W(z)D(z)W(z){\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{O}}(|{z}|^{1+\epsilon-\kappa}),

showing that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)+{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W(z){\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}-\kappa})={\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1-\kappa}).

In particular (
317
) holds.

The bound (
318
) follows by interpolating the bounds

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W(z)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}(|{z}|^{\epsilon^{\prime}})\text{ in }{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-\tfrac{5}{2}+\delta},{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu}),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W(z)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{O}}(|{z}|^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon^{\prime}})\text{ in }{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-\tfrac{3}{2}+\delta},{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu}),

in turn valid due to
(
304a
) and (
304b
), respectively. (The interpolation
requires the bounds with
\epsilon^{\prime}=0
only.)

Calculation of
{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,j}
,
j=1,2
.
To compute
explicitly these leading terms, we distinguish between different situations
according to the spectral properties of the threshold zero.

Case 1
.
Suppose zero is a resonance, but not an eigenvalue of
P
.

In this case,
\mu=1
or
2
and

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}=(\langle{U\phi_{i},G_{1}U\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq\mu}.

If
\mu=1
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}=\langle{U\phi_{1},G_{1}U\phi_{1}}\rangle_{0}
. Note that

G_{1}={\mathrm{diag}}(G_{1,1},G_{1,2})
with
G_{1,i}
,
i=1,2
, given by the rank-one operator

G_{1,i}=\mathrm{i}\langle{g_{i},\cdot}\rangle g_{i},

(319)

where
g_{i}
is the constant function in
x_{i}
introduced in
(
273a
). Using (
273b
) we then obtain

G_{1}U\phi_{1}=\mathrm{i}(c_{1}(\phi_{1})g_{1},c_{2}(\phi_{1})g_{2})

and

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}=\mathrm{i}(|c_{1}(\phi_{1})|^{2}+|c_{2}(\phi_{1})|^{2})=\mathrm{i}|c(\phi_{1})|^{2}

(320)

with
c(\phi_{1})=(c_{1}(\phi_{1}),c_{2}(\phi_{1}))\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
. If
\mu=2
, a similar computation gives

G_{1}U\phi_{j}=\mathrm{i}(c_{1}(\phi_{j})g_{1},c_{2}(\phi_{j})g_{2});\quad j=1,2.

Therefore

\langle{U\phi_{i},G_{1}U\phi_{j}}\rangle=\mathrm{i}(\overline{c_{1}(\phi_{i})}c_{1}(\phi_{j})+\overline{c_{2}(\phi_{i})}c_{2}(\phi_{j})).

It follows that

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}=(\langle{U\phi_{i},G_{1}U\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}=\mathrm{i}B_{0}

(321)

where
B_{0}=C_{0}^{*}C_{0}
and

C_{0}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}c_{1}(\phi_{1})&c_{1}(\phi_{2})\\
c_{2}(\phi_{1})&c_{2}(\phi_{2})\end{array}\right)

(322)

Summing up, one obtains in Case 1

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}=\mathrm{i}B_{0}

(323)

where
B_{0}
is a
\mu\times\mu
matrix given by

B_{0}=|c(\phi_{1})|^{2}\quad\mbox{ if }\mu=1,\text{ and }\quad B_{0}=C_{0}^{*}C_{0}\quad\mbox{ if }\mu=2.

(324)

In both cases
B_{0}
is invertible due to Theorem
5.3
. The explicit formula of
{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}
is not needed for the leading term of the resolvent expansion in Case 1.

Case 2
.
Suppose zero is an eigenvalue, but not a resonance of
P
.
In this case, all
\phi_{j}
’s are eigenfunctions and
by Theorem
5.3
on the characterization of resonance states, one has
W_{1}{\mathcal{S}}=0
which implies

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}=-{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W_{1}{\mathcal{S}}=0.

(325)

Assume
\rho_{0}>3
, so that (
311b
) and (
316
)
apply. This means more explicitly that

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}=(\langle{U\phi_{i},(G_{2}U+G_{0}U_{1})\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{i,j=1,\dots,\mu}.

Moreover, since
\phi_{j}\in L^{2},j=1,\dots,\mu
,
we can check as in
[
JK
]
that

\displaystyle\langle{U\phi_{i},G_{2}U\phi_{j}}\rangle=\langle{\phi_{i},\phi_{j}}\rangle.

(326)

In fact, writing

G_{2}=z^{-1}(r_{0}(z)-G_{0}-\sqrt{z}G_{1})+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon})

for
\operatorname{Im}z>0
and
z
near zero, one obtains using the 1st resolvent
equation that

G_{2}U\phi_{j}=-r_{0}(z)\phi_{j}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon})U\phi_{j},

and

\langle{U\phi_{i},G_{2}U\phi_{j}}\rangle=-\langle{r_{0}(\overline{z})U\phi_{i},\phi_{j}}\rangle+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon}).

Similarly
r_{0}(\overline{z})U\phi_{i}=-\phi_{i}-\overline{z}r_{0}(\overline{z})\phi_{i}
, and by taking the limit
z=\mathrm{i}\gamma\to 0
with
\gamma\in{\mathbb{R}}_{+}
,
we indeed obtain (
326
).

It is clear that
\langle{U\phi_{i},G_{0}U_{1}\phi_{j}}\rangle=-\langle{\phi_{i},U_{1}\phi_{j}}\rangle
. Therefore in Case 2, one has

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}=0,\quad{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}=(\langle{\phi_{i},(1-U_{1})\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq\mu}.

(327)

By Lemma
5.9
,
(\langle{\phi_{i},(1-U_{1})\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq\mu}
is a positive definite matrix.

Case 3
.
Suppose zero is both a resonance and an eigenvalue of
P
.

Let
\kappa
be the multiplicity of zero resonance of
P
. Then
\kappa=1
or
2
and
\kappa<\mu
.
For
\kappa+1\leq j\leq\mu
,
\phi_{j}
is an eigenfunction and therefore

W_{1}\phi_{j}=G_{1}U\phi_{j}=0,\quad j=\kappa+1,\cdots,\mu.

(328)

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}
can be computed as in Case 1. One has in
{\mathcal{M}}_{\mu\times\mu}({\mathbb{C}})

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mathrm{i}B_{0}&0&\cdots&0\\
0&0&\cdots&0\\
\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
0&0&\cdots&0\end{array}\right)

(329)

where
B_{0}
is the
\kappa\times\kappa
matrix given by (
324
) with
\mu
replaced by
\kappa
.

Using the method of Case 2 and taking notice of (
328
), we find that under the condition
\rho_{0}>3

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\mathcal{E}}_{11}^{(2)}&{\mathcal{E}}_{12}^{(2)}\\
{\mathcal{E}}_{21}^{(2)}&{\mathcal{E}}_{22}^{(2)}\end{array}\right),

(330)

where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{11}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=\left(\langle{U\phi_{i},(W_{2}-W_{1}D_{0}W_{1})\phi_{j}}\rangle\right)_{1\leq i,j\leq\kappa},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{12}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=(\langle{U\phi_{i},W_{2}\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{1\leq i\leq\kappa,\;\kappa+1\leq j\leq\mu},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{21}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=(\langle{U\phi_{i},W_{2}\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{\kappa+1\leq i\leq\mu,\;1\leq j\leq\kappa},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{22}^{(2)}

\displaystyle=(\langle{\phi_{i},(1-U_{1})\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{\kappa+1\leq i,j\leq\mu}.

If
0
is an eigenvalue of
P
we let
\Pi_{0}
denote the spectral projection
in
{\mathcal{H}}
onto the zero-eigenspace of this operator. The quantity

\Pi_{0}
enters for
Cases 2 and 3 below.

Proposition 5.12
.

The
following asymptotics as
z\to 0
and
z\not\in[0,\infty)
hold in
{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s;1,-s)
for
s>1
and
close to
1
.

Case 1
.  Suppose that zero is an exceptional point
of the first kind. Then

\displaystyle W(z)^{-1}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z}}Q_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|{z}|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}).

(331)

Here

Q_{0}=\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{-U\psi_{j},\cdot}\rangle\psi_{j}

(332)

with
\psi_{j}\in{\mathcal{K}}
such that if
\kappa=1
,
\psi_{1}
verifies the normalization condition (
274
)
and if
\kappa=2
,
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
verify the normalization condition (
275
).

Cases 2 and 3
.  Suppose that zero is an exceptional point of the
second or the third kind, respectively, and suppose that
\rho_{0}>3
. Then

W(z)^{-1}=z^{-1}(\Pi_{0}(1-U_{1})\Pi_{0})^{-1}\Pi_{0}U+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-1+{\epsilon}}).

(333)

Proof.

Case 1
.  For any
s\in(\tfrac{1}{2},\rho_{0}-\tfrac{1}{2})
and for some
{\epsilon}>0

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(z)

\displaystyle=D_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon}),\quad\mbox{ in }{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s;1,-s),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon}),\quad\mbox{ in
}{\mathcal{L}}({\mathbb{C}}^{\mu},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})\mbox{ or }{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s},{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu}),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\sqrt{z}B_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}})

where
B_{0}
is given by (
324
).
Note that
{\mathcal{E}}_{+,0}={\mathcal{S}}
,
{\mathcal{E}}_{-,0}={\mathcal{S}}^{*}
and
{\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}=Q
. It follows from (
309
) that

W(z)^{-1}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z}}{\mathcal{S}}B_{0}^{-1}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}).

(334)

If
\kappa=1
,
B_{0}=|c(\phi_{1})|^{2}
. Set

\psi_{1}=\frac{1}{|c(\phi_{1})|}\phi_{1}.

(335)

Then
\psi_{1}
verifies (
274
) and

{\mathcal{S}}B_{0}^{-1}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}=\frac{1}{|c(\phi_{1})|^{2}}\langle{-U\phi_{1},\cdot}\rangle\phi_{1}=\langle{-U\psi_{1},\cdot}\rangle\psi_{1}=Q_{0}.

If
\kappa=2
, then
B_{0}=C_{0}^{*}C_{0}
. Take
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in{\mathcal{K}}
such that

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\psi_{1}\\
\psi_{2}\end{array}\right)=\;^{t}C_{0}^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\phi_{1}\\
\phi_{2}\end{array}\right).

(336)

For
f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
, set
(v_{1},v_{2})=(\langle{-U\phi_{1},f}\rangle,\langle{-U\phi_{2},f}\rangle)\in{\mathbb{C}}^{2}
.
One has

{\mathcal{S}}B_{0}^{-1}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}f=(\phi_{1},\phi_{2})C_{0}^{-1}C_{0}^{*-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\end{array}\right)=u_{1}\psi_{1}+u_{2}\psi_{2}

where

\left(\begin{array}[]{c}u_{1}\\
u_{2}\end{array}\right)=C_{0}^{*-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}v_{1}\\
v_{2}\end{array}\right)=\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\langle{-U\psi_{1},f}\rangle\\
\langle{-U\psi_{2},f}\rangle\end{array}\right).

It follows that

{\mathcal{S}}B_{0}^{-1}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}f=\langle{-U\psi_{1},f}\rangle\psi_{1}+\langle{-U\psi_{2},f}\rangle\psi_{2}=Q_{0}f.

To show that
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
verify the normalization
condition (
275
), set
C_{0}=(c_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}
,
{}^{t}C_{0}^{-1}=(d_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq 2}
and
\psi_{k}=(\psi_{k1},\psi_{k2})
. Then

c_{j}(\psi_{k})=\langle{g_{j},U_{j1}\psi_{k1}+U_{j2}\psi_{k2}}\rangle=\sum_{m=1}^{2}d_{km}c_{jm}=\delta_{jk};\quad j,k=1,2.

Therefore
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
are resonance states verifying the
normalization condition (
275
), and
(
331
) is proved.

Case 2
.  By (
327
) and Lemmas
5.9
and
5.11

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)=zM_{1}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}}),

where the matrix
M_{1}=(\langle{\phi_{i},(1-U_{1})\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq\mu}
is positive definite.
We deduce from (
309
) that

W(z)^{-1}=-z^{-1}{\mathcal{S}}M_{1}^{-1}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-1+{\epsilon}})).

Let
{\mathcal{S}}^{\#}
be the adjoint of
{\mathcal{S}}
with respect to the scalar product

\langle{\cdot,\cdot}\rangle
of
{\mathcal{H}}
. Then
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}=-{\mathcal{S}}^{\#}U
and
M_{1}={\mathcal{S}}^{\#}(1-U_{1}){\mathcal{S}}
. The orthogonal projection
\Pi_{0}
of
{\mathcal{H}}
onto
the zero eigenspace of
P
can be expressed in terms of
{\mathcal{S}}
and

{\mathcal{S}}^{\#}
as

\displaystyle\Pi_{0}={\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{S}}^{\#})^{-1}{\mathcal{S}}^{\#}

(337a)

and obeys

\displaystyle\Pi_{0}{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}},\quad{\mathcal{S}}^{\#}\Pi_{0}={\mathcal{S}}^{\#}

(337b)

Letting
T={\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{S}}^{\#}(1-U_{1}){\mathcal{S}})^{-1}{\mathcal{S}}^{\#}
we compute
using (
337a
) and (
337b
)

\displaystyle-{\mathcal{S}}M^{-1}_{1}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}

\displaystyle=TU,

\displaystyle(\Pi_{0}(1-U_{1})\Pi_{0})T

\displaystyle={\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{S}}^{\#})^{-1}{\mathcal{S}}^{\#}=\Pi_{0},

\displaystyle T(\Pi_{0}(1-U_{1})\Pi_{0})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{S}}({\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{S}}^{\#})^{-1}{\mathcal{S}}^{\#}=\Pi_{0}.

This leads to the identity
T=(\Pi_{0}(1-U_{1})\Pi_{0})^{-1}\Pi_{0}
, and
consequently that

-{\mathcal{S}}M^{-1}_{1}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}=TU=(\Pi_{0}(1-U_{1})\Pi_{0})^{-1}\Pi_{0}U,

which proves (
333
)

Case 3
.  We use again (
311b
). We want to show
{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)^{-1}

exists and to calculate its leading term as
z\to 0
and
z\not\in[0,\infty)
. To do this, let
M(z)=\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}+z{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\mu\times\mu}({\mathbb{C}})
.
Decompose
M(z)
into blocks:

M(z)=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sqrt{z}M_{11}(z)&zM_{12}\\
zM_{21}&zM_{22}\end{array}\right)

with

\displaystyle M_{11}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}B_{0}+\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{E}}_{11}^{(2)}

\displaystyle M_{ij}

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{ij}^{(2)}\quad\mbox{ for }(ij)\neq(11).

M_{22}
is positive definite by Lemma
5.9
. The diagonal part of
M(z)
is invertible and one has

M(z){\mathrm{diag}}((\sqrt{z}M_{11}(z))^{-1},(zM_{22})^{-1})=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&a\\
b&1\end{array}\right)

where

a=M_{12}M_{22}^{-1},\quad b=b(z)=\sqrt{z}M_{21}M_{11}(z)^{-1}.

Since
b={\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{|z|})
, the matrix
\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&a\\
b&1\end{array}\right)
is invertible for

z\not\in[0,\infty)
and
|z|

small, and an elementary calculation gives

\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&a\\
b&1\end{array}\right)^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}(1-ab)^{-1}&-a(1-ba)^{-1}\\
-b(1-ab)^{-1}&(1-ba)^{-1}\end{array}\right).

Consequently we obtain a formula for the inverse of
M(z)

for
z\not\in[0,\infty)
with
|z|
small, and from that we read off that

zM(z)^{-1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\sqrt{z}}M_{11}(z)^{-1}&-{\sqrt{z}}M_{11}(z)^{-1}M_{12}M_{22}^{-1}\\
-{\sqrt{z}}M_{22}^{-1}M_{21}M_{11}(z)^{-1}&M_{22}^{-1}\end{array}\right)+{\mathcal{O}}(\sqrt{\lvert z\rvert}).

Recalling our choice of basis
\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{\mu}\}
in
{\mathcal{K}}

the map
{\mathcal{S}}
splits naturally as
{\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{S}}_{r}\oplus{\mathcal{S}}_{e}:{\mathbb{C}}^{\kappa}\oplus{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu-\kappa}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
with the range of
{\mathcal{S}}_{r}
(
{\mathcal{S}}_{e}
, respectively) included in the zero-resonance space (the zero-eigenspace, respectively) of
P
. Similarly, decompose
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}={\mathcal{S}}_{r}^{*}\oplus{\mathcal{S}}_{e}^{*}
. Then,

{\mathcal{S}}_{r}{\mathcal{S}}_{r}^{*}=Q_{r},\,{\mathcal{S}}_{r}^{*}{\mathcal{S}}_{r}=I_{\kappa},\quad{\mathcal{S}}_{e}{\mathcal{S}}_{e}^{*}=Q_{e},\,{\mathcal{S}}_{e}^{*}{\mathcal{S}}_{e}=I_{\mu-\kappa},

where
Q_{r}=\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{-U\phi_{j},\cdot}\rangle\phi_{j}
and
Q_{e}=\sum_{j=\kappa+1}^{\mu}\langle{-U\phi_{j},\cdot}\rangle\phi_{j}
.
Since
{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)=M(z)+o(|z|)
, we obtain that

-{\mathcal{E}}_{+}(z){\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}_{-}(z)=\frac{1}{z}\big(-{\mathcal{S}}_{e}M_{22}^{-1}{\mathcal{S}}_{e}^{*}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon})\big).

Since
M_{22}=(\langle{(1-U_{1})\phi_{i},\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{\kappa+1\leq i,\;j\leq\mu}
, we can verify as in Case 2 that

-{\mathcal{S}}_{e}M_{22}^{-1}{\mathcal{S}}_{e}^{*}=(\Pi_{0}(1-U_{1})\Pi_{0})^{-1}\Pi_{0}U.

Whence (
333
) is proved for Case 3 also.
∎

Recall that

\displaystyle E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+z)^{-1}=-W(z)^{-1}(P_{0}-z)^{-1},\text{ and }G_{0}U=-1\text{ on }{\mathcal{K}}.

(338)

The following result follows immediately from Proposition
5.12
.

Proposition 5.13
.

The following asymptotics hold in

{\mathcal{L}}(-1,s;1,-s)
for any
s>1
and
z\not\in[0,\infty)
with
|z|
small.

Case 1
.  Suppose zero is an exceptional point of the first kind.
Let
\kappa
be the multiplicity of zero resonance. If
\kappa=1
,
let
\psi_{1}
be a resonance state normalized according to
(
274
), and if
\kappa=2
, let
\psi_{1}
and
\psi_{2}
be
resonance states normalized according to (
275
). Then for some
{\epsilon}>0

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+z)^{-1}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{\psi_{j},\cdot}\rangle\psi_{j}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}).

(339a)

Cases 2 and 3
.  Suppose that zero is an exceptional point of the
second or the third kind, respectively, and suppose that
\rho_{0}>3
. Then for some
{\epsilon}>0

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+z)^{-1}=z^{-1}(\Pi_{0}(1-U_{1})\Pi_{0})^{-1}\Pi_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-1+{\epsilon}}).

(339b)

Proof.

We apply Proposition
5.12
, (
338
) and
the leading order expansion

\displaystyle\forall s>1:\quad(P_{0}-z)^{-1}=G_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon})\in{\mathcal{L}}(-1,s;1,-s).

(340)

∎

We are now able to give the asymptotics of the resolvent of
N
-body operator
H
near its first threshold
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
.
Let
R(\lambda_{0}+z)=(H-\lambda_{0}-z)^{-1}
. By (
259
),

R(\lambda_{0}+z)=R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+z)-(1-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+z)H)SE_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+z)^{-1}S^{*}(1-HR^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+z)).

(341)

Here
R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+z)
is the reduced resolvent which under the condition (
265a
) is holomorphic in
z
in a neighborhood of zero.
The proof of Lemma
5.2
shows that
\psi
is a zero-resonance
state of
P
if and only if
u=(1-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})H)S\psi
is an

\lambda_{0}
-resonance state of
H
and
\phi
can be recovered from
u

by
\phi=T^{*}u
, where
T^{*}=(S^{*}S)^{-1}S^{*}
.

If
\lambda_{0}
is a
resonance of
H
its multiplicity is denoted by
\kappa
.
If
\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue of
H

the spectral projection of
H
onto the corresponding
eigenspace is denoted by
\Pi_{H}
. Define

S_{I}f=I_{1}\varphi_{1}f_{1}+I_{2}\varphi_{2}f_{2},\quad f=(f_{1},f_{2})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-\infty}.

Theorem 5.14
.

Assume (
265a
)
and (
265b
). The following asymptotics hold for
R(\lambda_{0}+z)
as an operator from
H^{-1}_{s}
to
H^{1}_{-s}
,
s>1
, for
z\to 0
and
z\not\in[0,\infty)
, and
\epsilon=\epsilon(s)>0
.

Case 0
.  Suppose
\lambda_{0}
is a regular point of
H
. Then one has

R(\lambda_{0}+z)=R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})+(S-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})S_{I})D_{0}G_{0}(S^{*}-S_{I}^{*}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}))+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon}).

(342a)

Case 1
.  Suppose
\lambda_{0}
is an exceptional point of the first kind of
H
. Then

R(\lambda_{0}+z)=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{u_{j},\cdot}\rangle u_{j}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}),

(342b)

where
u_{j}=S\psi_{j}-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})S_{I}\psi_{j}

with
\psi_{j}
given in Proposition
5.13
. The
quantities
u_{j}
are resonance states of
H
obeying (
276
) and
(
277
) for
\kappa=1
and
\kappa=2
, respectively.

Cases 2 and 3
.  Suppose that
\lambda_{0}
is an exceptional point of the
second or the third kind, respectively, and suppose that
\rho_{0}>3
. Then

R(\lambda_{0}+z)=-z^{-1}\Pi_{H}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-1+{\epsilon}}).

(342c)

Proof.

Case 0
.  One has

(1-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+z)H)S=S-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})S_{I}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|)=E_{+}(\lambda_{0})+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|),

in
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s},H^{1}_{-s})
for
0\leq s\leq\tfrac{3}{2}
.
Similarly,
S^{*}(1-HR^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+z))=S^{*}-S_{I}^{*}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})+{\mathcal{O}}(z)
in
{\mathcal{L}}(H^{-1}_{s},{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{s})
.
One obtains then from (
338
), (
307a
),
(
340
) and (
341
) that

R(\lambda_{0}+z)=R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})+(S-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})S_{I})D_{0}G_{0}(S^{*}-S_{I}^{*}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}))+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{{\epsilon}})

(343)

in
{\mathcal{L}}(-1,s;1,-s)
for
s>1
,
proving (
342a
).

Case 1
.  One obtains from (
341
) and Proposition

5.13
that

\displaystyle R(\lambda_{0}+z)=-E_{+}(\lambda_{0})E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+z)^{-1}E_{-}(\lambda_{0})+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}})

in
{\mathcal{L}}(-1,s;1,-s)
for
s>1
. Let
u_{j}=E_{+}(\lambda_{0})\psi_{j}
. Since
E_{-}(\lambda_{0})^{*}=E_{+}(\lambda_{0})
it
follows from (
339a
) that

E_{+}(\lambda_{0})E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+z)^{-1}E_{-}(\lambda_{0})=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{u_{j},\cdot}\rangle u_{j}+(|{z}|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}),

proving (
342b
).

Cases 2 and 3
.  We can apply (
339b
) and
(
341
) to obtain

\displaystyle R(\lambda_{0}+z)=-z^{-1}B+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-1+{\epsilon}}),

where in terms of the spectral projection
\Pi_{0}
of
P
(for the eigenvalue zero)

B=E_{+}(\lambda_{0})(\Pi_{0}(1-U_{1})^{-1}\Pi_{0})^{-1}\Pi_{0}E_{-}(\lambda_{0}).

It remains to check that
B
is equal to the spectral
projection
\Pi_{H}
, cf.
[
Wa2
]
. Introducing
\tau=E_{+}(\lambda_{0})\Pi_{0}

it follows that
\tau^{*}=\Pi_{0}E_{-}(\lambda_{0})
, and by using the
properties
R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})S=S^{*}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})=0
and
(
298
) we compute

\tau^{*}\tau=\Pi_{0}(S^{*}(1-HR^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}))(1-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0})H)S\Pi_{0}=\Pi_{0}(1-U_{1})\Pi_{0}.

Consequently

B
can be written as

B=\tau(\tau^{*}\tau)^{-1}\tau^{*}.

It follows that
B
is an orthogonal projection with
\ran B\subset\ran\tau
and
\ker B=\ker\tau^{*}
, and therefore in fact
\ran B=\ran\tau
. Since
\ran\tau
is equal to the
\lambda_{0}
-eigenspace of

H
, we conclude
B=\Pi_{H}
as wanted.
∎

For the case where
\rho_{0}=3
and
\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue of
H

there is no information in Theorem
5.14
.
Furthermore we only extracted the leading term asymptotics. Assuming
that all eigenfunctions of
H
at
\lambda_{0}
have a certain
(weak) power decay we can obtain a resolvent expansion for that case
too, and that expansion will be to second order without further assumptions,
see Theorem
5.16
stated below.

Remark 5.15
.

For possible higher order expansions the condition
(
265c
) is needed and give limitations. For example it
could be that (
265b
) is fulfilled for Coulombic systems (for
which
\rho=1
) with
a big
\rho_{0}
in some cases, but then the condition
(
265c
) would read
\rho_{0}\leq 4
and we dont see how to
avoid this restriction. More precisely, for example we can not improve
(
264
) to be valid for any
\rho_{0}>4
for such systems
(not even with
s=\rho_{0}/2
only).
This is rooted in the fact that we do not know how to estimate the diagonal parts of the
matrix
K(\lambda_{0})
better than
{\mathcal{O}}(\langle x_{j}\rangle^{-4})
for
\rho=1
.
The main reason for not studying higher order resolvent expansions is
that
the conditions would be too strong to be interesting for Coulombic systems.

Theorem 5.16
.

Assume (
265a
),
(
265b
) and
\rho_{0}=3
. Suppose
\lambda_{0}
is an
eigenvalue of
H
and that
\ran\Pi_{H}\subset L^{2}_{t}
for some
t>3/2
. Then the following
asymptotics hold for
R(\lambda_{0}+z)
as an operator from

H^{-1}_{s}
to
H^{1}_{-s}
,
s>1
, for
z\to 0
and
z\not\in[0,\infty)
, and
\epsilon=\epsilon(s)>0
.

R(\lambda_{0}+z)=-z^{-1}\Pi_{H}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{u_{j},\cdot}\rangle u_{j}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}).

(344)

Here the second term on the right-hand side appears only if
\kappa\geq 1
, that is if
\lambda_{0}
is a
resonance of
H
,
and in that case
the
states
u_{j}
constitute a basis of resonance states of
H

(recall that either
\kappa=1
and
\kappa=2
). If on the other hand

\lambda_{0}
is not a
resonance of
H
, then
R(\lambda_{0}+z)+z^{-1}\Pi_{H}
has a limit in
norm as
z\to 0
.

One can show Theorem

5.16
by mimicking the proof of the assertion
on Case 1 in Theorem
5.14
with
H
replaced by
H_{\sigma}:=H-\sigma\Pi_{H}
,
\sigma>0
. Note that
\lambda_{0}
is not an
eigenvalue of
H_{\sigma}
. Letting

R_{\sigma}(\zeta)=(H_{\sigma}-\zeta)^{-1}
we decompose

\displaystyle R(\lambda_{0}+z)=R_{\sigma}(\lambda_{0}+z)-z^{-1}\Pi_{H}+(z+\sigma)^{-1}\Pi_{H},

(345)

and conclude that it suffices to show that for a fixed small
\sigma>0

R_{\sigma}(\lambda_{0}+z)=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{u_{j},\cdot}\rangle u_{j}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}).

(346)

This can largely be shown
as before since the last assumption in (
265a
) is valid with

H^{\prime}
replaced by
H_{\sigma}^{\prime}:=\Pi^{\prime}H_{\sigma}\Pi^{\prime}
for
\sigma>0

small (by an argument
of perturbation). We can largely mimic the previous procedure, now for
H_{\sigma}

rather than for
H
, cf. Remark
3.21

2
which will be particularly relevant
for similar constructions in the next sections. However we need to argue
that the resonance states
u_{j}
in (
346
), which a priori are
resonance states of
H_{\sigma}
, in fact also are resonance states
of

H
. For that it suffices in turn to note that
\Pi_{H}u_{j}=0
, which
follows from the fact that

-\sigma\Pi_{H}u_{j}=\Pi_{H}(H_{\sigma}-\lambda_{0})u_{j}=0
. If

\kappa_{\sigma}
denotes the dimension of the space of resonance
states
of
H_{\sigma}
, we see that
\kappa_{\sigma}\leq\kappa
. The converse

\kappa_{\sigma}\geq\kappa
follows by modifying any given base of resonance states
of
H
by projecting out the corresponding components in
\ran\Pi_{H}
, yielding a base of resonance states
of
H_{\sigma}
. Whence our use of the notation
\kappa
in
(
346
), rather than the initially correct quantity
\kappa_{\sigma}
,
is justified.

Another comment relates to (
266
). The added term
-\sigma\Pi_{H}
is not local, and if we introduce the notation
U_{\sigma}
and

P_{\sigma}
for the corresponding reduced quantities, the corresponding
version of (
266
) does not follow the same way unless
\ran\Pi_{H}\subset L^{2}_{t}
for some
t>2
. (But we only assume
this for some
t>3/2
, so if
t\in(3/2,2]
a different
argument is needed.) Nevertheless we have the following version
of (
267
) for any
v=(v_{1},v_{2})\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}
and
P_{\sigma}v=0
:

v=0\Longleftrightarrow\langle 1,U_{\sigma,j1}v_{1}+U_{\sigma,j2}v_{2}\rangle=0;\quad j=1,2.

(347)

This follows by noting that if
the functionals to the right
vanish on
v
, then the proof of
(
266
) shows that
v\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(-1/2)^{+}}
. Then the
corresponding
u\in H^{1}_{(-1/2)^{+}}
(given by the
inverse eigentransform), and this
cannot hold unless
u\in L^{2}
since
by the above discussion

(H-\lambda_{0})u=(H_{\sigma}-\lambda_{0})u=0
,
and therefore we can conclude that

u\in L^{2}
using Theorem

4.7
.
Since
\lambda_{0}
is not an
eigenvalue of
H_{\sigma}
it follows that
u=0
and then in turn
that
v=0
. With (
347
)
in place we can mimic the proof of the assertion
on Case 1 in Theorem
5.14
with
H
replaced by
H_{\sigma}=H-\sigma\Pi_{H}
and conclude (
346
).

Remarks 5.17
.

1.

We used, among others, the conditions
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}\not\in\sigma_{\mathrm{pp}}(H^{\prime})
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{2}=\{0\}
. The
case
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{\mathrm{pp}}(H^{\prime})
can be treated as in
[
Wa2
]

when
\Sigma_{2}
is a non-multiple two-cluster threshold. The same
statements as those of Theorem
5.14
remain true, but their
proof requires a more complicated Grushin reduction, cf. Sections

8
and
15
. If

{\mathcal{F}}_{1}\cap{\mathcal{F}}_{2}\neq\{0\}
, one can still reduce the spectral
analysis of
H
at
\lambda_{0}
to a one-body problem, cf. Sections

9
and

15
. The conditions
\dim{\mathbf{X}}_{j}=3
and

\rho_{0}\geq 3
of (
265a
) and (
265b
), as well as
(
265c
), simplify the resolvent expansion at the threshold

\lambda_{0}
and fit well with the physics models of Sections

2
and
3
.

2.

The very last assertion of Theorem
5.16
only needs
\ran\Pi_{H}\subset L^{2}_{t}
for some
t>1
(rather than this decay for
some
t>3/2
, as required in Theorem
5.16
) due to the fact that
\lambda_{0}
, under the given hypotheses, is neither an eigenvalue
nor a resonance of
H_{\sigma}
.

3.

The cases
where
\dim{\mathbf{X}}_{j}\neq 3
or the effective potential decays slowly
or has a critically decaying part were studied in Chapter 4 and we
obtained some threshold spectral properties of
H
. One may for these
cases try to combine the existing results for one-body problems and
the reduction made in Chapter 2 to establish the asymptotics of the
resolvent of
H
near
\lambda_{0}
. Although leaving this issue
partly as an open question to
the interested reader, see however Section

18
, let us remark that the effectively slowly
decaying potential cases corresponding to Subsections

13.2
,

13.3
,

14.2
and
14.3
are
relatively easy to treat due to the fact that the threshold is not a
resonance and eigenfunctions (if existing) have arbitrary polynomial
decay (cf. Theorem
4.17

1
in the context of
physics models).
We make use of this simplicity in Chapter

6
, see Remark
6.1
. This is in
the context of a possible higher two-cluster
threshold subject to conditions of Subsections

13.2
,

13.3
,

14.2
and
14.3
. However we
do treat a special case with a critical decaying part of the
effective potential, see Subsection
21.2
. Using the theory of the present chapter we treat in
Subsections
21.3
and
21.4
the fastly decaying
case for the physics models with the occurrence of a resonance at a possible higher two-cluster
threshold.

16.3
Resolvent asymptotics near higher two-cluster thresholds

Assume (
279
) and (
285a
)–(
285c
)
as in Subsection
16.1.2
. We shall tacitly use
the notation from that subsection. Let
P(z)=-E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+z)
for
z
near zero and
\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0
. Then

P(z)
can be written as

P(z)=P_{0}+U(z)-z,

(348)

where
P_{0}=-\Delta_{x_{a_{0}}}{\textbf{1}}_{m}
and
U(z)=S^{*}I_{0}S-S^{*}I_{0}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+z)I_{0}S
. Here
{\textbf{1}}_{m}
denotes the
identity matrix in
{\mathcal{M}}_{m\times m}({\mathbb{C}})
.
Applying Theorem 3.18, one has for
\pm\operatorname{Im}z>0
and
j\in{\mathbb{N}}_{0}

with
j<\rho_{1}

\displaystyle U(z)

\displaystyle=U^{\pm}+zU_{1}^{\pm}+\cdots+z^{j}U_{j}^{\pm}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{j+{\epsilon}}),

(349)

as bounded operators from
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
to
{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{s}
for any
s<\rho_{1}-j
and for some
{\epsilon}>0
depending on
s
and

\rho
. Furthermore

\displaystyle U^{\pm}

\displaystyle=S^{*}I_{0}S-S^{*}I_{0}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)I_{0}S,

\displaystyle U_{j}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=-S^{*}I_{0}(R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0))^{j+1}I_{0}S;\quad j\geq 1.

See the proof of Lemma
5.19

for an elaboration for the relevant cases (we shall only need
(
349
) for
j=0
and

j=1
).

Recall that
{\mathcal{K}}=\ker_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}}(1+G_{0}U^{\pm})
for

s\in(\tfrac{1}{2},\tfrac{3}{2})
, but that this space is independent of
such
s
as well as of the sign
\pm
.

Lemma 5.18
.

The quadratic form
q(\cdot)=\langle{(1-U_{1}^{\pm})\cdot,\cdot}\rangle_{0}
defined on
{\mathcal{K}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}
is positive
definite and independent of sign
\pm
.

Proof.

For
v\in{\mathcal{K}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}
one has
\Pi^{\prime}I_{0}Sv\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{(3/2)^{+}}
, allowing us to conclude that
R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)^{2}I_{0}Sv\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{(-3/2)^{-}}
. Hence
q
is
well-defined. Now
R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)I_{0}Sv=R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)I_{0}Sv

and
R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)I_{0}Sv\in L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
(cf. Theorem
4.15
, Theorem
5.6
(a) and the part of the
proof of Theorem

5.6
based on microlocal resolvent estimates). These facts
allow us to compute

\displaystyle q(v)

\displaystyle=\langle{(1+S^{*}I_{0}R^{\prime}\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)^{2}I_{0}Sv,v}\rangle

\displaystyle=\|v\|^{2}+\langle{R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)I_{0}Sv,R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)I_{0}Sv}\rangle

\displaystyle=\|v\|^{2}+\|R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)I_{0}Sv\|^{2}\geq\|v\|^{2},

proving that
q(\cdot)
is positive definite on
{\mathcal{K}}\cap{\mathcal{H}}
.
∎

We shall study the asymptotics of the resolvent
R(\lambda_{0}+z)=(H-\lambda_{0}-z)^{-1}
as
z\to 0
and
\pm\operatorname{Im}z>0
. Set

r_{0}(z)=(P_{0}-z)^{-1};\quad z\not\in[0,\infty).

(350)

To obtain the asymptotic expansion
of
P(z)^{-1}
for
z\not\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
z
near zero, we use as in
Section
16.2
the resolvent equation

P(z)^{-1}=(1+r_{0}(z)U(z))^{-1}r_{0}(z)

(351)

and apply the Grushin method to study
(1+r_{0}(z)U(z))^{-1}
. For
simplicity we shall mostly consider the case
\operatorname{Im}z>0
only.

Let
N\geq 1
and
s>N+\frac{1}{2}
. Similarly to the (partly scalar) case
studied in Section
16.2
, the free resolvent
r_{0}(z)
can be expanded

{\mathcal{L}}(-1,s;1,-s)
as

r_{0}(z)=G_{0}+\sqrt{z}G_{1}+\cdots+z^{\frac{N}{2}}G_{N}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\frac{N}{2}+{\epsilon}}),

(352)

for some
{\epsilon}>0
depending on
N
and
s
, where
G_{j}
is the

m\times m
diagonal matrix (with operator-valued entries) whose
integral kernel is given by

\displaystyle\tfrac{\mathrm{i}^{j}}{4\pi}|x_{a_{0}}-y_{a_{0}}|^{j-1}{\textbf{1}}_{m};\quad j\in{{\mathbb{N}}_{0}}.

(353)

In addition to the higher order expansions
(
352
) we record the zero’th order expansion (
303
) (with an
obvious change of interpretation).

Since
U^{+}=U^{-}
on
{\mathcal{K}}
it follows that

\langle{-U^{+}\cdot,\cdot}\rangle
is a quadratic form on
{\mathcal{K}}
. By the
identity
\langle{-U^{+}\cdot,\cdot}\rangle=\langle{P_{0}\cdot,\cdot}\rangle
and an
integration by parts it follows that
\langle{-U^{+}\cdot,\cdot}\rangle
is a
positive quadratic form.
Hence there exists a basis
\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{\mu}\}
of
{\mathcal{K}}

which is orthonormal with respect to
\langle{-U^{+}\cdot,\cdot}\rangle
. If zero
is a resonance of
P^{+}
, we denote by
\kappa
its multiplicity. We
use the convention that the functions
\phi_{j}
for
1\leq j\leq\kappa

are resonance states of
P^{+}
while the other
\phi_{j}
’s are eigenfunctions of
P^{+}
.

With the above notation, we can apply the Grushin method to obtain
asymptotic expansions of
W(z)^{-1}
as
z\to 0
,
\operatorname{Im}z>0
, where

W(z):=1+r_{0}(z)U(z).

The leading order expansion of

W(z)
is given by

W(z)=1+G_{0}U^{+}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon})\in{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s^{\prime};1,-s),

(354)

for
\operatorname{Im}z>0
and
\tfrac{1}{2}<s,s^{\prime}<\rho_{1}
and for some
\epsilon=\epsilon(s,s^{\prime})>0
. In particular this holds under the minimum
conditions
\rho=1
and
\rho_{1}=\tfrac{3}{2}
, see Lemma

5.19

1
below and the
outlined proof.

A higher order expansion of

W(z)
needs a stronger condition than

\rho_{1}=\tfrac{3}{2}
and therefore also
\rho>1
. With such condition we can apply Theorem

3.18
on higher powers of the reduced resolvent and
obtain the following extension of (
354
). The assertions

2
and
3
clearly exclude the interesting case

\rho=1
. Nevertheless we will later show a higher order expansion of

W(z)\phi
when applied to vectors
\phi\in{\mathcal{K}}
, even when
\rho=1
,
see the proof of Lemma
5.21
. This will in fact yield a non-trivial
resolvent expansion in the exceptional case of the first kind when
\rho=1
.

Lemma 5.19
.

Suppose (
279
) and
(
285a
)–(
285c
). In terms of the quantities

W_{0}=1+G_{0}U^{+},\quad W_{1}=G_{1}U^{+},\quad W_{2}=G_{2}U^{+}+G_{0}U^{+}_{1},

(355)

the following expansions in
z
(with
\operatorname{Im}z>0
) hold for some
\epsilon=\epsilon(s,s^{\prime})>0
.

1.

For
s,s^{\prime}\in(\frac{1}{2},\rho_{1})
,
s\geq s^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle W(z)=W_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon})\in{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s^{\prime};1,-s).

(356a)

2.

If
\rho_{1}>\frac{3}{2}
, then for
s,s^{\prime}\in(\frac{3}{2},\rho_{1})
with
s\geq s^{\prime}
,

\displaystyle W(z)=W_{0}+\sqrt{z}W_{1}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon})\in{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s^{\prime};1,-s).

(356b)

3.

If
\rho_{1}>\frac{5}{2}
, then for
s\in(\frac{5}{2},\rho_{1})
,
s\geq s^{\prime}
and
s^{\prime}\in(\frac{3}{2},\rho_{1}-1)

\displaystyle W(z)=W_{0}+\sqrt{z}W_{1}+zW_{2}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+\epsilon})\in{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s^{\prime};1,-s).

(356c)

Proof.

By Theorem
3.18
there exists for any
j\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
{\epsilon}>0
a constant
C>0
such that

\|\langle{x}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}-j-{\epsilon}}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+z)^{j}\langle{x}\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}-j-{\epsilon}}\|\leq C,

and therefore

\|\langle{x}\rangle^{-\frac{3}{2}-{\epsilon}}\big(R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+z)-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)\big)\langle{x}\rangle^{-\frac{3}{2}-{\epsilon}}\|\leq C|z|

for
z
near zero with
\operatorname{Im}z>0
. Using complex
interpolation we then obtain the following Hölder estimate for the reduced
resolvent for
0<r^{\prime}<r\leq 1
,

\lVert\langle{x}\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}-r}\big(R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+z)-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)\big)\langle{x}\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}-r}\rVert\leq C_{r,r^{\prime}}|z|^{r^{\prime}},\quad\operatorname{Im}z>0.

(357a)

In particular this bound yield the expansion (
349
) with

j=0
.

By a similar argument it follows that for
0<r^{\prime}<r\leq 1
and
\operatorname{Im}z>0
,

\lVert\langle{x}\rangle^{-\frac{3}{2}-r}\big(R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+z)-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)-zR^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)^{2}\big)\langle{x}\rangle^{-\frac{3}{2}-r}\rVert\leq C_{r,r^{\prime}}|z|^{1+r^{\prime}},

(357b)

implying the expansion (
349
) with

j=1
.

We only prove
3
since the pattern of proof of
(
356a
) and (
356c
) is the same. So suppose

\rho_{1}>\frac{5}{2}
. Applying (
352
) (with
N=2
) we first
obtain that

W(z)=1+(G_{0}+\sqrt{z}G_{1}+zG_{2})U(z)+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}})

in
{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s^{\prime};1,-s)
with
s,s^{\prime}\in(\frac{5}{2},\rho_{1})
. Note
here
that
U(z)\in{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s^{\prime};-1,s^{\prime})
with a uniform bound, cf. Theorems

3.13
and
3.18
. Making
(again) use of the boundedness of

G_{2}
and the expansion (
349
) with
j=0
we obtain

G_{2}U(z)=G_{2}U^{+}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{{\epsilon}})\text{ in }{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s^{\prime};1,-s).

It follows from (
357a
) that for
s\in(\frac{5}{2},\rho_{1})
and
s^{\prime}\in(2,\rho_{1}-\frac{1}{2})

\displaystyle\|\langle{x_{a_{0}}}\rangle^{-s}G_{1}(U(z)-U^{+})\langle{x_{a_{0}}}\rangle^{s^{\prime}}\|

(358)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C\|\langle{x_{a_{0}}}\rangle^{s-1}S^{*}I_{0}(R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+z)-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0))I_{0}S\langle{x_{a_{0}}}\rangle^{s^{\prime}}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C^{\prime}\|\langle{x}\rangle^{-\rho_{1}-\frac{1}{2}+s-1}(R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+z)-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0))\langle{x}\rangle^{-\rho_{1}-\frac{1}{2}+s^{\prime}}\|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C^{\prime\prime}|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+\delta};

this is for some
\delta>0
.

It remains to show that

G_{0}U(z)=G_{0}U^{+}+zG_{0}U_{1}^{+}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}})\text{ in }{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s^{\prime};1,-s).

This expansion for
s\in(\frac{5}{2},\rho_{1})
and
s^{\prime}\in(\frac{3}{2},\rho_{1}-1)
follows
from (
349
) with
j=1
(justified by (
357b
)). We conclude

3
.
∎

Let
{\mathcal{S}}:{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
,
s\in(\frac{1}{2},\rho_{1})
, be defined by

{\mathcal{S}}c=\sum_{j=1}^{\mu}c_{j}\phi_{j},\;\;c=(c_{1},\dots,c_{\mu})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu},

and
{\mathcal{S}}^{*}:{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}\to{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu}
by

{\mathcal{S}}^{*}f=(\langle{-U^{+}\phi_{1},f}\rangle\dots,\langle{-U^{+}\phi_{\mu},f}\rangle),\quad f\in{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}.

Let
Q:{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}\to{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
be given by

Qf=\sum_{j=1}^{\mu}\langle{-U^{+}\phi_{j},f}\rangle\phi_{j}.

One can then prove, cf. Section
16.2
, that
Q
is a projection from
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
onto
{\mathcal{K}}
,

Q^{\prime}=1-Q
is a projection from
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
onto
\ran(1+G_{0}U^{+})

and that

D^{+}_{0}=(Q^{\prime}(1+G_{0}U^{+})Q^{\prime})^{-1}Q^{\prime}

(359)

exists and is continuous on
{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}
,
s\in(\frac{1}{2},\rho_{1})
. By an argument
of perturbation, it follows that for
|z|
small enough and
\operatorname{Im}z>0
,
Q^{\prime}W(z)Q^{\prime}
is invertible on
\ran(1+G_{0}U^{+})
with continuous inverse. Let

D(z)=(Q^{\prime}W(z)Q^{\prime})^{-1}Q^{\prime}.

Then according to the varying conditions on
\rho_{1}
in Lemma

5.19
one has the following expansions in

{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s;1,-s)
:

\displaystyle D(z)

\displaystyle=D_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon}),\mbox{ if }s\in(\tfrac{1}{2},\rho_{1}).

(360a)

\displaystyle D(z)

\displaystyle=D_{0}+\sqrt{z}D_{1}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+\epsilon_{1}}),\mbox{ if }\rho_{1}>\tfrac{3}{2}\text{ and }s\in(\tfrac{3}{2},\rho_{1}).

(360b)

Here

\displaystyle D_{0}=D_{0}^{+}\text{ and }D_{1}=-D_{0}W_{1}D_{0}.

Using the operator
D(z)
, we can establish the following representation formula for
W(z)^{-1}
for
\operatorname{Im}z>0
:

W(z)^{-1}={\mathcal{E}}(z)-{\mathcal{E}}_{+}(z){\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}_{-}(z),

(361)

where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(z)=D(z),\quad{\mathcal{E}}_{+}(z)={\mathcal{S}}-D(z)W(z){\mathcal{S}},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-}(z)={\mathcal{S}}^{*}-{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W(z)D(z),\quad{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)={\mathcal{S}}^{*}(-W(z)+W(z)D(z)W(z)){\mathcal{S}}.

The operators
{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm}(z)
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)
can be expanded similarly as
D(z)
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,0}+\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,1}+z{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,2}+\dots

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{-+,0}+\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}+z{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}+\cdots

More precisely one has the following two lemmas on these expansions.

Lemma 5.20
.

Suppose (
279
) and (
285a
)–(
285c
). One has in
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathbb{C}}^{\mu},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})
(for the
+
case) or
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s},{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu})
(for the
-
case):

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon}),\mbox{ if }s\in(\tfrac{1}{2},\rho_{1}).

(362a)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,0}+\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,1}+{\mathcal{O}}(|{z}|^{\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}),\mbox{ if }\rho_{1}>\tfrac{3}{2}\text{ and }s\in(\tfrac{3}{2},\rho_{1}).

(362b)

Here

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{+,0}={\mathcal{S}},\quad

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-,0}={\mathcal{S}}^{*},

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{+,1}=-D_{0}W_{1}{\mathcal{S}},\quad

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-,1}=-{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W_{1}D_{0}.

The proof of Lemma
5.20
is the same as that for the
corresponding assertions in Lemma

5.11
and will not be repeated here. For the leading order
resolvent expansion only (
362a
) is needed.

Formally one can expand the matrix

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)={\mathcal{S}}^{*}(-W(z)+W(z)D(z)W(z)){\mathcal{S}}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\mu\times\mu}({\mathbb{C}})

as

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)=\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}(z)+z{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}}),

(363a)

where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W_{1}{\mathcal{S}},

(363b)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}

\displaystyle=-{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(W_{2}-W_{1}D_{0}W_{1}){\mathcal{S}}.

(363c)

The following lemma gives a precise meaning to this expansion. Note
that (
285c
) is not imposed. Furthermore none of the
assertions (
356c
) or (
360b
) is used, and (
356b
) is not used neither for treating the first case of the lemma
(consequently the stated result applies for
\rho=1
and
\rho_{1}=\frac{3}{2}
). Note that consequently the strong condition
\rho_{1}>\tfrac{5}{2}
of
(
356c
) is not relevant for the lemma, although superficially
this condition might
appear necessary for justifying (
363a
)–(
363c
); we can
do without (
356c
).

Lemma 5.21
.

Suppose (
279
),
(
285a
) and
(
285b
).

Case 1
.  If zero is an exceptional point of the first kind of
P^{+}
, then

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)=\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}+{\mathcal{O}}(|{z}|^{\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}})

(364a)

for
z
near zero and
\operatorname{Im}z>0
, where the matrix
{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}
is given by

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}=\mathrm{i}C_{0}^{*}C_{0}

(364b)

with the
j
’th column of
