C_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{m\times\mu}({\mathbb{C}})
,
1\leq j\leq\mu
, specified as

c(\phi_{j})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}(\langle{1,\sum_{k=1}^{m}U^{+}_{1k}\phi_{j,k}}\rangle_{0},\cdots,\langle{1,\sum_{k=1}^{m}U^{+}_{mk}\phi_{j,k}}\rangle_{0})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}.

(364c)

Here
\phi_{j}
is denoted as
\phi_{j}=(\phi_{j,1},\cdots,\phi_{j,m})\in{\mathcal{K}}
.

Case 2
.  Assume zero is an exceptional point of the second kind of

P^{+}
,
\rho>1
and
\rho_{1}>\frac{3}{2}
. Then

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)=z{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}+{\mathcal{O}}(|{z}|^{1+{\epsilon}})

(365a)

for
z
near zero and
\operatorname{Im}z>0
, where

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}=(\langle{(1-U_{1}^{+})\phi_{i},\phi_{j}}\rangle_{0})_{1\leq i,j\leq\mu}.

(365b)

Case 3
.  Assume zero is an exceptional point of the third kind of

P^{+}
,
\rho>1
and
\rho_{1}>\frac{3}{2}
. Let
1\leq\kappa<\mu
denote
the multiplicity of zero resonance of
P^{+}
. Then

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)=\sqrt{z}{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}+z{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}+{\mathcal{O}}(|{z}|^{1+{\epsilon}})

(366a)

for
z
near zero and
\operatorname{Im}z>0
, where
{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}
can be written as block matrices

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\mathrm{i}C_{0}^{*}C_{0}&0\\
0&0\end{array}\right)\quad\text{ and }\quad{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,2}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}{\mathcal{E}}_{11}^{(2)}&{\mathcal{E}}_{12}^{(2)}\\
{\mathcal{E}}_{21}^{(2)}&{\mathcal{E}}_{22}^{(2)}\end{array}\right)

(366b)

with the
j
’th column of
C_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{m\times\kappa}({\mathbb{C}})
,
1\leq j\leq\kappa
, equal to

c(\phi_{j})
and with
{\mathcal{E}}_{22}^{(2)}=(\langle{(1-U_{1}^{+})\phi_{i},\phi_{j}}\rangle_{0})_{\kappa+1\leq i,j\leq\mu}
.

Proof.

Case 1
.  We start by estimating the contribution from the
second term of the representation

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)=(\langle{U^{+}\phi_{i},W(z)\phi_{j}-W(z)D(z)W(z)\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq\mu}.

Using the identity

\phi_{j}=-G_{0}U^{+}\phi_{j}
,
j\leq\mu
, one can estimate

\|W(z)\phi_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}}
for any
s\in(1,\rho_{1})
as

\|W(z)\phi_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}}\leq\|(r_{0}(z)-G_{0})U(z)\phi_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}}+\|G_{0}(U(z)-U^{+})\phi_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}}.

By the (uniform) bound
U(z)\phi_{j}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{(3/2)^{-}}
and a free
resolvent bound, possibly deduced by interpolating (
303
) and (
352
) (with
N=1
),

\|(r_{0}(z)-G_{0})U(z)\phi_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}}\leq C|z|^{\frac{1}{4}+{\epsilon}}.

From this point we fix any
s\in(1,\tfrac{5}{4})
. Then
1+\rho-s>\tfrac{3}{4}
and by (
357a
)

\displaystyle\|G_{0}(U(z)-U^{+})\phi_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}}

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}\|\langle{x}\rangle^{-1-\rho+s}(R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+z)-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0))\langle{x}\rangle^{-1-\rho+s}\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}|z|^{\frac{1}{4}+{\epsilon}}.

This proves that

\displaystyle\|W(z)\phi_{j}\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s}}\leq C|z|^{\frac{1}{4}+{\epsilon}}.

(367a)

Similarly we can prove that

\displaystyle\|W(z)^{*}U^{+}\phi_{i}\|_{{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{s}}\leq C|z|^{\frac{1}{4}+{\epsilon}}.

(367b)

Due to (
360a
) the operator
D(z)
is uniformly bounded in

{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})
, and we conclude using
(
367a
) and (
367b
) that

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)=(\langle{U^{+}\phi_{i},W(z)\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq\mu}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+2{\epsilon}}).

Next we simplify the first term to the right.
It follows by similar arguments that it is expanded as

\displaystyle\langle{U^{+}\phi_{i},W(z)\phi_{j}}\rangle_{0}=(\langle{U^{+}\phi_{i},\phi_{j}+r_{0}(z)U^{+}\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq\mu}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}),

Since
U^{+}=U^{-}
on
{\mathcal{K}}
, it follows from Corollary

3.19
that
U^{+}\phi_{i},U^{+}\phi_{j}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{(3/2)^{+}}
. Then we obtain from (
352
) (with

N=1
) that

\displaystyle\langle{U^{+}\phi_{i},W(z)\phi_{j}}\rangle_{0}=(\langle{U^{+}\phi_{i},\sqrt{z}G_{1}U^{+}\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq\mu}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}),

Using the explicit formula for the integral kernel of
G_{1}
, one obtains

\langle{U^{+}\phi_{i},G_{1}U^{+}\phi_{j}}\rangle_{0}=\mathrm{i}(c(\phi_{i}),c(\phi_{j}))

where
c(\phi_{j})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
is given by (
364c
). This shows
{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}=\mathrm{i}C_{0}^{*}C_{0}
, and we have proven the assertion for Case
1.

Case 2
.  If zero is an exceptional point of the second kind, then
\phi_{j}\in{\mathcal{H}}
and Theorem
5.6
shows
W_{1}\phi_{j}=G_{1}U^{+}\phi_{j}=0
for
1\leq j\leq\mu
.
Therefore
{\mathcal{E}}_{-+,1}=-{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W_{1}{\mathcal{S}}=0
.

By possibly making
\rho_{1}
smaller we can
assume that (
285c
) is fulfilled and therefore that
(
360a
) is valid for some
s>\frac{3}{2}
. We invoke the implied
uniform boundedness of
D(z)\in{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s;1,-s)
for such
s
, apply
(
356b
) twice and conclude that

\displaystyle{\mathcal{S}}^{*}W(z)D(z)W(z){\mathcal{S}}={\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}}).

It remains to show that

\displaystyle(\langle{U^{+}\phi_{i},W(z)\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq\mu}=z(\langle{(1-U_{1}^{+})\phi_{i},\phi_{j}}\rangle_{0})_{1\leq i,j\leq\mu}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}}).

(368)

For that we first invoke (
349
) with
j=1
and write

\displaystyle(\langle{U^{+}\phi_{i},W(z)\phi_{j}}\rangle)_{1\leq i,j\leq\mu}=-{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(1+r_{0}(z)(U^{+}+{z}U_{1}^{+})){\mathcal{S}}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{1+{\epsilon}}).

Next we note
that
U^{+}\phi_{j}\in{\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{(5/2)^{+}}
(cf. Corollary

3.19
), so that we can use the
expansion (
352
)
with
N=2
. Finally we proceed as in the proof of (
327
) to
rewrite the leading term obtained this way,
concluding (
368
).

Case 3
.  If zero is an exceptional point of the third kind, we can
combine methods used for Case 1 and Case 2 to show (
366a
) and
(
366b
). The details are omitted. ∎

We remark that for Case 1,
\rank C_{0}=\kappa=\mu
and

C_{0}^{*}C_{0}
is positive definite. Moreover
Lemma
5.18
ensures that the matrices
(\langle{(1-U_{1}^{+})\phi_{i},\phi_{j}}\rangle_{0})_{1\leq i,j\leq\mu}
for Case 2 and
(\langle{(1-U_{1}^{+})\phi_{i},\phi_{j}}\rangle_{0})_{\kappa+1\leq i,j\leq\mu}

for Case 3 of Lemma
5.21
are invertible. These properties
are used in the following calculation of the leading term for the
inverse of
W(z)
. The proof of the proposition goes largely along the lines of that of Proposition
5.12
.

If
0
is an eigenvalue of
P^{+}
(relevant for
Cases 2 and 3 below) we let
\Pi_{0}
denote the spectral projection
in
{\mathcal{H}}
onto the zero-eigenspace of this operator.

Proposition 5.22
.

Assume Conditions (
279
), (
285a
) and (
285b
). The
following asymptotics hold in
{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s;1,-s)
for
s>1

sufficiently
close to
1
and for
|z|
small with
\operatorname{Im}z>0
.

Case 1
.  Suppose that zero is an exceptional point of the first
kind. Then

W(z)^{-1}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z}}Q_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|{z}|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}).

(369a)

Here

Q_{0}=\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{-U^{+}\psi_{j},\cdot}\rangle_{0}\psi_{j}

(369b)

with vectors
\psi_{j}\in{\mathcal{K}}
obeying

(c(\psi_{j}),c(\psi_{k}))=\delta_{jk}\quad\mbox{ for }j,k=1,\cdots,\kappa.

(369c)

Here

c(\psi_{j})\in{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
is defined as
c(\phi_{j})
in Lemma
5.21
with
\phi_{j}
replaced by
\psi_{j}
and
(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the scalar product of
{\mathbb{C}}^{m}
.

Cases 2 and 3
.  Suppose that zero is an exceptional point of the
second or the third
kind, respectively,
\rho>1
and
\rho_{1}>\frac{3}{2}
.
Then

W(z)^{-1}=z^{-1}(\Pi_{0}(1-U^{+}_{1})\Pi_{0})^{-1}\Pi_{0}U^{+}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-1+{\epsilon}}).

(370)

Proof.

The results follow from Lemmas
5.20
and
5.21
and the
formula (
361
).
We only study Case 1. For
s\in(1,\rho_{1})
, one has for some
{\epsilon}>0

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(z)

\displaystyle=D_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon})\,\mbox{ in }{\mathcal{L}}(1,-s;1,-s),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm}(z)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{E}}_{\pm,0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon})\,\mbox{ in }{\mathcal{L}}({\mathbb{C}}^{\mu},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s})\mbox{ or in }{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{-s},{\mathbb{C}}^{\mu}),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(z)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\sqrt{z}B_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}),

where
B_{0}=C_{0}^{*}C_{0}
is positive definite. Therefore

W(z)^{-1}=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z}}{\mathcal{S}}B_{0}^{-1}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}).

(371)

Decompose
B_{0}^{-1}
as
B_{0}^{-1}=M_{0}^{2}
where
M_{0}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\kappa\times\kappa}({\mathbb{C}})
is a positive definite Hermitian matrix.
Set
M_{0}=(m_{ij})_{1\leq i,j\leq\kappa}
and

\psi_{i}=\sum_{k=1}^{\mu}m_{ki}\phi_{k},\quad i=1,\cdots,\kappa.

(372)

Then
\{\psi_{1},\cdots,\psi_{\kappa}\}
is a basis of resonance
functions of
P^{+}
. Similarly to the proof of
Proposition
5.8

1
we can check that

\{\psi_{1},\cdots,\psi_{\kappa}\}
verifies the normalization condition

(c(\psi_{i}),c(\psi_{j}))=\delta_{ij};\quad i,j\leq\kappa.

Hence the expansion (
369a
), with the
specification (
369b
)-(
369c
), is proved.
∎

Since
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+z)^{-1}=-W(z)^{-1}(P_{0}-z)^{-1}
and

G_{0}U=-1
on
{\mathcal{K}}
, the following result follows immediately
from Proposition
5.22
(cf. (
303
)).

Proposition 5.23
.

Assume (
279
), (
285a
) and (
285b
).
The following asymptotics hold in
{\mathcal{L}}(-1,s;1,-s)
for
s>1
and for
|z|
small with
\operatorname{Im}z>0
.

Case 1
.  Suppose zero is an exceptional point of the first kind of
P^{+}
. Let
\kappa
be the multiplicity of zero resonance. Then for some
{\epsilon}>0

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+z)^{-1}=-\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{\psi_{j},\cdot}\rangle\psi_{j}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}),

(373a)

where
\psi_{j}
,
j=1,\cdots,\kappa
, are resonance states verifying (
369c
).

Cases 2 and 3
.  Suppose zero is an exceptional point of the
second or the third
kind, respectively,

\rho>1
and
\rho_{1}>\frac{3}{2}
. Then for some
{\epsilon}>0

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+z)^{-1}=z^{-1}(\Pi_{0}(1-U^{+}_{1})\Pi_{0})^{-1}\Pi_{0}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-1+{\epsilon}}).

(373b)

A main result of this section is the following. In agrement with
previous usage, if
\lambda_{0}
is a
resonance of
H
its multiplicity is denoted by
\kappa
, and
if
\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue of
H

the spectral projection of
H
onto the corresponding
eigenspace is denoted by
\Pi_{H}
.

Theorem 5.24
.

Assume (
279
), (
285a
)
and (
285b
). The following asymptotics hold for
R(\lambda_{0}+z)
as a bounded operator from
H^{-1}_{s}
to
H^{1}_{-s}
for
any
s>1
, as
z\to 0
and
\pm\operatorname{Im}z>0
, and
\epsilon=\epsilon(s)>0
.

Case 0
.  Suppose
\lambda_{0}
is a regular point of
H
. Then one has

\displaystyle\begin{split}R(\lambda_{0}&+z)=R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\ \mathrm{i}0)\\
&+(S-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)I_{0}S)D_{0}G_{0}(S^{*}-S^{*}I_{0}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0))+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon}).\end{split}

(374)

Here the boundary value of resolvent
R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)
(resp.,
R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}-\mathrm{i}0)
) is used when
\operatorname{Im}z>0
(resp.
-\operatorname{Im}z>0
).

Case 1
.  Suppose
\lambda_{0}
is an exceptional point of the
first kind of
H
. Then

R(\lambda_{0}+z)=\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{u_{j},\cdot}\rangle u_{j}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}})

(375a)

where
u_{j}
’s are resonance states of
H
given by

u_{j}=(1-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)I_{0})S\psi_{j},\quad 1\leq j\leq\kappa,

(375b)

where
\psi_{j}
’s are the resonance states of
P^{\pm}
given in
Proposition
5.22
.

Cases 2 and 3
.  Suppose
\lambda_{0}
is an exceptional point of the
second or the third
kind of
H
, respectively,
\rho>1
and
\rho_{1}>\frac{3}{2}
.
Then

R(\lambda_{0}+z)=-z^{-1}\Pi_{H}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-1+{\epsilon}}).

(376)

Mimicking the proof of Theorem
5.14
we see that Theorem
5.24
for
\operatorname{Im}z>0
is a consequence of
Proposition
5.23
and the formula (
282
).
The case
\operatorname{Im}z<0
can be proved in the same way with
P^{+}
replaced
by
P^{-}
. Note that due to Theorem
5.6

1
, the resonance states
u_{j}
given by
(
375b
) are independent
of the choice of the sign
\pm
.

Remark 5.25
.

For Case 1 of Theorem
5.24
the leading term of the
resolvent
R(\lambda_{0}+z)
is expressed in terms of a specific
basis of resonance states
\{u_{1},\dots,u_{\kappa}\}
of
H
such
that
\psi_{j}=S^{*}u_{j}
,
j=1,\dots,\kappa
, are given as in
Proposition
5.22
. The normalization condition (
369c
)
expressed in terms of the
u_{j}
’s reads

(c(S^{*}u_{j}),c(S^{*}u_{k}))=\delta_{jk},\quad j,k=1,\dots,\kappa.

(377)

By Proposition
5.8
(b), (
375a
) remains
valid for
any
basis of resonance states
\{u_{1},\dots,u_{\kappa}\}
of
H
verifying (
377
). Note also the formula

\displaystyle c(S^{*}u_{j})=\frac{1}{2\sqrt{\pi}}\Big(\int_{\mathbf{X}}\varphi_{1}I_{0}u_{j}{\mathrm{d}}x,\dots,\int_{\mathbf{X}}\varphi_{m}I_{0}u_{j}{\mathrm{d}}x\Big),\quad j=1,\dots,\kappa.

(378)

For Cases 0 and 1,

\rho=1
is legitimate. However
Cases 2 and 3
of Theorem
5.24
have the stronger condition
\rho>1
and
therefore exclude the physics models which would imply a limitation on the application in Section

21
, see Remark
6.29

1
. However with an a priori weak decay
property of the corresponding
L^{2}
-eigenfunctions if
\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue of

H
(as for
Cases 2 and 3) we can almost verbatim mimic the proof of Theorem
5.16

and obtain a resolvent expansion also for
\rho=1
. Note however
that the property
\lambda_{0}\not\in\sigma_{\mathrm{pp}}(H_{\sigma}^{\prime})
for small
\sigma

is based on a perturbation of Proposition
3.3
,
cf.
[
AHS
]
, and note also the relevance of Remark
3.21

2
. This
expansion is
up to second
order (at least for Case 3).

Theorem 5.26
.

Assume (
279
) and
(
285a
), as well as
(
285b
) with
\rho=1
and
\rho_{1}=3/2
. Suppose
\lambda_{0}
is an
eigenvalue of
H
and that
\ran\Pi_{H}\subset L^{2}_{t}
for some
t>3/2
. Then the following
asymptotics hold for
R(\lambda_{0}+z)
as an operator from

H^{-1}_{s}
to
H^{1}_{-s}
,
s>1
, for
z\to 0
and
\pm\operatorname{Im}z>0
, and
\epsilon=\epsilon(s)>0
.

R(\lambda_{0}+z)=-z^{-1}\Pi_{H}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{u_{j},\cdot}\rangle u_{j}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}).

(379)

Here the second term on the right-hand side appears
only if
\kappa\geq 1
, that is if
\lambda_{0}
is a
resonance of
H
, and in that case

\{u_{1},\dots,u_{\kappa}\}
is a basis of resonance states of

H
being independent
of the choice of the sign
\pm
. If on the other hand

\lambda_{0}
is not a
resonance of
H
, then
R(\lambda_{0}+z)+z^{-1}\Pi_{H}
has limits in
norm as
z\to 0
,
\pm\operatorname{Im}z>0
.

Remark 5.27
.

As in Remark

5.17

2
the
condition
\ran\Pi_{H}\subset L^{2}_{t}
for some
t>1
suffices for the last assertion
of Theorem
5.26
.

17
Positive slowly decaying effective potentials

Low-energy scattering for one-body Schrödinger operators with
positive slowly decaying potentials is studied in
[
Na
,
Ya1
,
Ya2
]
.
It is shown in
[
Wa6
]
that this kind of operators satisfies Gevrey
type resolvent estimates at the threshold and this can be used to
establish large time asymptotics of the quantum dynamics with
sub-exponential time decay estimates on the remainder
[
AW
,
Wa6
]
.
In this section, we want to show similar results for the N-body
Schrödinger operator at the lowest threshold
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
,
assuming that the effective potential is positive outside a compact
set and slowly decaying at infinity.

Recall first from
[
Wa6
]
some results for one-body operators.
The model operator
H_{0}
in this framework is a closed second order elliptic operator of the form

H_{0}=-\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\partial_{x_{i}}a^{ij}(x)\partial_{x_{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}b_{j}(x)\partial_{x_{j}}+v(x),

(380)

where
a^{ij}(x)
,
b_{j}(x)
and
v(x)
are complex-valued functions in
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
,
n\geq 1
.

We assume that

a^{ij},b_{j}
are bounded
C^{1}
functions with bounded derivatives and there exists
c>0

such that

\operatorname{Re}(a^{ij}(x))\geq cI_{n},\quad\forall x\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}.

(381a)

Assume also that
v
is relatively bounded with respect to
-\Delta
with relative bound zero and there exist some constants
0<\mu<1
and
c_{0}>0
such that

\displaystyle|\langle{H_{0}u,u}\rangle|\geq c_{0}(\|\nabla u\|^{2}+\|\langle{x}\rangle^{-\mu}u\|^{2}),\quad\mbox{for all }u\in H^{2},

(381b)

\displaystyle\sup_{x}|\langle{x}\rangle^{\mu}b_{j}(x)|<\infty,\quad j=1,\cdots,n.

(381c)

The bound
(
381b
) is called a weighted coercive condition and is essential for Gevrey estimates of the resolvent at the threshold zero.

Under the assumptions (
381b
) and (
381c
),
H_{0}
is bijective from
D(H_{0})
to
R(H_{0})
.
Let
R_{0}(0):R(H_{0})\to D(H_{0})
be its algebraic inverse.

R_{0}(0)
is a densely defined and closed operator, continuous from
L^{2}_{s}
to
L^{2}_{s-2\mu}
and compact from
L^{2}_{s}
to
L^{2}_{s-2\mu-{\epsilon}}
,
{\epsilon}>0
,
for any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
(
[
Wa6
,
Ya2
]
). Thus
R_{0}(0)^{N}:L^{2}_{s}\to L^{2}_{s-2\mu N}
is bounded.
If
\operatorname{Re}H\geq 0
, one can check that the strong limit

\slim_{z\in\Omega(\delta),z\to 0}\langle{x}\rangle^{-2N\mu}(R_{0}(z)^{N}-R_{0}(0)^{N})=0,

where
\Omega(\delta)=\{z:\lvert\arg z\rvert>\frac{\pi}{2}+\delta\}
with
\delta>0
small. The following Gevrey-type estimates hold for the resolvent at the threshold zero.

Theorem 5.28

(
[
Wa6
, Theorem 2.1]
)
.

Assume conditions (
381a
), (
381b
) and (
381c
). For any
a>0
there exists
C_{a}>0
such that

\|{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x}\rangle^{1-\mu}}R_{0}(0)^{N}\|+\|R_{0}(0)^{N}{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x}\rangle^{1-\mu}}\|\leq C_{a}^{N+1}N^{\gamma N},

uniformly in
N\geq 1
. Here
\gamma=\frac{2\mu}{1-\mu}
.

From Theorem
5.28
one deduces the following result.

Corollary 5.29

(
[
Wa6
, Corollary 4.2]
)
.

Let
H_{0}=-\Delta+v(x)
be self-adjoint and positive and satisfy the assumptions of Theorem
5.28
. Then for any
a>0
there exists
C_{a}>0
such that

\|{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x}\rangle^{1-\mu}}R_{0}(z)^{N}\|+\|R_{0}(z)^{N}{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x}\rangle^{1-\mu}}\|\leq C_{a}^{N+1}N^{\gamma N},\quad\forall N\geq 1,

uniformly in
N\geq 1
and
z\in\Omega=\{\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}:|\arg\zeta|>\delta\}
with
\delta>0
.

Since
R_{0}^{(N)}(z)=N!R_{0}(z)^{N+1}
, Corollary
5.29
means that
{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x}\rangle^{1-\mu}}R_{0}(z)
belongs to the Gevrey class
{\mathcal{G}}^{(1+\gamma)}(\Omega)
, where

{\mathcal{G}}^{(1+\gamma)}(\Omega)=\{F:\Omega\to{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2})\mid\exists C>0\mbox{ s.t. }\|F^{(N)}(z)\|\leq C^{N+1}N!N^{\gamma N},\;\forall z\in\Omega,N\in{\mathbb{N}}\}

For
n=3
, the repulsive Coulomb Hamiltonian
-\Delta+\frac{c}{|x|}
,
c>0
, satisfies all conditions of Corollary
5.29
with
\mu=\frac{1}{2}
, so its resolvent belongs to
{\mathcal{G}}^{(3)}(\Omega)
in exponentially weighted spaces.
In
[
AW
,
Wa6
]
, non-selfadjoint perturbations
H
of
H_{0}
are studied and large time expansions are obtained for the quantum dynamics
e^{-itH}
and
e^{-tH}
as
t\to+\infty
.

To study the N-body Schrödinger operator
H
at its lowest threshold
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
, we assume

\mbox{
$\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}$ is a non-multiple two-cluster threshold in the sense
of Condition \ref {cond:uniq}}.

(382)

This means there exists a unique

a_{0}\in{\mathcal{A}}\setminus\{a_{\max}\}
such that
\Sigma_{2}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{a_{0}})
, and this cluster
decomposition is a two-cluster decomposition obeying Condition

1.6
.
We keep the notation of Section
6
(in the present case,
m=1
,
\varphi_{1}
is a normalized eigenfunction of
H^{a_{0}}
with eigenvalue
\Sigma_{2}
and
{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}})
).
Denote
x_{0}=x_{a_{0}}\in{\mathbf{X}}_{a_{0}}
.
From Section
6
we have the representation formula for the resolvent,

R(z)=E(z)-E_{+}(z)E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}E_{-}(z)\,\text{ for }\,\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0,

where

\displaystyle E(z)

\displaystyle=R^{\prime}(z)=(H^{\prime}-z)^{-1}\Pi^{\prime},

\displaystyle E_{+}(z)

\displaystyle=(1-R^{\prime}(z)I_{0})S,

\displaystyle E_{-}(z)

\displaystyle=S^{*}\big(1-I_{0}R^{\prime}(z)\big),

\displaystyle E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)

\displaystyle=(z-\lambda_{0})-(-\Delta_{0}+S^{*}{I_{0}}S-S^{*}I_{0}R^{\prime}(z)I_{0}S),

and
\Delta_{0}=\Delta_{x_{0}}
,
I_{0}(x)=\sum_{b\not\subset a}V_{b}(x^{b})
and
Sf(x)(x)=\varphi_{1}(x^{a_{0}})f(x_{0})
. The effective potential here is

S^{*}I_{0}S(x_{0})=\langle{I_{0}\varphi_{1},\varphi_{1}}\rangle_{0},

where
\langle{\cdot,\;\cdot}\rangle_{0}
is the scalar product of
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a_{0}})
.

In addition to (
382
) we assume that

\displaystyle\lambda_{0}\not\in\sigma_{\mathrm{pp}}(H^{\prime}),\quad\rho\in(0,2),

(383a)

\displaystyle\exists c,R>0\mbox{ such that }S^{*}I_{0}S\geq\frac{c}{|x_{0}|^{\rho}}\text{ for }|x_{0}|>R.

(383b)

Conditions (
382
) and (
383a
) show that

R^{\prime}(z)
is holomorphic for
z
near
\lambda_{0}
. Condition
(
383b
) implies that in some sense
\lambda_{0}
can not be a
resonance of
H
(cf. Lemma
5.32
). Therefore we need only
to distinguish the cases
\lambda_{0}
be an eigenvalue of
H
or not. In
the case
\lambda_{0}
is not an eigenvalue of
H
we prove the following result.

Theorem 5.30
.

In addition to conditions (
382
), (
383a
) and (
383b
), we assume
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}\not\in\sigma_{\mathrm{pp}}(H)
.
Then for any
a>0
,
{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}R(z)
belongs to the Gevrey class
{\mathcal{G}}^{(\frac{1+\mu}{1-\mu})}(\Omega_{\lambda_{0}}(\delta))
, where
\mu=\frac{\rho}{2}
and
\Omega_{\lambda_{0}}(\delta)=\{z\in{\mathbb{C}}:|z-\lambda_{0}|<\delta,|\arg(z-\lambda_{0})|>\delta\}
,
\delta>0
small.

The proof of Theorem
5.30
is divided into several steps. The main task is to prove that
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(\frac{1+\mu}{1-\mu})}(\Omega_{\lambda_{0}}(\delta))
if
\lambda_{0}
is not an eigenvalue of
H
.
Set

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)=\lambda-(-\Delta_{0}+S^{*}{I_{0}}S-S^{*}I_{0}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)I_{0}S):=\lambda-(-\Delta_{0}+U(\lambda))

and

W(\lambda)=-S^{*}I_{0}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)I_{0}S=-\langle{\varphi_{1},I_{0}R^{\prime}(\lambda+\lambda_{0})I_{0}(\varphi_{1}\otimes\cdot)}\rangle_{0}.

(384)

W(\lambda)
is holomorphic for
\lambda
near
0
and satisfies

\|\langle{x}\rangle^{1+2\mu}W(\lambda)\langle{x}\rangle^{1+2\mu}\|\leq C

uniformly in
|\lambda|<2\delta
for some
\delta>0
small, because
\Pi I_{0}\Pi^{\prime}={\mathcal{O}}(\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-1-2\mu})
.
Under the assumption (
383b
), the results of
[
Wa6
]
can be applied to
-\Delta_{0}+S^{*}{I_{0}}S
. However the non-local term
W(\lambda)
can not be treated as a perturbation in the Gevrey setting. To prove Theorem
5.30
we follow the approach of
[
Wa6
]
from the very beginning and exploit the holomorphicity of
W(\lambda)
in
\lambda
to prove some uniform energy estimates.

For
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, let
\varphi_{s}
be the weight function defined by

\varphi_{s}(x_{0})=\left(1+\frac{|x_{0}|^{2}}{R_{s}^{2}}\right)^{s/2}\mbox{ with }R_{s}=M\langle{s}\rangle^{\frac{1}{1-\mu}},

where
M>1
is to be chosen sufficiently large and is independent of
s
.

Lemma 5.31
.

There exist constants
M,\delta,C>0
such that

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{r}\varphi_{s}W(\lambda)\varphi_{-s}\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{r^{\prime}}\|\leq C

for
r,r^{\prime}\in{\mathbb{R}}
with
r,r^{\prime}\leq 1+2\mu
,
for
\lambda\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
|\lambda|<\delta
and for
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof.

Since
[\varphi_{s},\Pi]=0
and
\Pi I_{0}\Pi^{\prime}={\mathcal{O}}(\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-1-2\mu})
, one has

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{r}\varphi_{s}W(\lambda)\varphi_{-s}\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{r^{\prime}}\|\leq C\|\varphi_{s}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)\varphi_{-s}\|.

Computing the commutator
[-\Delta_{0},\varphi_{s}]
one obtains

\varphi_{s}H^{\prime}\varphi_{-s}=H^{\prime}+{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{s}{M\langle{s}\rangle^{\frac{1}{1-\mu}}}\big)=H^{\prime}+{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{1}{M}\big),

where the term
{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{1}{M}\big)
satisfies the bound

\|{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{1}{M}\big)(H^{\prime}+i)^{-1}\|\leq\frac{C}{M}

uniformly in
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Since
\lambda_{0}
is in the resolvent set of
H^{\prime}
, there exists
\delta>0
such that
R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)
is a well defined holomorphic function for
|\lambda|<2\delta
and we can take
M>1
large so that

\|{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{1}{M}\big)R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)\|\leq\frac{1}{2}

for
|\lambda|<\delta
. From the equation

\varphi_{s}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)\varphi_{-s}=R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)\left(1+{\mathcal{O}}\big(\frac{1}{M}\big)R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)\right)^{-1}\Pi^{\prime},

it follows that
\varphi_{s}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)\varphi_{-s}
is
uniformly bounded for
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
|\lambda|<\delta
. Whence Lemma
5.31
is proven.
∎

Let
\chi_{R}(x_{0})=\chi_{1}(\frac{x_{0}}{R})
,
R\geq 1
, where
\chi_{1}\in C^{\infty}
is a cut-off such that
0\leq\chi_{1}\leq 1
,
\chi_{1}(x_{0})=0
if
|x_{0}|\leq 1
and

\chi_{1}(x_{0})=1
if
|x_{0}|\geq 2
. Set

\displaystyle F(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-\Delta_{0}+1-\chi_{R}+\chi_{R}U(\lambda)\chi_{R}

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=U(\lambda)-(1-\chi_{R}+\chi_{R}U(\lambda)\chi_{R})

\displaystyle h_{0}

\displaystyle=-\Delta_{0}+1-\chi_{R}+\chi_{R}^{2}S^{*}I_{0}S.

Then
E_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)
can be decomposed as

\displaystyle E_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)

\displaystyle=\lambda-(F(\lambda)+\widetilde{U}(\lambda))

(385)

\displaystyle=\lambda-(h_{0}+(1-\chi_{R})((1+\chi_{R})S^{*}I_{0}S-1)+W(\lambda))

For
R>1
sufficiently large,
h_{0}
is a one-body Schrödinger operator with globally positive and slowly decaying potential
v_{0}=1-\chi_{R}+\chi_{R}^{2}S^{*}I_{0}S
:

v_{0}(x_{0})\geq\frac{c}{\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{2\mu}},\quad x_{0}\in{\mathbf{X}}_{0},

for some
c>0
. The operator
F(0)=h_{0}+\chi_{R}W(0)\chi_{R}
is a non-local perturbation of
h_{0}
and
F(0)\geq 0
.
Note that

\widetilde{U}(\lambda)=(1-\chi_{R})(U(\lambda)-1)+\chi_{R}U(\lambda)(1-\chi_{R}).

Since
1-\chi_{R}
has compact support and
\lambda_{0}
is in the resolvent set of
H^{\prime}
, making use of the relation

{\mathrm{e}}^{a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle}H^{\prime}{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle}=H^{\prime}+{\mathcal{O}}(a)

for
a>0
small, one sees that
\widetilde{U}(\lambda)
is exponentially decaying in the sense that

\|{\mathrm{e}}^{a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle}\widetilde{U}(\lambda){\mathrm{e}}^{a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle}\|\leq C

(386)

uniformly for
\lambda
near
0
and
|a|\leq\delta
,
\delta>0
small.

Lemma 5.32
.

Assume (
383b
) and let
\mu=\frac{\rho}{2}\in(0,1)
. One has

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-\mu}\varphi_{s}u\|+\|\nabla(\varphi_{s}u)\|\leq C\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{\mu}\varphi_{s}(F(\lambda)-\lambda)u\|

(387)

uniformly in
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
u\in{\mathcal{S}}
and
\lambda\in\Omega_{0}(\delta)=\{\zeta\in{\mathbb{C}}:|\zeta|<\delta,|\arg\zeta|>\delta\}\cup\{0\}
.

Proof.

By construction,
h_{0}=-\Delta_{0}+v_{0}(x_{0})
satisfies the weighted coercive condition (
381b
). Moreover,
for
\lambda=\tau e^{i\phi}\in{\mathbb{C}}
with
\tau>0
and
\phi\neq 0
,
|\phi|\geq\delta>0
,
e^{-i\phi}(h_{0}-\lambda)
also satisfied (
381b
) with a lower bounded independent of
\lambda
so long as
|\phi|\geq\delta
and
|\tau|<\delta
for
\delta>0
small. Applying
[
Wa6
, Lemma 3.1]
to
h_{0}
and
e^{-i\phi}(h_{0}-\lambda)
, we deduce

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-\mu}\varphi_{s}u\|+\|\nabla(\varphi_{s}u)\|\leq C\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{\mu}\varphi_{s}(h_{0}-\lambda)u\|

(388)

uniformly in
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\lambda\in\Omega_{0}(\delta)
.

Since

F(\lambda)=h_{0}+\chi_{R}W(\lambda)\chi_{R}
, Lemma
5.31
shows that

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{\mu}\varphi_{s}(h_{0}-\lambda)u\|\leq\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{\mu}\varphi_{s}(F(\lambda)-\lambda)u\|+CR^{-2-2\mu}\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-\mu}\varphi_{s}u\|

uniformly in
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
|\lambda|
small. Now Lemma
5.32
follows if
R>1
is taken appropriately large.
∎

Lemma
5.32
shows that
G(\lambda)=(F(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}
satisfies the estimate

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-\mu}\varphi_{s}G(\lambda)\varphi_{-s}\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-\mu}\|+\|\nabla\varphi_{s}G(\lambda)\varphi_{-s}\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-\mu}\|\leq C

uniformly in
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\lambda\in\Omega_{0}(\delta)
. It follows that

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-2\mu}\varphi_{s}G(\lambda)\varphi_{-s}\|\leq C^{\prime}\langle{s}\rangle^{\gamma},\quad\gamma=\tfrac{2\mu}{1-\mu},

(389)

uniformly in
s
and
\lambda
. The following technical estimates are the main step in the proof of Gevrey estimates of
G(\lambda)
, cf.
[
Wa6
, Theorem 3.4]
.

Proposition 5.33
.

There exist constants
C,\delta>0
such that for any
r\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\lambda\in\Omega_{0}(\delta)

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-2\mu}\langle{x_{N,r}}\rangle^{-(2N+r)\mu}G^{(N)}(\lambda)\langle{x_{N,r}}\rangle^{r\mu}\|\leq C^{N+1}N!\langle{(2N+r)\mu}\rangle^{\gamma(N+1)}.

(390)

Here

x_{N,r}=\frac{x_{0}}{R_{N,r}}\mbox{ with }R_{N,r}=M\langle{(2N+r)\mu}\rangle^{\frac{1}{1-\mu}}

and
\langle{x_{N,r}}\rangle=(1+|x_{N,r}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}
.

Proof.

The case
N=0
and
r\geq 0
follows from (
389
) with
s=r\mu
.
For the general case
N\geq 1
and
r\geq 0
, we write

G^{(N)}(\lambda)=(G(\lambda)^{2}-G(\lambda)\chi_{R}W^{\prime}(\lambda)\chi_{R}G(\lambda))^{(N-1)},

and prove by an induction on
N
that

\displaystyle\|\langle{x}\rangle^{-2\mu}\langle{x_{N,r}}\rangle^{-(2N+r)\mu}G^{(N)}(\lambda)\langle{x_{N,r}}\rangle^{r\mu}\|

\displaystyle\leq C_{N}^{N+1}N!\langle{(2N+r)\mu}\rangle^{\gamma(N+1)}

with
C_{N+1}\leq C_{N}(1+\frac{c}{N^{1+\gamma}})
for some
c>0
independent of
N
. The details are the same as the proof of
[
Wa6
, Theorem 3.4]
and are omitted here.
∎

To convert polynomial weight depending on
N
into exponential weight independent of
N
, we use the following estimate.

\forall{a>0}\,\exists{A_{a}>0}:\quad\|\langle{x_{N,r}}\rangle^{(2N+r)\mu}{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq A_{a}^{\max\{2N+r,1\}},

(391)

uniformly in
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
r\in{\mathbb{R}}
.
In fact, if
2N+r\leq C
for some constant
C>0
, the left-hand side of (
391
) is uniformly bounded by some constant
C_{1}
. For

2N+r>C
with
C>1
large but fixed, consider the function

f(\tau)=\langle{\frac{\tau}{R_{N,r}}}\rangle^{(2N+r)\mu}e^{-a\tau^{1-\mu}},\quad\tau=|x_{0}|.

One has

f^{\prime}(\tau)=\frac{f(\tau)}{\tau^{\mu}(R_{N,r}^{2}+\tau^{2})}\left((2N+r)\mu\tau^{1+\mu}-(1-\mu)a(R_{N,r}^{2}+\tau^{2})\right).

Since
\mu\in]0,1[
, for each
a>0
, one can find some constant
A>0
such that

f^{\prime}(\tau)<0,\quad\mbox{ for }\tau>AR_{N,r}.

Therefore for
2N+r>C
,

\|\langle{x_{N,r}}\rangle^{(2N+r)\mu}e^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq\sup_{0\leq\tau\leq AR_{N,r}}f(\tau)\leq\langle{A}\rangle^{(2N+r)\mu}

This proves (
391
) for some appropriate constant
A_{a}>0
.

Proposition
5.33
implies the following Gevrey estimates for
G(\lambda)=(F(\lambda)-\lambda)^{-1}
:

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-\tau}{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}G^{(N)}(\lambda)\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{\tau}\|\leq C_{a}^{N+1+\tau}N!\langle{N+\tau}\rangle^{\gamma(N+1)+\frac{\tau}{1-\mu}}

(392)

uniformly in
\tau\geq 0
,
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\lambda\in\Omega_{0}(\delta)
.

Lemma 5.34
.

Assume (
382
), (
383a
) and (
383b
). Let
u
be a solution to the equation
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0})u=0
and
u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau}
for some
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then there exists
some positive constant
b_{0}
such that
{\mathrm{e}}^{b_{0}\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}u\in{\mathcal{H}}
. Here
{\mathcal{H}}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{0})
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{0},\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{2\tau}{\mathrm{d}}x_{0})
.

Proof.

u
satisfies the equation
u=G(0)(1-\chi_{R})(1-U(0))u
. Since
U(0)
is continuous in
{\mathcal{H}}_{s}
for any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\tau}
and
1-\chi_{R}
is of compact support,
(1-\chi_{R})(1-U(0))u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{s}
for any
s>0
. Proposition
5.33
with
N=0
and
r<0
shows that
u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\infty}
. See also Subsection 4.1.3.
To show the sub-exponential decay of
u
, we write

E_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{0})=-(h_{0}+(1-\chi_{R})(S^{*}I_{0}S-1)+W(\lambda_{0})).

Since
h_{0}\geq-\Delta_{0}+\frac{c}{\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{2\mu}}
for some
c>0
, the following Agmon energy estimate holds true:
\exists b,C>0
such that

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-\mu}{\mathrm{e}}^{b\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}f\|^{2}+\|\nabla_{x_{0}}({\mathrm{e}}^{b\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}f)\|^{2}\leq C(|\langle{{\mathrm{e}}^{2b\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}h_{0}f,f}\rangle|+\|f\|^{2})

(393)

for
f\in D(h_{0})
with
{\mathrm{e}}^{2b\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}h_{0}f\in{\mathcal{H}}
(cf.
[
Wa6
, (5.37)]
).

R^{\prime}(z)
being holomorphic for
z
near
\lambda_{0}
, one has for
b>0
small enough

\displaystyle|\langle{{\mathrm{e}}^{2b\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}W(\lambda_{0})f,f}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C_{1}(\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-1-2\mu}{\mathrm{e}}^{b\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}f\|^{2}+\|f\|^{2})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle{\epsilon}\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-\mu}{\mathrm{e}}^{b\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}f\|^{2}+C_{\epsilon}\|f\|^{2}

for any
{\epsilon}>0
.
We deduce from (
393
) that

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-\mu}{\mathrm{e}}^{b\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}f\|^{2}+\|\nabla_{x_{0}}({\mathrm{e}}^{b\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}f)\|^{2}\leq C(|\langle{{\mathrm{e}}^{2b\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}E_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{0})f,f}\rangle|+\|f\|^{2})

(394)

with possibly another constant
C
. Since
u\in{\mathcal{H}}_{\infty}
and
E_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{0})u=0
, the above inequality applied to
u
shows that
{\mathrm{e}}^{b_{0}\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}u\in{\mathcal{H}}
for
0<b_{0}<b
. ∎

Proof of Theorem
5.30
.

Since
\lambda_{0}
is not an eigenvalue of
H
, Lemma
5.34
shows that
E_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{0})
is injective in
{\mathcal{H}}_{s}
for any
s
.
Writing

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)=-(F(\lambda)-\lambda)(1+G(\lambda)\widetilde{U}(\lambda))

(395)

for
\lambda\in\Omega_{0}(\delta)
, one sees that
1+G(0)\widetilde{U}(0)
is injective. The mapping

\lambda\to G(\lambda)\widetilde{U}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}_{s})
is a continuous and compact operator-valued. Consequently,
1+G(0)\widetilde{U}(0)
is invertible and
(1+G(0)\widetilde{U}(0))^{-1}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}_{s})
which implies, by continuity,
(1+G(\lambda)\widetilde{U}(\lambda))^{-1}
exists and

\|(1+G(\lambda)\widetilde{U}(\lambda))^{-1}\|\leq C

for
\lambda\in\Omega_{0}(\delta)
,
\delta>0
small. This proves that
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)
is invertible with the inverse given by

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)^{-1}=-(1+G(\lambda)\widetilde{U}(\lambda))^{-1}G(\lambda)=-G(\lambda)(1+\widetilde{U}(\lambda)G(\lambda)))^{-1}.

(396)

As operator from
{\mathcal{H}}_{s}
to
{\mathcal{H}}_{s-2\mu}
,
E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)^{-1}
is uniformly bounded for
\lambda\in\Omega_{0}(\delta)
. Therefore the formula

R(z)=E(z)-E_{+}(z)E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}E_{-}(z)

initially valid for
\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0
can be extended to
z\in\Omega_{\lambda_{0}}(\delta)
. We conclude that
H
has no eigenvalue in
(\lambda_{0}-\delta,\lambda_{0})
, hence
\sigma_{d}(H)
is finite.

E(z)
and
E_{\pm}(z)
are holomorphic for
z
near
\lambda_{0}
. Since

{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}H^{\prime}{\mathrm{e}}^{a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}=H^{\prime}+O(a),

{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}R^{\prime}(z){\mathrm{e}}^{a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}

is holomorphic and uniformly bounded for
|z-\lambda_{0}|
small, provided that
a>0
is small. It follows that

{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}E_{+}(z){\mathrm{e}}^{a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}
is holomorphic and uniformly bounded for
z\in\Omega_{\lambda_{0}}(\delta)
. Therefore
to prove Theorem
5.30
, it is sufficient to show that

{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}E_{\mathcal{H}}(z)^{-1}
belongs to
{\mathcal{G}}^{(\frac{1+\mu}{1-\mu})}(\Omega_{\lambda_{0}}(\delta))
.

For
a>0
small,
\widetilde{U}(\lambda)e^{a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}
is holomorphic and uniformly bounded for

\lambda\in\Omega_{0}(\delta)
. We conclude from (
392
) (with
\tau=0
) that

\widetilde{U}(\lambda)G(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(\frac{1+\mu}{1-\mu})}(\Omega_{0}(\delta))
which, together with the uniform bound

\|(1+\widetilde{U}(\lambda)G(\lambda))^{-1}\|\leq C,

shows that
(1+\widetilde{U}(\lambda)G(\lambda))^{-1}\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(\frac{1+\mu}{1-\mu})}(\Omega_{0}(\delta))
. For
z=\lambda_{0}+\lambda
, it follows from
(
392
) that

{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(z)^{-1}=-{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}G(z-\lambda_{0})(1+\widetilde{U}(z-\lambda_{0})G(z-\lambda_{0}))^{-1}

belongs to
{\mathcal{G}}^{(\frac{1+\mu}{1-\mu})}(\Omega_{\lambda_{0}}(\delta))
. This finishes the proof of Theorem
5.30
.
\Box

Remarks 5.35
.

1.

Making use of (
389
) and repeating the proof of Theorem
5.30
, one can prove that for any
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}
, one has

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-2(N+1)\mu-\tau}R^{(N)}(z)\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{\tau}\|\leq C_{N,\tau}

(397)

uniformly for
\lambda\in\Omega_{\lambda_{0}}(\delta))
. This implies in particular that the limit

R(\lambda_{0})=\lim_{\lambda\in\Omega_{\lambda_{0}}(\delta)),\lambda\to\lambda_{0}}R(\lambda)

(398)

exists in
{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{\tau},L^{2}_{\tau-2\mu-{\epsilon}})
for any
{\epsilon}>0
and

R(\lambda_{0})\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{\tau},L^{2}_{\tau-2\mu})
for any

\tau\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Here and in the remaining part of this section,
L^{2}_{r}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}},\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{2r}{\mathrm{d}}x)
.

2.

Making use of (
392
), one can show the following improvement of Theorem
5.30
: For any
a>0
and
\tau\geq 0
, there exists some constant
C
such that

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-\tau}{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x}\rangle^{1-\mu}}R^{(N)}(z)\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{\tau}\|\leq C^{N+1}N!\langle{N+1}\rangle^{\gamma(N+1)}

(399)

uniformly in
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
z\in\Omega_{\lambda_{0}}(\delta)
.

Proposition 5.36
.

Assume the conditions of Theorem
5.30
and let
s>\frac{1+\mu}{2}
. Then the boundary values of the resolvent

R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)=\lim_{{\epsilon}\to 0_{+}}R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}{\epsilon})

exist in
{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{s},L^{2}_{-s})
for
\lambda\in[\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\delta]
,
\delta>0
and

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-s}R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-s}\|\leq C

(400)

uniformly in
\lambda\in[\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\delta]
.

Proof.

We keep the notation used before. It is known
[
Na
]
that
(
400
) holds true for
r_{0}(z)=(h_{0}-z)^{-1}
. Whence

\forall{s>\tfrac{1+\mu}{2}}\,\,\exists C>0:\quad\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-s}r_{0}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-s}\|\leq C

(401)

uniformly in
\lambda\in[0,\delta]
.
For
F(z)=h_{0}+\chi_{R}W(z)\chi_{R}
, we write

F(z)-z=(h_{0}-z)(1+r_{0}(z)\chi_{R}W(z)\chi_{R}),\quad\operatorname{Im}z\neq 0.

For
\lambda\geq 0
small,
r_{0}(\lambda+i0)\chi_{R}W(\lambda)\chi_{R}
is compact in
{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
and continuous for
\lambda\in[0,\delta]
,
\delta>0
.
For
R>1
large,
F(0)\geq-\Delta_{0}+\frac{c}{\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{2\mu}}
,
c>0
, in sense of selfadjoint operators and Lemma
5.32
remains true if
E_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{0})
is replaced by
F(0)
. Consequently,

F(0)
is injective in
{\mathcal{H}}_{t}
for any
t\in{\mathbb{R}}
because
0
is not an eigenvalue of
F(0)
. This implies that
1+r_{0}(0)\chi_{R}W(0)\chi_{R}
is injective in
{\mathcal{H}}_{-s}
, hence
1+r_{0}(0)\chi_{R}W(0)\chi_{R}

is invertible. By the continuity in
\lambda
, we conclude that
(1+r_{0}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\chi_{R}W(\lambda)\chi_{R})
is invertible in
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}_{-s})
for
\lambda>0
small and
its inverse is continuous in
\lambda\in[0,\delta]
. Consequently the boundary values of
G(z)=(F(z)-z)^{-1}
exist in
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}_{s},{\mathcal{H}}_{-s})
and

G(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)=\big(1+r_{0}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\chi_{R}W(\lambda)\chi_{R}\big)^{-1}r_{0}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)

are continuous for
\lambda\in[0,\delta]
. A similar argument shows that the boundary values

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)^{-1}=-\big(1+G(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\widetilde{U}(\lambda)\big)^{-1}G(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)

exist in
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}_{s},{\mathcal{H}}_{-s})
and are continuous in
\lambda\in[0,\delta]
. Finally we obtain

R(\mu\pm\mathrm{i}0)=E(\mu)-E_{+}(\mu)E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\mu\pm\mathrm{i}0)^{-1}E_{-}(\mu)

exist in
{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{s},L^{2}_{-s})
and are continuous in
\mu\in[\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\delta]
.
∎

Corollary 5.37
.

Let
e(\lambda)
denote the spectral projector of
H
on
]-\infty,\lambda]
. Assume the conditions of Theorem
5.30
. Then for any
a>0
and
s>\frac{1+\mu}{2}
, there exist some constants
b,B>0
such that

\|{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}e^{\prime}(\lambda)\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-s}\|\leq Be^{-b|\lambda-\lambda_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{\gamma}}},\quad\lambda\in(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\delta).

(402)

Proof.

Since
e^{\prime}(\lambda)=\frac{1}{2\pi i}(R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)-R(\lambda-\mathrm{i}0))
,

\|\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-s}e^{\prime}(\lambda)\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-s}\|
is uniformly bounded for
\lambda\in(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\delta)
.
Iterating the first resolvent equation, one obtains for any
N\in{\mathbb{N}}

e^{\prime}(\lambda)=(\lambda-\lambda_{0})^{N}R(\lambda_{0})^{N}e^{\prime}(\lambda).

(403)

Applying (
399
) with
\tau=s
, one deduces that for any
a>0
, there exist some constants
c,C>0
such that

\|{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}e^{\prime}(\lambda)\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{-s}\|\leq Cc^{N}N^{\gamma N}(\lambda-\lambda_{0})^{N}

(404)

for all
N\in{\mathbb{N}}
and
\lambda\in(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\delta)
. It remains to minimize the right-hand side by choosing
N
in terms of
\lambda-\lambda_{0}
such that

N\approx A|\lambda-\lambda_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{\gamma}}
as
\lambda\to\lambda_{0}
for some appropriate constant
A>0
. Then

\displaystyle c^{N}N^{\gamma N}|\lambda-\lambda_{0}|^{N}

\displaystyle\approx e^{A|\lambda-\lambda_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{\gamma}}(\gamma\ln A+\ln c)}

\displaystyle\leq Be^{-b|\lambda-\lambda_{0}|^{-\frac{1}{\gamma}}},\quad\lambda\in(\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\delta),

for some constants
b,B>0
, if
A>0
is such that
\gamma\ln A+\ln c<0
. This proves (
402
).
∎

When
\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue of
H
we prove the following
analogue of Theorem
5.30
.

Theorem 5.38
.

Assume the conditions (
382
), (
383a
) and (
383b
). Let
\lambda_{0}
be an eigenvalue of
H
and
\Pi_{\lambda_{0}}
be the eigenprojection of
H
associated with
\lambda_{0}
. Then one has

R(z)=-\frac{\Pi_{\lambda_{0}}}{z-\lambda_{0}}+R_{1}(z)

(405)

where, for any
a>0
,
e^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}R_{1}(z)
belongs to the Gevrey class
{\mathcal{G}}^{(\frac{1+\mu}{1-\mu})}(\Omega_{\lambda_{0}}(\delta))
.

Proof.

As in the proof of Theorem
5.30
, we are led to study
E_{\mathcal{H}}(z)
for
z\in\Omega_{\lambda_{0}}(\delta)
.
We use formula (
395
) and another Grushin reduction to study
E_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)^{-1}
. The smoothness of
G(\lambda)
and
\widetilde{U}(\lambda)
at
\lambda=0
implies that they can be expanded in appropriate spaces in powers of
\lambda
for
\lambda
near
0
. Set

\displaystyle G(\lambda)

\displaystyle=G_{0}+\lambda G_{1}+o(\lambda),

(406)

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{U}_{0}+\lambda\widetilde{U}_{1}+o(\lambda).

(407)

One has
G_{j}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}_{s},{\mathcal{H}}_{s-(j+1)\rho})
and
\widetilde{U}_{j}\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}_{s},{\mathcal{H}}_{\infty})
for any
s\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Let
k
denote the multiplicity of eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
of
H
. Then
0
is an eigenvalue of
E_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{0})
with multiplicity
k
. Let

K(z)=G(z)\widetilde{U}(z)
and
K_{0}=G_{0}\widetilde{U}_{0}
. Then
K_{0}
is a compact operator in
{\mathcal{H}}
and
\ker(1+G_{0}\widetilde{U}_{0})=\ker E_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{0})
.
As in Subsection 5.1.2, we can choose a basis,
\{\phi_{1},\dots,\phi_{k}\}
, of
\ker E_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{0})
verifying

\langle{-\widetilde{U}_{0}\phi_{i},\phi_{j}}\rangle=\delta_{ij},\quad 1\leq i,j\leq k

and construct a Grushin problem for
1+K(\lambda)
. Let

{\mathcal{S}}:{\mathbb{C}}^{k}\to{\mathcal{H}},c=(c_{1},\cdots,c_{k})\to{\mathcal{S}}c=\sum_{j=1}^{k}c_{j}\phi.

Then
Q={\mathcal{S}}{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
is a projection onto
\ker(1+K_{0})
. Let
Q^{\prime}=1-Q
. One has
Q^{\prime}(1+K_{0})Q^{\prime}
is invertible on
\ran Q^{\prime}
and by continuity,

Q^{\prime}(1+K(\lambda))Q^{\prime}
is invertible on
\ran Q^{\prime}
with uniformly bounded inverse for
\lambda\in\Omega_{0}(\delta)
,
\delta>0
small. Let

D(\lambda)=(Q^{\prime}(1+K(z)Q^{\prime})^{-1}Q^{\prime}

Since
(1+K(\lambda))\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(1+\gamma)}(\Omega_{0}(\delta))
with
\gamma=\frac{2\mu}{1-\mu}
, one has
D(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(1+\gamma)}(\Omega_{0}(\delta))
.
By studying the Grushin problem for
1+K(z)
using
{\mathcal{S}}
defined above, we obtain

(1+K(\lambda))^{-1}={\mathcal{E}}(\lambda)-{\mathcal{E}}_{+}(\lambda){\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(\lambda)^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}_{-}(\lambda)

(408)

where

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=D(\lambda),

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{+}(\lambda)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{S}}-D(\lambda)(1+K(\lambda)){\mathcal{S}}

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-}(\lambda)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{S}}^{*}-{\mathcal{S}}^{*}(1+K(\lambda))D(\lambda)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(\lambda)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{S}}^{*}(-(1+K(\lambda))+(1+K(\lambda))D(\lambda)(1+K(\lambda)){\mathcal{S}}.

{\mathcal{E}}_{\pm}(\lambda)
and
{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(\lambda)
all belong to Gevrey classes of order
1+\gamma
. As in Subsection 5.2.1 Case 2, we can compute the

k\times k
matrix
{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(\lambda)
et obtain

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(\lambda)=\lambda M_{0}+\lambda^{2}r_{1}(\lambda)

(409)

where
M_{0}
is invertible and
r_{1}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(1+\gamma)}(\Omega_{0}(\delta))
. This leads to

{\mathcal{E}}_{-+}(\lambda)^{-1}=\lambda^{-1}M_{0}^{-1}+r_{2}(\lambda)

with
r_{2}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(1+\gamma)}(\Omega_{0}(\delta))
. By (
396
) and (
408
), we obtain

E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+\lambda)^{-1}=-G(\lambda)(1+K(\lambda))^{-1}=\frac{C_{0}}{\lambda}+\widetilde{R}_{1}(\lambda)

(410)

in
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}_{s},{\mathcal{H}}_{s-2\mu-{\epsilon}})
, where
C_{0}=G_{0}{\mathcal{E}}_{+}(0)M_{0}^{-1}{\mathcal{E}}_{-}(0)
and for
a>0
,
e^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}\widetilde{R}_{1}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(1+\gamma)}(\Omega_{0}(\delta))
.
Therefore,
R(z)
verifies the expansion

R(z)=-\frac{B_{0}}{z-\lambda_{0}}+R_{1}(z),\quad B_{0}=E_{+}(\lambda_{0})C_{0}E_{-}(\lambda_{0})

One can show as in the proof of Theorem
5.30
that

{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}R_{1}(z)\in{\mathcal{G}}^{(1+\gamma)}(\Omega_{\lambda_{0}}(\delta)).

One has necessarily
B_{0}=\Pi_{\lambda_{0}}
by the spectral theorem for

H
.
∎

Using Cauchy integral formula to represent
{\mathrm{e}}^{-tH}
in terms of the resolvent, we obtain from Theorem
5.38
a large-time expansion for the heat semi-group
{\mathrm{e}}^{-tH}
(cf.
[
Wa6
, Theorem 2.3]
).

Corollary 5.39
.

Assume the conditions of Theorem
5.30
. For any
a>0
, there exist some constants
C,c>0
such that

\|{\mathrm{e}}^{-a\langle{x_{0}}\rangle^{1-\mu}}\big({\mathrm{e}}^{-tH}-\sum_{\lambda\in\sigma_{d}(H)}{\mathrm{e}}^{-t\lambda}\Pi_{\lambda}-{\mathrm{e}}^{-t\lambda_{0}}\Pi_{\lambda_{0}}\big)\|\leq C{\mathrm{e}}^{-t\lambda_{0}-ct^{\frac{1-\mu}{1+\mu}}}.

(411)

Here
\Pi_{\lambda}
is the eigenprojector of
H
associated with eigenvalue
\lambda
.

Remarks 5.40
.

1.

For one-body operators it is shown in
[
AW
,
Wa6
]
that under some
additional conditions the quantum dynamics
{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}tH}
, when regarded as operator from
L^{2}_{\rm comp}
to
L^{2}_{\rm loc}
, can be expanded as
|t|\to\infty
with the same sub-exponential estimates on the remainder as in
Corollary
5.39
. The conditions used there exclude a
possible accumulation of quantum resonances towards threshold
zero. It is an interesting and non-trivial open question to see if a
similar result holds true for
{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}tH}
in the
N
-body problem.

2.

If
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
is a multiple two-cluster threshold one can apply the Grushin reduction of Section 2.3 to show that Theorems
5.30
and
5.38
still hold true. Since the proof of Theorems
5.30
and
5.38
relies heavily on the continuity of
R^{\prime}(z)
in exponentially weighted spaces, which is deduced from the holomorphicity of
R^{\prime}(z)
for
z
near
\lambda_{0}
, one can not expect these results to be true for higher thresholds
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
.

Example 5.41
.

Theorems
5.30
and
5.38
can be applied to physics models with Coulomb interactions given by (
13
). Assume that the lowest threshold
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
is non-multiple two-cluster and is equal to the lowest eigenvalue of a two-cluster Hamiltonian
H^{a}
with
a=(C_{1},C_{2})
. Let
Q_{j}=\sum_{k\in C_{j}}q_{k}
be the total charge of particles in cluster
C_{j}
,
j=1,2
. Assume that

Q_{1}Q_{2}>0.

Then the effective potential is positive and slowly decreasing outside a compact set and (
383b
) is satisfied with
\rho=1
(see (
14
)). In this case, Theorems
5.30
and
5.38
hold true in Gevrey class
{\mathcal{G}}^{(3)}(\Omega_{\lambda_{0}}(\delta))
.

18
Resolvent asymptotics for physics models near two-cluster thresholds

We will discuss some extensions of Section 5.1 for the physics models
of Sections
2

and
3

(with
N\geq 3
and
N\geq 2
, respectively) using the same notation
as in Section
15
. As in the previous sections of
this chapter the single particle space dimension is fixed as

n=3
. In agreement with the settng of Section
15
we shall not distinguish between the cases
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
and

\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
.

We recall
that for a given two-cluster threshold
\lambda_{0}
for the models of Sections
2
and
3
we group the set of two-cluster decompositions
a
for which
\lambda_{0}\in\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{a})
into
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
,
{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
and
{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
for which

{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
:

the effective
inter-cluster interaction is to leading order attractive Coulombic,

{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
:

the effective
inter-cluster interaction is to leading order repulsive Coulombic,

{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
:

the effective
inter-cluster interaction is
{\mathcal{O}}(|x_{a}|^{-2})
.

This distinction
does not depend on choices of corresponding sub-Hamiltonian
bound states
\varphi^{a}
(i.e. channels); it is determined by charges only
(cf. Case 1 introduced independently in each of Sections
2

and
3
).

Let for
a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}:={\mathcal{A}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{2}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
the operator
P^{a}
be the corresponding orthogonal projection onto

\ker(H^{a}-\lambda_{0})
in

L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}^{a})
and let
m_{a}
be the dimension of this
space. Obviously
\Pi^{a}:=P^{a}\otimes 1
projects onto
the span of functions of the form
\varphi^{a}\otimes f_{a}
,

\varphi^{a}\in\ker(H^{a}-\lambda_{0})
, in
L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
. We identify
\ran P^{a}
, say spanned by an
orthonormal basis

\varphi^{a}_{1},\dots\varphi^{a}_{m_{a}}
, with
{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}}
(using the
basis), and similarly

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{a}:=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}})

\displaystyle\simeq\oplus_{m\leq m_{a}}\,L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a})\ni\oplus_{m\leq m_{a}}f_{a,m}=f_{a}

\displaystyle\simeq S_{a}f_{a}:=\sum_{m\leq m_{a}}\varphi^{a}_{m}\otimes f_{a,m}\in\ran\Pi^{a}.

The effective potential for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}
obeys

W_{a}:=S^{*}_{a}I_{a}S_{a}=Q_{a}|x_{a}|^{-2}+B_{a}
, where
Q_{a}
is a
m_{a}\times m_{a}
matrix-valued function depending only on

\theta=\hat{x}_{a}=|x_{a}|^{-1}x_{a}
while

B_{a}=B_{a}(x_{a})={\mathcal{O}}(|x_{a}|^{-3})
. We shall here only study the case
where
Q_{a}=0
, meaning that the effective potential is
fastly
decaying
. The ‘right generalization’ to the case
m_{a}>1
of the
distinction between Cases 2) and 3) (discussed primarily for

m_{a}=1
in both of Sections
2
and
3
) is to let Case 2) correspond to
Q_{a}\neq 0
and let Case 3) correspond to
Q_{a}=0
, respectively. This means that we shall not consider Case 2)
defined in Section
15
. Whence, letting
{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{3}=\{a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}|\,Q_{a}=0\}
, we assume that

{\mathcal{A}}_{3}={\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{3}.

(412)

For simplicity we shall also assume (
245a
) and
(
245b
) leaving us with studying the Grushin resolvent
representation (
36
) where (as in Section

15
)
{\mathcal{H}}=\sum_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}\oplus{\mathcal{H}}_{a}
,

{\mathcal{H}}_{a}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}})=\oplus_{m\leq m_{a}}\,L^{2}({\mathbf{X}}_{a})
and

S=(S_{a}):{\mathcal{H}}\to{\mathcal{F}}\subset{\mathcal{G}}=L^{2}({\mathbf{X}})
is given by

\displaystyle f=\oplus_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}\,f_{a}\to Sf=\sum_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}\,S_{a}f_{a};\quad f_{a}=\sum_{m\leq m_{a}}\oplus f_{a,m},\quad S_{a}f_{a}=\sum_{m\leq m_{a}}\varphi^{a}_{m}\otimes f_{a,m}.

Let (as usual)
T=(SS^{*})^{-1}S
. These operators
S
and
T
will
freely be used on weighted spaces, and we also adapt the following
notation of Theorem
5.14
.

\displaystyle S_{I}f:=\sum_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}\,I_{a}S_{a}f_{a};\quad f=\oplus_{a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}}\,\,f_{a}.

We write
-E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda_{0}+z)=P(z)=P_{0}+U(z)-z
, where

P_{0}=\oplus_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\,h_{a}
with
h_{a}
specified as
follows: If
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}
or
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
we take
h_{a}=p_{a}^{2}+w_{a}

(acting as a diagonal operator if
m_{a}>1
) where

w_{a}
is constructed as in Subsection
13.2

or

13.3
, respectively. If
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{3}={\mathcal{A}}_{3}
we take
h_{a}=p_{a}^{2}
. We define
r_{0}(z)=(P_{0}-z)^{-1}=\oplus_{a\in\widetilde{\mathcal{A}}}\,r_{a}(z)
for
any
z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}}
. Next we write

\displaystyle P(z)^{-1}=\big(1+r_{0}(z)U(z)\big)^{-1}r_{0}(z)=W(z)^{-1}r_{0}(z),\quad z\in{\mathbb{C}}\setminus{\mathbb{R}},

(413)

as in the previous sections.

If
\lambda_{0}
is regular, meaning that
\lambda_{0}
is neither an
eigenvalue nor a resonance of
H
(or, more precisely, that the set
{\mathcal{E}}
in
Theorem
4.17
is the zero set), then
\ker W^{\pm}(0)=0
where

\displaystyle W^{\pm}(0)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}W^{\pm}(\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)=1+\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}r_{0}(\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}U(\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)=1+r^{\pm}_{0}U^{\pm}_{0}

(414)

with limits taken in appropriate spaces. By Fredholm
theory we would then obtain limits
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}(P\mp\mathrm{i}\epsilon)^{-1}

and then in turn, due to (
259
), limits
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}R(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}\epsilon)
.
Actually for
\lambda_{0}
being regular we can take limits for

z\to 0
in the
quadrants

{\mathcal{Z}}_{\pm}=\{\operatorname{Re}z\geq 0,\,\pm\operatorname{Im}z>0\}.

To be more precise about these assertions we first look at the three
types of (diagonal) building blocks
r_{a}(z)=(h_{a}-z)^{-1}
. Recall the
notions from Section
15
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{a,s}^{k}=H_{s}^{k}({\mathbf{X}}_{a},{\mathbb{C}}^{m_{a}})\quad\text{for}\quad a\in\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}},\,k\in{\mathbb{R}}\text{ and }s\in{\mathbb{R}}.

If
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}

there are limits

\displaystyle r_{a}(0\pm\mathrm{i}0)=\lim_{z\to 0,z\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{\pm}}r_{a}(z)\text{ in
}{\mathcal{L}}\big({\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{a,s},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{a,-s}\big),\quad s>3/4,

cf. Subsection

13.2
. Similarly, if
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{2}

there are limits

\displaystyle r_{a}(0\pm\mathrm{i}0)=\lim_{z\to 0,z\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{\pm}}r_{a}(z)\text{ in
}{\mathcal{L}}\big({\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{a,s},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{a,-s}\big),\quad s>3/4,

however in this case
r_{a}(0+\mathrm{i}0)=r_{a}(0-\mathrm{i}0)
and the
common limit coincides with the quantity
h_{a}^{-1}
of Lemma

4.4
, cf.
[
Na
]
and
[
Ya2
]
. Finally,
for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{3}
we know from the previous sections that

\displaystyle r_{a}(0\pm\mathrm{i}0)=\lim_{z\to 0,z\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{\pm}}r_{a}(z)\text{ in
}{\mathcal{L}}\big({\mathcal{H}}^{-1}_{a,s},{\mathcal{H}}^{1}_{a,-s}\big),\quad s>1,

again with
r_{a}(0+\mathrm{i}0)=r_{a}(0-\mathrm{i}0)
and in this case the
common limit
G_{0}
is given explicitly (each diagonal entry has the
kernel
(4\pi)^{-1}\lvert x_{a}-y_{a}\rvert^{-1}
).

We are lead to consider for
k\in{\mathbb{R}}
,
r,s<-3/4
and
t<-1
the spaces

\displaystyle{\mathcal{H}}_{r,s,t}^{k}=\oplus_{b\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}}\,{\mathcal{H}}_{b,r}^{k}\bigoplus\oplus_{b\in{\mathcal{A}}_{2}}\,{\mathcal{H}}_{b,s}^{k}\bigoplus\oplus_{b\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}}\,{\mathcal{H}}_{b,t}^{k}.

We could for example fix
(r,s,t)=\bar{t}:=-(1,1,4/3)
, and the corresponding spaces
{\mathcal{H}}_{\bar{t}}^{k}
could then be used by considering
r_{0}(z)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}_{-\bar{t}}^{-1},{\mathcal{H}}_{\bar{t}}^{1})
,
U(z)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\bar{t}}^{1},{\mathcal{H}}_{-\bar{t}}^{-1})
and therefore, in turn,
W(z)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\bar{t}}^{1})
;
z\in{\mathcal{Z}}_{\pm}
.
With these interpretations we can check that the limits in
(
414
) exist. Since
\lambda_{0}
is regular for
H
it
follows that
\ker W^{\pm}(0)=0
. Since
W^{\pm}(0)\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{H}}_{\bar{t}}^{1})
this allows us to take the
z\to 0
limits in
(
413
) as well.

We made an unnecessary simplifying assumption on the parameters, and using the spaces

{\mathcal{H}}_{r,s,t}^{k}
we may similarly deduce the following result.

Proposition 5.42
.

Suppose the conditions of
Theorem
4.17
and in addition (
412
), (
245a
) and
(
245b
). Suppose
\lambda_{0}
is regular. Then for all sufficiently big
r,s<-3/4
and
t<-1

there is an

\epsilon>0
such that the following asymptotics holds in

{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}_{r,s,t}^{1})
for
z\to 0
in
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\pm}
.

\displaystyle W(z)^{-1}=W^{\pm}(0)^{-1}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{{\epsilon}}).

(415a)

Similarly, in the space
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{H}}_{-r,-s,-t}^{-1},{\mathcal{H}}_{r,s,t}^{1})

\displaystyle P(z)^{-1}=(P^{\pm})^{-1}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{{\epsilon}});\quad(P^{\pm})^{-1}:=W^{\pm}(0)^{-1}r^{\pm}_{0}.

(415b)

Theorem 5.43

(Regular case)
.

Under the conditions of
Proposition
5.42
the following asymptotics hold for
R(\lambda_{0}+z)
as an operator from
H^{-1}_{t}
to
H^{1}_{-t}
,
t>1
, for

z\to 0
in
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\pm}
and for some
\epsilon=\epsilon(t)>0
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}R(\lambda_{0}&+z)=R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)\\
&+(S-R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)S_{I})(P^{\pm})^{-1}(S^{*}-S_{I}^{*}R^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0))+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon}).\end{split}

(416)

This result is immediate from (
36
) and Proposition

5.42
, however the latter results
actually give
more detailed information on the (anisotropic) behaviour of the resolvent. For
example the ‘most singular part’ of
R(\lambda_{0}+z)
is given by the
term
S(P-z)^{-1}S^{*}\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{-1}_{t},H^{1}_{-t})
, but

S(P-z)^{-1}S^{*}\approx Sr_{0}(z)S^{*}\approx\Sigma_{a\in{\mathcal{A}}}P^{a}\otimes r_{a}(z)
needs
t>1
only for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}^{\mathrm{fd}}_{3}
;
r_{a}(z)
is ‘smaller’ for
a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\cup{\mathcal{A}}_{2}
.

We can also
derive a result if
\lambda_{0}
is an exceptional point of the second
kind, meaning that the set
{\mathcal{E}}
in Theorem

4.17
obeys
0\neq{\mathcal{E}}\subset L^{2}
. For that
we need the additional condition

\ran\Pi_{H}\subset L^{2}_{t}\text{ for some }t>1,

(417)

where
\Pi_{H}
is the eigenprojection corresponding to
the eigenvalue
\lambda_{0}
of
H
(note that
\Pi_{H}L^{2}={\mathcal{E}}
). We
can argue as for the last assertion of Theorem
5.16
,
see also Remark
5.17

2
. This means more precisely that we use the above
procedure for
H_{\sigma}:=H-\sigma\Pi_{H}
,
\sigma>0
small. Under
the given hypotheses
\lambda_{0}
is neither an eigenvalue nor a
resonance of
H_{\sigma}
, and therefore there is an analogous version of
(
416
) for
H_{\sigma}
and we can then (as in the
previous sections) invoke (
345
). Hence we obtain the
following result, where quantities depending on the ‘potential’

-\sigma\Pi_{H}
are equipped with the subscript
\sigma
.

Theorem 5.44

(Exceptional
point of
2
nd kind)
.

Suppose the conditions of
Theorem
4.17
and in addition
(
412
), (
245a
), (
245b
) and (
417
). Suppose
\lambda_{0}
is an eigenvalue but not a
resonance of
H
. Then the following asymptotics hold for
R(\lambda_{0}+z)
as an operator from
H^{-1}_{s}
to
H^{1}_{-s}
,
s>1
, for

z\to 0
in
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\pm}
and for some
\epsilon=\epsilon(s)>0
.

\displaystyle\begin{split}R(\lambda_{0}&+z)=-z^{-1}\Pi_{H}+\sigma^{-1}\Pi_{H}+R_{\sigma}^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)\\
&+(S-R^{\prime}_{\sigma}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)S_{I_{\sigma}})(P_{\sigma}^{\pm})^{-1}(S^{*}-S_{I_{\sigma}}^{*}R_{\sigma}^{\prime}(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0))+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{\epsilon}).\end{split}

(418)

Note that the
z^{-\frac{1}{2}}
-term is absent in (
418
). This looks as if there is a discrepancy with the known results given in
[
JK
,
Wa2
]
where there are
z^{-\frac{1}{2}}
-terms in the resolvent expansions for exceptional point of the second kind. However there is not the case, because under the decay assumption on the threshold eigenstates used in Theorem
5.44
, one can check by an explicit calculation that the
z^{-\frac{1}{2}}
-terms of
[
JK
,
Wa2
]
also disappear.

It remains to examine the cases where
\lambda_{0}
is an exceptional
point of the first or of the third
kind, meaning (treating them uniformly) that the set
{\mathcal{E}}
in Theorem

4.17
is not strictly a subset of
L^{2}
.
For that
we need the additional condition

\ran\Pi_{H}\subset L^{2}_{t}\text{ for some }t>3/2,

(419)

where
\Pi_{H}
is the orthogonal projection onto
\ker(H-\lambda_{0})
(i.e. the eigenprojection if
\lambda_{0}
is
an eigenvalue of
H
). We know from
Theorem
4.17
that the condition

{\mathcal{E}}\not\subset L^{2}
needs
{\mathcal{A}}_{3}\neq\emptyset
. If
{\mathcal{A}}_{1}\neq\emptyset
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{fd}}_{3}\neq\emptyset
there is a technical problem on
identifying the geometric and algebraic multiplicities for the
eigenvalue
-1
of certain
operators
K^{\pm}
, cf. (
305
). The same method as the
one used in the previous sections does not work (and in fact we dont
know if the multiplicities are equal). On the other hand under the additional condition

\widetilde{{\mathcal{A}}}={\mathcal{A}}_{2}\cup{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{fd}}_{3},

(420)

the analogue of
(
305
) holds (for
H_{\sigma}
), by the same proof (see
also Remark
3.21

2
). In
this case we can mimic the proof of Theorem
5.16

and obtain the following result, which is very similar to Theorems

5.16
and
5.26
.

Theorem 5.45

(Exceptional
point of
1
st or
3
rd kind)
.

Suppose the conditions of
Theorem
4.17
and in addition
(
245a
), (
245b
),
(
419
) and (
420
). Suppose
\lambda_{0}
is a
resonance of
H
. Then the following asymptotics hold for
R(\lambda_{0}+z)
as an operator from
H^{-1}_{s}
to
H^{1}_{-s}
,
s>1
, for

z\to 0
in
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\pm}
and for some
\epsilon=\epsilon(s)>0
.

R(\lambda_{0}+z)=-z^{-1}\Pi_{H}+\frac{\mathrm{i}}{\sqrt{z}}\sum_{j=1}^{\kappa}\langle{u_{j},\cdot}\rangle u_{j}+{\mathcal{O}}(|z|^{-\frac{1}{2}+{\epsilon}}).

(421)

Here

\{u_{1},\dots,u_{\kappa}\}\subset H^{1}_{(-1/2)^{-}}
is a basis of resonance states of

H
being independent
of the choice of the sign of
{\mathcal{Z}}_{\pm}
.

Remarks 5.46
.

The basis of
resonance states can be specified in a fashion similar to
normalization procedures in Theorem
5.14
and Remark

5.25
, cf. (
271a
)
and (
271b
) (and subsequent the computations).

We
consider the imposed conditions (
245a
) and
(
245b
) as technically convenient but not being
crucial. The interested reader may trace the outlined proof of
Theorem
4.17
for the general case where
these conditions are not imposed and see how the above theory
modifies.

We already discussed our need for (
420
),
however we remark that (
412
) is not strictly
necessary. In fact there are some results for the case where

{\mathcal{A}}_{3}\setminus{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{fd}}_{3}\neq\emptyset
(this is an
interesting case for the physics models). Then the spherical
potential
Q_{a}
should not to be ‘too negative’, more precisely we
need that
\sigma(-\Delta_{\theta}+Q_{a})\subset(-1/4,\infty)
for
all
a
in this set. With this extension there should be analogous
results on the resolvent expansion at
\lambda_{0}
, not to be
elaborated on here, see
[
Wa5
]
. However we shall later do a
version of Theorem
5.43
in a special
case where indeed
{\mathcal{A}}_{3}\setminus{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{fd}}_{3}\neq\emptyset
, see Subsection
21.2
. If
the above spectral condition on

a\in{\mathcal{A}}_{3}\setminus{\mathcal{A}}^{\textrm{fd}}_{3}
is not fulfilled the
resolvent asymptotics would be expected to be oscillatory, see

[
SW
]
where oscillatory behaviour is detected for a one-body
‘toy model’. We shall not study this case; it does not seem to be
an ‘easy problem’.

Chapter 6
Applications

We will give applications of the
previous chapters to scattering theory. We shall primarily study the non-multiple case imposing Conditions

1.5
and
2.4
. With
additional efforts the
multiple is treatable somewhat similarly, see Subsectons
19.3
and
22.3
for actual
accounts of the
multiple case. The non-multiple case appears rather complicated already,
and we believe that treating only this case may be considered as ‘heart of
the matter’. In Sections
20
,

21
and
22
we consider only the physics models of Subsections

2.1
and
3.1

with the particle
dimension
n=3

(in Section
22
even more specialized).

Although most of the material presented in this chapter is new it depends on the
literature, obviously most importantly for example
[
DS1
,
JKW
]
. Since scattering
theory is an old well-studied subject the literature is large, let us
here mention the related works
[
Bo
,
Do
,
CT
,
De1
,
De2
,
De3
,
DG
,
Is2
,
Is3
,
Is4
,
IT
,
Ne
,
Sk2
,
Sk4
,
Sk5
,
Sk6
,
Sk7
,
SW
,
Wa6
,
Ya1
,
Ya3
,
Ya4
,
Ya5
]
. Obviously this list is not complete.

19
Negative slowly decaying effective potentials

We impose the attractiveness condition (
183
) for the cluster
decomposition
a=a_{0}
given in
Condition
2.4
. More precisely suppose

\rho<2
and that the inter-cluster potential

I_{a}(x_{a})=I_{a_{0}}(x_{a})=I_{0}(x_{a})
fulfills the condition

\displaystyle\begin{split}\exists R\geq 0&\quad\exists\epsilon>0\quad\forall y\in{\mathbf{X}}_{a}\text{ with }|y|\geq R:\\
&I_{a}(y)\leq-\epsilon\langle y\rangle^{-\rho}\text{ and }-2I_{a}(y)-y\cdot\nabla I_{a}(y)\geq\epsilon\langle{y}\rangle^{-\rho}.\end{split}

(422)

We assume

\lambda_{0}\notin\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{\prime}),

(423)

or equivalently
\lambda_{0}\notin\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(\breve{H})
. If (
423
) is not fulfilled we may modify the theory to be
discussed
in agreement with Subsection
8
, see Remark
4.18
.

We know from Chapter
3
that the
boundary values
\breve{R}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)
are smooth (in weighted
spaces) in a real neighbourhood
I\ni\lambda_{0}
. We take
I
,

R=R_{0}\geq 1
and the operator
B=B_{R}=:\widecheck{B}
exactly as done
in Subsection
12.2
(this
B
should not to be mixed
up with the operator

B_{\kappa}=B_{\kappa,R}=B(\kappa^{2}B^{2}+1)^{-1}
in the same chapter).

In addition we assume

\lambda_{0}\notin\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H).

(424)

If (
424
) is not fulfilled and
\Pi_{H}
is the
corresponding eigenprojection then
\lambda_{0}\notin\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H-\sigma\Pi_{H})
for
\sigma>0
, and we can consider the
H^{\prime}

construction for
H_{\sigma}=H-\sigma\Pi_{H}
, say denoted by
H^{\prime}_{\sigma}
,
which fits well onto the framework of Chapter
3
since the eigenfunctions decay
polynomially, cf. Theorems
4.2
,

4.12
and
4.17

1
. In fact if
\sigma>0
is taken small enough the condition

\lambda_{0}\notin\sigma_{{\mathrm{pp}}}(H^{\prime})
implies the same property with
H^{\prime}

replaced by
H^{\prime}_{\sigma}
, see the discussion before Theorem

5.26
. This would lead to a resolvent formula similar to
(
418
). For simplicity we impose (
423
) as well as (
424
), which
leads to the following analogue (
425
) of the physics models
resolvent
formula (
416
).

With these conditions we know from Chapters
3
and
4
(see also Remark
5.17

3
) that there exist continuous
boundary values
R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)
(in appropriate spaces) in an interval of the form

I_{\delta}^{+}=[\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}+\delta]\subset I
with
\delta>0
small. In
fact we have the formulas

\displaystyle R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)=E^{\pm}(\lambda)-E^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda)E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda)^{-1}E^{\pm}_{-}(\lambda),

(425)

where, abbreviating

I_{0}=I_{a_{0}}
and
p_{0}=p_{a_{0}}
,

\displaystyle E^{\pm}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=R^{\prime}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0),

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{+}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(1-R^{\prime}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)I_{0})S,

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{-}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=S^{*}(1-I_{0}R^{\prime}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)),

\displaystyle E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=(\lambda-\lambda_{0})-(p_{0}^{2}+S^{*}{I_{0}}S-S^{*}I_{0}R^{\prime}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)I_{0}S).

We also have

\displaystyle R^{\prime}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)=\breve{R}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)-(p_{0}^{2}-\lambda)^{-1}\Pi=\breve{R}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\Pi^{\prime}.

The matrix-valued operator
W=S^{*}{I_{0}}S
is
well approximated by a diagonal one fulfilling a global virial
condition,
in fact approximated by a multiple of the
identity say denoted by
w{\mathbf{1}}
, cf. the discussion
in the beginning of Subsection
13.2
. As in
the same subsection we modify the potential
V(z)

correspondingly and denote the result by
v(z)
. Let

h=(p_{0}^{2}+w){\mathbf{1}}
,

r(z)=(h-z)^{-1}
and

\displaystyle r^{\pm}_{\lambda}=r(\lambda-\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)\text{ and }v^{\pm}_{\lambda}=v(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0),

and note that

\displaystyle\begin{split}-E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda)&=h+v^{\pm}_{\lambda}-(\lambda-\lambda_{0}),\\
-E^{\pm}_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda)^{-1}&=r^{\pm}_{\lambda}(\lambda)\big({\mathbf{1}}+v^{\pm}_{\lambda}r^{\pm}_{\lambda}(\lambda)\big)^{-1};\\
v^{\pm}_{\lambda}&=-S^{*}I_{0}R^{\prime}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)I_{0}S+{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1-\rho}).\end{split}

(426)

If
m_{a}:=\dim\ker(H^{a}-\lambda_{0})=1
,
a=a_{0}
, we can here
replace
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-1-\rho})
by
{\mathcal{O}}(r^{-\infty})
which would refer to a polynomially
decreasing term. In any case the term is a
\lambda
-independent
local potential (i.e. a function).
The above expressions
are substituted into (
425
) to obtain formulas for

R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)
to be studied.

Note that

\displaystyle\big({\mathbf{1}}+v^{\pm}_{\lambda}r^{\pm}_{\lambda}\big)^{-1}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{s})\cap{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}});\quad s\in(s_{0},1/2+\rho 3/4),\,s_{0}=1/2+\rho/4.

(427)

This is the best we can do when

m_{a}>1
. If
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
and

m_{a}=1
then any
s\in(s_{0},1/2+\rho)

works. On
the other hand if
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
and

m_{a}=1
then this inverse exists in

{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{s})
for any
s\in(s_{0},2\rho+2-s_{0})
,
cf. (
189
). In particular we have the following formula for any
\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}

(we abbreviate
throughout this section
{\mathcal{B}}:={\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}
,
{\mathcal{B}}^{*}={\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{1/2}
and

{{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{0}}:={\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{1/2,0}
),

\displaystyle\begin{split}R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi&=S\phi_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda)+\breve{R}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda);\\
\phi_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=r^{\pm}_{\lambda}f_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{a}),\\
f_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=\big({\mathbf{1}}+v^{\pm}_{\lambda}r^{\pm}_{\lambda}\big)^{-1}S^{*}\big(1-I_{0}\breve{R}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\Pi^{\prime}\big)\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{a}),\\
\psi_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda)&=\Pi^{\prime}\big(\psi-I_{0}S\phi_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big)\in{\mathcal{B}}.\end{split}

(428)

It follows that for any
s>s_{0}
the
{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{s},L^{2}_{-s})
–valued
functions
R(\cdot\pm\mathrm{i}0)
are continuous on
I_{\delta}^{+}
. For each

\lambda\in I_{\delta}^{+}
the operators
R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\in{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}},{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0}})
. It will be convenient to isolate the ‘main
parts‘ of (
428
) writing

\displaystyle R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)=Sr^{\pm}_{\lambda}S^{*}+\breve{R}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\Pi^{\prime}+\widecheck{R}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0).

(429)

Here
\widecheck{R}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)
are represented as sums of various terms. Note for
example that for any
\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}
(as above)
f_{a}^{\pm}(\lambda)-S^{*}\psi\in L^{2}_{s}
for
any
s\in(s_{0},1/2+\rho 3/4)
.

Remark 6.1
.

Note that in particular (
425
) as well as (
428
) and
(
429
) are valid for
\lambda=\lambda_{0}
. This is
a consequence of the imposed regularity condition
(
424
). For other models, to be treated in Section

21
, there are similar
formulas as (
428
) and (
429
). Again this
requires regularity, i.e. absence of bound and resonance
states at the threshold. Note also that the trick of replacing
H
by

H_{\sigma}=H-\sigma\Pi_{H}
in the case (
424
) is not fulfilled is
not restricted to the case of attractive slowly decaying effective
potentials, i.e. the condition (
422
), but can be used
as well
for repulsive slowly decaying effective potentials fulfilling
the following version of (
192
),

\exists R\geq 0\,\exists\epsilon>0\,\exists\bar{\rho}\in[\rho,\tfrac{2}{3}(1+\rho))\,\forall y\in{\mathbf{X}}_{a}\text{ with }|y|\geq R:\quad I_{a}(y)\geq\epsilon\langle y\rangle^{-\bar{\rho}}.

This is manifestly done already in
the proof of Theorem
5.44
and as before
doable thanks to the polynomial decay of the eigenfunctions. For possible threshold eigenfunctions
for non-slowly decaying effective
potentials (cf. the asymptotics
condition (
197
)) the
polynomial decay is missing making (
424
) a
non-trivial assumption in such cases, see however Theorem
5.44
.

19.1
Sommerfeld’s theorem

We impose the above conditions
(
422
)–(
424
) on the threshold
\lambda_{0}
.
First we recall the following version of the Sommerfeld’s theorem above

\lambda_{0}
, see
[
AIIS
, Corollary 1.10]
which extends the seminal
work
[
Is4
]
.

Theorem 6.2
.

For any

\lambda\in I_{\delta}^{+}\setminus\{\lambda_{0}\}
there exist

R=R_{0}\geq 1
and
\sigma>0
such that for any real function

\chi(\cdot<\sigma)\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
,
which is supported in
(-\infty,\sigma)
and whose derivative
has
compact support, and for any
\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}

\displaystyle\chi(\pm\widecheck{B}<\sigma)R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{0};\,\widecheck{B}=B_{R}.

(430)

Moreover (for each sign)
\phi=R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi\,(\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*})
solves
(H-\lambda)\phi=\psi
.

Conversely if
\phi\in L_{-\infty}^{2}
solves

(H-\lambda)\phi=\psi
for a given
\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}
and (for either
‘plus’ or ‘minus’)
\chi(\pm\widecheck{B}<\sigma)\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{0}
for some
\sigma>0
and for all
functions
\chi(\cdot<\sigma)
of this type, then

\phi=R(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi
(with the same sign).

Here (most likely)
R=R(\lambda)\to\infty
and
\sigma=\sigma(\lambda)\to 0
for
\lambda\downarrow\lambda_{0}
. We state a new version of the Sommerfeld theorem,
now at
\lambda=\lambda_{0}
, but otherwise under the same
conditions. (A one-body version of the theorem at zero energy is given in

[
Sk4
]
.) Recall for comparison the quantity
{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s_{0},0}
of
Lemma
4.10
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s_{0},0}=\{u\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0},0}\mid(H-\lambda_{0})u=0,\quad\Pi^{\prime}u\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{0}\}.

Let

\displaystyle\widecheck{B}_{\rho}=r^{\rho/4}\,\widecheck{B}\,r^{\rho/4};\quad r=r_{R},\,\widecheck{B}=B_{R},\,R=R_{0},

recalling that

R_{0}\geq 1
is chosen in agreement with our version of the Mourre
estimate at
\lambda_{0}
(as done
in Subsection
12.2
).

Theorem 6.3

(Sommerfeld’s theorem at threshold)
.

There exists

\sigma>0
such that for
any real function

\chi(\cdot<\sigma)\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{R}})
,
which is supported in
(-\infty,\sigma)
and whose derivative
has
compact support, and for any
\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}
, the function

\phi=\phi^{\pm}=R(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0}}
obeys

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Pi^{\prime}\phi&\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*},\\
\chi(\pm\widecheck{B}<\sigma)\Pi^{\prime}\phi&\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{0},\\
\chi(\pm\widecheck{B}_{\rho}<\sigma)\Pi\phi&\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0},0},\\
(H-\lambda_{0})\phi&=\psi.\end{split}

(431)

Conversely suppose that (for either
‘plus’ or ‘minus’)

\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0}}
fulfills (
431
)
for some
\sigma>0
, for all
functions
\chi(\cdot<\sigma)
of this type and for a given

\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}
, then
\phi=R(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi
(with the same sign).

Proof.

We shall only
consider the case of ‘
+
’. By Proposition

3.16
, for some
\sigma>0

\displaystyle\chi(\widecheck{B}<\sigma)\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)\Pi^{\prime}\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{0}.

(432)

(This is for
\lambda_{0}>\Sigma_{2}
; if
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
the statement is trivial.) Note that
\Pi^{\prime}\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}
and
that (
432
) holds with
\widecheck{B}
replaced by

B_{\kappa}=\widecheck{B}(\kappa^{2}\widecheck{B}^{2}+1)^{-1}
(
\kappa>0

small). Then (
432
) follows by using another function
of the same type, say
\tilde{\chi}(\cdot<\sigma)
, such that

\displaystyle\chi(b<\sigma)=\chi(b<\sigma)\tilde{\chi}(b/(\kappa^{2}b^{2}+1)<\sigma).

By using
[
FS
, Theorems 4.1 and
4.2]
one can show (here omitting the argument) that for all small
\sigma>0

\displaystyle\begin{split}\langle x_{a}\rangle^{t-s_{0}}\chi\big(\widecheck{B}_{\rho}&<\sigma\big)Sr^{+}_{\lambda_{0}}\langle x_{a}\rangle^{-t-s_{0}-\epsilon}f\in L^{2};\\
\quad&f\in L^{2},\,t,\epsilon>0.\end{split}

(433)

We use (
428
) writing

\displaystyle\phi^{+}=R(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)\psi=S\phi_{a}^{+}(\lambda_{0})+\breve{R}(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)\psi_{a}^{+}(\lambda_{0}).

(434)

Now
(
431
) follows by using
(
432
) and (
433
) to treat the second and
the first terms of (
434
), respectively.

To show the second assertion (the uniqueness part) note that
\phi^{+}=R(\lambda_{0}+\mathrm{i}0)\psi
is a particular solution of this problem. Whence
we may assume that
\psi=0
.
Due to Theorems
4.2
and

4.12
it suffices to
show that
\phi\in{\mathcal{E}}^{\mathcal{G}}_{-s_{0},0}
(here we use the same notation
if
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
), and whence that

\displaystyle\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0},0},\quad\Pi^{\prime}\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{0}.

(435)

Introduce a smooth quadratic partition of unity
1=\chi(\cdot<\sigma)^{2}+\chi(\cdot>\sigma)^{2}
such that

\chi(b<\sigma)=1
for
b<\sigma/2
. Let for
R\geq 1
the function
\chi_{R}=\chi_{R}(r)
be
given by (
4.1
).
Abbreviating

\theta_{R}=\sqrt{-\chi_{R}^{\prime}}
we can then estimate

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\langle\chi^{\prime}_{R}(r)\rangle_{\Pi^{\prime}\phi}-\langle r^{-\rho/2}\chi^{\prime}_{R}(r)\rangle_{\Pi\phi}

\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{2}{\sigma}\big(s_{R}+t_{R}\big)+v_{R};

\displaystyle s_{R}

\displaystyle=\langle\widecheck{B}\rangle_{\chi(\widecheck{B}>\sigma)\theta_{R}\Pi^{\prime}\phi},

\displaystyle t_{R}

\displaystyle=\langle\widecheck{B}_{\rho}\rangle_{\chi(\widecheck{B}_{\rho}>\sigma)r^{-\rho/4}\theta_{R}\Pi\phi},

where
v_{R}\to 0
for
R\to\infty
. Next we write

\displaystyle t_{R}

\displaystyle=\tilde{t}_{R}+\tilde{v}_{R};

\displaystyle\tilde{t}_{R}

\displaystyle=\langle\widecheck{B}\rangle_{\chi(\widecheck{B}_{\rho}>\sigma)\theta_{R}\Pi\phi},

and note that
\tilde{v}_{R}={\mathcal{O}}(R^{\rho/2-1})\to 0

for
R\to\infty
. After further commutation we (should) obtain that

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\langle\chi^{\prime}_{R}(r)\rangle_{\Pi^{\prime}\phi}-\langle r^{-\rho/2}\chi^{\prime}_{R}(r)\rangle_{\Pi\phi}
