\displaystyle\leq\tfrac{2}{\sigma}\big(\langle\widecheck{B}\rangle_{\theta_{R}\Pi^{\prime}\phi}+\langle\widecheck{B}\rangle_{\theta_{R}\Pi\phi}\big)+o(R^{0})

\displaystyle=-{\sigma}^{-1}\langle\mathrm{i}[H,\chi_{R}]\rangle_{\phi}+o(R^{0}),

\displaystyle=0+o(R^{0}),

yielding
(
435
) and therefore the uniqueness part. However we need to
argue for the validity of the above estimates. In the second step we
used that

\displaystyle\operatorname{Re}{\Pi\theta_{R}\widecheck{B}\theta_{R}\Pi^{\prime}}={\mathcal{O}}(R^{-2}),

cf. (
223
).
In the first step we
used that

\displaystyle s_{R}-\langle\widecheck{B}\rangle_{\theta_{R}\Pi^{\prime}\phi}

\displaystyle=-\langle\widecheck{B}\rangle_{\chi(\check{B}<\sigma)\theta_{R}\Pi^{\prime}\phi}=o(R^{0}),

\displaystyle\tilde{t}_{R}-\langle\widecheck{B}\rangle_{\theta_{R}\Pi\phi}

\displaystyle=o(R^{0}).

The first bound is easy since
\widecheck{B}\Pi^{\prime}\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}
(the
latter seen by an energy bound).

To get the second bound it
suffices to show that also
\widecheck{B}_{\rho}\Pi\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}^{*}
.
Let us first
note that due to the assumption
\chi(\widecheck{B}<\sigma)\Pi^{\prime}\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{0}
we can verbatim use Step V of the proof
of Lemma
4.10
to conclude that
E^{+}_{\mathcal{H}}(\lambda_{0})f=0
,
f=T^{*}\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}^{*}_{s_{0}}({\mathbf{X}}_{a})
. Next we decompose

\displaystyle\begin{split}\widecheck{B}_{\rho}\Pi\phi&=\widecheck{B}_{\rho}ST^{*}\phi=S\widecheck{B}_{a,\rho}f+\hat{\phi},\\
&\widecheck{B}_{a,\rho}=r_{a}^{\rho/4-1/2}\operatorname{Re}\big(x_{a}\cdot p_{a}\big)r_{a}^{\rho/4-1/2};\quad r_{a}=r(x_{a}).\end{split}

(436)

By the arguments
of the proof of Lemma
3.4

indeed
\hat{\phi}\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}^{*}
, so it remains only to show that
\widecheck{B}_{a,\rho}f\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}^{*}({\mathbf{X}}_{a})
. Noting that the operator
\widecheck{B}_{a,\rho}
has symbol

\check{b}_{a,\rho}\in S\big(s,g\big);\quad s=\langle\xi/g_{a}\rangle^{2},\quad g_{a}=\sqrt{-w},\quad g=\langle x\rangle^{-2}{\mathrm{d}}x^{2}+g_{a}^{-2}{\mathrm{d}}\xi^{2},

(437a)

it suffices in turn to show that
g^{-1}_{a}{\operatorname{Op\!^{w}\!}}(s)g_{a}f\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}^{*}({\mathbf{X}}_{a})
. As in
[
DS1
, (4.15)]

{\operatorname{Op\!^{w}\!}}(s)-g_{a}^{-1}hg_{a}^{-1}-2\in S(\langle x\rangle^{\rho-2},g)\subset S(1,g).

(437b)

By writing
h=(h+v^{+}_{\lambda_{0}})-v^{+}_{\lambda_{0}}

we end up with bounding
-g^{-2}_{a}v^{+}_{\lambda_{0}}f
, which clearly belongs to
{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}^{*}({\mathbf{X}}_{a})
. Whence
\widecheck{B}_{a,\rho}f\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}^{*}({\mathbf{X}}_{a})
is proven.
∎

Corollary 6.4
.

For any
\psi\in{\mathcal{B}}
the function
\phi=R(\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)\psi

obeys the bounds

\widecheck{B}_{\rho}\Pi\phi,\,\,g_{a}^{-1}\widecheck{B}_{\rho}g_{a}\Pi\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}^{*}.

(438)

Proof.

We substitute (
429
) and use (
436
) and
(
437b
). Note then that
g^{-2}_{a}hT^{*}\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}_{s_{0}}^{*}
since
2s_{0}>\rho
.
∎

19.2
Elastic part of the scattering matrix at
\lambda_{0}

We will to a large degree use
[
DS1
]
. We recall from (
47
) that
S
is given in terms
of cluster bound states
\varphi_{1},\dots,\varphi_{m_{a}}
,
m_{a}=\dim\ker(H^{a}-\lambda_{0})
,
a=a_{0}
. The quantity

\alpha=\alpha_{j}=(a,\lambda_{0},\varphi_{j})
,
j\leq m_{a}
, is referred to as a

channel
.

Let us for convenience here assume
m_{a}=1
and denote

\varphi_{1}
by
\varphi_{\alpha}
(see Subsecton
19.3
for an example where
m_{a}=2
). To make contact to
[
DS1
]
it is
convenient to change notation:
Recall that up to a polynomially decreasing potential

w(x_{a})\approx\langle I_{a}^{(1)}(\cdot+x_{a})\rangle_{\varphi^{a}}
. Let us now assume that

\displaystyle w=V_{1}+V_{2},

(439)

where
V_{1}
and
V_{2}
fulfill the following conditions of

[
DS1
]
. (For Coulomb systems one can take
V_{1}(x)=-\gamma r^{-1}

for
r:=\lvert x\rvert\geq 1
and
V_{2}={\mathcal{O}}(r^{-2})
.)

Let
n=\dim{\mathbf{X}}_{a}
.

Condition 6.5
.

The function
w
can be written as a sum of two
real-valued measurable functions,
w=V_{1}+V_{2}
, such that:
For some
\rho\in(0,2)
we
have

1.

V_{1}
is a smooth negative function that only
depends on the
radial variable
r
in the region
r\geq 1
(that is

V_{1}(x)=V_{1}(r)
for
r=|x_{a}|\geq 1
). There exists
\epsilon_{1}>0
such
that

V_{1}(r)\leq-\epsilon_{1}r^{-\rho},\;r\geq 1.

2.

For all
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}

there exists

C_{\gamma}>0
such that

\langle x\rangle^{\rho+|\gamma|}|\partial^{\gamma}V_{1}(x)|\leq C_{\gamma}.

3.

There exists
\tilde{\epsilon}_{1}>0
such that

rV_{1}^{\prime}(r)\leq-(2-\tilde{\epsilon}_{1})V_{1}(r),\;r\geq 1.

(440)

4.

V_{2}=V_{2}(x)
is smooth and there exists
\epsilon_{2}>0
such that for all
\gamma\in\mathbb{N}_{0}^{n}

\langle x\rangle^{\rho+\epsilon_{2}+|\gamma|}|\partial^{\gamma}V_{2}(x)|\leq C_{\gamma}.

The following condition will be needed (and imposed) only in the case
V_{2}\neq 0
.

Condition 6.6
.

Let
V_{1}
be given as in Condition
6.5
and
\alpha:=\tfrac{2}{2+\rho}
. There exists
\bar{\epsilon}_{1}>\max(0,1-\alpha(\rho+2\epsilon_{2}))
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{r\to\infty}r^{-1}V_{1}^{\prime}(r)\Big(\int^{r}_{1}(-V_{1}(s))^{-\frac{1}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}s\Big)^{2}<2^{-1}(1-\bar{\epsilon}_{1}^{2}),

\displaystyle\limsup_{r\to\infty}V_{1}^{\prime}{}^{\prime}(r)\Big(\int^{r}_{1}(-V_{1}(s))^{-\frac{1}{2}}{\mathrm{d}}s\Big)^{2}<2^{-1}(1-\bar{\epsilon}_{1}^{2}).

19.2.1
Scattering for the one-body problem at zero energy,
[
DS1
]

We review a number of results from
[
DS1
]
valid under Conditions

6.5
and
6.6
. (For a
different approach to one-body scattering theory, see
[
Is1
]
.) Recall that
for any
\omega\in{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
,
\lambda\in[0,\infty)
and
x
from an
appropriate outgoing/incoming region there exists a solution to the
system of equations

\displaystyle\begin{split}\ddot{y}(t)&=-2\nabla w(y(t)),\\
\lambda&=\tfrac{1}{4}\dot{y}(t)^{2}+w(y(t)),\\
y(\pm 1)&=x,\\
\omega&={\pm}\lim_{t\to{\pm}\infty}y(t)/|y(t)|.\end{split}

(441)

One obtains a family
y^{\pm}(t,x,\omega,\lambda)
of solutions depending regularily (at least
continuously) on
parameters. Moreover all ‘scattering orbits’ are of this form.
Using these solutions one can construct a solution

\phi^{\pm}(x,\omega,\lambda)
to
the eikonal equation

\left(\nabla_{x}\phi^{\pm}(x,\omega,\lambda)\right)^{2}+w(x)=\lambda

(442)

satisfying
\nabla_{x}\phi^{\pm}(x,\omega,\lambda)=\tfrac{1}{2}\dot{y}(\pm 1,x,\omega,\lambda)
.

For
R\geq 1
and
\sigma\in(0,2)

\displaystyle\begin{split}\Gamma^{+}_{R,\sigma}(\omega)&:=\{y\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\ |\ y\cdot\omega\geq(1-\sigma)|y|,\;|y|\geq R\};\;\omega\in{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1},\\
\Gamma^{+}_{R,\sigma}&:=\{(y,\omega)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{n}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\ |\ y\in\Gamma^{+}_{R,\sigma}(\omega)\}.\end{split}

(443)

Lemma 6.7
.

There exist
R_{0}\geq 1
and
\sigma_{0}\in(0,2)

such that for
all
R\geq R_{0}
and for all positive

\sigma\leq\sigma_{0}
the system (
441
)
is
solved for all data

(x,\omega)\in\Gamma^{+}_{R,\sigma}
and
\lambda\geq 0

by a unique
function
y^{+}(t,x,\omega,\lambda),\;t\geq 1
, such that

y^{+}(t,x,\omega,\lambda)\in\Gamma^{+}_{R,\sigma}(\omega)
for all
t\geq 1
.
Define a vector field
F^{+}(x,\omega,\lambda)

on
\Gamma^{+}_{R_{0},\sigma_{0}}(\omega)
by

F^{+}(x,\omega,\lambda)=\tfrac{1}{2}\dot{y}^{+}(t=1,x,\omega,\lambda).

(444)

Then

\operatorname{rot}_{x}F^{+}(x,\omega,\lambda)=0.

We define

\phi^{+}(x,\omega,\lambda)
at

(x,\omega,\lambda)\in\Gamma^{+}_{R_{0},\sigma_{0}}\times[0,\infty[
by
requiring

\nabla_{x}\phi^{+}=F^{+}
and

\phi^{+}(R_{0}\omega,\omega,\lambda)=\sqrt{\lambda}R_{0}
. We let

\phi^{-}(x,\omega,\lambda):=-\phi^{+}(x,-\omega,\lambda)\;\text{for }x\in\Gamma^{-}_{R_{0},\sigma_{0}}(\omega):=\Gamma^{+}_{R_{0},\sigma_{0}}(-\omega).

For
\xi\neq 0
we write

\xi=\sqrt{\lambda}\omega
,
\omega\in{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
, and then

\displaystyle\phi^{\pm}(x,\xi)=\phi^{\pm}(x,\omega,\lambda);\quad(x,\omega)\in\Gamma^{\pm}_{R_{0},\sigma_{0}}.

We are motivated to write, slightly abusely,
(x,\xi)\in\Gamma^{\pm}_{R_{0},\sigma_{0}}

instead of
(x,\omega,\lambda)\in\Gamma^{\pm}_{R_{0},\sigma_{0}}\times(0,\infty)
(in fact even for the case
\xi=0
).

Fixing
0<\sigma<\sigma^{\prime}<\sigma_{0}

we
introduce a smoothed out characteristic function

\chi_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(t)=\begin{cases}1,&\text{for}\;t\geq 1-\sigma,\\
0,&\text{for}\;t\leq 1-\sigma^{\prime}.\end{cases}\;

(445a)

Next define,
in terms of (
445a
) and the function
\bar{\chi}_{R}=1-\chi_{R}
of (
4.1
),

a_{0}^{\pm}(x,\xi):=\chi_{\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}}(\pm\hat{x}\cdot\hat{\xi})\bar{\chi}_{R_{0}}(\lvert x\rvert);\quad\hat{z}=z/|z|.

We introduce then a Fourier integral operator
J_{0}^{+}
on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})
by

(J_{0}^{\pm}f)(x)=(2\pi)^{-n/2}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}\phi^{\pm}(x,\xi)}a_{0}^{\pm}(x,\xi)\hat{f}(\xi){\mathrm{d}}\xi,

(445b)

where

\hat{f}(\xi):=(2\pi)^{-n/2}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}x\cdot\xi}f(x){\mathrm{d}}x

denotes the Fourier transform of
f
.

The WKB method suggests to approximate the wave operator by
a Fourier integral operator
J^{+}
on
L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
of the form

(J^{+}f)(x)=(2\pi)^{-n/2}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}\phi^{+}(x,\xi)}a^{+}(x,\xi)\hat{f}(\xi){\mathrm{d}}\xi,

(446a)

where the symbol
a^{+}(x,\xi)
is
supported in
\Gamma^{+}_{R_{0},\sigma_{0}}
and
constructed by an iterative procedure (partly recalled in Subsection

19.2.4
) attempting to make the difference

T^{+}:=\mathrm{i}(hJ^{+}-J^{+}p^{2})
small in
\Gamma^{+}_{R_{0},\sigma}
. We have

(T^{+}f)(x)=(2\pi)^{-n/2}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}\phi^{+}(x,\xi)}t^{+}(x,\xi)\hat{f}(\xi){\mathrm{d}}\xi,

(446b)

where

t^{+}(x,\xi)=\left((2\nabla_{x}\phi^{+}(x,\xi))\cdot\nabla_{x}+(\triangle_{x}\phi^{+}(x,\xi))\right)a^{+}(x,\xi)-{\mathrm{i}}\triangle_{x}a^{+}(x,\xi).

(446c)

The symbols
a^{+}(x,\xi)
and
a_{0}^{+}(x,\xi)
coincide
to leading order away from
\xi=0
, more precisely

\displaystyle a^{+}(x,\xi)\approx\left(\det\nabla_{\xi}\nabla_{x}\phi^{+}(x,\xi)\right)^{1/2}a_{0}^{+}(x,\xi)={\mathrm{e}}^{\zeta^{+}(x,\xi)}a_{0}^{+}(x,\xi),

(446d)

and
a^{+}(x,\xi)
should be thought of as an
‘improvement’ of the right-hand side.
For details of construction, see
[
DS1
, Section 5]
. However,
since we are partly going to mimic this construction in Subsection

19.2.4
, let us
here recall that the equation

\left((2\nabla_{x}\phi^{+}(x,\xi))\cdot\nabla_{x}+(\triangle_{x}\phi^{+}(x,\xi))\right){\mathrm{e}}^{\zeta^{+}(x,\xi)}=0,

(446e)

takes (
446c
) onto the form

\displaystyle\begin{split}t^{+}&={\mathrm{e}}^{\zeta^{+}}\big((2\nabla_{x}\phi^{+})\cdot\nabla_{x}-\mathrm{i}A^{+}\big)b^{+},\quad b^{+}={\mathrm{e}}^{-\zeta^{+}}a^{+},\\
A^{+}&=\Delta+2(\nabla\zeta^{+})\cdot\nabla+(\Delta\zeta^{+})+(\nabla\zeta^{+})^{2}.\end{split}

(446f)

Similar to (
446a
)–(
446c
) we introduce a Fourier integral
operator
J^{-}
and
T^{-}
as

\displaystyle\begin{split}(J^{-}f)(x)&=(2\pi)^{-n/2}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}\phi^{-}(x,\xi)}a^{-}(x,\xi)\hat{f}(\xi){\mathrm{d}}\xi,\\
(T^{-}f)(x)&=(2\pi)^{-n/2}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}\phi^{-}(x,\xi)}t^{-}(x,\xi)\hat{f}(\xi){\mathrm{d}}\xi.\end{split}

(447)

For all
\tau\in L^{2}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
we introduce

\displaystyle\begin{split}(J^{\pm}(\lambda)\tau)(x)&=(2\pi)^{-n/2}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}\phi^{\pm}(x,\omega,\lambda)}\tilde{a}^{\pm}(x,\omega,\lambda)\tau(\omega){\mathrm{d}}\omega,\\
(T^{\pm}(\lambda)\tau)(x)&=(2\pi)^{-n/2}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}\phi^{\pm}(x,\omega,\lambda)}\tilde{t}^{\pm}(x,\omega,\lambda)\tau(\omega){\mathrm{d}}\omega,\end{split}

(448)

where

\displaystyle\tilde{a}^{\pm}(x,\omega,\lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{\lambda^{(n-2)/4}}{\sqrt{2}}a^{\pm}(x,\sqrt{\lambda}\omega),

\displaystyle\tilde{t}^{\pm}(x,\omega,\lambda)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{\lambda^{(n-2)/4}}{\sqrt{2}}t^{\pm}(x,\sqrt{\lambda}\omega).

The functions
\tilde{a}^{\pm}
and
\tilde{t}^{\pm}
are
continuous
in

(x,\omega,\lambda)\in{\mathbb{R}}^{d}\times{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}\times[0,\infty)
, and
therefore we can
define

J^{\pm}(\lambda)
and
T^{\pm}(\lambda)

at
\lambda=0
by the expressions (
448
). These properties
hinge on
[
DS1
, Proposition 5.3]
stating properties of the function

\tilde{\zeta}^{+}(x,\omega,\lambda)={\zeta}^{+}(x,\sqrt{\lambda}\omega)-\ln\lambda^{(2-n)/4};\quad\lambda>0.

In particular it follows that there exist locally
uniform limits (along with derivatives)

\tilde{\zeta}^{+}(x,\omega,0)=\lim_{\lambda\to 0_{+}}\tilde{\zeta}^{+}(x,\omega,\lambda).

(449)

It will be
convenient to use a splitting
T^{\pm}(\lambda)=T^{\pm}_{\mathrm{bd}}(\lambda)+T^{\pm}_{\mathrm{pr}}(\lambda)
in agreement
with a certain decomposition of
\tilde{t}^{\pm}(x,\sqrt{\lambda}\omega)
,
see Lemma
6.8
and references given before the lemma.
There are
wave operators

\displaystyle W^{\pm}f=\lim_{t\to{\pm}\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}th}J_{0}^{\pm}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}tp^{2}}f=\lim_{t\to{\pm}\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}th}J^{\pm}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}tp^{2}}f;\;\hat{f}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\{0\}).

(450)

The two operators
W^{\pm}
extend isometrically on

L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{d})
with extensions satisfying
hW^{\pm}=W^{\pm}p^{2}
. Moreover,

\displaystyle 0=\lim_{t\to{\mp}\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}th}J_{0}^{\pm}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}tp^{2}}f=\lim_{t\to{\mp}\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}th}J^{\pm}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}tp^{2}}f;\;\hat{f}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}\setminus\{0\}).

(451)

For
\rho\in(1/2,2)
we may write
W^{\pm}=W_{\rm dol}^{\pm}\,{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}\psi_{\rm{\mathrm{dol}}}^{\pm}(p)}
in terms of the familiar Dollard wave operators

[
Do
]
(cf. (
543
) in Section
22
) and explicit real momentum-depending
phase factors
\psi_{\rm dol}^{\pm}
, see
[
DS1
, Theorem 6.15]
.

Let
\Delta_{\omega}
denote the Laplace-Beltrami operator on the sphere

{\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}
. For
k\in{\mathbb{R}}
we define the Sobolev spaces on the sphere

H^{k}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})=(1-\Delta_{\omega})^{-k/2}L^{2}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
. Let
{\mathcal{L}}_{s}^{k}={\mathcal{L}}_{s}^{k}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})={\mathcal{L}}(H^{k}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}),L_{s}^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}))
and

{\mathcal{L}}^{k}={\mathcal{L}}_{0}^{k}
for any
k,s\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

For
\lambda>0
we introduce the restricted Fourier transform
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\lambda)
as

{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\lambda)f(\omega)=\tfrac{\lambda^{(n-2)/4}}{\sqrt{2}}\hat{f}(\sqrt{\lambda}\omega).

(452)

Let
s>\frac{1}{2}
and
k\geq 0
.
Note that
{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\lambda)\in\mathcal{L}(L^{2}_{s+k}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}),H^{k}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}))

with a continuous dependence on
\lambda>0
.
Likewise,

{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\lambda)^{*}\in\mathcal{L}^{-k}_{-s-k}
with a continuous dependence on

\lambda>0
. Note also that the operator

\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}^{\oplus}{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\lambda)\,{\mathrm{d}}\lambda:L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\to\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}^{\oplus}L^{2}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})\;{\mathrm{d}}\lambda

(453)

is unitary, and
consequently that it diagonalizes the operator
p^{2}
.
Formally, we have

J^{\pm}(\lambda)=J^{\pm}{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\lambda)^{*}
and

T^{\pm}(\lambda)=T^{\pm}{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\lambda)^{*}
. The formal identity

W^{\pm}(\lambda)=W^{\pm}{\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\lambda)^{*}=(J^{\pm}+\mathrm{i}r(\lambda{\mp}\mathrm{i}0)T^{\pm}){\mathcal{F}}_{0}(\lambda)^{*}
leads us then to consider the
wave matrices

W^{\pm}(\lambda):=J^{\pm}(\lambda)+\mathrm{i}r(\lambda{\mp}\mathrm{i}0)T^{\pm}(\lambda),

(454)

which in fact belong to
\mathcal{L}^{-k}_{-s}
for any

k\geq 0
for a suitable
s=s(k)>s_{0}
. In this space

W^{\pm}(\lambda)
have continuous dependence of
\lambda\geq 0

(including
\lambda=0
!).

The scattering operator commutes with
p^{2}
, which is diagonalized by the
direct integral mapping (
453
).
Because of
that the general theory of decomposable operators yields a
measurable family
{\mathbb{R}}_{+}\ni\lambda\mapsto S(\lambda)
with the

scattering matrix

S(\lambda)

being a unitary operator on
L^{2}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
for almost all

\lambda
, and such
that in terms of the mapping (
453
)

S\simeq\int_{{\mathbb{R}}_{+}}^{\oplus}S(\lambda)\;{\mathrm{d}}\lambda.

(455)

A main result of
[
DS1
]
reads, that for
\lambda\geq 0
the
scattering matrix

\displaystyle\begin{split}S(\lambda)&=-2\pi J^{+}(\lambda)^{*}T^{-}(\lambda)+2\pi\mathrm{i}T^{+}(\lambda)^{*}r(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)T^{-}(\lambda)\\
&=-2\pi W^{+}(\lambda)^{*}T^{-}(\lambda)\end{split},

(456)

defining a unitary operator
L^{2}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
with a
strongly continuously dependence on
\lambda\geq 0
. Moreover (
455
) is true,
and

\forall k\in{\mathbb{R}}\,\forall\epsilon>0:\quad S(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}(H^{k}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}),H^{k-\epsilon}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})),

(457)

depending norm-continuously on

\lambda\geq 0
.
Hence in particular
S(\lambda)
maps
C^{\infty}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
into
itself.

Another main result of
[
DS1
]
adopted to the setting discussed here (in
particular not including a certain singular term
V_{3}
) is the following result:

Suppose in addition to Conditions
6.5
and

6.6
that
V_{1}(r)=-\gamma r^{-\rho}

for
r\geq 1
. Then
the kernel
S(0)(\omega,\omega^{\prime})
is smooth
outside the set
\{(\omega,\omega^{\prime})\mid\omega\cdot\omega^{\prime}=\cos\frac{\rho}{2-\rho}\pi\}
.

19.2.2
Elastic scattering for the
N
-body problem at
\lambda_{0}

Using the constructions
J_{0}^{\pm}
in the previous subsection for
the potential
w=V_{1}+V_{2}
in the variable
x_{a}
and recalling

-E_{{\mathcal{H}}}(\lambda\pm\mathrm{i}0)=h+v^{\pm}_{\lambda}+\lambda_{0}-\lambda
,
h=p^{2}+w
, we introduce wave operators

\displaystyle\begin{split}\lim_{t\to{\pm}\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}\big(1\otimes J_{0}^{\pm}\big)&{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}tH_{a}}Sf=\lim_{t\to{\pm}\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}\big(1\otimes J_{0}^{\pm}\big)S{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}t(p_{a}^{2}+\lambda_{0})}f;\\
\quad&\hat{f}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbf{X}}_{a}\setminus\{0\})^{m_{a}}.\end{split}

(458)

We consider a channel
\alpha=(a,\lambda_{0},\varphi_{\alpha})

(recall that this means that
H^{a}\varphi_{\alpha}=\lambda_{0}\varphi_{\alpha}

with

\lVert\varphi_{\alpha}\rVert=1
)
assuming for simplicity from this point that

m_{a}=\dim\ker(H^{a}-\lambda_{0})=1

(459)

(making
\varphi_{\alpha}
essentially unique). With this assumption the above limit is nothing but the
channel
wave operator

\displaystyle\begin{split}W_{\alpha}^{\pm}f&=\lim_{t\to{\pm}\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}\big(1\otimes J_{0}^{\pm}\big)\varphi_{\alpha}\otimes{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}t(p_{a}^{2}+\lambda_{0})}f\\
&=\lim_{t\to{\pm}\infty}{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}tH}\big(\varphi_{\alpha}\otimes J_{0}^{\pm}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}t(p_{a}^{2}+\lambda_{0})}f\big);\quad\hat{f}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbf{X}}_{a}\setminus\{0\}).\end{split}

(460)

This leads us to define

\displaystyle{\mathcal{F}}_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)f(\omega)

\displaystyle=\tfrac{(\lambda-\lambda_{0})^{(n-2)/4}}{\sqrt{2}}\hat{f}\big(\sqrt{\lambda-\lambda_{0}}\,\omega\big),

\displaystyle(J_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)\tau)(x)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-n/2}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}\phi^{\pm}(x,\omega,\lambda-\lambda_{0})}\tilde{a}^{\pm}(x,\omega,\lambda-\lambda_{0})\tau(\omega){\mathrm{d}}\omega,

\displaystyle(T_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)\tau)(x)

\displaystyle=(2\pi)^{-n/2}\int{\mathrm{e}}^{\mathrm{i}\phi^{\pm}(x,\omega,\lambda-\lambda_{0})}\tilde{t}^{\pm}(x,\omega,\lambda-\lambda_{0})\tau(\omega){\mathrm{d}}\omega,

\displaystyle J_{\alpha}^{\pm}f

\displaystyle={\varphi_{\alpha}}\otimes J^{\pm}f;\quad f=f(x_{a}),

\displaystyle T_{\alpha}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{i}\big(HJ_{\alpha}^{\pm}-J_{\alpha}^{\pm}(p_{a}^{2}+\lambda_{0})\big),

\displaystyle J_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\tau

\displaystyle={\varphi_{\alpha}}\otimes J_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)\tau,

\displaystyle T_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\tau

\displaystyle={\varphi_{\alpha}}\otimes T_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)\tau+\widecheck{T}_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\tau;

\displaystyle\quad\widecheck{T}_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\mathrm{i}I^{(2)}_{a}J_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)+\mathrm{i}\big(I^{(1)}_{a}-w\big)J_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda),

\displaystyle W^{\pm}_{\alpha}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=J^{\pm}_{\alpha}(\lambda)+\mathrm{i}R(\lambda{\mp}\mathrm{i}0)T^{\pm}_{\alpha}(\lambda),

\displaystyle S_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=-2\pi J^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}T^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)+2\pi\mathrm{i}T^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)T^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda).

Here formally

\displaystyle J_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)=J^{\pm}(\lambda-\lambda_{0})=J^{\pm}{\mathcal{F}}_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)^{*}\text{ and }T_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)=T^{\pm}(\lambda-\lambda_{0})=T^{\pm}{\mathcal{F}}_{\lambda_{0}}(\lambda)^{*},

where
J^{\pm}
is the ‘improvement’ of
J_{0}^{\pm}
and

T^{\pm}=\mathrm{i}(hJ^{\pm}-J^{\pm}p_{a}^{2})

as defined by (
446a
)–(
446c
) and (
447
).

The first term of
\widecheck{T}_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)

has arbitrary polynomial decay, for example stated precisely as

\widecheck{T}_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}_{s}^{k}={\mathcal{L}}_{s}^{k}({\mathbf{X}}):={\mathcal{L}}(H^{k}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}),L_{s}^{2}({\mathbf{X}}))
for any
k,s\in{\mathbb{R}}
, and this is also the case for

\Pi\big(I^{(1)}_{a}-w\big)J_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)
. But

\Pi^{\prime}\big(I^{(1)}_{a}-w\big)J_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)=\Pi^{\prime}I^{(1)}_{a}\Pi J_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)
is only one power better than
w
,
more precisely it has the form

{\mathcal{O}}(\langle x\rangle^{-1-\rho})J_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)
. Thus we can
record

\displaystyle\Pi\widecheck{T}_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)={\mathcal{O}}\big(\langle x\rangle^{-\infty}\big),\quad\Pi^{\prime}\widecheck{T}_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)={\mathcal{O}}\big(\langle x\rangle^{-1-\rho}\big)J_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda).

(461)

A
similar remark is due for the ‘non-restricted’
quantity
\widecheck{T}_{\alpha}^{\pm}

in the formula

\displaystyle T_{\alpha}^{\pm}={\varphi_{\alpha}}\otimes T^{\pm}+\widecheck{T}_{\alpha}^{\pm};\quad\widecheck{T}_{\alpha}^{\pm}=I^{(2)}_{a}J_{\alpha}^{\pm}+\big(I^{(1)}_{a}-w\big)J_{\alpha}^{\pm}.

Moreover, since for any
f
with
\hat{f}\in C^{\infty}_{\mathrm{c}}({\mathbf{X}}_{a}\setminus\{0\})
the quantity
\lVert T_{\alpha}^{\pm}{\mathrm{e}}^{-\mathrm{i}t(p_{a}^{2}+\lambda_{0})}f\rVert
is integrable at
\pm\infty
, the Cook
argument gives the existence of the wave operator

W_{\alpha}^{\pm}
. Note that this integrability may be shown by a
stationary phase argument (for example by using
(
464
) and a version of Lemma
6.8

3
).

Using
[
DS1
, Appendix A]
the elastic part of the scattering
matrix defined by (
460
) may be shown to be given by the expression

S_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)
,
\lambda>\lambda_{0}
, introduced above. We will
study some properties of this operator, which is an operator on

{\mathcal{L}}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
with norm at most one.

We state some basic properties of
J^{\pm}(\lambda)

and
T^{\pm}(\lambda)=T^{\pm}_{\mathrm{bd}}(\lambda)+T^{\pm}_{\mathrm{pr}}(\lambda)
, see

[
DS1
, (5.8), (5.16), (5.19) and Lemmas 6.8 and 6.9]
and the proof of
[
DS1
, Theorem 6.11]
(which we adapt to the present problem). First we recall some
notation. The most basic one is the function

\displaystyle g(r)=g_{\lambda}=\sqrt{\lambda-\lambda_{0}-V_{1}(r)},\quad\lambda\geq\lambda_{0},

which roughly controls the momentum. Next we introduce
the
symbols

\displaystyle a(x,\xi)=\frac{\xi^{2}}{g_{\lambda}(|x|)^{2}},\quad b(x,\xi)=\frac{\xi}{g_{\lambda}(|x|)}\cdot F(x),

(462)

where
F
is an arbitrary (henceforth fixed) vector field on
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}

extending
F(x)=\hat{x}=x/r
for
r=\lvert x\rvert\geq 1
. Of course the symbols
a
and
b
also have
\lambda
-dependence, but
for convenience this is here and henceforth omitted in the notation.
Let

\tilde{\chi}_{-},\tilde{\chi}_{+}\in C^{\infty}
be non-negative
functions obeying

\tilde{\chi}_{-}+\tilde{\chi}_{+}=1
and

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\tilde{\chi}_{-}\subseteq(-\infty,1-\bar{\sigma}],

(463a)

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}\tilde{\chi}_{+}\subseteq[1-2\bar{\sigma},\infty),

(463b)

where the number
\bar{\sigma}>0
needs to be taken sufficiently small,
depending on the parameter
\sigma
used in the previous subsection
(see (
445a
)) and properties of
the phase
\phi^{\pm}(x,\xi)
. Let
{\chi}_{-},{\chi}_{+}\in C^{\infty}
be non-negative
functions obeying

{\chi}_{-}+{\chi}_{+}=1
and

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}{\chi}_{-}\subseteq(-\infty,2),

(463c)

\displaystyle\operatorname{supp}{\chi}_{+}\subseteq(1,\infty).

(463d)

Introduce then symbols

\displaystyle\begin{split}\chi_{1}&={\chi}_{+}(a),\\
\chi_{2}^{\pm}&={\chi}_{-}(a)\tilde{\chi}_{-}(\pm b),\\
\chi_{3}^{\pm}&={\chi}_{-}(a)\tilde{\chi}_{+}(\pm b).\end{split}

(463e)

These symbols belong to a class of (parameter-depending)
pseudodifferential operators studied in
[
FS
,
DS1
]
. The ‘Planck
constant’ for this class is
\langle x\rangle^{-1}g_{\lambda}(|x|)^{-1}
, in
particular at most
\langle x\rangle^{\rho/2-1}
.

Note the partition of unity in terms
of corresponding (right-quantized) operators

\displaystyle 1={\operatorname{Op^{r}\!}}(\chi_{1})+{\operatorname{Op^{r}\!}}(\chi^{\pm}_{2})+{\operatorname{Op^{r}\!}}(\chi^{\pm}_{3}).

(464)

Recall
{\mathcal{L}}^{k}:={\mathcal{L}}(H^{k}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}),L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n}))
,
k\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Lemma 6.8
.

Let
\chi_{1}
,

\chi^{\pm}_{2}
and
\chi^{\pm}_{3}
be given by
(
463e
).

1.

For all
k\in[0,\infty)
and
\epsilon>0
,

(\langle x\rangle g_{\lambda})^{-k}\langle x\rangle^{-1/2-\epsilon}g_{\lambda}^{1/2}J_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)

(465a)

is a continuous
{\mathcal{L}}^{-k}
–valued function of

\lambda\in[\lambda_{0},\infty)
. With a bounding constant
independent of
\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}
,

g_{\lambda}^{1/2}J_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)\in\mathcal{L}\big(L^{2}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}),{\mathcal{B}}_{1/2}^{*}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\big).

2.

For all
k\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\epsilon>0
,

(\langle x\rangle g_{\lambda})^{-k}\langle x\rangle^{1/2-\epsilon}g_{\lambda}^{-1/2}{\operatorname{Op^{r}\!}}(\chi^{\pm}_{2})T_{{\mathrm{bd}}}^{\pm}(\lambda-\lambda_{0})

(465b)

is a continuous
{\mathcal{L}}^{-k}
–valued function of

\lambda\in[\lambda_{0},\infty)
.

3.

For all
k,m\in{\mathbb{R}}
,

\langle x\rangle^{m}{\operatorname{Op^{r}\!}}(\chi_{1}+\chi^{\pm}_{3})T_{{\mathrm{bd}}}^{\pm}(\lambda-\lambda_{0}),\,\,\langle x\rangle^{m}T_{\mathrm{pr}}^{\pm}(\lambda-\lambda_{0})\text{ and }\langle x\rangle^{m}{\operatorname{Op^{r}\!}}(\chi_{1})J_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)

(465c)

are continuous
{\mathcal{L}}^{-k}
–valued function of

\lambda\in[\lambda_{0},\infty)
.

This lemma will be used in combination with the following excerpts of
[
DS1
, Proposition 4.1]

(adapted to the present problem). Note that

{\operatorname{Op^{l}\!}}(\chi^{\pm}_{2})={\operatorname{Op^{r}\!}}(\chi^{\pm}_{2})^{*}
is given by left-quantization.

Lemma 6.9
.

Let
\Lambda
denote any
interval of the form
\Lambda=[\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}^{\prime}]
, and let

r^{\pm}_{\lambda}=r(\lambda-\lambda_{0}\pm\mathrm{i}0)
for
\lambda\in\Lambda
. Then the
following bounds hold unifomly in
\lambda\in\Lambda
, and the
corresponding
{\mathcal{L}}\big(L^{2}({\mathbb{R}}^{n})\big)
–valued functions are continuous.

1.

For all
\epsilon>0

there exists

C>0
such that

\displaystyle\|\langle x\rangle^{-\epsilon-1/2}g_{\lambda}^{1/2}r^{\pm}_{\lambda}g_{\lambda}^{1/2}\langle x\rangle^{-\epsilon-1/2}\|\leq C.

(466a)

2.

For all

s\geq 0
and
0\leq\epsilon<\epsilon^{\prime}
there exists
C>0

\displaystyle\|(\langle x\rangle g_{\lambda})^{s}\langle x\rangle^{\epsilon-1/2}g_{\lambda}^{1/2}{\operatorname{Op^{l}\!}}(\chi^{\pm}_{2})r^{\pm}_{\lambda}g_{\lambda}^{1/2}\langle x\rangle^{-\epsilon^{\prime}-1/2}(\langle x\rangle g_{\lambda})^{-s}\|\leq C.

(466b)

Now taking
\tau\in H^{k}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
with a sufficiently big
k

(actually any
k>0
suffices), we
will show
by combining (
428
) with Lemmas
6.8
and
6.9

that
S_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)\tau
is a well-defined element of

L^{2}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
, in fact with a continuous dependence of
\lambda\in I_{\delta}^{+}
.

We insert (
428
)
into the formula

\displaystyle S_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)\tau=-2\pi J^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}T^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)\tau+2\pi\mathrm{i}T^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)T^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)\tau.

(467)

Ignoring the
contribution from
\widecheck{T}^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)
(its contribution
is a ‘partial smoothing operator’
as exemplified in the beginning of the
proof of Theorem
6.10
) we obtain by using Lemma

6.8
that the first term
-2\pi J^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}T^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)\tau\approx-2\pi J_{N}^{+}(\lambda)^{*}T_{N}^{-}(\lambda)\tau\in L^{2}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
.

For
the second term in (
467
) we (again) ignore
terms containing
\widecheck{T}^{\pm}_{\alpha}(\lambda)
and consider only

{\varphi_{\alpha}}\otimes T_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)
using (
464
)
to
write

\displaystyle T_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)={\operatorname{Op^{r}\!}}(\chi_{1}+\chi^{\pm}_{3})T_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)+{\operatorname{Op^{r}\!}}(\chi^{\pm}_{2})T_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)=T_{1,3}^{\pm}(\lambda)+T_{2}^{\pm}(\lambda).

By Lemma
6.8

3
the first term has
strong decay, so let us consider the seemingly worse term given (up to a
constant) by

\displaystyle\Big(\varphi_{\alpha}\otimes

\displaystyle T_{2}^{+}(\lambda)\Big)^{*}R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)\varphi_{\alpha}\otimes T_{2}^{-}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=T_{2}^{+}(\lambda)^{*}S^{*}R(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)ST_{2}^{-}(\lambda).

The contribution from the
’leading terms’, cf. (
429
), are

\displaystyle T_{2}^{+}(\lambda)^{*}r^{+}_{\lambda}T_{2}^{-}(\lambda)\text{ and }T_{2}^{+}(\lambda)^{*}S^{*}\breve{R}(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)\Pi^{\prime}ST_{2}^{-}(\lambda),

respectively. The second term vanishes. For the first
term we
use Lemma
6.9

2
for the case of ‘+’ and see that indeed
T_{2}^{+}(\lambda)^{*}r^{+}_{\lambda}T_{2}^{-}(\lambda)\tau\approx T_{N}^{+}(\lambda)^{*}r^{+}_{\lambda}T_{N}^{-}(\lambda)\tau
is a well-defined element of

L^{2}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
.

Of course there are other terms to consider, and for some of those
also Lemma
6.9

1
is needed. We can
check all other terms (cf. the proof of Theorem
6.10
) and see
that they are well-defined with a continuous dependence of

\lambda
. Furthermore we can write the formula for the action by

S_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)
as

\displaystyle S_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)\tau=-2\pi W_{\alpha}^{+}(\lambda)^{*}T_{\alpha}^{-}(\lambda)\tau,

(468)

and this is a continuous
L^{2}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
–valued function of
\lambda\in I^{+}_{\delta}
for
\tau\in H^{k}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
,
k>0
.

Next we will examine the degree of regularity that is needed on
\tau
,
measured by the size of
k
. Let

\displaystyle S_{w}(\lambda-\lambda_{0})

\displaystyle=-2\pi J_{N}^{+}(\lambda)^{*}T_{N}^{-}(\lambda)+2\pi\mathrm{i}T_{N}^{+}(\lambda)^{*}r(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)T_{N}^{-}(\lambda);\quad\lambda\geq\lambda_{0}.

Note that
S_{w}(\cdot)
is the scattering
matrix for the one-body problem given by (
456
).
We will
examine the quantity

\displaystyle\widecheck{S}_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)\tau=S_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)\tau-S_{w}(\lambda-\lambda_{0})\tau.

By the above
preliminary investigation the term
S_{w}(\cdot)

is the ‘leading term’, and we know
that
k=0
works for this term
although
k=\epsilon
for any
\epsilon>0
is neeeded for
operator-continuity, see (
457
) and the discussion there. So
we expect well-definedness of
\widecheck{S}_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)\tau
and continuous dependence of
\lambda

with less regularity imposed on
\tau
. In fact we can show
that
k=-\epsilon
for a computable
\epsilon>0
works for this term,
even for operator-continuity. More generally we have the following
result given in terms of any
k\geq 0
satisfying one of the options

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&k+1/2+(1/2-k)\rho/2<1/2+\rho/2,\quad\,k\in[0,1/2],\\
&k+1/2<1/2+\rho/2,\quad\,k>1/2,\end{cases}

(469)

or equivalently stated, one of the options

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&0\leq k\leq 1/2\quad\text{ and }\quad k<\rho(4-2\rho)^{-1},\\
&1/2<k<\rho/2,\quad\,\rho>1.\end{cases}

(470)

Theorem 6.10
.

Suppose (
459
), i.e.
m_{a}=1
. For any
k\geq 0
obeying (
470
) the operator

\displaystyle\widecheck{S}_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)=S_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)-S_{w}(\lambda-\lambda_{0})\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(H^{-k}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}),H^{k}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})\big)

with a continuous dependence of
\lambda\in I_{\delta}^{+}
.

Remarks 6.11
.

1.

The number
s=1/2+\rho/2
on the
right-hand side in (
469
) is the number appearing in
(
475
). The left-hand side
comes from estimating the factor
\langle x\rangle^{k+1/2}g^{k-\frac{1}{2}}
; it is uniformly bounded by the power
C{\langle x\rangle}^{c}
with
c
given as the expression appearing to the left in
(
469
). If we replace
I_{\delta}^{+}
by
I_{\delta}^{+}\setminus\{\lambda_{0}\}
in Theorem
6.10
any
k<\rho/2
suffices.

2.

We assumed
m_{a}=1
. However the interested
reader may check that if
m_{a}>1
, then Theorem
6.10

remains valid.
Moreover the off-diagonal elements of
the scattering matrix, denoted by
S_{\beta\alpha}(\lambda)
with

\alpha\neq\beta
, fulfill the same assertion as the one for
\widecheck{S}_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)
in Theorem
6.10
.

3.

Under the additional condition that
\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
one
can show that
\widecheck{S}_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)
is bounded on any of the
spaces
H^{l}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
,
l\in{\mathbb{R}}
, with a continuous dependence of

\lambda\in I_{\delta}^{+}
, in fact it is partially
smoothing. More precisely one can show in this case that for all

l\in{\mathbb{R}}
the
operator
\widecheck{S}_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(H^{l-k},H^{l+k}\big)
with a continuous dependence on

\lambda\in I_{\delta}^{+}
. This is for any
k\geq 0
obeying
(
470
), in fact for a computable bigger
k
using
(
475
) below
for
s=1+\rho
(recall from the discussion after
(
426
) that the latter boundedness condition is fulfilled for

\lambda_{0}=\Sigma_{2}
). The proof consists of combining ideas from Subsection
19.2.4
with
[
DS1
, Proposition 4.1]
, however we shall not
elaborate. In particular since
S_{w}(\lambda-\lambda_{0})
has a similar
property up a loss of an ‘
\epsilon
-smoothness’, cf.
[
DS1
, Theorem 7.2]
, we
conclude (more precisely stated) that for all
l\in{\mathbb{R}}
and

\epsilon>0
the operator
S_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(H^{l},H^{l-\epsilon}\big)
with a continuous dependence of

\lambda\in I_{\delta}^{+}
. Note that
this implies that
S_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)\tau\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
for
\lambda\in I_{\delta}^{+}
and
\tau\in C^{\infty}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})
.

Proof of Theorem
6.10
.

We need to treat the term
-2\pi J^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}\widecheck{T}^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)
left out when
discussing
-2\pi J^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}T^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)

above. (Note that
-2\pi J^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}\big({\varphi_{\alpha}}\otimes T_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)\big)

already is subtracted in the definition of
\widecheck{S}_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)
.) Writing
J^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}\widecheck{T}^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)=J^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}\Pi\widecheck{T}^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)
we can invoke (
461
).

The remaining terms of
\widecheck{S}_{\alpha\alpha}(\lambda)

fall into three disjoint groups according to whether there is a dependence of:

1.

\breve{R}(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0))
, and no dependence of
r_{\lambda}^{+}\big(1+v^{+}_{\lambda}r^{+}_{\lambda}\big)^{-1}
.

2.

r_{\lambda}^{+}\big(1+v^{+}_{\lambda}r^{+}_{\lambda}\big)^{-1}
, and no dependence of
\breve{R}(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0))
.

3.

\breve{R}(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0))
as well as a dependence of
r_{\lambda}^{+}\big(1+v^{+}_{\lambda}r^{+}_{\lambda}\big)^{-1}
.

1
.  We need to treat

\displaystyle T^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}\breve{R}(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)\Pi^{\prime}T^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)=\widecheck{T}^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}\Pi^{\prime}\breve{R}(\lambda+\mathrm{i}0)\Pi^{\prime}\widecheck{T}^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda).

Due to (
461
) we need
to bound

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle^{-1/2-\rho+\epsilon}J_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}^{-k}={\mathcal{L}}^{-k}({\mathbf{X}})\quad\text{ for some }\epsilon>0.

(471)

By Lemma
6.8

1
this requires

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&k+(1/2-k)\rho/2<\rho,\quad\,k\in(0,1/2],\\
&k<\rho,\quad\,k>1/2,\end{cases}

(472)

which is weaker than (
470
).

2
.  We need to treat

\displaystyle T^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}Sr^{+}_{\lambda}\big(1+v^{+}_{\lambda}r^{+}_{\lambda}\big)^{-1}S^{*}T^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)-T_{N}^{+}(\lambda)^{*}r^{+}_{\lambda}T_{N}^{-}(\lambda)

\displaystyle=\Big(T^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}Sr^{+}_{\lambda}S^{*}T^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)-T_{N}^{+}(\lambda)^{*}r^{+}_{\lambda}T_{N}^{-}(\lambda)\Big)

\displaystyle-T^{+}_{\alpha}(\lambda)^{*}Sr^{+}_{\lambda}\big(1+v^{+}_{\lambda}r^{+}_{\lambda}\big)^{-1}v^{+}_{\lambda}r^{+}_{\lambda}S^{*}T^{-}_{\alpha}(\lambda)

Due to (
461
) the first term is a smoothing
operator, i.e. in
{\mathcal{L}}(H^{-l}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}),H^{l}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1}))
for any
l\in{\mathbb{R}}
. We shall frequently in the
rest of the proof use

\displaystyle 1=\langle x\rangle^{-s}\langle x\rangle^{s};\quad s=1/2+\rho/2.

(473)

For the second term it suffices, due to
(
427
) and (
473
), to bound

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle^{-s}r^{\mp}_{\lambda}S^{*}T_{\alpha}^{\pm}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}^{-k}.

(474)

Here we used that

\displaystyle\quad v^{+}_{\lambda}\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}_{-s},L^{2}_{s}).

(475)

(We could do better using (
459
), cf. the discussion after (
426
),
but we prefer to use a method that more or less obviously generalizes to
the case where
m_{a}>1
, c.f. Remark

6.11

2
.) To show
(
474
) it suffices due to (
461
) and Lemma

6.8

3
to bound

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle^{-s}r^{\mp}_{\lambda}T_{2}^{\pm}(\lambda)\in{\mathcal{L}}^{-k},

(476)

or equivalently that

\displaystyle T_{N}^{\pm}(\lambda)^{*}{\operatorname{Op^{l}\!}}(\chi_{2}^{\pm})r^{\pm}_{\lambda}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L_{s}^{2},H^{k}({\mathbb{S}}^{n-1})\big).

(477)

Due to Lemma

6.8
it suffices in turn to show that

\displaystyle(\langle x\rangle g_{\lambda})^{k}\langle x\rangle^{\epsilon-1/2}g_{\lambda}^{1/2}{\operatorname{Op^{l}\!}}(\chi_{2}^{\pm})r^{\pm}_{\lambda}\in{\mathcal{L}}\big(L_{s}^{2},L^{2}\big)\,\text{ for some }\epsilon>0.

(478)

We need to bound

\displaystyle(\langle x\rangle g_{\lambda})^{k}\langle x\rangle^{\epsilon-1/2}g_{\lambda}^{1/2}{\operatorname{Op^{l}\!}}(\chi_{2}^{\pm})r^{\pm}_{\lambda}g_{\lambda}^{1/2}

\displaystyle\langle x\rangle^{-1/2-2\epsilon}(\langle x\rangle g_{\lambda})^{-k}

\displaystyle\Big(g_{\lambda}^{-1/2}\langle x\rangle^{1/2+2\epsilon}(\langle x\rangle g_{\lambda})^{k}\langle x\rangle^{-s}\Big)\in{\mathcal{L}}(L^{2}).

Using (
466b
) we need to check that the last factor is
bounded. This amounts to

\displaystyle\begin{cases}&k+1/2+2\epsilon+(1/2-k)\rho/2\leq s,\quad\,k\in(0,1/2],\\
&k+1/2+2\epsilon\leq s,\quad\,k>1/2,\end{cases}

(479)

which indeed is fulfilled for small
\epsilon>0
